to the reason behind it.

Learn Emerald Housing through its business rules, service boundaries, runtime flows, and design choices. Use the same guide to read the code, run the project, and prepare your interview explanation.

How to read this guide

Choose your starting point.

Learn the projectRead the chapters in order.Scope → implementation → runtime → operations.Prepare for an interviewStart with the short answers.Revise the story, then investigate the follow-up questions.Work hands onRun one workflow at a time.Choose a runtime, authenticate, call an API, and inspect evidence.

Each chapter gives the explanation first, then groups the implementation references and scenario questions beside that topic. Configuration describes the repository; recorded verification results describe a particular run.

New questions and practical checks

Find the concept. Follow its project implementation.

These answers explain the terms, locate the implementation, and show what to check. Saved test results are dated evidence; verification instructions are not a claim that a new live test has run.

Quick revision

Eight answers to keep in your own words.

Use these as opening answers. Follow the linked chapter when the interviewer asks how the behavior is implemented.

01

Java and Spring

Spring Boot configures the service infrastructure; the application code defines authorization, business rules, transactions, and failure behavior. Java 21 features support DTOs and transformations without changing the blocking nature of JPA work.

Read the explanation →
02

Service ownership

Society owns building data and Member owns people, memberships, accounts, and access. Each owns its database. Gateway provides one entry path; service-to-service validation uses an explicit contract.

Read the explanation →
03

Security

Gateway authenticates sessions; backend permissions and record scopes authorize operations. CSRF protects cookie-authenticated writes. A valid session is not permission to read every record.

Read the explanation →
04

Data correctness

Transactions keep local business and audit writes together. Row locks coordinate competing updates, unique constraints guard integrity, and version checks detect stale edits. Cross-service calls do not create a distributed transaction.

Read the explanation →
05

The two Redis workloads

The main Redis holds sessions, rate limits, and Member locks. Separate cache Redis holds disposable Society reads and load coordination. Different eviction policies keep cache pressure apart from session and lock state.

Read the explanation →
06

Dependency failure

Timeouts bound waiting, selected retries attempt recovery, a circuit breaker limits repeated failing calls, and a bulkhead caps concurrent dependency work. Cache fallback is separately bounded.

Read the explanation →
07

Deployment

Images package code; Deployments manage Pods; Services supply stable DNS. Probes and graceful shutdown support availability. Replicas increase execution capacity and also increase demand on shared dependencies.

Read the explanation →
08

Design reasoning

Start with scope and ownership, trace a concrete request, identify a failure or race, and explain the mechanism and its limit. Distinguish implemented source, dated runtime evidence, and future design proposals.

Read the explanation →

Chapter 01

The project and its users

Business scope, ownership, roles, and the project story.

The business problem

Emerald Housing manages the people and homes in the EMERALD society. Society Service owns the society, floors, and flats. Member Service owns members, flat-membership history, accounts, and access administration. Gateway is the common HTTP entry point. This division keeps the owner of each business record clear.

The repository also supports registered ownership, family relationships, scoped resident access, audit history, searchable directories, and conditional updates. The detailed feature and permission inventories below explain their rules. Society and floor data are seeded; the current application is scoped to one society.

A project explanation you can say aloud

“I built a Java service-based housing application. Society owns building data, Member owns people and assignments, and Gateway handles the entry path and sessions. Each service owns its database. We use Redis for shared sessions, rate limits, membership locks, and disposable Society caches. Database constraints, scoped permissions, auditing, and bounded dependency calls protect the important workflows.”

When discussing your contribution, name the part you implemented and explain one request from entry to persistence. Use the source references to support the details you claim.

Understand the users before the technology

Actor or conceptMeaning in this applicationBoundary to explain
AdministratorMaintains permitted business records and application access.Administrative authority is checked by the relevant operation.
Committee userHas the grants assigned to its enabled role.A role name is not a substitute for checking permissions.
ResidentUses self or owned-flat scopes linked to a member.Being authenticated does not grant access to every record.
MembershipA dated association between a member and a flat.History and current eligibility are different concerns.
Registered owner and familyBusiness relationships used by the newer access workflows.Ownership, family membership, and account identity are related but distinct.
Current scope

Multi-society onboarding, billing, and database sharding are design extensions rather than completed features. Treat each extension as a separate requirement and migration project.

Keep reading

Implementation references and scenarios

Implementation referenceCurrent business requirements and implemented features

Current business requirements and implemented features

Emerald Housing manages members and flats for one housing society: EMERALD. Administrators maintain flats and members, assign people to flats as owners, tenants, or residents, and retain membership history with controlled access.

This inventory describes the current repository source. It does not confirm which version is deployed.

Implemented business functionality

Business areaImplemented functionality
Society informationView society code, name, and floor count. EMERALD and floors 1–18 are seeded through database migrations.
Floor directoryList floors, view an individual floor, and list flats on a floor.
Flat managementCreate flats, update flat number and floor, view flats, and deactivate flats.
Member managementRegister members with name, mobile number, and optional email; view, update, and deactivate members.
Member validationPrevent duplicate mobile numbers among active members; validate and normalize member details.
Flat assignmentsLink a member to a flat as OWNER, TENANT, or RESIDENT, with a start date.
Assignment validationRequire an active member and an existing active flat; prevent duplicate active member–flat–relationship assignments.
Membership historyEnd an assignment with an end date and retrieve history by member or flat. Ending an assignment preserves its record.
Login and accountsSession-based login and logout. Administrators can create accounts, change permissions, and enable or disable accounts.
Role-based accessSupport ADMIN, SOCIETY_ADMIN, COMMITTEE, and RESIDENT roles, with record-level access checks.
Search and browsingSearch members by name, mobile, or email and flats by flat number. Filter by status and, for flats, floor. Support pagination and sorting, including cursor pagination for members and flats.
Audit historyRecord member, flat, membership, and account changes, including the actor and before/after values. Administrators can retrieve audit history.
Conflicting updatesVersion checks and optional conditional updates help prevent users from overwriting concurrent changes.
Repeated submissionsMember creation supports idempotency keys to avoid duplicate creation when the same request is retried.

Who can do what?

RoleBusiness access
ADMIN / SOCIETY_ADMINManage business records and accounts; view audit history.
COMMITTEERead society, floor, flat, and member information.
RESIDENTRead society and floors, their own linked member record and membership history, and currently assigned flats. A flat's membership list is restricted to their own entries.

Technical support for these workflows

The repository includes separate PostgreSQL databases, Redis caching and sessions, gateway routing and rate limiting, service-call resilience, membership locking, Docker and Kubernetes configuration, and observability configuration.

Current scope and limitations

  • The application serves one society; multi-society onboarding is not implemented.
  • Society and floor information is seeded. The reviewed services expose reads for these records.
  • Account management, audit history, server search, and conditional updates are implemented in the backend. Existing frontend screens do not yet expose these features, according to the backend documentation.
  • S3 uploads, document management, billing, maintenance payments, complaints, visitor management, and email/SMS notifications are not currently implemented. S3 document uploads are a proposed addition.

Source references: Backend access and query features, Flat service, Member service, and Membership persistence and history.

Implementation referenceSimple word guide

Simple word guide

WordEveryday meaningProject example
MicroserviceA separate program with one clear job.Society Service handles building facts.
APIA web address and rules for asking a program to do something.GET /api/v1/floors asks for floors.
DatabaseA place that remembers data after a program stops.member_db.
PodOne running copy of an app in Kubernetes.Two Society Pods mean two copies.
ClusterThe environment Kubernetes manages.The local kind cluster.
NamespaceA named project area inside the cluster.emerald-housing.
Kubernetes ServiceA stable network name that points to ready Pods.society-service.
DeploymentAn instruction saying how many copies should run.Society Deployment requests 2.
ReplicaOne copy of an application.3 replicas means aim for 3 copies.
GatewayA front desk that forwards a request to the right app.Member URL goes to Member Service.
MigrationA saved database change, such as making a table.Flyway applies SQL files.
Implementation referenceImportant terms, explained with everyday analogies

Important terms, explained with everyday analogies

Use these comparisons as a starting point. The project column connects each term to what this repository actually configures or implements.

TermEveryday analogyWhat it means in this project
ScalingLike a shop preparing for a lunch rush: add more checkout counters or make one counter faster.Increase system capacity to handle more work. The repository has multiple app Pods and Kubernetes HPA configuration.
Horizontal scalingOpen more checkout counters staffed by more cashiers.Run more copies of a service in separate Pods. Each copy adds its own database connection pool.
Vertical scalingGive one cashier a faster register and more workspace.Give one server or Pod more CPU or memory. This project also sets Kubernetes resource requests and limits.
ThroughputHow many customers the shop serves in an hour.The number of requests the system completes per second or minute. It is the amount of work completed over time.
LatencyHow long one customer waits from joining the line until checkout finishes.The time one request takes from the caller’s view. A system can have high throughput while some requests still have high latency.
PodOne working station in the shop.Kubernetes’ smallest usual unit for running an app. A Society Pod runs one Society Service copy.
ReplicaAnother copy of the same shop station with the same job.An additional application Pod. A database read replica is different: it is a copied database that can serve reads and may lag behind its primary.
DeploymentA staffing plan that says how many stations should be open and replaces a station that breaks.A Kubernetes object that maintains the requested Pods and performs application updates.
Kubernetes ServiceA stable help desk name that directs people to an available station.A stable in-cluster address such as society-service; it sends traffic to matching ready Pods.
HPAA manager who opens or closes checkout stations after checking how busy the workers are.The Horizontal Pod Autoscaler changes Pod count using metrics such as CPU, within configured minimum and maximum values. It needs a metrics provider.
Rolling updateReplace shop stations one at a time while keeping enough open for customers.Kubernetes gradually replaces old Pods with new ones. This repo configures rollout availability and surge settings; readiness controls when a Pod receives traffic.
Readiness probeThe green sign that says a station is ready to serve the next customer.Kubernetes uses readiness to decide whether a Pod should receive normal Service traffic.
Liveness probeA check that tells the manager whether a station is stuck and needs a restart.Kubernetes restarts a container when its liveness check repeatedly fails. It should not fail just because another service is down.
Resource request and limitA reserved amount of workspace, plus a safety boundary the station cannot exceed.A request helps Kubernetes schedule CPU or memory; a limit caps usage. CPU can be throttled at its limit, while a memory limit can cause a restart.
Load balancingDirect customers to several open checkout stations instead of one.Spread requests across ready service Pods. Kubernetes Services do this for traffic inside the cluster.
Connection poolA small set of reusable phone lines between the app and its database.HikariCP keeps database connections ready for reuse. Pool size is per Pod, so scaling Pods also increases the possible total connections.
Read replicaA photocopy of the shop ledger used for lookups; the copy may take a moment to receive the latest changes.An optional PostgreSQL copy can serve read-only transactions. The application supports routing, but the replica server must be provisioned separately and replica routing is off by default.
CacheA nearby copy of frequently used menu information so staff do not fetch the master book each time.Redis temporarily stores selected Society floor and flat responses. PostgreSQL remains the source of truth.
Cache-asideCheck the quick reference card first; if it is missing, read the master book and make a new card.A read checks Redis, loads PostgreSQL on a miss, and stores the response in Redis for later reads.
TTL (time to live)An expiry date printed on a quick reference card.How long a cached value can be used before it expires and needs to be loaded again.
Cache invalidationTake an old reference card off the counter after the master book changes.After a database update commits, the app evicts the affected cache entries so a later read reloads current data.
Cache stampedeMany staff discover the same missing card at once and all crowd the master book.Concurrent cache misses can trigger duplicate database reads. This project coordinates refreshes across Society Pods with Redis-based miss protection.
Rate limitingA turnstile that lets only a set number of people through in a time window.Gateway uses shared Redis-backed limits to cap request rates and protect services from bursts.
TimeoutA caller decides how long to wait before leaving a phone call that is not answered.A bound on connecting to or waiting for another service. Member’s Society lookup uses configured timeouts.
Retry with backoffIf a line is briefly busy, wait a little before trying again instead of redialing rapidly.Selected transient Society read failures can be retried a limited number of times with increasing delays. Membership writes are not blindly retried.
Circuit breakerAn electrical breaker that stops repeated attempts while a faulty circuit is being repaired.Member Service temporarily stops calling Society after enough failures, then later allows calls to test recovery.
BulkheadWatertight compartments keep one leak from sinking the whole ship.A concurrency limit keeps slow Society calls from occupying every request worker in Member Service.
Distributed lockA shared sign-out sheet gives one worker at a time permission to update a member’s record.Member Pods coordinate membership updates through Redis. A PostgreSQL row lock and unique constraint also protect database rules.
Idempotency keyA receipt number that tells staff “this is the same order again,” so it is not processed twice.A client can safely repeat a member-creation request with the same key; the service reuses the original result instead of creating another member.
Database shardingSplit one very large filing system among several offices, with rules for which office owns each file.Not implemented here. The services own separate databases, but each service’s data is not split across shards.

Some terms describe features this repository uses; others, such as database sharding, are included for interview context and are marked as not implemented.

Source documentdocs/dynamic-access-and-family-management.md

docs/dynamic-access-and-family-management.md

Dynamic access, registered owners, and families

This phase extends the existing Member, Society, and Gateway applications. Member owns users, role assignments, permissions, ownership memberships, family records, and their audit events. Society continues to own flats and floors. The deployment remains a single society, EMERALD.

Start and use the portal

Build and restart all three applications together using the repository's existing local or Kubernetes setup. Member automatically applies Flyway migration V6 to its configured database at startup. Existing migrations and passwords are unchanged. Sign in again after updating the applications.

Open /portal.html on the Gateway origin, for example http://localhost:8080/portal.html when the Gateway uses port 8080. The portal uses the existing cookie session and CSRF flow. No frontend package installation or separate web server is required.

  1. Sign in with the existing provisioned administrator.
  2. In Owners & flats, register an owner, select their flat, and provide initial login credentials. The person, OWNER membership, and FLAT_OWNER account are created atomically.
  3. The owner signs in and opens My family to add or update family records. A family record does not create a user account.
  4. In Roles, an administrator creates a role and chooses permissions and their scopes. In Users, assign one or more roles to an account.
  5. For a family login, an administrator creates/links a person record, links it to the family record, and creates a FAMILY_MEMBER account for that person. The link is an administrative operation; owners cannot attach arbitrary existing identities.

Seeded roles

RoleInitial access
ADMIN, SOCIETY_ADMINAll initially implemented permissions at society scope. These two recovery roles cannot be edited or disabled.
COMMITTEERead society information, members, and flats.
FLAT_OWNERRead own person/assigned flats and manage family records under their current OWNER memberships.
RESIDENTRead own person/assigned flats and explicitly linked family record.
FAMILY_MEMBERRead own person, permitted flat information, and explicitly linked family record.
TENANTRead own person/assigned flats and explicitly linked family record.

Custom roles are data, not a Java enum. An account can have multiple enabled roles; their grants are combined. Disabling a role leaves its assignments in place but removes its effective grants. Re-enabling restores them. Accounts with no enabled role cannot authenticate.

Any role granting SELF or OWN_FLATS access requires an active linked person, including custom roles. This requirement follows the configured scope rather than a fixed list of role names. Ownership start dates are evaluated in UTC consistently with existing membership access.

Role codes and permission codes are stable identifiers. Update descriptions, grants, status, or assignments instead of renaming codes. To retire a role, disable it. To retire a permission, remove it from roles; audit history is retained. Existing single-role account requests remain accepted using role; new requests should use roles.

Scopes and business rules

  • SOCIETY: all applicable records in EMERALD.
  • SELF: the linked person, or an explicitly linked active family record for FAMILY_VIEW.
  • OWN_FLATS: permitted flat records; for family operations, only records belonging to the actor's own active OWNER membership. Joint owners manage the family records registered under their respective ownerships.

MEMBER_VIEW accepts SOCIETY or SELF. FLAT_VIEW accepts SOCIETY or OWN_FLATS. FAMILY_VIEW accepts all three scopes; family mutations accept SOCIETY or OWN_FLATS. Other implemented permissions require SOCIETY. One role assigns one scope to each permission; multiple roles can combine scopes.

Backend checks enforce the permissions independently of visible controls. A role name alone grants no network access. Member refreshes enabled roles, grants, linked-person status, and flat assignments during Gateway session validation on subsequent requests. The existing trusted internal tokens are still required.

Only users granted owner-registration and person/account administration permissions can register an owner. The seeded owner role cannot create registered persons, users, roles, or ownership assignments. Adding FLAT_OWNER to an account does not create ownership: a current OWNER membership must exist. Owners may hold multiple flats; multiple owners may be linked to a flat.

Ending ownership immediately removes its current access, including linked family flat access, even if a future end date is recorded. Transfers are immediate and must use a date no later than today. They end the selected previous ownership and create the new ownership in one transaction. Joint ownerships are independent: select each outgoing ownership that must end. Old family records remain attached to historical ownership and visible to authorized society staff. They are not silently transferred to the incoming owner. An outgoing owner retains any access independently granted by other memberships or staff roles.

Family records support names, free-text relationships, optional mobile/email, optional administrator-controlled person links, active status, and versions. Family updates and deactivation require the last-read version; stale changes return 409. Inactive records cannot be edited. Account and role edits are versioned too.

Self-permission changes are rejected. Account changes and linked-person deactivation cannot remove the last usable built-in administrator. Built-in administrator-role definitions are protected; create custom roles for configurable administration. Access changes and family/ownership mutations use a shared database transaction lock, plus existing membership locks, to serialize changes. Audit writes commit or roll back with business writes. Passwords are excluded from account audit snapshots.

API reference

MethodPathPurpose
GET/api/v1/accounts/meCurrent account, assigned roles, effective permissions, and permitted flat IDs
GET / POST/api/v1/accountsPage accounts / create a login
GET/api/v1/accounts/{id}Read account with version
PUT/api/v1/accounts/{id}/permissionsSet roles, person link, enabled status, and expected version
GET / POST/api/v1/access/rolesList / create roles
PUT/api/v1/access/roles/{code}Edit description, enabled status, and grants with expected version
GET / POST/api/v1/access/permissionsList / register permission definitions
PUT/api/v1/access/permissions/{code}Update a permission description without changing its stable code
GET / POST/api/v1/ownersPage scoped ownerships / register owner, ownership, and account
POST/api/v1/owners/transferAtomic ownership transfer to an existing active person
GET / POST/api/v1/familiesPage scoped families / add family record
PUT/api/v1/families/{id}Update details with expected version
PATCH/api/v1/families/{id}/deactivateDeactivate with expected version
GET/api/v1/members/auditExisting audit timeline, now also including roles, permissions, and families

Accounts, owners, and families support zero-based page and size (1–100). Family lists accept flatId. Existing member/flat search, membership assignment/end APIs, and society audit endpoints remain available.

Example custom role:

{
  "code": "FAMILY_REVIEWER",
  "description": "Review the society family directory",
  "enabled": true,
  "version": 0,
  "grants": [{"permission": "FAMILY_VIEW", "scope": "SOCIETY"}]
}

Example account creation:

{
  "username": "reviewer",
  "password": "REPLACE_WITH_PRIVATE_PASSWORD",
  "roles": ["COMMITTEE", "FAMILY_REVIEWER"],
  "societyCode": "EMERALD",
  "memberId": null
}

New role definitions and assignments need no code change. Registering a brand-new permission code adds catalogue data; implementing a new business action still requires code that checks that permission. The system does not execute arbitrary permission definitions or provide a general policy language. Built-in administrators receive the initially shipped catalogue; explicitly grant new permissions to a configurable role when adding new actions.

Validation

Run Maven tests in Member and Society, and use the Member wrapper with -f ../../infrastructure/api-gateway/pom.xml test for Gateway. Existing Docker-based tests retain their normal prerequisites.

For the new real-database integration suite without Docker:

powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File scripts/test-access-local.ps1

The script uses locally installed PostgreSQL 18 by default (-PostgresBin overrides its bin directory), initializes a fresh temporary cluster, chooses a local port, runs tests in a unique schema, and stops that cluster in finally. Logs and test data remain in the printed temporary directory for diagnosis. It does not connect to the application's databases. The integration test class is skipped during normal Maven runs unless access.test.jdbc-url is supplied.

The portal has DOM component tests for administration screens, owner-only screens, form payloads, CSRF headers, and sign-out. Install their optional test dependency in a temporary directory:

npm.cmd install --prefix "$env:TEMP/emerald-portal-dom-tests" --no-audit --no-fund jsdom
node scripts/test-portal.cjs

These simulate the DOM and API responses; they do not replace visual browser testing or a full Redis/Gateway/backend deployment test.

Original Markdown source
# Dynamic access, registered owners, and families

This phase extends the existing Member, Society, and Gateway applications. Member owns users, role assignments, permissions, ownership memberships, family records, and their audit events. Society continues to own flats and floors. The deployment remains a single society, `EMERALD`.

## Start and use the portal

Build and restart all three applications together using the repository's existing local or Kubernetes setup. Member automatically applies Flyway migration **V6** to its configured database at startup. Existing migrations and passwords are unchanged. Sign in again after updating the applications.

Open **`/portal.html` on the Gateway origin**, for example `http://localhost:8080/portal.html` when the Gateway uses port 8080. The portal uses the existing cookie session and CSRF flow. No frontend package installation or separate web server is required.

1. Sign in with the existing provisioned administrator.
2. In **Owners & flats**, register an owner, select their flat, and provide initial login credentials. The person, OWNER membership, and FLAT_OWNER account are created atomically.
3. The owner signs in and opens **My family** to add or update family records. A family record does not create a user account.
4. In **Roles**, an administrator creates a role and chooses permissions and their scopes. In **Users**, assign one or more roles to an account.
5. For a family login, an administrator creates/links a person record, links it to the family record, and creates a FAMILY_MEMBER account for that person. The link is an administrative operation; owners cannot attach arbitrary existing identities.

## Seeded roles

| Role | Initial access |
| --- | --- |
| ADMIN, SOCIETY_ADMIN | All initially implemented permissions at society scope. These two recovery roles cannot be edited or disabled. |
| COMMITTEE | Read society information, members, and flats. |
| FLAT_OWNER | Read own person/assigned flats and manage family records under their current OWNER memberships. |
| RESIDENT | Read own person/assigned flats and explicitly linked family record. |
| FAMILY_MEMBER | Read own person, permitted flat information, and explicitly linked family record. |
| TENANT | Read own person/assigned flats and explicitly linked family record. |

Custom roles are data, not a Java enum. An account can have multiple enabled roles; their grants are combined. Disabling a role leaves its assignments in place but removes its effective grants. Re-enabling restores them. Accounts with no enabled role cannot authenticate.

Any role granting SELF or OWN_FLATS access requires an active linked person, including custom roles. This requirement follows the configured scope rather than a fixed list of role names. Ownership start dates are evaluated in UTC consistently with existing membership access.

Role codes and permission codes are stable identifiers. Update descriptions, grants, status, or assignments instead of renaming codes. To retire a role, disable it. To retire a permission, remove it from roles; audit history is retained. Existing single-role account requests remain accepted using `role`; new requests should use `roles`.

## Scopes and business rules

- `SOCIETY`: all applicable records in EMERALD.
- `SELF`: the linked person, or an explicitly linked active family record for FAMILY_VIEW.
- `OWN_FLATS`: permitted flat records; for family operations, only records belonging to the actor's own active OWNER membership. Joint owners manage the family records registered under their respective ownerships.

MEMBER_VIEW accepts SOCIETY or SELF. FLAT_VIEW accepts SOCIETY or OWN_FLATS. FAMILY_VIEW accepts all three scopes; family mutations accept SOCIETY or OWN_FLATS. Other implemented permissions require SOCIETY. One role assigns one scope to each permission; multiple roles can combine scopes.

Backend checks enforce the permissions independently of visible controls. A role name alone grants no network access. Member refreshes enabled roles, grants, linked-person status, and flat assignments during Gateway session validation on subsequent requests. The existing trusted internal tokens are still required.

Only users granted owner-registration and person/account administration permissions can register an owner. The seeded owner role cannot create registered persons, users, roles, or ownership assignments. Adding FLAT_OWNER to an account does not create ownership: a current OWNER membership must exist. Owners may hold multiple flats; multiple owners may be linked to a flat.

Ending ownership immediately removes its current access, including linked family flat access, even if a future end date is recorded. Transfers are immediate and must use a date no later than today. They end the selected previous ownership and create the new ownership in one transaction. Joint ownerships are independent: select each outgoing ownership that must end. Old family records remain attached to historical ownership and visible to authorized society staff. They are not silently transferred to the incoming owner. An outgoing owner retains any access independently granted by other memberships or staff roles.

Family records support names, free-text relationships, optional mobile/email, optional administrator-controlled person links, active status, and versions. Family updates and deactivation require the last-read `version`; stale changes return 409. Inactive records cannot be edited. Account and role edits are versioned too.

Self-permission changes are rejected. Account changes and linked-person deactivation cannot remove the last usable built-in administrator. Built-in administrator-role definitions are protected; create custom roles for configurable administration. Access changes and family/ownership mutations use a shared database transaction lock, plus existing membership locks, to serialize changes. Audit writes commit or roll back with business writes. Passwords are excluded from account audit snapshots.

## API reference

| Method | Path | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| GET | `/api/v1/accounts/me` | Current account, assigned roles, effective permissions, and permitted flat IDs |
| GET / POST | `/api/v1/accounts` | Page accounts / create a login |
| GET | `/api/v1/accounts/{id}` | Read account with version |
| PUT | `/api/v1/accounts/{id}/permissions` | Set roles, person link, enabled status, and expected version |
| GET / POST | `/api/v1/access/roles` | List / create roles |
| PUT | `/api/v1/access/roles/{code}` | Edit description, enabled status, and grants with expected version |
| GET / POST | `/api/v1/access/permissions` | List / register permission definitions |
| PUT | `/api/v1/access/permissions/{code}` | Update a permission description without changing its stable code |
| GET / POST | `/api/v1/owners` | Page scoped ownerships / register owner, ownership, and account |
| POST | `/api/v1/owners/transfer` | Atomic ownership transfer to an existing active person |
| GET / POST | `/api/v1/families` | Page scoped families / add family record |
| PUT | `/api/v1/families/{id}` | Update details with expected version |
| PATCH | `/api/v1/families/{id}/deactivate` | Deactivate with expected version |
| GET | `/api/v1/members/audit` | Existing audit timeline, now also including roles, permissions, and families |

Accounts, owners, and families support zero-based `page` and `size` (1–100). Family lists accept `flatId`. Existing member/flat search, membership assignment/end APIs, and society audit endpoints remain available.

Example custom role:

```json
{
  "code": "FAMILY_REVIEWER",
  "description": "Review the society family directory",
  "enabled": true,
  "version": 0,
  "grants": [{"permission": "FAMILY_VIEW", "scope": "SOCIETY"}]
}
```

Example account creation:

```json
{
  "username": "reviewer",
  "password": "REPLACE_WITH_PRIVATE_PASSWORD",
  "roles": ["COMMITTEE", "FAMILY_REVIEWER"],
  "societyCode": "EMERALD",
  "memberId": null
}
```

New role definitions and assignments need no code change. Registering a brand-new permission code adds catalogue data; implementing a new business action still requires code that checks that permission. The system does not execute arbitrary permission definitions or provide a general policy language. Built-in administrators receive the initially shipped catalogue; explicitly grant new permissions to a configurable role when adding new actions.

## Validation

Run Maven tests in Member and Society, and use the Member wrapper with `-f ../../infrastructure/api-gateway/pom.xml test` for Gateway. Existing Docker-based tests retain their normal prerequisites.

For the new real-database integration suite without Docker:

```powershell
powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File scripts/test-access-local.ps1
```

The script uses locally installed PostgreSQL 18 by default (`-PostgresBin` overrides its bin directory), initializes a fresh temporary cluster, chooses a local port, runs tests in a unique schema, and stops that cluster in `finally`. Logs and test data remain in the printed temporary directory for diagnosis. It does not connect to the application's databases. The integration test class is skipped during normal Maven runs unless `access.test.jdbc-url` is supplied.

The portal has DOM component tests for administration screens, owner-only screens, form payloads, CSRF headers, and sign-out. Install their optional test dependency in a temporary directory:

```powershell
npm.cmd install --prefix "$env:TEMP/emerald-portal-dom-tests" --no-audit --no-fund jsdom
node scripts/test-portal.cjs
```

These simulate the DOM and API responses; they do not replace visual browser testing or a full Redis/Gateway/backend deployment test.
Next chapterJava and Spring in the code →

Chapter 02

Java and Spring in the code

Language choices, framework responsibilities, and execution boundaries.

What Spring Boot provides—and what your code must provide

Spring Boot configures the application around the dependencies and properties you supply. It wires HTTP infrastructure, persistence, validation, caching, security integrations, and operational endpoints. It does not choose the business rules, permission scopes, transaction boundaries, or safe retry policy for you.

The project uses Java 21. Records carry request and response values, streams map data, and lambdas supply behavior to existing APIs. These choices make the code smaller when they fit the task; they do not make database calls asynchronous or business state deeply immutable.

Language or framework choiceUse it to explainDo not claim
RecordsCompact API carriers; constructors can validate or defensively copy values.A record automatically makes nested mutable objects immutable.
Collection factories and copiesReturn fixed values or a shallow defensive snapshot.Every stream collector returns a mutable ArrayList.
Streams and method referencesMap results, construct orders, and make transformations explicit.Sorting a Java list replaces database ordering and pagination.
Comparator and ComparableSeparate an external ordering strategy from a type’s natural order.Every domain class must implement Comparable.
WebFluxGateway uses reactive HTTP infrastructure.The MVC/JPA backends become nonblocking because Gateway is reactive.
Virtual threadsUnderstand where a configured executor is used and how it is closed.More threads remove connection-pool or downstream capacity limits.

Read a backend request through its layers

  1. Transport and identity

    Filters establish the validated request identity and enforce transport-level protections.

  2. Controller and DTO validation

    HTTP inputs are parsed and checked before the application operation is invoked.

  3. Service and authorization

    The service applies the permission and business checks appropriate to the operation.

  4. Persistence and transaction

    Repository calls use the service-owned database and the transaction currently in effect.

  5. Response and operations

    The result is mapped to an API representation; logs, metrics, and tracing provide operational context.

Keep request authorization and transactional work within their intended execution context. Moving work to another executor needs an explicit lifecycle and context strategy.

Project questions · explanation and verification

Where are Java virtual threads used in this project?

Member Service defines a dedicated virtual-thread executor for its Society HTTP client. In SocietyRestClientConfig, societyHttpExecutor() creates a thread-per-task executor whose threads are named society-http-.

Executors.newThreadPerTaskExecutor(
    Thread.ofVirtual().name("society-http-", 0).factory()
);

The executor is passed to the JDK HttpClient; that client backs JdkClientHttpRequestFactory and the societyRestClient used for Member-to-Society calls. Spring closes the HTTP client before its executor when the context shuts down.

What does that improve?

Virtual threads are lightweight Java-managed threads suited to many waiting tasks. They can reduce the cost of thread concurrency for supported blocking work. They do not make CPU instructions faster or increase the database’s connection limit.

Scope matters: this executor configures HTTP-client task execution. It does not mean every request controller, JPA transaction, or synchronous RestClient caller runs on a virtual thread. Member/Society remain MVC/JPA backends; Gateway uses WebFlux. There is no global virtual-thread request configuration being claimed here.

How to verify

Inspect SocietyRestClientConfigTest.numericTaskBatchUsesVirtualThreadsAndResourcesCloseWithContext. It submits tasks to that exact executor, checks Thread.currentThread().isVirtual(), verifies the results, and checks client/executor termination after context close. The configuration test also verifies the HTTP client is wired to the executor.

A thread name alone is not proof that a thread is virtual. The existing test establishes executor behavior; it does not establish that all production requests execute on virtual threads or prove a throughput improvement.

Sources: client configuration and executor and transport tests.

Implementation referenceKeep business execution and transport tasks distinct

Keep business execution and transport tasks distinct

1. Business requestMVC filters and services retain the request's authorization and transaction boundaries.
2. Society lookupMember applies retry, circuit-breaker and bulkhead policy to the outbound call.
3. Transport tasksThe JDK HTTP client uses its dedicated virtual-thread ExecutorService for internal work.
4. ShutdownSpring closes the HTTP client before its executor. Gateway retains its reactive execution model.

No throughput increase is claimed without measurement. A virtual-thread executor does not automatically make all servlet requests virtual-thread requests.

Java language and collection features in this repository

The code uses Java 21. These are concrete application examples for interviews,

not features added to domain entities solely for demonstration.

Important distinctions

  • Java has no List.modifiableList() factory. Use new ArrayList<>(),

new ArrayList<>(existing) or Collectors.toCollection(ArrayList::new) when

later changes are required. Query ordering needs a mutable list because an ID

tie-breaker is appended after mapping client-supplied sort orders.

  • List.of, List.copyOf, Set.of and Map.of produce unmodifiable collections.

Their elements/values are not necessarily deeply immutable. Copy factories make

a shallow snapshot and may reuse an already suitable unmodifiable input.

  • Stream.toList() is unmodifiable but permits null elements. List.of,

List.copyOf and Collectors.toUnmodifiableList() reject null elements.

Collectors.toList() does not guarantee a particular implementation or mutability.

  • Collections.unmodifiableList(existing) is a read-only view: changes made through

the original list remain visible. It is not the same as List.copyOf(existing).

The application does not need an extra view wrapper where it already returns

an unmodifiable stream result or a defensive copy.

  • Comparable defines a type's natural order through compareTo.

Comparator selects an external ordering strategy. Existing UUID, Duration,

Instant and other JDK types already supply natural orders; no custom domain

implements Comparable class is needed for the current workflows.

  • UUID sorting in cursor scope construction makes the access-scope fingerprint

deterministic. It does not perform database pagination. PostgreSQL still sorts

and filters the actual cursor query; do not replace it with Java-side sorting.

Short interview answer

“I use lambdas and method references with streams to map records and build query

orders. Collection factories create fixed values, and copy factories protect

returned identity and cursor collections. I collect into ArrayList when I need to

append an ordering tie-breaker. Comparator.naturalOrder uses UUID's Comparable

implementation to canonicalize the access scope. I choose mutable or unmodifiable

collections according to their purpose rather than using one everywhere.”

Static, default and private methods

  • Static: belongs to the class/interface, not an instance. Use it for factories

and operations that need no object state. Call MembershipPeriod.currentOn(date)

on the interface; it returns a predicate for a stream's filter.

  • Default: an interface method with an inherited implementation. The entity

implements the period's date accessors and inherits isCurrentOn(date).

This method uses that particular entity's dates. Default methods arrived in Java 8.

  • Private: implementation detail available only inside its declaring

class/interface. The interface's hasStartedOn(date) helper supports the default

method without becoming part of the public contract. Private interface methods

arrived in Java 9 and are supported by this Java 21 project.

  • Private static: combine restricted visibility with no instance state. Existing

entity-to-response mappers are examples. Static/private helpers do not receive

instance-based Spring proxy interception; retain transaction and method-security

boundaries on suitable instance service methods.

The resident flat lookup now reads:

.filter(MembershipPeriod.currentOn(today))
.map(FlatMembershipEntity::getFlatId)
.collect(Collectors.toUnmodifiableSet());

The policy remains the previous one: a membership must have started and must have

no end date. A future-dated start does not yet grant access; any explicitly recorded

end date removes current access. It is not a general historical date-range check.

The caller still supplies the UTC reference date used for account validation.

Interview answer: “I use static methods for factories, default interface methods

for reusable instance behavior, and private methods for internal implementation.

For example, a membership inherits a default current-access check, a static factory

turns that check into a stream predicate, and a private helper checks its start date.

I keep transaction and authorization methods as Spring-managed instance methods.”

Records, instanceof and collection interfaces

  • Record: a concise data carrier with generated accessors, equals, hashCode and

toString. It is not automatically deeply immutable. Page and cursor records now

copy their lists so caller mutation cannot change the response's list structure.

Their JSON fields remain unchanged. List.copyOf rejects null lists/elements.

Entity objects are still mutable JPA classes rather than being converted to records.

  • Pattern-matching instanceof: checks the runtime type and binds a typed variable

in one expression. Existing security and resilience checks already use it.

  • Interfaces versus implementations: declare List<Order> while collecting into

ArrayList<Order> when an ID ordering tie-breaker must be appended. Existing

code also uses Set, Map, Collection, ConcurrentMap, HashMap and ConcurrentHashMap.

Choose each implementation for ordering, uniqueness or concurrent access needs;

do not replace them simply to demonstrate another class.

Functional interfaces in real workflows

A functional interface has one abstract method and can be implemented with a lambda

or method reference. Default, static and private methods do not add abstract methods.

InterfaceMethodRepository example
Function<S,T>applyPageResponse.from accepts a mapping function
Supplier<T>getRedisMembershipLock and SocietyCacheMetrics wrap protected operations
Predicate<T>testMembershipPeriod.currentOn builds a filter policy
Consumer<T>acceptforEach processes cache names or sort entries
Comparator<T>compareNatural ordering canonicalizes the flat-access scope
Callable<T>callExecutorService.submit accepts result-producing HTTP-executor test tasks
RunnablerunExecutorService.execute accepts work with no result; the HTTP client schedules internal work
ThreadFactorynewThreadThe configured factory creates named virtual threads

There is no need to wrap these standard contracts in an unused custom functional

interface solely for interview coverage. Collectors is a utility class; Collector

is the interface describing reduction into a result container. Streams are lazy

until a terminal operation, and parallelStream is not a virtual-thread executor.

Virtual threads and executor lifecycle

Member's SocietyRestClientConfig registers a dedicated ExecutorService backed by:

Executors.newThreadPerTaskExecutor(
    Thread.ofVirtual().name("society-http-", 0).factory());

HttpClient uses it for asynchronous/dependent transport tasks. Spring owns both

resources and closes the HTTP client before closing its executor. The executor is

marked defaultCandidate=false so it does not replace Boot's general application

executor. This is a live application use, not an unused demonstration bean.

This does not enable virtual threads for every servlet request, change database

transactions into async transactions, or replace Gateway's Reactor event loop.

Security context and transactions remain on the calling business request. Existing

Society bulkhead, retry and timeout policies continue to limit logical calls. The

executor itself creates an unbounded number of lightweight threads; it is not a

concurrency limiter and does not increase database capacity or speed up CPU work.

No throughput improvement is claimed without measurement.

Tests check virtual task execution, resource closure, actual HTTP transport and

read-timeout behavior. Java 21 does not require preview flags for virtual threads.

IntStream and infinite streams

Interpreting “IninitStream” as IntStream, the executor test generates numbered tasks:

IntStream.rangeClosed(1, 8)
    .mapToObj(index -> executor.submit(() ->
        new TaskObservation(index, Thread.currentThread().isVirtual())))
    .toList();

IntStream is a primitive-int stream. range excludes its upper bound, rangeClosed

includes it, boxed converts to Stream<Integer>, and mapToObj creates object results.

The example is intentionally in a concurrency test; the application has no new

numeric business loop that needs an artificial IntStream refactor.

If “IninitStream” meant an infinite stream, the test also covers a bounded form:

Stream.iterate(1, index -> index + 1).limit(3);

The two-argument iterate and generate can produce unbounded streams. Add a finite

termination policy before collecting or submitting tasks. Do not put an unbounded

stream into an API request or use it to enqueue unlimited executor work.

Interview answer: “I use records for API data, pattern-matching instanceof for

typed checks, and standard functional interfaces with streams. A dedicated managed

virtual-thread ExecutorService handles the Society HTTP client's transport tasks.

I retain timeouts, bulkheads and database limits. IntStream and a limited infinite

stream generate finite task batches in tests.”

References: Java 21 Executors,

HttpClient executor contract),

Spring Boot custom executors.

## Important distinctions

- Java has no `List.modifiableList()` factory. Use `new ArrayList<>()`,
  `new ArrayList<>(existing)` or `Collectors.toCollection(ArrayList::new)` when
  later changes are required. Query ordering needs a mutable list because an ID
  tie-breaker is appended after mapping client-supplied sort orders.
- `List.of`, `List.copyOf`, `Set.of` and `Map.of` produce unmodifiable collections.
  Their elements/values are not necessarily deeply immutable. Copy factories make
  a shallow snapshot and may reuse an already suitable unmodifiable input.
- `Stream.toList()` is unmodifiable but permits null elements. `List.of`,
  `List.copyOf` and `Collectors.toUnmodifiableList()` reject null elements.
  `Collectors.toList()` does not guarantee a particular implementation or mutability.
- `Collections.unmodifiableList(existing)` is a read-only view: changes made through
  the original list remain visible. It is not the same as `List.copyOf(existing)`.
  The application does not need an extra view wrapper where it already returns
  an unmodifiable stream result or a defensive copy.
- `Comparable` defines a type's natural order through `compareTo`.
  `Comparator` selects an external ordering strategy. Existing UUID, Duration,
  Instant and other JDK types already supply natural orders; no custom domain
  `implements Comparable` class is needed for the current workflows.
- UUID sorting in cursor scope construction makes the access-scope fingerprint
  deterministic. It does not perform database pagination. PostgreSQL still sorts
  and filters the actual cursor query; do not replace it with Java-side sorting.

## Short interview answer

“I use lambdas and method references with streams to map records and build query
orders. Collection factories create fixed values, and copy factories protect
returned identity and cursor collections. I collect into ArrayList when I need to
append an ordering tie-breaker. Comparator.naturalOrder uses UUID's Comparable
implementation to canonicalize the access scope. I choose mutable or unmodifiable
collections according to their purpose rather than using one everywhere.”

- **Static:** belongs to the class/interface, not an instance. Use it for factories
  and operations that need no object state. Call `MembershipPeriod.currentOn(date)`
  on the interface; it returns a predicate for a stream's `filter`.
- **Default:** an interface method with an inherited implementation. The entity
  implements the period's date accessors and inherits `isCurrentOn(date)`.
  This method uses that particular entity's dates. Default methods arrived in Java 8.
- **Private:** implementation detail available only inside its declaring

  method without becoming part of the public contract. Private interface methods
  arrived in Java 9 and are supported by this Java 21 project.

  entity-to-response mappers are examples. Static/private helpers do not receive
  instance-based Spring proxy interception; retain transaction and method-security
  boundaries on suitable instance service methods.

```java
.map(FlatMembershipEntity::getFlatId)
.collect(Collectors.toUnmodifiableSet());
```

- **Interfaces versus implementations:** declare `List<Order>` while collecting into
  `ArrayList<Order>` when an ID ordering tie-breaker must be appended. Existing
  code also uses Set, Map, Collection, ConcurrentMap, HashMap and ConcurrentHashMap.
  Choose each implementation for ordering, uniqueness or concurrent access needs;
  do not replace them simply to demonstrate another class.

## Functional interfaces in real workflows

A functional interface has one abstract method and can be implemented with a lambda
or method reference. Default, static and private methods do not add abstract methods.

| Interface | Method | Repository example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Function<S,T> | apply | PageResponse.from accepts a mapping function |
| Supplier<T> | get | RedisMembershipLock and SocietyCacheMetrics wrap protected operations |
| Predicate<T> | test | MembershipPeriod.currentOn builds a filter policy |
| Consumer<T> | accept | forEach processes cache names or sort entries |
| Comparator<T> | compare | Natural ordering canonicalizes the flat-access scope |
| Callable<T> | call | ExecutorService.submit accepts result-producing HTTP-executor test tasks |
| Runnable | run | ExecutorService.execute accepts work with no result; the HTTP client schedules internal work |
| ThreadFactory | newThread | The configured factory creates named virtual threads |

There is no need to wrap these standard contracts in an unused custom functional
interface solely for interview coverage. Collectors is a utility class; Collector
is the interface describing reduction into a result container. Streams are lazy
until a terminal operation, and parallelStream is not a virtual-thread executor.

## Virtual threads and executor lifecycle

Member's SocietyRestClientConfig registers a dedicated ExecutorService backed by:

```java
Executors.newThreadPerTaskExecutor(
    Thread.ofVirtual().name("society-http-", 0).factory());
```

HttpClient uses it for asynchronous/dependent transport tasks. Spring owns both
resources and closes the HTTP client before closing its executor. The executor is
marked `defaultCandidate=false` so it does not replace Boot's general application
executor. This is a live application use, not an unused demonstration bean.

This does **not** enable virtual threads for every servlet request, change database
transactions into async transactions, or replace Gateway's Reactor event loop.
Security context and transactions remain on the calling business request. Existing
Society bulkhead, retry and timeout policies continue to limit logical calls. The
executor itself creates an unbounded number of lightweight threads; it is not a
concurrency limiter and does not increase database capacity or speed up CPU work.
No throughput improvement is claimed without measurement.

Tests check virtual task execution, resource closure, actual HTTP transport and
read-timeout behavior. Java 21 does not require preview flags for virtual threads.

## IntStream and infinite streams

Interpreting “IninitStream” as IntStream, the executor test generates numbered tasks:

```java
IntStream.rangeClosed(1, 8)
    .mapToObj(index -> executor.submit(() ->
        new TaskObservation(index, Thread.currentThread().isVirtual())))
    .toList();
```

IntStream is a primitive-int stream. range excludes its upper bound, rangeClosed
includes it, boxed converts to Stream<Integer>, and mapToObj creates object results.
The example is intentionally in a concurrency test; the application has no new
numeric business loop that needs an artificial IntStream refactor.

If “IninitStream” meant an **infinite stream**, the test also covers a bounded form:

```java
Stream.iterate(1, index -> index + 1).limit(3);
```

The two-argument iterate and generate can produce unbounded streams. Add a finite
termination policy before collecting or submitting tasks. Do not put an unbounded
stream into an API request or use it to enqueue unlimited executor work.

**Interview answer:** “I use records for API data, pattern-matching instanceof for
typed checks, and standard functional interfaces with streams. A dedicated managed
virtual-thread ExecutorService handles the Society HTTP client's transport tasks.
I retain timeouts, bulkheads and database limits. IntStream and a limited infinite
stream generate finite task batches in tests.”

References: [Java 21 Executors](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/21/docs/api/java.base/java/util/concurrent/Executors.html),
[HttpClient executor contract](https://docs.oracle.com/en/java/javase/21/docs/api/java.net.http/java/net/http/HttpClient.Builder.html#executor(java.util.concurrent.Executor)),
[Spring Boot custom executors](https://docs.spring.io/spring-boot/reference/features/task-execution-and-scheduling.html).
Scenario questionsWebFlux and its use in this repository

WebFlux and its use in this repository

What is WebFlux, and where do we use it?

Spring WebFlux is Spring's reactive web framework. Nonblocking I/O lets a small set of event-loop threads process other work while network operations are pending. Mono<T> represents zero or one result; Flux<T> represents zero or many results. The main benefit is efficient concurrency during I/O waits, rather than automatically faster queries.

Current implementation: infrastructure/api-gateway/pom.xml declares spring-cloud-starter-gateway-server-webflux. Gateway authentication, session validation and filters return Mono; AdminAuthenticationManager uses reactive WebClient for credential validation. Member and Society declare spring-boot-starter-webmvc and use JPA/JDBC.

Example: while Gateway waits for Member to validate a login, its event-loop thread can handle other connections. Member's database query still executes using blocking database access.

Follow-up: returning a Mono does not by itself make every operation inside it nonblocking. Avoid blocking database calls, sleeps and block() on Gateway event-loop threads.

Scenario questionsSpring Boot infrastructure, business rules and blocking boundaries

Spring Boot infrastructure, business rules and blocking boundaries

What does the Spring Boot limitation mean, and what is the solution?

Spring Boot configures infrastructure such as application startup, the web server and integration with persistence and security libraries. It does not know which resident owns a record, whether a member is active or whether a duplicate membership is allowed. We must encode those policies explicitly.

RequirementRepository mechanism
Only active members and flats receive assignmentsMemberService.requireActive(), MembershipService.assign() and the writer's member-status recheck.
No duplicate active membershipRepository checks, database locking and uniqueness constraints.
End date cannot precede start dateMembershipWriter.end().
Access matches role and record scopeMemberAccess, SocietyAccess and method authorization.
Local writes remain consistentTransactions, database constraints, version checks and tests.

A reactive Gateway and a blocking backend are separate applications. If a JPA query takes 200 milliseconds, Gateway can wait reactively for the HTTP response while the backend's executing thread waits for JDBC. Gateway does not change the backend driver or repository behavior.

Practical solution: keep the existing WebFlux Gateway and MVC/JPA services, protect event-loop threads, and tune queries, indexes, connection pools, timeouts and concurrency using measurements. Cache suitable reads and rate-limit incoming traffic.

Optional redesign: if measured requirements justify end-to-end reactive processing, migrate the relevant backend path to WebFlux and reactive database access such as R2DBC. Wrapping JPA in Mono does not make JDBC nonblocking; offloading it to a bounded worker isolates blocking work but does not remove it. Reactive processing also does not replace business rules or authorization.

Chapter 03

Architecture and request journeys

Trace routes, service calls, data ownership, and membership assignment.

Three applications with separate responsibilities

ApplicationOwns or coordinatesDependency
API GatewayRouting, authenticated sessions, CSRF, and request rate limits.Member credential/identity validation and main Redis.
Society ServiceSociety, floors, flat records, and relevant audits.society_db and the dedicated cache Redis.
Member ServiceMembers, membership history, application accounts, access, and family workflows.member_db, Society validation, and main Redis.

Database per service means that service replicas share their service’s data store while other services use its API. It does not mean one database for every Pod, nor does it require every database to run on a separate physical machine.

Follow a membership assignment

  1. Gateway receives the request

    The request must have a valid session and satisfy the applicable CSRF and rate-limit checks. Routing sends it to Member Service.

  2. Member checks authority and eligibility

    The assignment operation requires its configured permission. The member must exist and be active. The current implementation also coordinates access changes with a database-backed registry lock.

  3. Member asks Society about the flat

    An authenticated internal lookup obtains the authoritative flat state; this validation deliberately avoids the disposable response cache. An inactive flat is not eligible.

  4. The assignment is coordinated

    A Redis lock serializes membership operations for the same member. Persistence rechecks state under a database row lock, rejects a duplicate active relationship, and relies on the database constraint as a final guard.

  5. The business change and audit are committed

    MembershipWriter persists the association and records the audit in the transaction. The returned result describes the saved relationship.

An important transaction detail

The current assign method itself is transactional and takes the access-registry lock before the Society lookup. Do not describe this implementation as always making the network call outside a short database transaction. Narrower transaction boundaries would be a separate improvement.

What this flow does not guarantee

There is no cross-service database transaction. Society can change after validation and before Member commits. Explain the accepted consistency window and the compensating or asynchronous design you would introduce if the business required stronger coordination.

Project questions · explanation and verification

Is this application stateful or stateless?

The overall system is stateful. Application Pods keep shared durable or session state outside the individual Pod. Those two statements describe different boundaries.

BoundaryProject behaviorScaling consequence
Business dataPostgreSQL holds members, flats, accounts, audits, and idempotency records.Application replicas share their owning service’s database.
LoginGateway uses a Redis-backed server-side session; the browser holds the SESSION identifier.Authentication is stateful. Compatible Gateway replicas can read the shared session without requiring one user to stay on one Pod.
Application PodsShared session and business state are externalized rather than kept only in a Pod’s memory. Local caches, connection pools, and resilience state can still exist per instance.Pods are replaceable and can serve shared workflows, provided they have compatible code/configuration and dependencies.
Redis workloadsSessions, rate limits, locks, and disposable cache data live in separate Redis processes.The current emptyDir deployments lose their data when their Pods are replaced.
Local resilience controlsCircuit-breaker state and fallback admission are per application instance in this design.Adding a Pod adds another local control instance; it is not a globally shared quota.

How to verify the boundary

  1. Log in through Gateway and confirm a SESSION cookie is issued. Inspect the configured Spring Session Redis namespace rather than exposing raw session contents.
  2. In a controlled multi-replica setup, use that session through different compatible Gateway replicas. Confirm the identity remains valid while Redis stays healthy.
  3. Restart an application Pod in a test environment and check persisted business data afterward. This demonstrates externalized state; it does not imply that replacing the temporary Redis Pod preserves sessions.

One successful request through a Service does not prove requests reached different replicas. Use a controlled routing method and available instance identifiers or correlated logs to establish which replica served each request.

Source: Gateway session configuration and Redis temporary-storage configuration.

Implementation references and scenarios

Implementation referenceTwo separate applications

Two separate applications

Different jobs, different ports

A microservice is a small application with one main responsibility. Society Service owns building information. Member Service owns people information. This clear job boundary is called a bounded context.

ApplicationIts jobLocal port
Society ServiceSociety and floors; later flats.8081
Member ServiceCreate, read, update and deactivate members.8082

How an API request moves

A Controller receives the web request. A Service does the job's rules. A DTO is a small data shape used to receive or return information. An exception handler turns errors into a clear response.

Examples: Society has GET /api/v1/society and GET /api/v1/floors. Member has POST /api/v1/members, GET /api/v1/members, update and deactivate operations.

GET reads. POST creates. PUT updates permitted details. PATCH changes part of something. HTTP 200 means okay, 201 means created, 400 means bad input, 404 means not found and 409 means conflict.

Phase 1 used temporary memory. Stop the app and that temporary data is gone. Phase 2 changes storage to PostgreSQL. Phase 1 created separate apps, REST APIs, validation, error responses and health checks.
Implementation referenceGive each service its own database

Give each service its own database

PostgreSQL · JPA · Flyway

Temporary memory is like a whiteboard: it clears when the app stops. PostgreSQL is like a filing cabinet that keeps information. Society Service uses society_db. Member Service uses member_db. Neither service reads the other's database.

JPA describes database rows using Java objects. Hibernate maps those objects to SQL tables. A repository finds and saves rows. A transaction groups database changes so they succeed or fail as a unit. An index helps locate rows faster.

Society owns society, floor and flat tables. Member owns member and flat-membership tables. Membership stores a flat ID as a reference; there is no foreign key to Society's separate database. Member deactivation keeps its record and status history rather than deleting the row.

Flyway: a numbered instruction book for databases

Flyway applies saved SQL changes in order. Think of a new database receiving a numbered instruction book: first create tables, then insert the building's initial records. Flyway remembers which instructions it has already applied.

  1. Spring Boot starts the service and reads application.yml. With spring.flyway.enabled: true, Spring Boot configures Flyway to run during startup.
  2. The service's datasource tells Flyway which database to connect to: Society uses society_db; Member uses member_db.
  3. Flyway uses its default migration location, classpath:db/migration. It checks flyway_schema_history in that database, creating the history table if this is the first run.
  4. Flyway scans the classpath location for versioned files named like V1__create_tables.sql. The two underscores separate the version from the description. It validates files already recorded, including their checksums.
  5. Any pending versioned migrations run from lowest version to highest. Flyway records each successful migration's version, description, script name, checksum, time and success status in the history table.
  6. Hibernate then uses ddl-auto: validate to check that the Java entity mappings match the database tables. It does not create or update tables here.
How does Flyway find db/migration? The source folder is src/main/resources (resources, plural), a standard Maven resource folder. Maven copies its contents to the application's classpath: for example, src/main/resources/db/migration/V1__create_tables.sql becomes classpath:db/migration/V1__create_tables.sql. When running from an IDE, the IDE also puts the resources folder on the application classpath. Flyway's default location looks for db/migration at that classpath root, so this project does not need a custom spring.flyway.locations setting.
What is flyway_schema_history? Flyway creates this tracking table in each service's own database and records which migration files succeeded. It stores migration metadata and checksums—not the query results, a copy of the SQL's changed data, or business records. If V1 is already recorded as successful, a normal restart skips it. A later V2__... file is applied once and then recorded.

Society files

V1__create_society_tables.sql creates societies, floors, flats, rules and an index.

V2__seed_society_and_floors.sql inserts Emerald Housing Society and floors 1–18.

Member file

V1__create_member_tables.sql creates members and flat memberships with constraints and indexes.

Do not edit a migration that has already run. Add a new higher-numbered migration for a new change. If SQL or the database is broken, Flyway normally makes startup fail so the issue is visible. Each service has its own migration history in its own database.

Phase 2 limit: Member Service can store a flat ID, but it does not yet confirm that the flat exists. Phase 3 adds that request to Society Service.
Implementation referenceAsk Society Service about a flat

Ask Society Service about a flat

Spring RestClient

Member Service owns people, not flats. For an assignment it asks the flat owner, Society Service, if the flat exists. It never connects directly to society_db.

This is synchronous HTTP: send a question, wait for an answer, then continue. Spring's RestClient performs the request.

  1. Member Service receives an assignment request.
  2. It checks its member and local rules.
  3. SocietyClient asks Society Service for the requested flat.
  4. Only after the flat is confirmed does Member Service save its membership.
SituationResultNew membership saved?
Flat exists and rules passSuccessYes
Member or flat missingNot-found errorNo
Bad requestBad-request errorNo
Society is down/slowControlled dependency error after timeoutNo
Duplicate active assignmentConflict errorNo duplicate

A timeout limits how long Member Service waits. A correlation ID is a tracking label shared by related request logs. This call adds a network dependency: if Society is unavailable, flat assignment must fail safely. The two databases do not share one transaction.

Typical call chain: security filters → controller/DTO validation → service authorization and business rules → repository → PostgreSQL → response DTO. Audit shares the write transaction. Cache invalidation follows a successful commit. External Society validation happens before the membership write transaction.

Scenario questionsSynchronous service-to-service validation

Synchronous service-to-service validation

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: Why validate a flat synchronously, and what if Society is down?

Answer: A synchronous call gives an immediate authoritative answer before Member records an assignment, but it couples availability and latency. Use a short timeout, bounded concurrency, clear error mapping, and fail safely when validation is required; do not save based on an unknown result.

Project flow: Member → Society GET flat → validate → persist membership. If the product can accept eventual consistency, consider an asynchronous workflow with explicit pending/rejected states instead.

Follow-up: How can synchronous validation evolve if request volume or dependency latency grows?

Answer: First measure the call path and keep timeouts, concurrency, and caching of stable reference facts bounded. If the business accepts delayed decisions, move to an asynchronous workflow with explicit pending, confirmed, and rejected states rather than silently accepting an unvalidated write.

Scenario questionsDatabase per service and data ownership

Database per service and data ownership

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: Why does each service own its database, and how should services share data?

Answer: Ownership lets a service change its schema without another service depending on its tables. In this project Member asks Society to validate a flat over HTTP; it does not query Society's database. For asynchronous facts, publish events and build a local read model.

Solution sketch: member → SocietyClient.validateFlat(flatId); avoid cross-service SQL joins or shared write access.

Follow-up: How do you keep data consistent when a service owns its database?

Answer: Use an API call when the caller needs an immediate decision, or publish an event and update a local read model when eventual consistency is acceptable. For reliable event publication, write the domain change and an outbox row in one database transaction, then publish the outbox asynchronously.
