Chapter 04

API behavior and client workflows

Use the right route, scope, version, cursor, and idempotency key.

Use the contract for the operation you are calling

Start from the saved Postman collection and controller mappings. Gateway routing covers society, floor, flat, member, membership, access, account, owner, and family paths. Route order matters: the Member-owned flat-membership path must be matched before the general Society flat path.

Caller concernWhat to doWhy it matters
AuthenticationObtain CSRF material, log in with a private account, then use the resulting session cookie.Session state and CSRF protection are separate mechanisms.
AuthorizationUse an account with the operation’s permission and the correct self, owned-flat, or society scope.The same route can return different permitted records for different identities.
Conditional updatesRead the current representation and ETag; send the supported If-Match value when editing.A stale editor should not silently overwrite a newer change.
Idempotent createsUse the supported idempotency key and keep the request meaning stable when retrying.A replay must not accidentally create another member.
Directory queriesUse supported filters, bounded page sizes, and allowed sort fields.Inputs must preserve safe and deterministic database queries.
Cursor queriesReturn the issued cursor with the same applicable query and authorization context.A cursor is continuation state, not an unrestricted list position.

Read errors as part of the interface

A successful HTTP connection does not imply a successful business operation. Separate invalid input, missing authentication, denied access, a missing resource, a conflict or stale version, an exceeded limit, and an unavailable dependency. The detailed API reference and saved requests show the operation-specific status and response fields.

Compatibility choices are configuration choices

The documented compatibility mode accepts updates without If-Match. The stricter setting is APP_HTTP_REQUIRE_IF_MATCH=true in both backends. Turn it on only with clients that send current validators. Cursor and page-number APIs also serve different client needs; keep their scope and ordering rules explicit.

Runnable contract: Option B Postman collection · Environment template. Credential values belong in private local variables.

Project questions · explanation and verification

How is idempotency handled here, and how do I verify it?

The supported member-create operation can return the original result for a retry instead of creating another member. This is PostgreSQL-backed idempotency; it is not stored in Redis.

Request and scope

POST /api/v1/members
Content-Type: application/json
Idempotency-Key: member-create-demo-001
X-XSRF-TOKEN: {{csrfToken}}

{
  "fullName": "Idempotency Demo",
  "mobileNumber": "{{unusedTestMobile}}",
  "email": "idempotency.demo@example.com"
}

Use an authenticated account with MEMBER_CREATE:SOCIETY, its cookie jar, and a valid current CSRF token. Supply a valid unused test mobile number. The key is optional on this endpoint; omitting it uses ordinary creation. A supplied key must match [A-Za-z0-9._:-]{1,128}.

  1. Member normalizes the request values and computes a SHA-256 fingerprint.
  2. It attempts an idempotency_records row scoped by actor ID + CREATE_MEMBER + key. A database unique constraint coordinates duplicate attempts across Pods.
  3. The idempotency row, member creation, creation audit, and saved response participate in the create transaction. The completed response is stored for replay.
  4. After a uniqueness conflict, a new primary-database lookup finds the existing record. A matching completed request returns the saved member. A changed fingerprint or non-completed record produces 409 IDEMPOTENCY_CONFLICT.

The default TTL is 24 hours, configurable through app.idempotency.ttl. Expired records are cleaned during idempotent creation. Do not promise replay forever or assume that another actor using the same key shares the result. Mobile-number uniqueness is a separate business constraint.

Verification caseExpected observation
First create with a fresh key201 Created; Idempotency-Replayed: false; save the returned memberId.
Same actor, key, and equivalent normalized payload201 Created again; Idempotency-Replayed: true; same saved member representation and memberId.
Same actor and key with a genuinely changed name or other fingerprinted value409 IDEMPOTENCY_CONFLICT; original member remains unchanged.
Invalid key, for example containing a space400 INVALID_IDEMPOTENCY_KEY, with otherwise valid authentication, CSRF, and request body.
Persistence checkExactly one member and one CREATE audit for that fixture; replay must not create another business write.

Existing verification you can reuse

In the collection, the Option B - Automated verification folder contains Idempotent creation, Idempotent replay, and Reject changed idempotent payload. Run the folder in order in a disposable test environment so its authentication, identifiers, and fixtures are initialized.

The saved report dated 24 October 2026 records 201, 201 with a same-record assertion, and 409 for those cases. This is historical evidence, not a new live run. FeaturesPersistenceTest also asserts one member and one audit for replay; it uses Testcontainers and is configured to skip without Docker.

Sources: MemberService, MemberController, database constraint, persistence tests, and saved verification report.

Create member and assign a flat

POST http://localhost:8082/api/v1/members
Content-Type: application/json

{"fullName":"Rahul Sharma","mobileNumber":"9876543210","email":"rahul@example.com"}

POST http://localhost:8082/api/v1/members/{memberId}/flats
Content-Type: application/json

{"flatId":"{flat-uuid}","relationshipType":"OWNER","startDate":"2026-10-15"}

Name is required (up to 120 characters). The default mobile format is 10 digits beginning 6–9. Email is optional and validated if supplied. Relationship values are OWNER, TENANT or RESIDENT. An end-membership request uses {"endDate":"2026-10-15"}; the date cannot be before its start date or in the future.

Page sorting is limited to fields supported by the API. Flats support flatNumber, status, createdAt and updatedAt. Members: createdAt, updatedAt, fullName, mobileNumber, email, status. Memberships: startDate, endDate, createdAt, updatedAt, relationshipType. Flat fields are listed in the Society table. Invalid paging and sorting inputs return 400.

Common error shape

{
  "timestamp": "2026-10-15T10:00:00Z",
  "status": 404,
  "error": "Not Found",
  "code": "RESOURCE_NOT_FOUND",
  "message": "Flat not found",
  "path": "/api/v1/flats/example",
  "details": {}
}

The exact message varies with the problem. The stable status and code are more useful to a client than relying on exact wording. Society is not authenticated; these are local learning APIs.

Gateway addresses

Locally the Gateway listens on http://localhost:8080. Through it use the same API paths, such as GET /api/v1/society or GET /api/v1/members. In Kubernetes, Gateway routes to the private names member-service:8080 and society-service:8080.

Implementation reference8. New API surface and failure behavior

8. New API surface and failure behavior

API / conditionBehaviorClient responsibility
POST /api/v1/accountsAdministrator creates an account; password is BCrypt-hashed and omitted from output.Use EMERALD, a supported role and a 14–72 UTF-8 byte password; link resident accounts.
GET /api/v1/accounts/{id}Returns account permissions, enabled state and version.Administrator access required.
PUT /api/v1/accounts/{id}/permissionsUpdates role/scope/member link/enabled with version validation.Send current version. Use another administrator for changes to your own permissions.
409 VERSION_CONFLICTAccount or legacy versioned write has conflicted.Reload and review current state before resubmitting.
412 PRECONDITION_FAILEDConditional edit used a stale ETag.Reload; reconcile the user's changes.
428 PRECONDITION_REQUIREDStrict If-Match mode is enabled and the header is missing.Read the object first and include its ETag.
400 INVALID_CURSORMalformed cursor or mismatched filters/access scope.Restart cursor pagination.
Account validation unavailableGateway internal session validation returns 503 and fails closed.Surface a temporary availability error; do not bypass authorization.
Implementation reference4. Safe updates: ETags, versions and audit

4. Safe updates: ETags, versions and audit

GET /api/v1/members/{memberId}
Response header: ETag: "member-{uuid}-v2"

PUT /api/v1/members/{memberId}
If-Match: "member-{uuid}-v2"
Content-Type: application/json
...current session cookie, CSRF token and valid update body...

GET /api/v1/members/{memberId}
If-None-Match: "member-{uuid}-v2"
Unchanged authorized representation: 304, no response body

With If-Match: *, the client asserts existence only. It does not identify the version displayed in the edit form. Authorization runs before either a 200 or a 304 response.

Implementation reference5. Search and cursor pagination: execution flow

5. Search and cursor pagination: execution flow

GET /api/v1/members/page?search=asha&status=ACTIVE&page=0&size=20
GET /api/v1/flats/page?search=A-1&status=ACTIVE&floorNumber=1&page=0&size=20
GET /api/v1/members/cursor?limit=20&status=ACTIVE
GET /api/v1/members/cursor?limit=20&status=ACTIVE&after={urlEncodedNextCursor}

Keep the filters unchanged when using nextCursor. An invalid or mismatched token returns 400 INVALID_CURSOR. Cursor indexes improve ordered continuation; they do not make arbitrary substring searches index-only.

Implementation referenceBackend additions: audit, permissions, search and concurrency

Backend additions: audit, permissions, search and concurrency

The backend includes transactional audits, ADMIN/SOCIETY_ADMIN/COMMITTEE/RESIDENT permissions, object access checks, contract tests, database search, cursor pagination, version columns and conditional HTTP requests. See the API and rollout guide for examples, permission rules and migration details.

Member V5 and Society V4 migrations are new. Rebuild and roll out all three Java applications together before testing these features. The deployment remains scoped to EMERALD. Existing ADMIN accounts and page-number APIs remain supported. Postman includes an Audit, Access, Search and Conditional Requests folder.

Missing If-Match remains accepted for existing clients. Set APP_HTTP_REQUIRE_IF_MATCH=true in both backends only when clients send current ETags.

Source documentdocs/backend-access-and-query-features.md

docs/backend-access-and-query-features.md

Version context: The current access flow uses permissions and scopes, including dynamically assigned roles and resident record restrictions. Read earlier role-only examples in that context.

Audit, permissions, search, cursors and concurrency

The later dynamic access and family management phase replaces the fixed role model below and adds the Gateway-hosted /portal.html administration interface. The remaining sections describe the original API behaviour and its compatibility contracts.

This change adds backend behavior only. Existing frontend screens remain compatible, but they do not yet expose account management, audit history, server search or conditional updates.

Scope and roles

The deployment still serves one society, EMERALD. This is not a multi-tenant schema. Identities from another society are denied. Existing ADMIN accounts continue to work.

RoleRead society/floorsRead membersRead flatsChange business recordsManage accounts / view audits
ADMIN / SOCIETY_ADMINYesAll in this deploymentAll in EMERALDYesYes
COMMITTEEYesAll in this deploymentAll in EMERALDNoNo
RESIDENTYesOwn linked member onlyCurrently assigned flats onlyNoNo

Residents see their own membership history. A flat's membership list is filtered to their own entries. Lists, search results and cursor queries enforce the same access scope as individual-record reads. Service-to-service flat validation retains its narrow existing token-based permission.

Gateway permits the supported roles through its API routes. Each backend independently validates the session and applies method/object permissions. Gateway refreshes account role, enabled state and resident flat assignments from Member during internal session validation. Permission changes therefore affect subsequent backend requests; a disabled or unlinked resident account is rejected. Gateway fails closed when that lookup is unavailable.

Account APIs (administrators only)

POST /api/v1/accounts

{
  "username": "resident-example",
  "password": "REPLACE_WITH_A_PRIVATE_PASSWORD",
  "role": "RESIDENT",
  "societyCode": "EMERALD",
  "memberId": "REPLACE_WITH_EXISTING_MEMBER_UUID"
}

Supported roles: ADMIN, SOCIETY_ADMIN, COMMITTEE, RESIDENT. Passwords must be 14–72 UTF-8 bytes and are BCrypt-hashed. Resident accounts must link to an existing active member to authenticate. A linked member is identified by an application account mapping, never a browser-supplied identity header. Account responses never include the password or hash.

GET /api/v1/accounts/{accountId} returns the account and its version.

PUT /api/v1/accounts/{accountId}/permissions

{
  "role": "COMMITTEE",
  "societyCode": "EMERALD",
  "memberId": null,
  "enabled": true,
  "version": 0
}

Send the version returned by GET. A stale version returns 409 VERSION_CONFLICT. An administrator cannot change their own permissions using this endpoint, to reduce accidental lockout. Use another administrator. The existing provisioning process still creates ADMIN accounts only and never resets an existing password.

All mutation examples require the existing session cookie and current CSRF token. Login, logout and /api/v1/auth/me URLs are unchanged. Re-login after rolling out the new principal/session representation.

Audit trail

Each service has its own audit_events table. Member records member, membership and account/permission changes. Society records flat changes. Audit inserts use the same transaction as the business write: either both persist or both roll back. A failed audit insert fails the business write. Replayed idempotent creates do not produce another creation event.

Events include a sequence, actor account ID/name, action, entity type/ID, before/after JSON snapshots, UTC timestamp and correlation ID. Passwords, hashes, cookies and internal tokens are not part of audit snapshots. Snapshots of member changes do contain personal record details, so only administrators can retrieve them. There is no public update/delete audit API. This is application-level auditing, not tamper-proof logging of arbitrary SQL run by a database administrator. Retention/archival is an operational policy, not an automatic deletion task in this change.

GET /api/v1/members/audit?entityType=MEMBER&entityId={memberId}&limit=50
GET /api/v1/society/audit?entityType=FLAT&entityId={flatId}&limit=50

Use before={sequence} from the last returned event to retrieve older events. Limit is 1–100. Entity filters are optional. These endpoints route through the existing Gateway member/society routes.

Server-side search and filters

GET /api/v1/members/page?search=asha&status=ACTIVE&page=0&size=20
GET /api/v1/flats/page?search=A-1&status=ACTIVE&floorNumber=1&page=0&size=20

Member search matches name, mobile or email. Flat search matches flat number. Search is case-insensitive, literal substring matching, capped at 120 characters. % and _ are treated literally. Allowed statuses are ACTIVE and INACTIVE. Existing paging and sort validation still applies; the response format is unchanged. Filtering and authorization happen in the database before paging/counting. Substring search may need trigram/full-text indexing at larger measured volumes; the new cursor indexes do not make arbitrary substring searches index-only.

Cursor pagination

GET /api/v1/members/cursor?limit=20&status=ACTIVE
GET /api/v1/flats/cursor?limit=20&floorNumber=1
{
  "items": [],
  "nextCursor": null,
  "hasNext": false
}

When hasNext is true, URL-encode nextCursor into after on the next request. Keep all other filters identical:

GET /api/v1/members/cursor?limit=20&status=ACTIVE&after={encodedNextCursor}

Cursor endpoints use ascending (createdAt, id) keyset ordering and read at most limit + 1 rows without a total-count query. Matching indexes are provided. Tokens bind to normalized filters and the current actor/role/object scope; changing them requires restarting pagination. Invalid tokens return 400 INVALID_CURSOR. Tokens are opaque encodings, not authentication credentials or signed security tokens: record authorization is always enforced independently. Pagination is not a frozen database snapshot, and concurrent changes to filters may move records into/out of the result set. Existing page-number endpoints remain available for direct page navigation.

Optimistic locking and ETags

JPA @Version columns protect members, flats, memberships and application accounts. Member/flat response bodies now contain version. Existing service constructors and older stored idempotent member responses remain supported.

GET an individual record:

GET /api/v1/members/{memberId}
GET /api/v1/flats/{flatId}

The response includes a strong ETag such as "member-{uuid}-v2". Responses use Cache-Control: private, no-cache. Revalidate a read with If-None-Match; an unchanged representation returns 304 without a body. Access is checked before returning either 200 or 304.

Send the current ETag in If-Match when updating or deactivating a member/flat. A stale tag returns 412 PRECONDITION_FAILED. Weak tags cannot satisfy If-Match. The service checks the expected version inside the transaction, and JPA's versioned update prevents a race between that check and the commit. Concurrent version conflicts return 412 for conditional writes or 409 for legacy writes.

To preserve existing clients, missing If-Match remains allowed by default. Enable app.http.require-if-match=true (environment variable APP_HTTP_REQUIRE_IF_MATCH=true) in both backends to require it; missing headers then return 428 PRECONDITION_REQUIRED. Enable this only after clients start sending ETags. Without If-Match, optimistic locking catches overlapping database transactions but cannot detect a browser form that was stale before its request started. If-Match: * asserts existence only; it does not protect against stale form versions.

The Society cache uses a versioned serialization prefix to avoid reusing pre-version flat responses. Membership workflows retain their Redis and database locks in addition to the new version column.

Contract verification and tests

The Member-owned contracts/member-to-society-flat-v1.json fixture is shared by:

  • SocietyConsumerContractTest: exercises the real Member SocietyClient against the contract's success and missing-resource responses.
  • SocietyProviderContractTest: exercises Society's actual controller/serialization/error handling against that contract, including ETag revalidation. Business persistence is isolated using a mocked service.

Normal mvn test runs these checks. From the repository root on Windows:

powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File .\scripts\test-contracts.ps1

The script stops if either consumer or provider verification fails. No broker or Pact server is required. This is shared contract verification in this repository, not a separately hosted contract-publishing platform.

PostgreSQL integration tests cover migrations, scoped search, cursor continuation, audit rollback and conflicting concurrent entity updates. Docker must be running for these tests; otherwise Testcontainers marks them skipped. Test-only docker-java.properties selects API 1.44 for compatibility with current Docker Desktop and the existing Testcontainers dependency.

Deployment

Flyway adds Member V5 and Society V4; previously applied migrations are untouched. No migration has been run against your live databases by this code change.

Build new images for all three applications and roll out compatible versions together: the Gateway now refreshes account permissions through Member's new internal endpoint. Kubernetes Gateway routing includes /api/v1/accounts. Redis session format changed, so sign in again after deployment. Keep the existing session validation and Gateway authentication tokens consistent across services. Do not expose the new internal account endpoint outside the trusted service path.

An old Member/Gateway image cannot supply or interpret the new authorization scope correctly. Prefer a coordinated development restart over mixed application versions. Do not delete PostgreSQL databases to deploy this change.

Scenario questionsValidated pagination and sorting

Validated pagination and sorting

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How do you make pagination and sorting safe and predictable?

Answer: Bound page size, reject negative page numbers, allowlist sortable fields and directions, and add a stable tie-breaker to ordering. Arbitrary sort properties can trigger expensive queries or expose unintended entity fields.

Solution sketch: size = clamp(requestedSize, 1, MAX_PAGE_SIZE); sortField = ALLOWED_SORTS.getOrDefault(input, DEFAULT_SORT); then order by the chosen field plus a unique ID for deterministic pages.

Follow-up: When would you use cursor pagination instead of page-number pagination?

Answer: Use cursor pagination for large or changing datasets where offset scans become costly or rows shift between pages. Encode a stable sort key and unique tie-breaker in the cursor, validate it, and add a matching database index.

Scenario questionsIdempotency keys for safe member creation

Idempotency keys for safe member creation

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How does an idempotency key make a create request safe to retry?

Answer: Store the key, a canonical request hash, and the original result under a uniqueness constraint. Repeating the same key and payload replays the result; the same key with different input returns a conflict. Retain records long enough for client retries.

Solution sketch: UNIQUE(actor_id, idempotency_key); compare storedRequestHash with the retry hash, then replay or return HTTP 409. Write the idempotency record and business result transactionally when possible.

Follow-up: What should be included in an idempotency record?

Answer: Scope the key to the authenticated actor and operation, store a canonical request hash, processing state, expiry, and the response needed for replay. Enforce uniqueness in the database so concurrent requests with the same key cannot both create the member.

PreviousArchitecture and request journeysNext chapterData, transactions, and concurrency →

Chapter 05

Data, transactions, and concurrency

Protect writes, explain query costs, and budget database capacity.

Give every correctness mechanism a specific job

MechanismProblem it addressesLimit
Database ownershipOne service controls its business tables and migrations.It does not provide a distributed transaction.
Local transactionRelated business and audit writes commit or roll back together.Its guarantees stop at that database boundary.
Pessimistic row lockSerializes competing operations on the selected member row.Lock wait and transaction duration consume capacity.
Database unique constraintRejects a duplicate active membership even if earlier checks race.The application must translate the violation into a useful response.
Optimistic version / ETagDetects an edit based on an older representation.The client must handle a conflict and refresh.
Idempotency recordRecognizes a retry of a supported create operation.Reusing a key for a different request is a conflict.
Redis member lockCoordinates protected member operations across replicas.A lease is bounded; database integrity remains essential.

Explain one concrete race

Two requests can both read that no active membership exists. An application-level existence check alone cannot guarantee uniqueness. The row lock coordinates the relevant write sequence; the unique constraint remains the final decision at persistence. Returning a business conflict is more useful than exposing a raw integrity exception.

Queries and connection budgets

DTO projections can avoid unnecessary entity loading, but query count and query plans must be measured. Choose indexes for actual filters, ordering, and selectivity. A composite index has an order; adding every searchable column is not a capacity plan.

Each Pod has its own connection pool. The potential application connection demand is the sum of replica counts multiplied by the corresponding pool limits, plus other database clients and operational headroom. Autoscaling the service can therefore increase database pressure even if each Pod is healthy.

Read replicas and sharding

The repository contains optional reader datasource configuration, disabled in the shared application ConfigMap. That does not prove a replica is deployed or that every read is routed there. Replica lag makes read-after-write behavior a design decision. Sharding is not implemented; the reference discusses how a partition key, routing, migrations, and cross-shard queries would change the design.

Implementation references and scenarios

Implementation referenceUnderstand the database capacity this system has today

Understand the database capacity this system has today

Society and Member each own a PostgreSQL database. Both services use Spring Data JPA and Flyway with explicitly tuned Hikari writer pools and optional read-only replica pools. Replica routing is configured in the applications; PostgreSQL replica servers remain externally provisioned.

Primary and optional read-replica routing

Implemented database features

FeatureCurrent implementation
Database per serviceSociety Service connects to society_db; Member Service connects to member_db. Each owns and changes its own schema.
PostgreSQL persistence and connection poolingEach service has an explicit Hikari writer pool (default maximum 8 connections per Pod) with 2-second connection wait, 1-second validation timeout, 10-minute idle timeout, and 30-minute maximum lifetime.
Optional read-replica routingWhen enabled with a replica JDBC URL, Spring routes @Transactional(readOnly = true) database work to a separate Hikari reader pool (default maximum 4 connections per Pod). Without a replica, reads use the writer pool.
Primary consistency pathWrites use the primary. Society's authenticated Member flat-validation lookup is explicitly routed to the primary so replication lag cannot cause a false “flat not found” result.
Versioned schema migrationsFlyway applies each service's numbered SQL migrations at startup; Hibernate validates mappings against the migrated schema.
Indexes and database constraintsMigrations define primary keys, foreign keys, checks, lookup indexes, and uniqueness rules, including the active membership and active mobile-number constraints.
Query shape and index coverageFlat list/page reads use a joined DTO projection instead of one floor query per flat. Composite indexes cover floor-flat ordering and the default member/flat membership-history ordering; actual plan notes are in Database performance review.
Environment-driven database connectionEach service accepts its own JDBC URL, username, and password from environment settings; database credentials are supplied to Kubernetes through Secret references.

Replica configuration and scaling boundary

Read replicas are opt-in. Set SOCIETY_DB_REPLICA_ENABLED=true and SOCIETY_DB_REPLICA_URL for Society, and/or MEMBER_DB_REPLICA_ENABLED=true and MEMBER_DB_REPLICA_URL for Member. Replica credentials can use the optional society-replica-username/password and member-replica-username/password Secret keys; when omitted, each service falls back to its writer credentials. Kubernetes does not create or configure PostgreSQL streaming replication.

Replica reads can be behind the primary. Use the primary path where read-after-write freshness is required. The router does not automatically retry a failed replica query on the primary; monitor replica health and lag and disable replica routing if the endpoint is unhealthy.

Connection budget: the default HPA maxima allow up to 4 Society Pods and 3 Member Pods. At defaults, the theoretical writer-pool maximum is (4 + 3) × 8 = 56 connections. If both read replicas are enabled, the reader pools can add another (4 + 3) × 4 = 28, for up to 84 application connections across both databases, plus migrations and other clients. Reduce pool or HPA limits to fit the actual PostgreSQL server and replica connection budgets. When replicas are disabled, the reader route reuses the primary pool and does not create a second pool.

Query plans and index changes

Flat list and page reads now load floor numbers through one joined DTO projection instead of querying the floor once per flat. Query plans from the local PostgreSQL data showed a sort after floor-filtered flat reads and after member/flat membership-history reads, so focused composite indexes now cover those common filter-and-order paths. Global list sorts did not justify more indexes with the current tiny sample tables.

The reviewed local PostgreSQL instance reported max_connections=100. Allowing one temporary rollout Pod per backend raises the conservative writer-pool budget from 56 to 72; reserve the remaining slots for migrations, monitoring, admin sessions and other clients. Reader-pool budgets apply on their separate replica endpoints. See Database performance review for query-plan details, repeatable SQL, and the per-endpoint calculation.

Database files

  • Society settings and migrations: services/society-service/src/main/resources/application.yml and services/society-service/src/main/resources/db/migration/.
  • Member settings and migrations: services/member-service/src/main/resources/application.yml and services/member-service/src/main/resources/db/migration/.
  • Service-owned JDBC and JPA dependencies: each service's pom.xml.

The services now support optional read-only routing and tuned Hikari pools. PostgreSQL replica provisioning, replication monitoring/failover, partitioning, sharding, database HA, and production capacity measurements remain outside the repo implementation and require runtime verification.

Implementation referenceDatabase query and connection review

Database query and connection review

Converted from the database performance review.

Database query and connection review

This review covers three measured changes: Society flat-list query count, indexes for the existing list/history query shapes, and Hikari connection capacity at the configured HPA limits.

1. Flat-list N+1 query

Society's uncached flat list and paged flat list previously loaded FloorEntity once for every returned flat. Both now use a JPQL DTO projection joining FlatEntity to its floor in the database. The paged projection has an explicit count query, so its expected database work is one page query and one count query, independent of the number of rows. Floor-specific listing already fetched the floor once and did not have this N+1.

FlatServiceReadQueryTest verifies that list and page reads call the flat projection repository method and do not call FloorRepository per row. The PostgreSQL Testcontainers integration suite also covers page content/sorting when Docker is available.

2. Query plans and indexes

The local PostgreSQL 18 instance was inspected with EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS). At review time, society_db had 1 flat and 18 floors; member_db had 2 members. Plans at this volume are useful for confirming the query shape and index conditions, but are too small to establish production latency or index selectivity.

Observed query shapes:

QueryObserved planRepo change
Society flats filtered by floor_id, ordered by flat_numberExisting ix_flats_floor was used, followed by a sort.Society Flyway V3 replaces it with (floor_id, flat_number), which supports the filter and ordering from one index.
Member memberships filtered by member_id or flat_id, ordered by start_date DESCExisting single-column index was used, followed by a sort.Member Flyway V4 replaces each single-column index with (member_id, start_date DESC) and (flat_id, start_date DESC).
Society all-flats projection ordered by flat_numberSmall table used a sequential scan, join, and sort.No global sort index added; measure with representative flat counts before paying its write/storage cost.
Members ordered by created_at DESCSmall table used a sequential scan and sort.No index added until real row counts and plans justify one.

The proposed composite indexes were also created temporarily inside BEGIN/ROLLBACK transactions. With sequential and bitmap scans disabled for this plan-shape check, PostgreSQL selected each composite index as an ordered index scan with no explicit sort. This confirms the index column order matches the common query; it does not claim a measured production speedup.

To repeat the checks against a populated local database:

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT f.id, f.flat_number, f.floor_id, fl.floor_number, f.status
FROM flats f
JOIN floors fl ON fl.id = f.floor_id
WHERE f.floor_id = '<floor-uuid>'
ORDER BY f.flat_number
LIMIT 20 OFFSET 0;

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT id, member_id, flat_id, relationship_type, start_date, end_date, created_at, updated_at
FROM flat_memberships
WHERE member_id = '<member-uuid>'
ORDER BY start_date DESC
LIMIT 20 OFFSET 0;

EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)
SELECT id, member_id, flat_id, relationship_type, start_date, end_date, created_at, updated_at
FROM flat_memberships
WHERE flat_id = '<flat-uuid>'
ORDER BY start_date DESC
LIMIT 20 OFFSET 0;

Do not compare one warm-cache execution as a benchmark. Capture representative row counts, repeated plans, buffers, and application latency before adding more sort indexes.

3. Hikari connection budget

Configured maxima are 8 writer connections and 4 reader connections per Pod. Reader pools are created only when the relevant replica flag is enabled. Current HPA maxima are Society 4 Pods and Member 3 Pods.

PostgreSQL endpointAt HPA steady maximumDuring a conservative one-Pod-per-service rollout surge
Primary writer pools, replica flags off(4 + 3) × 8 = 56(5 + 4) × 8 = 72
Society reader endpoint, if enabled4 × 4 = 165 × 4 = 20
Member reader endpoint, if enabled3 × 4 = 124 × 4 = 16

The local PostgreSQL server reported max_connections = 100. At the configured maximum, the current primary writer pools fit with 44 slots left at steady scale or 28 under the conservative rollout estimate. Those remaining slots must also cover migrations, monitoring, administration, and any other applications. The optional reader connections consume capacity on their respective reader servers; if both point to one shared standby, budget their totals together there.

The pg_stat_activity sample showed one session for each project database during this review, including the inspection sessions. That is only a point-in-time count, not a workload capacity test. Recheck max_connections, reserved slots, active sessions, and pool metrics in the deployment environment before changing pool or HPA maxima.

SHOW max_connections;
SELECT datname, state, count(*) AS connections
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE datname IN ('society_db', 'member_db')
GROUP BY datname, state
ORDER BY datname, state;

Pool defaults and HPA limits are in services/*/src/main/resources/application.yml, kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml, and kubernetes/autoscaling/*-hpa.yaml.

Implementation referenceProtect concurrent membership updates

Protect concurrent membership updates

This phase helps keep membership data correct when many requests arrive together, including requests handled by different app instances. Redis and PostgreSQL help those requests take turns and prevent duplicate assignments.

Concurrent membership update flow

What do “distributed” and “concurrent” mean?

Concurrent means two or more requests happen at the same time. Distributed means the app runs as more than one instance. A local lock can coordinate requests in one instance; a distributed lock coordinates through a shared service such as Redis.

Implemented features

FeatureWhat it doesWhere it works
Redis membership lockMember takes a Redis lock for the member before assigning or ending a membership. Other updates for that member wait their turn. The lock uses a unique owner value, expires after 30 seconds, and waits up to 2 seconds to be acquired.Distributed: Member instances share Redis. If Redis is unavailable, the write fails with 503 MEMBERSHIP_LOCK_UNAVAILABLE. If the lock is still busy after 2 seconds, the API returns 409 MEMBERSHIP_UPDATE_IN_PROGRESS.
PostgreSQL member row lockThe database also makes writes for the same member take turns. It protects the write while the database transaction runs.Across all app instances using the same PostgreSQL database.
Active membership duplicate ruleThe app checks for an existing active assignment. A database index also prevents saving the same member, flat, and relationship type twice.Across all app instances using the same database.
Society cache lockWhen a cached item is missing, requests in one Society instance coordinate briefly so they do not all load the same item at once. It waits up to 10 milliseconds.One Society instance only. It is not a distributed lock.
Society call limitMember limits how many calls it sends to Society at once.Each Member instance applies its own limit. This controls traffic; it does not lock membership data.

How a membership assignment works

POST /api/v1/members/{memberId}/flats
        |
        v
MembershipService.assign()
  1. Check the member and ask Society to check the flat
        |
        v
RedisMembershipLock
  2. Get the shared lock for this member
        |
        v
MembershipWriter.persist() starts a database transaction
        |
        v
MemberRepository.findByIdForUpdate()
  3. PostgreSQL locks the member row
        |
        v
  4. Check for an active duplicate, then save
        |
        v
  5. Transaction finishes; release the Redis lock

The call to Society happens before the Redis and database locks are held. This keeps the locked part short.

What happens in common cases?

ExampleWhat happensResult
First valid assignment for M1 to F101 as OWNERThe app checks the flat, gets the locks, checks for an existing assignment, and saves it.201 Created
Send the same assignment againThe app finds the active assignment already saved. The database index is an extra guard.409 DUPLICATE_MEMBERSHIP
Two matching requests arrive togetherOne gets the Redis lock and saves. The other waits, then checks the database and finds the assignment.Usually one 201 and one 409 DUPLICATE_MEMBERSHIP. If the Redis lock stays busy for 2 seconds, the waiting request gets 409 MEMBERSHIP_UPDATE_IN_PROGRESS.
Two updates for the same member, different flatsThey share that member's Redis lock and database row, so they take turns. Each assignment is checked separately.Both can be saved if both are valid and not already active.
Two updates for different membersEach member has a different Redis lock and database row.They can usually run at the same time.
Member does not exist or is inactiveThe app checks the member before saving.Missing: 404. Inactive: 409 MEMBER_INACTIVE.
Redis is unavailableThe app does not start the membership write because it cannot get the shared lock.503 MEMBERSHIP_LOCK_UNAVAILABLE
End an existing membershipThe app uses the same Redis lock and PostgreSQL member row lock before ending it.The update is protected from another membership change for the same member.

Code names to know

CodeWhat it does
MembershipService.assign() and end()For an assignment, checks the member and asks Society to check the flat before getting the lock. Ending a membership also uses the lock.
RedisMembershipLock.withMemberLock()Creates a Redis lock key for the member, waits up to 2 seconds, and releases only the lock owned by this request. The 30-second expiry clears a lock if its owner stops unexpectedly.
MembershipWriter.persist() and end() with @TransactionalRun the database update. They lock the member row before changing membership data.
MemberRepository.findByIdForUpdate() with @Lock(PESSIMISTIC_WRITE)Asks PostgreSQL to make another write for this member wait.
FlatMembershipRepository.existsByMemberIdAndFlatIdAndRelationshipTypeAndEndDateIsNull()Checks whether this active assignment already exists.
uq_active_membership in V1__create_member_tables.sqlDatabase rule that blocks a second matching active assignment.
SocietyCacheConfigurationCoordinates Society cache cold misses with short per-key Redis locks on dedicated cache Redis. These are separate from the Member membership lock.

Confirm the behavior with Postman

Use the local Option A setup with Redis, Society on 8081, Member on 8082, and Gateway on 8080. Select the Emerald Housing - Option B Localhost environment and use the existing collection. Requests go through http://localhost:8080.

  1. Get a CSRF token with GET /api/v1/auth/csrf, sign in with POST /api/v1/auth/login, then get a fresh CSRF token again.
  2. Use POST Assign Member to Flat with a member and flat that have no active matching assignment. Expect 201 Created, then use GET Member Memberships to confirm it was saved.
  3. Send the same assignment again with POST Duplicate Assignment. Expect 409 DUPLICATE_MEMBERSHIP.
  4. For the concurrent case, use a new member and flat assignment. Send Concurrent Assignment A and Concurrent Assignment B with the same values as close together as possible. Expect one request to save and the other to receive a conflict. Then use GET Member Memberships and confirm there is only one matching active assignment.

Postman confirms the API behavior, but cannot prove the requests overlapped or show which safeguard handled the second request. The steps above are manual; no automated concurrency test is included.

Implementation reference6. Data ownership and audit structure

6. Data ownership and audit structure

Society database
societies
   | 1:N
floors
   | 1:N

audit_events
  flat before/after snapshots
Member database
members [version]
   | 1:N
flat_memberships [version]
   flat_id -> external Society UUID

admin_users [version]
   member_id -> members (optional*)
idempotency_records
audit_events
* A RESIDENT account requires a linked member. flat_id is a service reference, not a cross-database foreign key.

Each audit event contains a local sequence, actor ID/name, action, entity type/ID, before/after JSON, UTC timestamp and correlation ID. The two service audit sequences are independent. Audit is application-level history; it does not guarantee protection from direct database administrator changes.

GET /api/v1/members/audit?entityType=MEMBER&entityId={memberId}&limit=50
GET /api/v1/society/audit?entityType=FLAT&entityId={flatId}&limit=50
Older audit events: append &before={lastReturnedSequence}

Audit limits are 1–100. Passwords, hashes and tokens are excluded from snapshots; member snapshots still contain personal details and require administrator access. Idempotent member-create replays return the saved result without another creation audit event.

Migrations: Member V5 adds roles, account member links, versions, audit and cursor/access indexes. Society V4 adds flat versions, audit and cursor indexes. Existing migrations remain unchanged.

Scenario questionsRead Replica

Read Replica

1. What is a read replica, and why would you use one?

A read replica is a database server that receives replicated changes from a primary database and can serve queries. It can spread read traffic and reduce load on the primary. The replica can lag behind the primary, so it may return older data for a short time. This project supports routing to an external PostgreSQL replica, but does not create or manage that replica.

Interview answer: “We keep writes on the primary and can offload suitable read-only transactions to a replica. We account for replication lag by keeping consistency-sensitive reads on the primary.”

2. How does this project route reads and writes?

Spring's transaction metadata is checked by ReadOnlyRoutingDataSource. A read-only transaction selects the reader; other transactions use the writer. The routing data source is wrapped in LazyConnectionDataSourceProxy so the JDBC connection is requested after Spring has set the transaction's read-only flag.

@Override
protected Object determineCurrentLookupKey() {
    return TransactionSynchronizationManager.isCurrentTransactionReadOnly()
            ? READ_ONLY
            : null; // default target is the writer
}

The same pattern is implemented in both backend services. The reader maps to the writer when replica support is off.

3. How do you mark a service query for replica routing?

Put @Transactional(readOnly = true) on the Spring service method that performs the database read. For example, Society's floor lookup methods use this annotation.

@Transactional(readOnly = true)
public List<FloorResponse> getFloors() {
    return floorRepository.findAllByOrderByFloorNumberAsc()
            .stream()
            .map(floor -> new FloorResponse(floor.getFloorNumber()))
            .toList();
}

Keep write operations in normal read-write transactions. The annotation is transaction metadata used for routing; it is not a guarantee that the database itself is read-only.

4. How do you enable and configure the replica?

Provision PostgreSQL replication separately, then give the service a reachable replica URL and credentials. The app defaults to disabled, so setting only a URL does not activate routing.

# Society Service environment
SOCIETY_DB_REPLICA_ENABLED=true
SOCIETY_DB_REPLICA_URL=jdbc:postgresql://society-replica:5432/society_db
SOCIETY_DB_REPLICA_USERNAME=emerald_reader
SOCIETY_DB_REPLICA_PASSWORD=${SOCIETY_REPLICA_PASSWORD}

# Member Service uses MEMBER_DB_REPLICA_ENABLED,
# MEMBER_DB_REPLICA_URL, MEMBER_DB_REPLICA_USERNAME,
# and MEMBER_DB_REPLICA_PASSWORD.

Use a read-only database account for the replica connection and store its password in a secret manager or Kubernetes Secret, not in committed configuration. Both service application.yml files expose these settings through environment variables.

5. What happens when replica routing is disabled?

The reader data source bean is not created unless its enabled property is true. The routing configuration then uses the writer as the reader target, so read-only transactions continue to work against the primary. In this repository's Kubernetes ConfigMap, both replica flags are currently false.

6. What is replica lag, and how can it affect the application?

Replication is usually asynchronous. After a successful write to the primary, the replica may not have received that write yet. A read routed immediately to the replica can therefore return stale data or report that a newly written record is missing.

Solution: route consistency-sensitive reads to the primary, such as validation immediately following a write. In this project, Society's flat-assignment validation explicitly uses a read-write transaction for that reason:

// Keep this authoritative validation on the primary
@Transactional(readOnly = false)
public FlatResponse validateFlatForAssignment(...) {
    // query the primary before Member saves the assignment
}

For workflows that need stronger guarantees across multiple requests, use an explicit primary-read policy or a database-specific replication-position strategy; do not assume the replica is instantly current.

7. How would you test that routing works?

Unit-test the routing data source with a reader and writer and assert which one is selected when Spring's transaction read-only flag is true or false. This repository has such tests in both services. Then add an integration test with distinguishable databases or connection identities to verify real transaction wiring and proxy order.

TransactionSynchronizationManager.setCurrentTransactionReadOnly(true);
// routing.getConnection() should reach the reader

TransactionSynchronizationManager.setCurrentTransactionReadOnly(false);
// routing.getConnection() should reach the writer

Also verify behavior with the feature disabled and with the configured replica unreachable. Unit routing tests do not prove replication health or replica freshness.

8. What happens if the replica is unavailable?

With replica routing enabled, a read-only transaction is directed to the configured reader. The current implementation does not include health-based failover or retry-to-primary behavior, so replica connection failures can fail the request.

Production improvement: monitor replica connectivity and lag, define a deliberate fallback policy, and test it. If falling back to the primary, make that policy observable and account for the added load; never silently claim that a replica is healthy just because the primary can serve the query.

9. What are the limits of this implementation?

The application supplies optional connection pools and read/write routing. PostgreSQL provisioning, replication setup, lag monitoring, automatic failover, and high availability are operational responsibilities outside this code. The feature is opt-in and currently disabled in the checked-in Kubernetes ConfigMap.

Interview summary: “The routing mechanism is implemented in Society and Member, but a production replica must be provisioned and configured separately. I would enable it only after validating credentials, connectivity, replication lag, failure handling, and query consistency requirements.”

Scenario questionsDatabase Sharding

Database Sharding

Project status: Database sharding is not implemented in Emerald Housing. The examples below are interview solution patterns, not existing project code. The services have separate databases and optional read-replica routing; neither distributes one service's records across shards.
1. What is database sharding, and how does it differ from replication?

Sharding splits a dataset across nodes, with each shard holding part of the data. Replication copies the same dataset to another node. Sharding can increase storage and write capacity; replicas often improve read capacity and availability. A system can use both, with each shard having replicas.

2. Why shard a database, and what signs indicate it may be needed?

Sharding can increase write throughput and storage capacity. Consider it after measurements show query/index tuning, vertical scaling, partitioning, or read replicas cannot meet targets. Track write rate, storage growth, latency, CPU, I/O, locks, and connection saturation; load-test alternatives first.

3. How do you choose a shard key?

Choose a stable, high-cardinality field used in common lookups that spreads writes evenly, such as tenant ID for tenant-scoped access. Validate against real query patterns and growth.

Conceptual Java: Shard shard = shardRouter.forTenant(tenantId); then query that shard using both tenantId and the record ID.

4. What makes a good shard key, and what can a poor one cause?

A good key distributes data and traffic while matching common queries. A poor key creates oversized or overloaded shards and cross-shard lookups. A low-cardinality status such as ACTIVE/INACTIVE is usually poor. Choose a higher-cardinality access key, test skew, and avoid mutable keys.

5. What is a hot shard, and how can you prevent one?

A hot shard receives a disproportionate share of requests or writes, often because one tenant dominates or sequential IDs target one range. Monitor per-shard load; improve distribution with hashing or buckets, and isolate unusually large tenants when needed.

6. What are range-based and hash-based sharding?

Range sharding assigns key intervals to shards; it supports range scans but can create hot spots. Hash sharding spreads keys more evenly, but range queries may touch many shards. Choose based on measured lookup and write patterns.

Illustrative syntax: int shardIndex = Math.floorMod(tenantId.hashCode(), shardCount); This fixed-count mapping is simple but changing the count requires data migration.

7. How does consistent hashing help when shard count changes?

Modulo hashing can remap most keys when shard count changes. Consistent hashing generally limits remapping to a subset, and virtual nodes can improve balance. It does not move data by itself: use versioned routing metadata and a controlled migration.

8. How do you route a query to the correct shard?

The application or a database proxy uses the shard key to choose the destination before querying. Keep the key in common repository operations and derive tenant identity from authenticated context.

Illustrative syntax: Shard shard = shardMap.forTenant(authenticatedTenantId); followed by shard.members().findByTenantIdAndId(authenticatedTenantId, memberId);. Do not use a caller-supplied shard name as authorization.

9. How do you handle queries that need data from multiple shards?

A query without a shard key may need scatter-gather: run against many shards and merge results. This increases latency and load. Keep common API queries shard-key-scoped; use an analytics store, read model, or asynchronous aggregation for broad reports.

10. How do you handle joins across shards?

Joins are efficient when related records share a shard key and are colocated. Cross-shard joins require fetching and merging data across nodes. Choose a shared key for commonly joined data, or use a documented read model/denormalization strategy with clear update consistency.

11. How do you enforce uniqueness across shards?

A unique index on one shard cannot guarantee global uniqueness if equal values can land on different shards. Include the shard key in local unique constraints, use globally unique IDs, or coordinate allocation for business identifiers that must be unique everywhere.

12. How do you handle transactions that span multiple shards?

Cross-shard transactions need coordination and can reduce performance and availability. Prefer shard-local transactions. For multi-step workflows use a saga: commit local steps and run compensating actions if a later step fails.

Solution sketch: reserveInventory(); try { chargePayment(); } catch (RuntimeException e) { releaseInventory(); throw e; }.

13. How do you rebalance data when adding or removing shards?

Move data in bounded, restartable batches while tracking writes made during the copy. Verify counts/checksums, switch routing in a controlled step, throttle migration load, and keep a rollback path. Changing the hash map alone does not move records safely.

14. How would you migrate an existing database to sharding?

Identify the shard key, add routing support, provision shards, backfill in batches, capture concurrent changes, and compare counts/checksums. Gradually switch reads and then writes with monitoring and rollback. Keep routing-map and migration versions explicit.

15. How do you handle shard failure and recovery?

Define per-shard health checks, replicas/failover, backups, recovery-point and recovery-time targets, and routing updates. Test restore and failover regularly, and retry only operations designed to be safe to repeat.

16. How do you back up and restore a sharded database?

Back up every shard and preserve a consistent recovery point across shards when workflows span them. Record the routing-map version with backup metadata and regularly test restoring one shard and the whole system.

17. How do you monitor shard health and load balance?

Compare latency, request/error rates, CPU, disk, pool usage, replication lag, and data size by shard. Use bounded shard labels in metrics and avoid customer IDs as labels. Alert on skew and saturation, which cluster-wide averages can hide.

18. How does sharding affect pagination and sorting?

Shard-local pagination is simple; globally sorted pages require merging ordered results from multiple shards. Use a stable cursor and deterministic sort key with tie-breakers. Prefer shard-scoped pagination when the product allows it.

19. How do you manage schema changes across shards?

Use backward-compatible expand-and-contract migrations: add schema, deploy code that supports both forms, backfill, switch usage, then remove old fields later. Track migration status per shard and make migrations restartable.

20. What security and access-control concerns arise with sharding?

Routing must not let callers access another tenant's data. Derive tenant/shard from authenticated identity, enforce tenant predicates, use least-privilege credentials and TLS, and test forged tenant IDs for cross-tenant access.

21. What should you try before introducing sharding?

Review query plans and indexes, pool sizing, vertical scaling, partitioning, caching, read replicas, and workload-specific read models. Shard only when measured bottlenecks remain and the team can operate its migration and failure paths.

22. Does this project need sharding, and how would you explain that in an interview?

There is no evidence in the current implementation that sharding is needed. This repository has separate databases per service and optional read-replica routing, but no shard key or multi-shard router.

Interview answer: “I would not add sharding without workload measurements showing that a service database cannot meet capacity targets after simpler options. If needed, I would choose a stable key aligned with access patterns, keep common transactions shard-local, and plan rebalancing, monitoring, migration, and recovery before rollout.”

Scenario questionsHikariCP pool sizing and connection budgeting

HikariCP pool sizing and connection budgeting

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How do you size a Hikari pool and budget connections across replicas?

Answer: Measure database capacity and request concurrency. Count every possible app Pod and each pool, then reserve connections for migrations, monitoring, admin tools, and failover.

Budget formula: appPods × poolMaxPerPod + otherClients ≤ databaseMaxConnections − safetyReserve. HPA max replicas matter, not only current replicas. Increasing each pool can make an overloaded database worse.

Follow-up: What metrics would you use to tune a HikariCP pool?

Answer: Measure active, idle, pending, and timed-out connections along with query latency and database saturation. If callers wait while the database is saturated, adding connections may worsen contention; first find slow queries and calculate the total pool budget at maximum Pod count.

Scenario questionsN+1 query prevention with DTO projections

N+1 query prevention with DTO projections

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: What is N+1, and when can a DTO projection help?

Answer: One query loads a page of parent rows, then one query per parent loads related data. A projection selects the fields needed for the response in one bounded query and avoids loading full entity graphs.

Solution sketch: select new FlatSummary(f.id, f.number, floor.number) from Flat f join f.floor floor where .... Check SQL query counts in tests; do not solve it with an unbounded fetch join on multiple collections.

Follow-up: How do you verify that a DTO projection actually removes N+1 queries?

Answer: Capture SQL or query counts for a representative page and assert the count stays bounded as the number of rows grows. Keep pagination in the database and project only required columns; avoid loading full entity graphs and then mapping them one row at a time.

Scenario questionsComposite indexes and query-plan analysis

Composite indexes and query-plan analysis

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How do you choose a composite index and verify that it helps?

Answer: Start from real predicates, joins, and ordering. Column order matters; equality filters often precede range/order columns. Use the query plan and representative data to see whether the database uses the index and whether it reduces rows and cost.

Example: CREATE INDEX idx_membership_member_start ON flat_memberships(member_id, start_date DESC); then inspect with EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) SELECT ... WHERE member_id = ? ORDER BY start_date DESC;. Add indexes for measured queries, accounting for write and storage cost.

Follow-up: What if EXPLAIN shows the database is not using your new index?

Answer: Check predicate and sort column order, data selectivity, stale statistics, implicit casts, and whether the query returns a large fraction of the table. Compare actual rows and buffer reads with and without the index, then keep the index only if measured workload improves.

Scenario questionsPessimistic database locking and unique constraints

Pessimistic database locking and unique constraints

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: When use pessimistic locking, and why keep a unique constraint?

Answer: Lock a row when concurrent changes must serialize around existing data, for example when ending a membership. A unique constraint protects correctness even if application locks are missed, expire, or multiple code paths race.

Spring/JPA pattern: @Lock(LockModeType.PESSIMISTIC_WRITE) on a repository lookup, plus a database UNIQUE(member_id, flat_id, relationship_type, active_key) constraint matching the invariant. Handle constraint violations as a controlled conflict.

Follow-up: How do you reduce deadlocks when transactions use pessimistic locks?

Answer: Keep transactions short, acquire rows in a consistent order, avoid remote calls while holding locks, and retry only recognized deadlock failures with a small bounded policy. Keep unique constraints as the final guard for invariants.

PreviousAPI behavior and client workflowsNext chapterRedis: connections, keys, and execution →

Chapter 06

Redis: connections, keys, and execution

Follow sessions, token buckets, locks, cache hits, and invalidation.

Two names, two Redis processes, four main uses

redis and redis-cache are independent Kubernetes workloads. They use the same Redis image but different Services, credentials, consumers, and eviction policies. The browser never connects to either instance directly.

Redis workloadConsumersStored stateConfigured policy
redis:6379Gateway and Member ServiceGateway sessions, rate-limit tokens, and membership locks.128 MB Redis maxmemory; noeviction.
redis-cache:6379Society ServiceCached response data, negative entries, and cache-load locks.128 MB Redis maxmemory; allkeys-lru.

Creation and connection

  1. Kubernetes creates the workloads

    Each Deployment starts one redis:7.4-alpine Pod. Its ClusterIP Service selects the matching app label and exposes port 6379.

  2. Configuration chooses the address

    Gateway and Member read REDIS_HOST=redis. Society reads SOCIETY_CACHE_REDIS_HOST=redis-cache. Kubernetes DNS resolves those names in the namespace.

  3. Secrets supply credentials

    The main instance reads redis-password; the cache instance reads redis-cache-password from emerald-security-credentials. Consumer Deployments receive the corresponding values.

  4. Spring supplies the client integration

    Gateway uses Spring Session and RedisRateLimiter. Member uses StringRedisTemplate. Society uses Spring Cache with a RedisCacheManager and explicit StringRedisTemplate operations.

1. A login session

After credentials are validated, Gateway stores authenticated session state through Spring Session. The browser receives the SESSION cookie containing a session ID. Later requests use that ID to locate shared session state. Any compatible Gateway replica can use the same store. The configured session timeout defaults to 30 minutes; the namespace is emerald:gateway:session.

2. A request token bucket

Gateway evaluates a Redis-backed token bucket for the matched route and resolved caller key. Member routes default to a replenish rate of 10 tokens per second and a burst capacity of 20; Society routes default to 20 and 40. A request costs one token. A depleted bucket returns HTTP 429. The login limiter has its own configuration and key behavior.

3. A membership write lock

Member builds a key under emerald:member:membership-lock:. An atomic setIfAbsent stores an ownership token with an expiration. A competing request waits within the configured bound. Release checks the token before deleting the key. A process crash leaves a lease that can expire rather than an indefinite lock.

4. A cached Society lookup

  1. Check the key

    A cacheable service operation checks the dedicated cache Redis. Authorization still determines which operation and data are permitted.

  2. Return a hit

    If the entry exists, the cached representation avoids the corresponding database load.

  3. Coordinate a miss

    The cache wrapper attempts a per-key load lock. Waiters recheck for the result; after the bounded wait, a waiter may load without caching.

  4. Load and store

    The loader queries PostgreSQL and saves the representation with its configured TTL. Warmup can populate selected entries before the first request.

  5. Invalidate after a write commits

    The flat-change listener removes the affected flat entry, list entries, floor-specific entries, and negative entry after commit.

Cache nameContentBase TTL
societyFloorsFloor list10 minutes
societyFlatsByFloorFlats on a floor5 minutes
societyFlatByIdOne flat2 minutes
societyFlatsAll-flat list5 minutes

TTL jitter subtracts up to 30 seconds from the base expiration. A not-found marker has a separate 30-second base TTL. Load-lock defaults are a 2-second wait, a 30-second lease, and a 25-millisecond retry interval.

Outages and consistency limits

On cache Redis errors, Society can bypass Redis briefly and load the database under a per-Pod fallback limit. The shared configuration allows 16 concurrent fallback loads; overload is reported as 503 CACHE_FALLBACK_OVERLOADED. This behavior applies to the disposable cache, not automatically to session state or membership coordination.

Separate memory; separate consequences

Cache eviction does not remove sessions because the instances are separate. Both Deployments use emptyDir storage, so Pod replacement loses their data. noeviction prevents automatic memory-pressure eviction; it does not make Redis durable and can cause writes to fail when memory is exhausted.

After-commit invalidation avoids eviction for rolled-back writes. It does not eliminate every stale-read race or guarantee delivery of a failed invalidation. TTL bounds the lifetime of cached entries; more stringent consistency requires a stronger design.

Start with the ConfigMap, main Redis Deployment, cache Redis Deployment, and the cache configuration and invalidation listener linked in the references.

Implementation referenceMake repeated Society lookups faster with Redis

Make repeated Society lookups faster with Redis

Society Service still owns the Society data in PostgreSQL. Spring Cache keeps short-lived copies of selected floor and flat reference responses in a dedicated Redis cache service. Gateway sessions and rate limits remain in a separate Redis service. A cache can save repeated database reads, but it does not become the source of truth.

Cache-aside read and after-commit invalidation

NEW — PHASE 10: Society floor and flat reference reads use Redis-backed Spring Cache with per-cache lifetimes, cross-Pod miss coordination, short negative entries, and after-commit invalidation. Flat assignment validation from Member Service still reads Society's database through its existing authenticated HTTP request.
UPDATED — PHASE 10: Society now uses a dedicated redis-cache Deployment and Service for disposable cache data. The original redis remains for Gateway sessions, rate limits, and Member locks. Cache settings, credentials, and Society-only network access are configured separately.

What caching means in this project

A cache is a nearby, temporary copy of data that is expensive or repetitive to fetch. The dedicated cache Redis keeps these copies in memory, so a later matching request can return the saved response without repeating the same PostgreSQL query. This is useful for Society reference data because floors and flat descriptions are read often and changed less often than they are viewed.

PostgreSQL remains the permanent record. If Redis is empty, an entry expires, or the cache is bypassed after an error, Society Service reads PostgreSQL. Cache Redis stores temporary copies; it does not replace Flyway, JPA, or either service's database ownership.
