What changed after the previous resilience work?

Before caching, a Society read went from the application service to a JPA repository and then PostgreSQL. Now Spring Cache checks Redis first for selected read methods. Step 9 resilience controls are still responsible for bounded dependency failures and safe Member-to-Society validation. Step 10 reduces repeated database reads; it does not replace those controls.

BEFORE
Society Service -> JPA Repository -> PostgreSQL

AFTER
Society Service -> Spring Cache
                     |-- HIT  -> Redis -> return response
                     `-- MISS -> JPA Repository -> PostgreSQL
                                                  `-> save response in Redis

Redis in plain English

Redis is a key-value store: an application supplies a key, and Redis stores a value under that key. It is commonly used for fast temporary data, such as cache entries, rate-limit counters, and web sessions. PostgreSQL is the durable relational database in this project. It stores the official Society and Member records and enforces database constraints. Redis cache entries can expire or disappear without losing the official Society records.

The main private redis Service holds Spring Session, Gateway rate-limit, and Member lock data. Society uses the separate redis-cache Service for response caches, negative entries, and per-key load locks. Separate Services have separate processes and memory budgets; the key prefix remains society:cache:. Cache Redis uses allkeys-lru, so it can discard least-recently-used cache keys under memory pressure without evicting session or rate-limit keys.

Runtime caveat: the manifests configure separate Redis Services and expose Society cache-memory metrics, but the cluster has not been applied or load-tested. Redis cache is still a single Pod with temporary storage, so cache data and locks can disappear when that Pod is replaced.

Spring Cache and the important annotations

  • @EnableCaching turns on Spring's cache interception in SocietyCacheConfiguration.
  • @Cacheable checks Redis before running a method. A hit returns the cached DTO; a miss loads the response and stores it in one of societyFloors, societyFlatById, societyFlatsByFloor, or societyFlats.
  • @TransactionalEventListener(phase = TransactionPhase.AFTER_COMMIT) handles flat-change events after the database transaction succeeds. The listener evicts the flat entry, matching negative marker, and affected all-flats and floor-list entries.
  • @CacheEvict and @CachePut are not used. Eviction is performed programmatically after commit; update responses are not written directly into the cache.
  • RedisCacheManager is the cache manager, not an annotation. It configures cache names, key/value serializers, prefixes, TTLs, and cache statistics.

Spring applies caching through a proxy around Spring-managed service methods. Calls from the controller enter that proxy. A method calling another cached method on the same object could bypass that proxy; these cache annotations are on public service entry points, not self-calls.

No Caffeine local cache or Hibernate/JPA second-level cache is configured. Redis is the shared application cache; PostgreSQL remains the source of truth.

Cache-aside: hit and miss flow

Postman / API client
       |
       v
NGINX -> API Gateway ---- redis (sessions / rate limits)
              |
              v
       Society Service Pod
                              |
                              v
                     Spring Cache checks redis-cache
                        /             \
                 CACHE HIT          CACHE MISS
                    |                   |
                    v                   v
              redis-cache       Society Service method
                    |                   |
                    |                   v
                    |             JPA Repository
                    |                   |
                    |                   v
                    |              society_db
                    |                   |
                    |          create response DTO
                    |                   |
                    |                   v
                    |             save in redis-cache
                    \___________________/
                              |
                              v
                     Return same API response

The cache value is a response DTO such as FloorResponse or FlatResponse, not a JPA entity with lazy database relationships. This keeps the cache value small and independent of an open Hibernate session. A per-cache-key Redis lock coordinates misses across Society Pods; if Redis is unavailable, reads bypass it and a per-Pod semaphore limits database fallback concurrency.

Cache names, keys, and expiration

DataCache name / keyTTLConfiguration property
All ordered floorssocietyFloors / all10-minute base, reduced by 0–30 seconds of jittersociety.cache.ttl.floors
Flat by UUID, admin readsocietyFlatById / UUID2-minute base, reduced by 0–30 seconds of jittersociety.cache.ttl.flat-by-id
Flats on a floorsocietyFlatsByFloor / floor number5-minute base, reduced by 0–30 seconds of jittersociety.cache.ttl.flats-by-floor
All flatssocietyFlats / all5-minute base, reduced by 0–30 seconds of jittersociety.cache.ttl.all-flats
Unknown flat UUID markersociety:negative:flat:UUID30 secondssociety.cache.ttl.not-found

The actual Redis key for a normal cached response is prefixed by society:cache:, followed by the cache name and Spring Cache key. Session and rate-limit keys live on a different Redis service. The YAML accepts environment overrides including SOCIETY_CACHE_TTL_JITTER and SOCIETY_CACHE_TTL_NOT_FOUND.

TTL means “how long this copy may live.” After expiry, the next request is a miss and reloads PostgreSQL. TTL alone does not immediately refresh data after an update, which is why successful flat changes also trigger cache eviction.

Example floor-list timeline
00:00  Society Pod starts -> warm floor list in redis-cache
00:20  GET /api/v1/floors -> hit -> redis-cache
09:30+ Expired entry may miss (TTL jitter spreads nearby expirations)
        -> PostgreSQL -> redis-cache -> return floors

Updating a flat and invalidating old copies

Admin updates a flat
       |
       v
FlatService.update() starts database transaction
       |
       +--> save updated flat in society_db
       |
       +--> publish FlatReferenceChangedEvent
       |
       v
Transaction commits successfully
       |
       v
@TransactionalEventListener(AFTER_COMMIT)
       |
       +--> evict flat-by-ID key
       +--> evict all-flats list
       +--> evict old floor's list
       `--> evict new floor's list, if different
       |
       v
Next read loads the committed value and repopulates cache

If the database transaction rolls back, the AFTER_COMMIT listener does not run, so it does not evict values for a change that was not saved. If Redis is unavailable during eviction, ordinary cached reads may temporarily see an old value until its TTL expires. Also, a read that started before an update may finish after invalidation and repopulate an older value; the TTL bounds that race, but this design is not linearizable. Assignment validation remains uncached, so these cache consistency limits cannot approve a membership using cached flat data.

Stampede control, negative caching, and warmup

A cache stampede happens when many requests miss the same key together. The cache wrapper now takes a short-lived Redis lock for the specific cache name and key. The owner token ensures a request only releases its own lock. Other Society Pods recheck Redis while waiting up to two seconds; if the lock remains unavailable, a request loads without caching the result, so a slow source cannot hold requests indefinitely. This coordinates the normal miss path across Pods; it is not a correctness lock for database writes.

Unknown flat IDs are remembered as negative entries for 30 seconds on the Admin cached-read path. The API still returns the normal 404 RESOURCE_NOT_FOUND. Member's assignment validation continues to use the uncached authoritative database path. Flat-change events also clear a matching negative marker after commit.

On startup, each Society Pod checks the shared cache and warms only the small floor list when it is absent. This avoids a cold floor-list read for the first Admin request in the usual case. The warmup is configurable with SOCIETY_CACHE_WARMUP_ENABLED; it does not preload the full flat inventory. If Pods start together while the shared entry is missing, more than one small warmup query can still occur.

Redis and PostgreSQL failure behavior

SituationBehavior in this implementationData safety
Cache hitReturn the typed DTO from Redis.Temporary read copy; limited by TTL and invalidation.
Cache miss or expired keyLoad from PostgreSQL, then put the DTO in Redis.PostgreSQL remains authoritative.
Redis command/connect failureCache error handler records the failure. Society bypasses cache Redis for five seconds per Pod, then retries; fallback database loads are limited to 16 concurrent calls per Pod.If fallback capacity is full, return 503 CACHE_FALLBACK_OVERLOADED instead of sending unlimited reads to PostgreSQL.
Cache Redis write failure / memory fullReturn the successful database result while the cache write is skipped. Cache Redis uses a dedicated allkeys-lru policy and memory metrics.Cache data may be evicted or rebuilt; sessions and rate limits are on a separate Redis service.
PostgreSQL unavailableA cache hit may avoid a database query, but transaction setup and runtime behavior have not been verified. A miss cannot be filled and returns the existing database error.Do not rely on cache as a database outage mode. Cached data is not used for flat assignment validation or writes.
Flat assignment validationMember calls the Society flat endpoint with its service identity. Controller selects FlatService.get(), which is deliberately not cached.Society checks its authoritative database before Member writes membership.

Actual code: where to look

Redis settings

services/society-service/src/main/resources/application.yml

Reads the Society cache Redis host, port, password, connection/command timeouts, and bounded Lettuce pool settings. Local development can use the legacy REDIS_* fallback; Kubernetes points Society at separate redis-cache.

spring.data.redis:
  host: ${SOCIETY_CACHE_REDIS_HOST:${REDIS_HOST:localhost}}
  port: ${SOCIETY_CACHE_REDIS_PORT:${REDIS_PORT:6379}}
  password: ${SOCIETY_CACHE_REDIS_PASSWORD:${REDIS_PASSWORD:}}
  connect-timeout: ${SOCIETY_CACHE_REDIS_CONNECT_TIMEOUT:500ms}
  timeout: ${SOCIETY_CACHE_REDIS_COMMAND_TIMEOUT:500ms}
Cache manager

services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java

Enables caching; configures per-cache TTL with downward random jitter and typed JSON; adds the Society key prefix and statistics; coordinates cold misses across Society Pods with a short Redis lock; and bypasses Redis briefly after cache errors.

@Cacheable(cacheNames = SocietyCacheConfiguration.FLAT_BY_ID,
           key = "#p0.toString()", sync = true)
public FlatResponse getCached(UUID id) {
    return cacheMetrics.recordDatabaseLoad(
            "flat-by-id", () -> loadFlat(id));
}
Cacheable reads

services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FloorService.java
services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java

Cache ordered floors, all flats, flats by floor, and admin flat-by-ID reads. DTOs are cached; JPA entities are not. The pageable read methods remain direct repository reads.

@Cacheable(cacheNames = FLOOR_LIST,
           key = "'all'", sync = true)
public List<FloorResponse> getFloors() {
    return floorRepository.findAllByOrderByFloorNumberAsc()
            .stream().map(...).toList();
}
Validation path

services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/controller/FlatController.java

The controller checks the authentication authority used by Member's existing service identity. That call goes to the uncached method; an Admin flat detail read uses the cached method.

boolean memberValidation = hasAuthority(
        "SERVICE_MEMBER_FLAT_READ");
return memberValidation
        ? flatService.get(flatId)       // database
        : flatService.getCached(flatId); // cache for Admin
After-commit invalidation

services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/event/FlatReferenceChangedEvent.java
services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/event/FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java

Create, update, and deactivate publish the affected flat and floor numbers. The listener evicts the flat, all-flats list, and old/new floor lists after commit.

@TransactionalEventListener(
    phase = TransactionPhase.AFTER_COMMIT)
public void invalidateAfterCommit(
        FlatReferenceChangedEvent event) {
    evict(FLAT_BY_ID, event.flatId().toString());
    evict(ALL_FLATS, "all");
    // evict affected floor lists
}
Fallback and hot-key metrics

services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java

Records cache hits/misses and per-key access gauges (bounded to 256 keys per Pod). During the five-second Redis bypass window, at most 16 fallback database loads run concurrently per Pod; excess calls receive 503. Metrics remain per Pod until a monitoring system aggregates them.

Negative cache and warmup

SocietyNegativeCache.java and SocietyCacheWarmup.java

Unknown Admin flat IDs get a 30-second marker. Startup warms only the small floor list; larger flat lists stay on demand. Member's authoritative flat validation bypasses both cache paths.

Redis memory metrics

SocietyRedisMemoryMetrics.java

Samples cache Redis used and maximum memory every 30 seconds and exposes gauges for memory use and ratio. Scrape these metrics and set alerts in the monitoring system; the application does not create alert rules.

Society module dependencies are in services/society-service/pom.xml: Spring Boot cache, Spring Data Redis, and Commons Pool support. No Flyway migration is needed because the cache contains derived copies of existing Society data.

Kubernetes files that connect the application to Redis

FileConnection it defines
kubernetes/config/application-config.yamlSets cache Redis host/port, bounded timeouts and pool sizes, TTL jitter, negative TTL, miss-lock settings, fallback concurrency, warmup, and memory scrape interval.
kubernetes/society/deployment.yamlLoads SOCIETY_CACHE_REDIS_PASSWORD from key redis-cache-password in Secret emerald-security-credentials.
kubernetes/redis/service.yamlProvides the stable private name redis:6379 for the existing Redis Pod.
kubernetes/networkpolicies.yamlAllows session/rate-limit Redis from Gateway and Member; dedicated cache Redis is reachable only from Society. Enforcement depends on the cluster CNI.
kubernetes/redis/deployment.yamlRetains the 128 MiB noeviction Redis for Gateway sessions, rate limits, and Member locks.
kubernetes/redis-cache/deployment.yaml and service.yamlAdd a separate 128 MiB Redis with allkeys-lru for disposable Society cache entries. It uses ephemeral storage and one replica; it is not highly available.

Separate Redis processes give cache eviction and memory pressure their own budget, protecting session, rate-limit, and Member lock keys from cache churn. This adds a Redis workload and credentials to operate; both Redis services are single replicas in this learning setup. Redis credentials remain in Secrets, not ConfigMaps.

Manual verification guide (not run here)

  1. Apply the Kustomize package after creating Secrets. Check that both redis and redis-cache Pods are ready; cache Redis expects Secret key redis-cache-password.
  2. Log in as an Admin and call GET /api/v1/floors. Startup normally warms this small list; repeat the request and inspect cache hit metrics.
  3. Call GET /api/v1/flats/by-floor/{floorNumber} twice, then inspect only society:cache: keys on the dedicated redis-cache service. Avoid broad key deletion, which can trigger a burst of database reads.
  4. Call GET /api/v1/flats/00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000099 twice within 30 seconds. Both calls should return the same 404; the second should use the short-lived negative marker. Member assignment validation still checks PostgreSQL directly.
  5. Read a flat by ID as Admin, update or deactivate it, then repeat the flat and floor-list reads. The next reads should reflect the committed database values after eviction.
  6. Wait beyond each base TTL and jitter window, then repeat a read. The expired item should be loaded from PostgreSQL again.
  7. For outage behavior, stop only redis-cache in a disposable environment. Society reads fall back to PostgreSQL, limited to 16 concurrent cache-fallback loads per Pod; overflow returns 503 CACHE_FALLBACK_OVERLOADED. The Gateway/Member redis should remain available.
  8. Inspect /actuator/metrics/society.cache.redis.memory.used, /actuator/metrics/society.cache.redis.memory.max, /actuator/metrics/society.cache.redis.memory.used.ratio, and /actuator/metrics/society.cache.key.accesses on each Society Pod. Metrics are per Pod; aggregate them for a cluster view.
kubectl get pods,services -n emerald-housing
kubectl logs deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl port-forward service/api-gateway 8080:8080 -n emerald-housing
# Send authenticated GET requests from Postman to the existing Gateway URL.
# Inspect society:cache:* keys only on the dedicated redis-cache Service.
# Never print Redis passwords or delete all keys during normal verification.

The existing Postman file postman/Emerald-Housing-Option-B.postman_collection.json combines Society and Member folders; its floor, flat, update, assignment, and outage descriptions now include manual Step 10 context. The only other existing JSON file under postman/ is the local environment, not a second collection.

System design terms to remember

Pattern or problemMeaningCurrent project behaviorCommon solution or lesson
Cache-asideCheck cache first; on a miss, load from the database and put the result in cache.Implemented for selected Society floor and flat reads.Keep the database authoritative; cache only reads that are safe to reuse.
Cache hit / missA hit returns a cached value. A miss means the value is absent or expired.A miss loads PostgreSQL and then stores the DTO in Redis.Track hit rate and database load to see whether caching helps.
Stale cache / invalidationThe database has new data while the cache still has the old copy.Flat changes evict affected keys after commit. If eviction fails, stale data may remain until TTL; the TTL bounds but does not remove the brief consistency gap.Use reliable invalidation, short TTLs, or read the database for operations requiring the latest value.
Cache stampedeMany requests miss the same key and load the database together.A Redis lock per cache key coordinates Society Pods for up to 2 seconds; timed-out waiters may load without caching.Monitor lock timeouts and database load; adjust the lock wait/lease only from measurements.
Cache avalancheMany keys expire together and cause a burst of database reads.Cache TTLs are shortened by a random 0–30 second jitter.Keep jitter proportional to base TTL; monitor expiry and database-load patterns.
Cache penetrationRepeated requests for nonexistent data keep reaching the database.Unknown Admin flat IDs are stored as negative markers for 30 seconds. Other uncached paths still query PostgreSQL.Use short negative TTLs only for safe lookups; keep Member assignment validation authoritative.
Write-through / write-behindWrite-through updates cache with the database; write-behind caches first and persists later.Neither pattern is used. Updates go to PostgreSQL, then affected Redis entries are evicted after commit.Choose a write strategy only when its consistency and failure behavior fit the data.
Redis outage or memory fullThe cache is unavailable or refuses new cache entries.Society bypasses cache Redis for five seconds per Pod and limits fallback database reads to 16 concurrent calls per Pod. Cache Redis uses allkeys-lru.Monitor cache and PostgreSQL capacity; fallback overload returns 503 CACHE_FALLBACK_OVERLOADED.
Hot keyOne popular key receives a large share of cache traffic.Bounded per-Pod key-hash access gauges track up to 256 cache keys.Compare metrics across Pods; scale Redis or change key usage if one key dominates.
TTLMaximum lifetime of a cached copy before it expires.Base TTLs: floors 10 minutes, flat lists 5 minutes, flat by ID 2 minutes; 0–30 seconds of downward jitter. Negative flat markers last 30 seconds.Balance freshness against database load; expiration alone is not immediate invalidation.
Distributed cacheMultiple app instances use the same cache.Society Pods share dedicated redis-cache. Gateway sessions, rate limits, and Member locks stay on redis.Monitor each service's memory and availability separately.
Cold cache / warmupAfter restart, cache entries are empty and first reads go to PostgreSQL.Each Society Pod warms the small floor list at startup; flat lists remain on-demand.Warm only small, frequently used data; avoid loading a large inventory without evidence.
Memory monitoringObserve Redis memory use against its configured limit.Society samples cache Redis used_memory and maxmemory every 30 seconds and exposes gauges.Scrape metrics and alert before the Redis limit is reached.
SerializationConvert cached values to and from Redis data.Typed JSON DTOs are cached; JPA entities are not.Consider compatibility when changing DTO shapes while old cache entries may still exist.

Phase 10 completion status

  • Implemented in source/configuration: cache-aside reads, after-commit invalidation, per-cache TTL with jitter, cross-Pod cache-miss locks, short negative caching for unknown Admin flat IDs, five-second Redis bypass, per-Pod bounded database fallback, floor-list startup warmup, hot-key and Redis-memory metrics, dedicated cache Redis in Kubernetes, and updated Postman request descriptions.
  • PostgreSQL remains authoritative; Member flat validation bypasses cached Admin reads. Hot-key metrics provide visibility, not automatic sharding or key replication. Memory metrics need an external scraper and alerting system.
  • The Society service compiled successfully with tests skipped. Runtime behavior remains unverified: Redis serialization, hit/miss behavior, load-lock expiry/failure behavior, TTL expiry, update invalidation, Redis outage recovery, Actuator memory metrics, NetworkPolicy enforcement, and latency change.
  • One combined Postman collection and one local environment file exist; the collection was updated. No migration is needed because cache data is derived and disposable.

Scenario questionsRedis-cache: 45 scenario questions and answers

Redis-cache: 45 scenario questions and answers

Current repository behavior, step-by-step flows, limitations and source locations. Open a question to read its answer. Suggested improvements are distinguished from implemented guarantees.

Concurrent loads and outagesQuestions 1–8Stale data and access controlQuestions 9–22Warmup, expiry and compatibilityQuestions 23–37Diagnosis and scalingQuestions 38–45
1. Two Society Pods receive requests for the same uncached flat simultaneously; how does our code coordinate the database load?

Answer: Both check Redis. InstrumentedCache attempts a per-cache, per-key load lock with a random owner token and a lease. The winner rechecks the cache before loading PostgreSQL; the other request polls and retries within its wait deadline.

Execution flow:

Both Pods miss → A acquires lock → A rechecks cache
          ↓
A loads database and caches result
          ↓
B sees cached result and returns it

This reduces duplicate loads rather than guaranteeing exactly one database query. Lease expiry, eviction and wait-timeout fallback can still allow concurrent loads.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
2. A request acquires the cache-load lock and its Pod crashes before caching the result; what happens to waiting requests?

Answer: The crashed process cannot release the lock, but the Redis lease eventually expires. Waiters can obtain it after expiry or reach their own wait deadline first.

Execution flow:

Loader crashes → Lock survives until lease expiry
          ↓
Waiter obtains lock OR reaches wait timeout
          ↓
Applicable path loads PostgreSQL

Defaults are a 30-second lease and a 2-second wait unless overridden. A timeout path loads without caching; it does not wait for the full lease.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
3. A database query takes longer than the cache-load lock lease; can another Pod start loading the same data?

Answer: Yes. The implementation does not renew the lease during loading. Another Pod can acquire the expired key while the first loader is still running.

Execution flow:

A starts slow load → Lease expires → B acquires lock
          ↓
A and B load concurrently and may both write the cache

Owner-token checking protects lock release, but does not fence an old loader’s cache write. Lease renewal or version-aware writes would be additional improvements, not current guarantees.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:237)
4. A waiting request reaches the configured lock-wait timeout; what does our implementation do next?

Answer: It records a load-lock timeout and calls load(valueLoader, key, false), loading the database without populating Redis from that path.

Execution flow:

Wait deadline → Timeout metric → Uncached database load → Response

A lock timeout alone does not activate Redis bypass. The fallback semaphore is used while bypass is active, so it does not automatically bound every healthy-Redis wait-timeout load.

Code or inspection example:

metrics.recordLoadLockTimeout(getName());
return load(valueLoader, key, false);

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:41)
5. Redis becomes unavailable after the database returns a result but before the result is cached; what does the client receive?

Answer: The instrumented put catches a runtime cache-write failure, records it, and does not rethrow. A successfully loaded value can still be returned.

Execution flow:

Database returns data → Cache write fails
          ↓
Record failure and bypass window → Return loaded data

The cache may remain empty, causing another database load later. This assumes no separate database, authorization or response-writing failure.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:256)
  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:117)
6. Redis goes down during peak traffic; how does bounded fallback prevent PostgreSQL from being overwhelmed?

Answer: A Redis failure starts a five-second local bypass window. During bypass, instrumented database loads must acquire a semaphore permit; excess loads are rejected.

Execution flow:

Redis fails → Bypass → Try fallback permit
          ├→ Available → Query database → Release permit
          └→ Full → Reject request

The default limit is 16 concurrent fallback loads per Pod. It is not fleet-wide and does not cover every Society database operation. More Pods increase the aggregate limit.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:117)
  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:41)
7. All database fallback slots are occupied when another cache-dependent request arrives; what response does it receive?

Answer: During active bypass, a failed tryAcquire throws CacheFallbackOverloadedException. The exception handler maps it to HTTP 503 with code CACHE_FALLBACK_OVERLOADED.

Execution flow:

Bypass + busy permits → CacheFallbackOverloadedException
          ↓
HTTP 503 CACHE_FALLBACK_OVERLOADED

The handler also searches wrapped exception causes. Clients should use bounded retry backoff rather than immediately multiplying fallback traffic.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:41)
  • GlobalExceptionHandler.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/exception/GlobalExceptionHandler.java:48)
8. Redis recovers after an outage; how does Society Service decide when to resume cache operations?

Answer: Bypass uses a per-Pod time deadline. When the five-second interval passes, subsequent eligible requests try Redis again. A new failure resets the deadline.

Execution flow:

Failure → Five-second bypass → Deadline passes
          ↓
Next request tries Redis → Success OR new bypass

There is no background recovery probe in this flag. Different Society Pods have independent deadlines and can resume at different times.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:117)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
9. A flat is updated successfully, but Redis eviction fails; how long could users continue receiving stale data?

Answer: The committed database update is not undone by handled eviction failure. Existing entries can survive until their remaining TTL expires, another eviction succeeds, or memory pressure removes them.

Execution flow:

Database commits → After-commit eviction fails
          ↓
Old entry survives → Expiry or eviction → Fresh reload

A failure triggers local bypass, but other Pods can still read stale entries. There is no durable invalidation retry here. Base defaults are two minutes for flat-by-ID and five minutes for flat lists, reduced by jitter and overridable by configuration.

Files and locations:

  • FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/event/FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java:29)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:100)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:316)
  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:117)
10. A request reads old database data just before another request commits an update and invalidates the cache; can the first request repopulate stale data?

Answer: Yes. After-commit invalidation removes existing entries but does not stop an earlier reader from writing an old result after that eviction.

Execution flow:

Reader loads old data → Writer commits and evicts
          ↓
Reader stores old result → Stale cache until removal/expiry

The load lock coordinates readers; the listener does not acquire that lock. Version checks, coordinated writes or another explicit invalidation design would be improvements rather than existing behavior.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
  • FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/event/FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java:29)
11. A flat-update transaction rolls back after publishing its event; does our after-commit listener evict anything?

Answer: No, not for the normal rolled-back transaction. The listener runs in AFTER_COMMIT, which is not reached for that transaction.

Execution flow:

Publish event inside transaction → Rollback
          ↓
No AFTER_COMMIT invalidation

Publishing the event itself is not a Redis operation. Waiting for commit avoids removing valid entries for a database change that did not persist.

Code or inspection example:

@TransactionalEventListener(phase = TransactionPhase.AFTER_COMMIT)

Files and locations:

  • FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/event/FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java:29)
12. A flat moves from floor 2 to floor 3; which individual-flat and list entries must be invalidated?

Answer: The listener removes the negative marker, flat-by-ID entry, all-flats list, and flats-by-floor entries for both the old and new floor.

Execution flow:

Move 2 → 3 → Commit
          ↓
Evict flat ID + negative marker + all flats + floors 2 and 3 lists

The separate floor-reference list is not invalidated merely because a flat moved. Later eligible reads rebuild each affected flat cache.

Files and locations:

  • FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/event/FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java:29)
13. A nonexistent flat ID is negatively cached, then that flat is created; how is the negative entry removed?

Answer: If an application create event concerns that same UUID, the listener calls negativeCache.evictFlat(event.flatId()) after commit.

Execution flow:

Negative ID X → Create ID X → Commit
          ↓
Evict negative X → Next read can load the new flat

Normal creation generates a UUID, so the IDs must actually match for this scenario. Direct SQL insertion bypasses the event; the marker can then remain until its negative TTL expires.

Files and locations:

  • FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/event/FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java:29)
  • SocietyNegativeCache.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyNegativeCache.java:29)
14. Thousands of requests arrive for the same nonexistent flat ID; which Redis operations and database queries occur?

Answer: For authorized cached reads, the positive cache misses and a loader checks the negative marker. A database miss creates the marker and throws FlatNotFoundException. Later negative hits avoid the flat-data query.

Execution flow:

Positive miss → Coordinate load → Negative lookup
          ├→ Hit → Not found without flat-data query
          └→ Miss → Database miss → Marker → Not found

The exception is not a positive cached FlatResponse. Initial concurrent or timeout paths can still duplicate queries, and authorization can reject a request before this code runs.

Files and locations:

  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:65)
  • SocietyNegativeCache.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyNegativeCache.java:29)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
15. An authorized user repeatedly requests different nonexistent flat IDs; how effective is negative caching in that case?

Answer: Negative caching is keyed by UUID. A marker for ID A cannot prevent the first database lookup for a different ID B.

Execution flow:

Missing A → Query → Marker A
Missing B → Query → Marker B
Repeat A → Marker hit while present

Distinct-ID traffic can still create many database reads and short-lived keys. Authorization, rate limiting and capacity limits remain necessary.

Files and locations:

  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:65)
  • SocietyNegativeCache.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyNegativeCache.java:29)
16. A staff user populates the all-flats cache, then a resident requests the same endpoint; can the resident receive the staff user’s cached list?

Answer: The annotation’s condition checks societyAccess.staff(). For a resident who is not staff, cache lookup and population are bypassed; the method queries and filters visible flats.

Execution flow:

Staff → Shared list cache eligible
Resident → Condition false → Method runs → Visibility filtering

The method also has authorization. The staff list cache is not a per-resident cache and must not be reused without that condition.

Code or inspection example:

@Cacheable(cacheNames = SocietyCacheConfiguration.ALL_FLATS,
    key = "'all'", condition = "@societyAccess.staff()", sync = true)

Files and locations:

  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:79)
  • SocietyAccess.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/security/SocietyAccess.java:7)
17. A flat-by-ID entry is already cached when an unauthorized user requests it; where is access denied?

Answer: Society request security and the method’s @PreAuthorize("@societyAccess.flat(#p0)") enforce access. Standard configured Spring method-security/cache advice ordering checks authorization before returning a cached method result.

Execution flow:

Request → Identity validation → Method authorization
          ├→ Denied → 403
          └→ Allowed → Cache lookup

The cache key is not permission to read it. If advice ordering changes, verify with an unauthorized request against an already populated flat key.

Files and locations:

  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:65)
  • SocietyAccess.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/security/SocietyAccess.java:7)
18. A resident’s flat permissions change while the flat data remains cached; how does the application enforce the updated permissions?

Answer: The session-validation flow obtains current account details through Gateway and Member. SocietyAccess checks the request identity before the protected cached read.

Execution flow:

Permission change → Next request validates current identity
          ↓
SocietyAccess → Deny OR allow cache lookup

The flat cache stores reference data, not access rights. An in-flight request still uses the identity obtained during its validation, so a concurrent permission change can race with that request.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyAccess.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/security/SocietyAccess.java:7)
  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:65)

19. A user requests paginated flats after the unpaginated list has been cached; does the paginated request reuse that cache?

Answer: No. FlatService page/search/cursor methods are not annotated with @Cacheable and query the directory/repository. They do not slice the cached all-flats list.

Execution flow:

Staff list → Eligible cache
Page/search/cursor request → Database query → Page response

A warm list key therefore does not imply that paginated traffic avoids PostgreSQL.

Files and locations:

  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:110)
  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:79)
20. Member Service validates a flat immediately after an update while Redis still holds the old value; which read path is used?

Answer: The controller’s service-authenticated Member-validation branch selects FlatService.get(id), not getCached(id). Its non-read-only transaction deliberately routes validation to the primary database.

Execution flow:

Member validation → FlatService.get(id)
          ↓
Primary PostgreSQL → Validation response

This avoids cached or replica-lagged validation data. Member still needs database constraints and concurrency controls for its own write.

Files and locations:

  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:59)
21. Someone changes a flat directly in PostgreSQL; which application invalidation steps are bypassed?

Answer: Direct SQL bypasses FlatService event publication and its after-commit listener. PostgreSQL does not automatically notify this Redis cache.

Execution flow:

Manual SQL update → No application event
          ↓
Old cache remains → TTL or explicit eviction → Reload

Prefer the application write path. For an intentional direct update, arrange targeted post-commit eviction; sync=true does not synchronize Redis with SQL changes.

Code or inspection example:

UPDATE ...;

  • FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/event/FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java:29)

22. Someone changes the floor table directly after startup warmup; when will the cached floor list reflect the change?

Answer: The FLOOR_LIST cache reuses key all until it expires or is evicted. Warmup runs at startup, and the flat invalidation listener does not clear this separate floor-reference cache.

Execution flow:

Direct floor update → Old list remains
          ↓
TTL or explicit eviction → Next list read reloads floors

The default base floor TTL is ten minutes, reduced by jitter. A future floor-update operation should evict after commit; the following illustrates that change, not an existing floor-update listener.

Code or inspection example:

cacheManager
    .getCache(SocietyCacheConfiguration.FLOOR_LIST)
    .evict("all");

  • FloorService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FloorService.java:30)

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:100)
23. Several Society Pods start together and attempt floor-cache warmup; how does the warmup code handle concurrent execution?

Answer: Each runner directly gets the CacheManager cache, checks all, queries floors if absent, and puts the result. That path does not use the custom distributed value-loader lock.

Execution flow:

A and B start → Both see missing key
          ↓
Both may query floors → Both may put the list

One Pod may see the other’s populated value and report already-warm, but warmup is not distributed single-flight. Duplication is limited by the small reference list, not by an explicit warmup lock.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheWarmup.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheWarmup.java:35)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
24. Redis is unavailable during startup warmup; does Society Service still start, and how is the failure recorded?

Answer: The runner catches DataAccessException and IllegalStateException, records a failed warmup, records a Redis failure/bypass window, and logs that floors will load on demand.

Execution flow:

Warmup failure → Handled exception → Metrics and warning
          ↓
Runner returns → On-demand loads remain possible

This handled failure alone does not abort startup. Other failures can. The catch also classifies a caught database DataAccessException as a Redis failure, so inspect the underlying cause.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheWarmup.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheWarmup.java:35)
  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:117)
25. Many cache entries are populated together; how does TTL jitter change their later expiration pattern?

Answer: cacheConfig subtracts a random reduction from each configured base TTL, spreading expiry times for entries written together.

Execution flow:

Bulk writes → Different randomized TTLs
          ↓
Staggered expiry → Less synchronized reload pressure

A two-minute base with thirty-second jitter gives roughly 90–120 seconds. This spreads entries’ expiry; it does not prevent an individual hot key from expiring.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:316)
26. A frequently accessed flat expires during heavy traffic; how do expiry, load locking, and database fallback interact?

Answer: Readers miss; one normally acquires the shared load lock and others poll. A waiter reaching its deadline loads without caching. Redis failures separately activate bypass and its fallback limit.

Execution flow:

Hot key expires → Concurrent misses
          ├→ Lock winner → Database → Cache
          ├→ Waiter sees value → Return
          └→ Wait timeout → Uncached database load

Healthy-Redis wait timeouts do not alone activate the fallback semaphore. Slow loads can therefore still create duplicate database work.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:41)
27. redis-cache reaches its memory limit while load-lock and negative-cache keys exist; can allkeys-lru evict those keys too?

Answer: Yes. allkeys-lru can evict any key in the instance, including positive values, negative markers and cache-load locks. Having an expiry does not protect a key.

Execution flow:

Memory pressure → allkeys-lru selection
          ↓
Data, negative marker or load lock can be removed

Evicted coordination keys weaken duplicate-load protection. These are cache-load locks, separate from Member membership locks in the operational noeviction Redis.

Files and locations:

  • deployment.yaml (kubernetes/redis-cache/deployment.yaml:33)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
  • SocietyNegativeCache.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyNegativeCache.java:29)
28. Redis evicts a distributed cache-load lock before its lease expires; what could happen to concurrent loaders?

Answer: Another request can acquire a replacement lock while the original loader continues. Both may query PostgreSQL and write the same cache entry.

Execution flow:

A holds lock → Redis evicts it → B acquires new lock
          ↓
Concurrent database loads and cache writes

Owner-token release avoids deleting B’s lock, but does not fence A’s write. This mechanism is best-effort load coordination, not a database consistency lock.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:237)
  • deployment.yaml (kubernetes/redis-cache/deployment.yaml:33)
29. Redis evicts a popular cached flat because of memory pressure; does the application treat this differently from normal TTL expiry?

Answer: Both appear as a missing cache value. The application takes its normal coordinated miss/load path and may repopulate the cache.

Execution flow:

Expiry OR eviction → Missing value → Cache-miss path

Application misses alone cannot identify the cause. Compare Redis INFO stats evicted_keys and expired_keys with memory and cache metrics.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
  • SocietyRedisMemoryMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyRedisMemoryMetrics.java:45)
30. A cache entry contains data serialized by the previous application version; what happens if the new version cannot deserialize it?

Answer: A runtime deserialization failure is caught by InstrumentedCache.get, recorded as a Redis failure and returned as a miss. The resulting bypass directs loading toward the database.

Execution flow:

Old value → Deserialization error → Failure metric and bypass
          ↓
Attempt database fallback

The catch does not delete the incompatible key. It can fail again after bypass until expiry/eviction. Compatible DTO changes or a planned new key prefix can avoid this; those require release planning.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:100)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:117)
31. Old and new Society Pods run together during RollingUpdate and share the same cache; what compatibility problems could arise?

Answer: Both versions can read/write the same keys. Incompatible JSON structures or changed semantics can cause deserialization failures or values interpreted differently across versions.

Execution flow:

Old and new Pods share keys → Each writes representation
          ↓
Other version reads → Compatible result OR failure

The current positive prefix is society:cache:v2:. Plan backward-compatible DTOs or separate versioned namespaces with compatible invalidation. RollingUpdate does not migrate Redis data.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:100)
  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:316)
32. Redis contains a malformed cached value; how does the cache error-handling path respond?

Answer: The wrapper catches the runtime read failure, records it, returns a miss, and activates temporary bypass. A database fallback can return valid data if capacity permits.

Execution flow:

Malformed value → Read failure → Bypass
          ↓
Database fallback → Response OR capacity rejection

The malformed value is not automatically deleted by this read catch. Inspect causes and arrange targeted eviction or wait for expiry rather than assuming a successful response repaired the entry.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)

33. A cached floor or flat list grows substantially; how could serialization time, network traffic, and Redis memory usage affect response latency?

Answer: A list hit still transfers and deserializes the entire value. Larger lists increase CPU, allocations, network transfer and memory pressure even when the database load is avoided.

Execution flow:

Larger list → Larger Redis payload
          ↓
More serialization/network work → Higher latency and eviction pressure

Measure lookup timers, payload sizes and Redis memory. Existing paginated queries go to the database; they are not an automatically paginated cache.

Files and locations:

  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:79)
  • SocietyCacheMetrics.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheMetrics.java:60)

34. A developer calls getCached() from another method in the same FlatService object; will Spring’s cache interception run?

Answer: An ordinary self-invocation bypasses Spring’s proxy, so cache advice does not intercept that direct call.

Execution flow:

Controller → Proxy → getCached → Cache advice
          versus
FlatService → this.getCached → No cache proxy interception

This also matters for proxy-based transaction and method-security advice on that invocation. Use an externally invoked proxied collaborator if those behaviors are needed.

Code or inspection example:

this.getCached(id); // self-invocation bypasses the proxy

Files and locations:

  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:65)
35. A cached method throws FlatNotFoundException; is the exception cached by @Cacheable, or does our negative-cache code handle it separately?

Answer: The exception is not stored as a positive FlatResponse. getCached catches FlatNotFoundException, writes a separate negative marker, then rethrows.

Execution flow:

Database miss → rememberMissingFlat(id)
          ↓
Throw not-found → No positive cached value

Later positive misses can find the marker and skip the flat query. The negative marker has its own configurable TTL, default thirty seconds.

Files and locations:

  • FlatService.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/service/FlatService.java:65)
  • SocietyNegativeCache.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyNegativeCache.java:29)
36. A flat is deleted while another request is loading it into the cache; what stale-data race is possible?

Answer: A reader can fetch the flat before deletion and put it after the delete commits and the listener invalidates. The cache can temporarily describe a deleted flat.

Execution flow:

Reader loads flat → Delete commits and evicts
          ↓
Reader stores pre-delete data → Stale cache

The listener does not fence in-flight readers. Primary-read Member validation avoids this particular stale-cache read, but normal cached reads remain eventually consistent.

Files and locations:

  • SocietyCacheConfiguration.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/config/SocietyCacheConfiguration.java:193)
  • FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java (services/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/event/FlatCacheInvalidationListener.java:29)

37. Redis restarts inside the same Pod versus being replaced by a new Pod; what data survives with our current persistence settings?

Answer: Do not assume data survives either event. Process memory is lost on container restart. emptyDir survives a container restart, but the command disables AOF and does not deliberately configure periodic snapshot persistence. Any recovery would require an existing usable snapshot.

Execution flow:

Container restart → Memory lost; only persisted data could restore
          ↓
Pod replacement → Memory lost and old emptyDir removed

These manifests do not provide durable recovery. Persistent volumes plus an explicit persistence policy would be separate work. Cold-cache rebuilding must be expected.

  • deployment.yaml (kubernetes/redis-cache/deployment.yaml:33)
38. Redis reports PONG, but Society requests still fall back to PostgreSQL; what connection, authentication, serialization, and bypass-state checks would you perform?

Answer: A PING inside Redis does not test Society’s address, password, network path or serializers. Check Society settings, Secret mappings, Service endpoints and the actual failure cause.

Execution flow:

Local PONG → Verify Society destination and credentials
          ↓
Check network/endpoints → Check serialization and bypass state

Also confirm the endpoint is cache-eligible and inspect each Pod’s five-second bypass state through logs/metrics. Healthy Redis can coexist with client authentication or data-format failures.

39. Cache-hit metrics rise, but PostgreSQL query counts remain high; which security checks and uncached endpoints could explain the queries?

Answer: A hit skips only the cached method’s data load. Identity/account validation, permission checks, primary assignment validation and uncached page/search/cursor requests can still query databases.

Execution flow:

Flat-data cache hit → Skip that method load
          ↓
Other security and uncached paths can still query PostgreSQL

Compare society.reference.database.loads by lookup with endpoint traffic and SQL traces. A cache hit does not promise zero queries for the whole HTTP request.

40. Repeated requests never increase cache-hit metrics; how would you check cache conditions, proxy interception, TTLs, eviction, and the actual endpoint being called?

Endpoint and role → Proxy and condition
          ↓
Keys/TTL/evictions → Errors and bypass → Per-Pod metrics

Positive keys start society:cache:v2:. A local bypass intentionally skips normal lookups; a warmup may populate floors without using the instrumented request-read metrics.

41. An update succeeds, but the next read returns stale data; how would you distinguish failed invalidation, a concurrent refill race, direct database changes, and an outdated application Pod?

Answer: Confirm the committed row/version, then correlate the application write event, eviction errors, key TTL/value and responding Pod image. Each cause has a different ordering of database writes and cache operations.

Execution flow:

Confirm database version → Trace event/eviction
          ↓
Inspect cached version and TTL → Check Pod images and rollout

Failed eviction preserves an old key; a refill race writes old data after eviction; SQL emits no event; mixed versions can behave differently. Reproduce with a controlled flat and request sequence.

High memory → Memory/fragmentation → Evictions and expirations
          ↓
Sample sizes/TTLs → Correlate cache traffic

Continuous writes can keep memory near the cap despite short entry lifetimes. Application memory gauges are periodic samples, not a real-time inventory.

Coordinate server/client credential rollout
          ↓
Verify authentication and application caching

Mismatched credentials produce authentication errors, bypass and fallback. Restarting ephemeral Redis also risks cache loss, so coordinate availability and cold-cache impact.

45. Society traffic doubles after increasing replicas with HPA; how do shared cache entries and distributed load locks affect the resulting database load?

Answer: All correctly configured Society Pods reuse shared keys and per-key locks. Warm hits can avoid repeated data loads across Pods; cold misses and timeout paths can still load PostgreSQL.

Execution flow:

HPA adds Pods → Shared warm keys reused
          ↓
Cold key → Shared lock and bounded wait
          ↓
Observe database connections and fallback across all Pods

Each Pod has a separate pool, bypass timer and semaphore. Scaling increases aggregate fallback concurrency and potential database connections; HPA is not a fleet-wide database safety limit.

Scenario questionsRedis cache-aside and after-commit invalidation

Redis cache-aside and after-commit invalidation

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How does cache-aside work, and why invalidate after commit?

Answer: Read Redis first; on a miss load PostgreSQL and populate Redis. A write commits the source-of-truth change before invalidating or refreshing cached data. Invalidating before commit can let a reader reload old database state and cache it again.

Spring solution: publish a domain event in the transaction and handle it with @TransactionalEventListener(phase = TransactionPhase.AFTER_COMMIT). For guaranteed delivery across crashes, use a transactional outbox rather than relying only on an in-process event.

Follow-up: What failure can still occur after using an after-commit cache event?

Answer: The process can crash after the database commit but before the in-process event invalidates Redis. Use a transactional outbox if that gap is unacceptable: commit the outbox record with the data, then retry delivery until cache invalidation succeeds.

Explore 45 Redis-cache scenario questions and answers →

Scenario questionsCache stampede protection across Pods

Cache stampede protection across Pods

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How do you prevent many Pods loading the same missing key at once?

Answer: Use a per-key distributed single-flight/lock so one Pod loads the value while others wait briefly, serve stale data if safe, or use a bounded fallback. A JVM-only lock protects only one Pod.

Solution sketch: SET cache-load:{key} {randomToken} NX PX 30000, then release only when the stored token still matches. Bound wait time and database fallback concurrency; a lease can expire while work continues, so protect writes with fencing/version checks where needed.

Follow-up: What should waiting Pods do while one Pod loads a cache key?

Answer: Wait only for a bounded interval, then serve stale data if allowed or return a controlled response. Limit concurrent database fallback loads per Pod and across the fleet; never make every waiter perform the same expensive query when the lock wait expires.

Scenario questionsNegative caching and TTL jitter

Negative caching and TTL jitter

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: What are negative caching and TTL jitter?

Answer: Negative caching stores a short-lived “not found” result to reduce repeated lookups for absent objects. TTL jitter randomizes expiry so many keys do not expire simultaneously.

Solution: cache absence briefly and invalidate it when the object is created. Compute ttl = baseTtl + random(0, jitter); keep the base and jitter small enough for the freshness requirement.

Follow-up: Which failures should not be stored as a negative cache result?

Answer: Do not cache timeouts, authorization failures, or database errors as not-found results. Cache only a confirmed absence, for a short TTL, and invalidate it when the object is created so a temporary failure cannot hide valid data.

Scenario questionsCache behavior during Redis outages

Cache behavior during Redis outages

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: What should an application do when Redis is unavailable?

Answer: Decide per Redis use: cache may fall back to PostgreSQL under a bounded concurrency limit; sessions or rate limits may fail closed because bypassing them changes security behavior. Provide timeouts, a short circuit/bypass interval, metrics, and recovery behavior.

Project context: Society has bounded database fallback loads for cache errors. Gateway sessions and shared rate limiting depend on Redis, so outage behavior differs from disposable cache behavior.

Follow-up: How do you keep cache fallback from causing a database outage?

Answer: Use short Redis timeouts, a per-Pod or distributed circuit/bypass window, and a strict concurrency limit for database fallback. Track fallback load and rejected requests. Fail closed for security-sensitive Redis use such as sessions or shared limits when bypassing would weaken policy.

Scenario questionsDistributed locking with Redis

Distributed locking with Redis

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: What makes a Redis lock safe enough for distributed work?

Answer: Acquire atomically with a unique ownership token and lease, and release only if the token still belongs to the caller. A lease can expire while a slow worker continues; use fencing tokens or database constraints when stale workers must not overwrite newer work.

Solution sketch: SET lock:{resource} {token} NX PX {leaseMs}; release with an atomic compare-token-and-delete script. Redis locking coordinates workers but does not replace database integrity.

Follow-up: When would you prefer a database lock over a Redis lock?

Answer: Prefer a database transaction lock when protecting a database invariant or coordinating a short update to rows in that database. Redis can reduce duplicate work across Pods, but a Redis lease alone cannot guarantee that a stale worker will not later commit an outdated database write.

Scenario questionsRate Limiting

Rate Limiting

Project implementation: The Spring Cloud Gateway uses Redis-backed token-bucket rate limiting for Member and Society routes. Authenticated API buckets use the security principal's admin identity; login attempts share an unauthenticated bucket. Current defaults are Member 10 tokens/second with burst 20, Society 20/second with burst 40, and login 2/second with burst 5 (Kubernetes overrides login to 1/second with burst 3). Limits are configurable and should be tuned to measured traffic. Exhausted limits return 429; Redis limiter failures return 503 (fail closed).
1. What is rate limiting, and why use it?

Rate limiting caps how many requests a client or key can make during a period. It protects capacity, reduces abuse, and helps allocate service fairly. It is not a substitute for authentication, authorization, or backend capacity planning.

Project example: Gateway limits authenticated Member and Society API traffic before forwarding requests.

2. What is the difference between rate limiting and throttling?

Rate limiting defines the allowed request budget. Throttling is the action taken when demand exceeds capacity, such as delaying work, queuing it, or rejecting requests. Many APIs implement limiting by rejecting excess requests with HTTP 429.

3. Which rate-limiting algorithms are common?

Common choices are fixed window, sliding window, token bucket, and leaky bucket. Choose based on burst tolerance, accuracy, state cost, and implementation complexity. This project uses Spring Cloud Gateway's Redis rate limiter with token-bucket settings.

4. How does the token bucket algorithm work?

Tokens refill at a configured rate up to a maximum capacity. Each request consumes tokens; if there are not enough, it is rejected. This allows short bursts up to the bucket capacity while controlling the long-term average.

Project settings: replenishRate controls refill per second, burstCapacity is the bucket size, and requestedTokens is normally 1 per request.

5. How does the leaky bucket algorithm work?

Requests enter a queue and leave at a steady rate. It smooths bursts, but a full queue means the system must reject or delay new work. Use it when steady downstream processing matters more than allowing brief bursts.

6. How does a fixed-window counter work, and what is its boundary problem?

It counts requests in fixed time blocks and resets at each boundary. A client can use nearly its whole allowance just before and after a reset, creating a burst close to twice the nominal limit. A sliding window or token bucket can soften that edge.

7. How does a sliding-window algorithm improve on a fixed window?

A sliding window measures requests over the most recent duration rather than resetting all at once. An exact log is accurate but stores more state; a weighted counter uses less memory with an approximation. Choose the accuracy and storage cost the endpoint needs.

8. How would you choose a rate limit for an API?

Measure sustainable service capacity, normal and peak client behavior, endpoint cost, and acceptable latency. Set a refill rate for sustained traffic and burst capacity for short spikes. Start conservatively, monitor 429 rates and backend saturation, and tune via configuration.

Project example: Member and Society have separate configurable values, so their limits can reflect different service capacity.

9. Should limits apply per user, API key, IP, endpoint, or service?

Use the identity that best represents fairness and abuse risk. Authenticated account or API-key limits are usually more reliable than IP alone; endpoint and global limits can protect especially expensive operations and total service capacity. Multiple layers can be combined.

Project behavior: protected route keys come from the authenticated admin principal; login uses a shared unauthenticated key.

10. How do you identify a client safely?

Prefer a verified principal or API key established by authentication. Do not trust client-supplied identity headers. If using IP, trust forwarding headers only when a known proxy removes untrusted incoming values and writes the real client address.

Project solution: the Gateway key resolver derives admin:{adminId} from the authenticated principal rather than an identity header.

11. How do you enforce limits across multiple application instances?

Use shared state or a centralized limiter so each Gateway instance sees the same bucket. A local in-memory counter is per process and can multiply the effective allowance as instances scale. The project uses Redis-backed Gateway rate limiting for shared buckets.

12. Why use Redis, and how do you make updates atomic?

Redis provides fast shared state for multiple Gateway instances. Concurrent requests must update/check tokens atomically; otherwise simultaneous requests can all observe the same remaining budget. Use a limiter's atomic Redis operation or Lua script instead of separate read-then-write commands. Spring Cloud Gateway's RedisRateLimiter supplies this coordination.

13. What happens if Redis is unavailable?

The policy must be deliberate: fail open to preserve availability, or fail closed to protect a sensitive system. This project fails closed for rate-limited login when Redis errors, returning HTTP 503 with a limiter-unavailable response. That prevents unmetered login attempts but makes login depend on Redis availability.

Production fix: monitor Redis, alert on limiter failures, and document whether each route should fail open or closed based on its risk and capacity.

14. How would you return a rate-limit response?

Return HTTP 429 Too Many Requests with a stable error body. Include Retry-After when the retry time is known so clients can back off instead of immediately repeating requests. The project's login response sets Retry-After: 1.

return ResponseEntity.status(HttpStatus.TOO_MANY_REQUESTS)
        .header("Retry-After", "1")
        .body(error("LOGIN_RATE_LIMITED", "Wait before trying again"));
15. What do HTTP 429 and Retry-After communicate?

429 tells the caller that its request budget is exhausted, not that the server has an internal error. Retry-After gives a delay or date after which retrying may be appropriate. Clients should use bounded exponential backoff and jitter and avoid retrying non-idempotent operations blindly.

16. How do you support different limits for users, plans, or endpoints?

Resolve a stable key and select policy by route, account tier, or operation cost. Keep policies configurable and prevent untrusted clients from choosing a more generous tier. The project already assigns distinct Member, Society, and login bucket settings through Gateway configuration.

redis-rate-limiter.replenishRate: ${GATEWAY_MEMBER_RATE_REPLENISH:10}
redis-rate-limiter.burstCapacity: ${GATEWAY_MEMBER_RATE_BURST:20}
redis-rate-limiter.requestedTokens: 1
17. How do you allow bursts while controlling sustained traffic?

Use a token bucket: set refill rate to the sustained allowance and capacity to the maximum short burst. For example, a refill rate of 10 tokens/second and capacity 20 allows a burst of up to 20 requests when full, then refills at 10 per second.

18. How do you prevent race conditions under concurrent requests?

Do not implement a distributed limiter as separate non-atomic read and write operations. Use an atomic Redis script/primitive, transaction, or a framework limiter designed for concurrent updates. Test with simultaneous requests across multiple Gateway instances, not only sequential calls.

19. How do you monitor and alert on rate limiting?

Track allowed and rejected requests, limiter errors, and Redis latency by bounded route name. Alert on unusual 429 spikes and limiter failures, and compare them with backend saturation. Avoid user IDs, session IDs, or tokens as metric labels because they create high-cardinality and sensitive telemetry.

Project example: Gateway records rate-limited requests by fixed route name and login limiter errors.

20. How do you test rate limiting under concurrent load?

Send controlled requests with the same key to exhaust a bucket, verify allowed/rejected counts and 429 responses, wait for refill, then verify requests are admitted again. Repeat across multiple Gateway instances and check Redis failure behavior. Avoid production load tests without an approved test plan.

21. How do you distinguish abuse from a legitimate traffic spike?

Correlate rate-limit events with authenticated identities, route, request patterns, error rates, and service health. Rate limiting should protect capacity without automatically treating every burst as malicious. Adjust limits, add anomaly detection or challenges, and investigate repeated distributed patterns.

22. How would you explain and improve this project's rate-limiting design?

Interview answer: “We enforce shared token buckets at Spring Cloud Gateway using Redis. Authenticated API requests are keyed by the verified admin principal, while login attempts share an unauthenticated bucket. Exhausted buckets return 429, and Redis failures fail closed with 503. I would tune refill and burst values from traffic measurements, monitor rejection and Redis error rates, and explicitly review the shared login bucket and failure policy.”

The project configuration is an implementation example; its learning defaults should be validated under runtime load before production use.

Scenario questionsWhere rate limiting happens

Where rate limiting happens

Where are we doing rate limiting, and what are the limits?

Rate limiting happens in the Spring API Gateway using Redis-backed token buckets. Member and Society routes use RequestRateLimiter filters in Gateway's application.yml. GatewayRateLimitConfiguration derives admin:{adminId} from the authenticated principal. Despite the name, this identifies the authenticated account, including supported non-admin roles. AuthController.login() explicitly applies the separate login limiter.

TrafficApplication default refill / capacityBucket key
Member and account routes10 tokens/second / 20 tokensAuthenticated account ID, scoped to the route
Society, floor and flat routes20 tokens/second / 40 tokensAuthenticated account ID, scoped to the route
Login2 tokens/second / 5 tokensShared unauthenticated bucket on admin-login

Environment distinction: Kubernetes overrides login to 1 token/second with capacity 3. Check deployed environment values before quoting effective limits. A routed request costs one token. Capacity permits short bursts; it is not a second fixed per-second quota. Exhausted limits produce HTTP 429; login sets Retry-After: 1.

Current limitation and improvement: the login bucket is shared across clients, so one client's attempts can throttle others. A proposed layered policy could combine a global limit with account and trusted-client-IP limits. Only trust forwarded addresses from a controlled proxy configuration.

The inspected NGINX policies set body-size and timeout controls rather than request-rate limits. Continue to the detailed rate-limiting question bank.
