Chapter 07

Dependency failures and recovery

Separate timeouts, retries, circuit breakers, bulkheads, and fallback.

Bound a dependency call before adding retries

Member calls Society to validate a flat. An unavailable dependency should not consume all request threads, database connections, or caller patience. Each resilience control addresses a different part of that problem.

ControlPurposeTradeoff to explain
Connect timeoutBounds establishing the connection.A responsive connection can still return a slow response.
Response/read timeoutBounds waiting at the configured client layer.This is not necessarily the entire end-to-end deadline.
RetryRepeats selected transient failures with bounded attempts and delay.It increases downstream traffic and latency; unsafe writes need explicit replay guarantees.
Circuit breakerStops repeated calls while a dependency is failing, then probes recovery.State is local to an instance unless a shared design is added.
BulkheadCaps concurrent dependency work.Excess traffic must be rejected or handled within a bounded queue.
IdempotencyRecognizes supported request replays.It does not make arbitrary operations safe to retry.

Explain one failure path

If Society cannot be validated within the configured policy, Member should return the mapped dependency failure rather than inventing a valid flat. A circuit breaker reduces repeated calls during a continuing outage; a bulkhead protects the rest of the service from unbounded waiting. Cache fallback serves a different goal: it can bypass a disposable accelerator when the database remains available.

Read the timeout chain

The Gateway response timeout defaults to 28 seconds. The NGINX reference uses 32-second upstream read/send timeouts. Those are separate proxy wait limits, not a single deadline shared by every component. Budget authentication/session validation, network overhead, selected retries, and work performed while a transaction is open.

Measure physical dependency calls and rejected work. A successful retry can hide a degraded first attempt if you record only the final response.

Keep reading

Implementation references and scenarios

Implementation referenceHandle dependency failures and repeated requests safely

Handle dependency failures and repeated requests safely

Member Service validates a flat through Society Service before recording a membership. This phase bounds that remote wait, rejects unsafe writes when Society cannot confirm a flat, adds shared Gateway rate limits, and makes member creation safely repeatable when a client supplies an idempotency key.

Protected dependency call

Implementation status: the source and configuration are present. The documented outage, timing, rate-limit, migration, metrics, and Kubernetes scenarios still require runtime verification.

Member-to-Society lookup protection

SocietyClient protects the read-only flat lookup with a Resilience4j circuit breaker and semaphore bulkhead, plus bounded retries for transient failures. The request retains its internal service credential and correlation ID. The call order is: check the member locally, obtain the circuit-breaker and bulkhead permits, call Society with bounded retries, confirm the flat is active, then save the membership in a local transaction. The remote wait happens outside the database transaction.

SettingDefaultPurpose
Society connect / read timeout2s / 3sBounds connection establishment and each Society response wait.
Retry attempts3 totalFirst call plus at most two retries; exponential delay starts at 200ms, multiplier 2, jitter 0.2, capped at 1s.
Circuit breaker window / minimum calls10 / 5Uses recent physical HTTP attempts; opens at 50% failures or 60% slow calls.
Slow call / open wait / half-open calls2s / 15s / 2Defines a slow attempt, recovery wait, and limited recovery trials.
Bulkhead concurrent calls / wait12 / 0msLimits concurrent lookups per Member process and rejects overflow promptly.

Only transient transport failures and remote 502, 503, and 504 responses are retried. A 404, bad request, conflict, malformed response, or other permanent failure is not retried. A normal flat-not-found response does not count as a breaker failure. An open breaker or full bulkhead returns a controlled 503; a Society timeout maps to 504. A failed lookup never permits the membership write. Circuit-breaker state is per Member Pod, not shared through Redis. Member operations that only use member_db remain available independently of Society.

Gateway rate limits, health, and shutdown

Spring Cloud Gateway uses Redis rate limiting for Society and Member routes. Authenticated requests are keyed from the security principal, not client-supplied identity headers. Login requests share an unauthenticated bucket. The limiter fails closed with 503 if Redis is unavailable; an exhausted bucket returns 429 with Retry-After.

BucketDefaultKubernetes setting
Login2 new tokens/second, burst 51/second, burst 3
Member routes10 requests/second, burst 20Configurable through environment-backed settings
Society routes20 requests/second, burst 40Configurable through environment-backed settings

Gateway's response timeout defaults to 28 seconds; NGINX upstream read/send timeouts are 32 seconds. These are separate proxy wait limits, not a single end-to-end deadline. Member Service has bounded Tomcat threads, connections, and accept queue; graceful shutdown has a 20-second phase timeout. Member readiness checks its own database, while Society availability does not control Member liveness. Applications expose Actuator metrics; Gateway metrics require ADMIN authorization. Resilience4j meters and bounded-cardinality Society lookup and Gateway rate-limit metrics are configured.

Durable idempotency for member creation

POST /api/v1/members accepts an optional Idempotency-Key containing 1 to 128 letters, digits, periods, underscores, colons, or hyphens. The key is scoped to the authenticated admin and CREATE_MEMBER. Member Service fingerprints the normalized request with SHA-256 and stores the response in the same member_db transaction as the create.

  • Repeating the key with the same payload returns the stored logical response and Idempotency-Replayed: true.
  • Reusing the key with different input returns 409.
  • A database uniqueness constraint coordinates concurrent submissions to prevent duplicate member creation.
  • Records expire after 24 hours by default. Expired rows are removed opportunistically during a later idempotent create; there is no background cleanup job.

Member creation and flat assignment are not automatically retried. Idempotency supports a deliberate client resubmission with the same key and payload; it does not make automatic Gateway retries safe. Assignment always requires live flat validation. The stored replay response may contain contact fields, so retention, database access, and backup policy should account for that data.

Manual verification guide

  1. Create a membership while Society is available and verify it is stored in member_db.
  2. Make Society unavailable and try an assignment. Expect a controlled 503 or 504 and verify no membership row was added; do not automatically retry the POST.
  3. While Society is unavailable, retrieve a Member record through Gateway and confirm the local database operation remains independent.
  4. Repeat safe flat GETs during an outage; observe the per-Pod breaker open, then limited half-open trials after 15 seconds.
  5. Generate controlled concurrency above 12 lookups for one Member Pod to observe prompt bulkhead rejection; do not use production traffic.
  6. Send a short burst of API and login requests through Gateway; inspect 429 and Retry-After. Redis must be reachable for shared limiting.
  7. Create a member with an idempotency key, repeat the exact request, then reuse the key with changed input. Expect a replay followed by 409.
  8. Inspect protected Actuator metrics for Resilience4j, rate-limit, and lookup measurements. Breaker state can differ by Member Pod.

The combined Postman collection includes idempotency examples and outage/bulkhead descriptions. Replace its sample mobile number with an unused value before creating a member.

Code locations: Member lookup resilience is in services/member-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/member/client/SocietyClient.java and its resilience configuration. Idempotency persistence is introduced by V3__create_idempotency_records.sql. Runtime settings are in the Member application.yml, Kubernetes application-config.yaml, and Gateway application.yml.

Verification note: this phase's original notes report static source/configuration review only. They do not report Maven builds, tests, Docker builds, application starts, Postman runs, or Kubernetes deployment for these changes.

Scenario questionsRetry, timeout, circuit breaker, and bulkhead

Retry, timeout, circuit breaker, and bulkhead

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How do these resilience controls work together?

Answer: A timeout bounds one call; limited retries handle transient failures; a circuit breaker stops calls to a dependency that is failing; a bulkhead caps concurrent calls so one dependency cannot consume all resources. The total retry budget must fit inside the caller's deadline, and retries need idempotent operations.

Solution sketch: overallDeadline > attempts × perAttemptTimeout + backoffBudget. Use exponential backoff with jitter and a small attempt count; do not retry validation errors or unsafe writes without idempotency.

Follow-up: How do retries affect downstream load during an outage?

Answer: Retries multiply traffic precisely when a dependency is unhealthy. Bound attempts and total time, add exponential backoff with jitter, retry only transient failures, and combine retries with a circuit breaker and concurrency bulkhead.

Chapter 08

Identity, permissions, and security

Understand login, scoped authorization, CSRF, revocation, and the edge.

Identity, permission, and record scope are separate checks

Gateway authenticates API requests; the backends enforce operation permissions and record-level scope. The current source permits authenticated /api/v1/** traffic at Gateway rather than restricting every business route to ADMIN. Backend access helpers evaluate grants such as MEMBER_VIEW:SOCIETY, MEMBER_VIEW:SELF, and FLAT_VIEW:OWN_FLATS.

  1. Establish the session

    The caller obtains CSRF material and logs in. Member owns credential data; Gateway creates the shared Redis-backed session.

  2. Validate the subsequent request

    Gateway checks the session and applicable CSRF protection. The backend validates the supplied session through the trusted Gateway path rather than trusting caller-supplied identity headers.

  3. Resolve current access

    The request identity includes the society, member association, grants, and permitted flat IDs derived by the configured identity flow.

  4. Authorize the operation and object

    Service rules check both the permission and the requested member or flat. An authenticated resident does not receive unrestricted directory access.

What CSRF protects

Session cookies are automatically sent by browsers, so state-changing cookie-authenticated requests need the expected CSRF material. The session cookie and CSRF token serve different purposes. A valid CSRF token does not grant a business permission, and a valid session does not bypass a record-scope check.

Prevent IDOR at the record boundary

Changing a UUID must not allow a caller to retrieve another member’s data or another flat’s history. Use two accounts with different permitted objects, then repeat the same operation with the other account’s identifier. Verify status, response content, and any resulting audit or business state—not only the response code.

Revocation and sensitive state

The current access model resolves permissions and associations through the identity validation flow so subsequent validated requests can reflect access changes. Revocation cannot undo a completed operation. Disabled roles, ended relationships, removed account grants, and member status changes each need tests for the relevant workflow.

Use the current access model

Some older implementation-phase examples discuss ADMIN-only APIs. Their request bodies and infrastructure examples remain reference material; use the current permission and scope rules for expected access. Gateway metrics still have a separate ADMIN requirement.

Protect the service path

Internal service tokens protect credential and session-validation calls; they are not public user credentials. NetworkPolicy and the HTTPS edge help only when the relevant configuration is applied and enforced. Keep credentials out of page examples, URL parameters, and logs. Audit snapshots exclude credential fields but can still contain personal business data and require restricted access.

Project questions · explanation and verification

CSRF protection and other security checks: how do I verify them?

CSRF means Cross-Site Request Forgery: an attacker tries to make a browser send an unwanted request using the victim’s automatically attached session cookie. This project protects cookie-authenticated state-changing requests with Spring Security CSRF validation at Gateway.

How the client attaches the protection

  1. Call GET /api/v1/auth/csrf. Preserve its cookies and use the returned token and headerName.
  2. Send the token in the indicated header, normally X-XSRF-TOKEN, for login and later protected writes. The cookie jar sends the appropriate cookies, including XSRF-TOKEN and, after login, SESSION.
  3. Obtain fresh CSRF material after login as the collection does. Keep cookie jars and token variables separate for different users.
Cookie: SESSION=<current-session>; XSRF-TOKEN=<csrf-cookie>
X-XSRF-TOKEN: {{csrfToken}}
Content-Type: application/json

The values above are placeholders. Use the endpoint’s token representation; do not invent a token or assume its serialized value must equal the raw cookie string. Gateway performs the authoritative token validation. Backend CookieCsrfValidationFilter additionally requires a nonblank cookie and header for authenticated unsafe requests; it does not itself compare their values.

Controlled verification matrix

Use a dedicated test account and fixture. Change one condition at a time. Confirm a rejected write did not create/update a record or produce an extra successful business audit.

CheckRequest or setupExpected result
Positive controlAuthenticated, authorized write with valid current CSRF material and a valid payload.The documented operation succeeds. This proves the fixture itself is valid.
Missing CSRFKeep the session/cookies, remove the CSRF header, and repeat a write.403 CSRF_TOKEN_INVALID at Gateway; no business mutation.
Invalid CSRFKeep the valid session/cookies but replace the header token with an invalid value.403 CSRF_TOKEN_INVALID at Gateway; no business mutation.
AuthenticationSend a protected read without the SESSION cookie.401 UNAUTHENTICATED. A read isolates authentication from write-CSRF checks.
PermissionUse a valid restricted-user session and CSRF token to attempt an administrator-only business operation.Access denied; the response and business state must match the operation’s access rules.
IDOR / object accessUse resident A’s session to request a member/flat outside A’s permitted scope.The scoped policy must not disclose or mutate another resident’s data. Check content as well as status.
LogoutLog out with the required CSRF material; reuse the old session for a protected read.The old session no longer grants access.
Cookie flagsInspect Set-Cookie over the intended transport.SESSION is HttpOnly and SameSite=Lax. Secure follows SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE; local HTTP configuration may set it false. XSRF-TOKEN is intentionally readable by client code.

For negative tests in Postman, check that cookie jars, collection scripts, or inherited headers have not silently reattached the value you meant to remove. A 403 by itself is not enough evidence; inspect the error and use a valid positive control.

Related protections are different

ConceptMeaningVerification focus
Authentication vs authorizationWho the caller is versus which operation and object the caller may access.Use missing-session, insufficient-permission, and out-of-scope-object tests separately.
CSRF vs CORSCSRF guards against unwanted cookie-authenticated actions; CORS governs browser cross-origin access to responses and preflights.Postman does not enforce browser CORS. Use an actual browser and the intended origin to verify CORS behavior.
XSSUntrusted content executes as script in a page’s origin.Review output handling and test rendering in a controlled browser fixture. CSRF protection does not prevent XSS.
SQL injectionUntrusted input changes the meaning of a database query.Review parameter binding and exercise supported query inputs in a test environment. One rejected string does not prove all queries are safe.
Rate limitingBounds accepted request bursts or sustained token consumption.Use an opt-in controlled test and inspect 429 responses, the matched route, and limiter metrics. It is separate from CSRF and permissions.

Existing evidence and where to rerun it

The collection’s Option B - Automated verification folder includes Reject mutation without CSRF. The saved 24 October 2026 report records HTTP 403 for that request. It is historical evidence; the invalid-token, cookie-flag, browser-CORS, and other cases above need their own corresponding evidence before being called verified.

Sources: Gateway security, backend CSRF presence checks, Postman collection, and saved report.

Implementation references and scenarios

Implementation referenceProtect APIs with sessions and role permissions

Protect APIs with sessions and role permissions

Spring Security + Redis sessions

Version context: The current access flow uses permissions and scopes, including dynamically assigned roles and resident record restrictions. Read earlier role-only examples in that context.

In simple words: a person signs in once with an administrator account. The Gateway remembers the signed-in session in Redis. For every protected request, the backend checks that the session is genuine and that it belongs to an ADMIN. A request without a valid session receives 401; a signed-in identity without the needed permission receives 403.

What changed in this phase?

Phases 1–6 built the services, their databases, internal HTTP calls, Kubernetes routing and Gateway. Those features stay in place. Phase 7 adds identity checks around them: it does not replace the Gateway, RestClient, PostgreSQL ownership or Kubernetes service names.

AreaPhase 1–6Phase 7
GatewayRoutes requests to a backend.Also checks the login session and ADMIN access before forwarding protected requests.
Admin passwordNo account authentication.Member Service owns the admin record and stores a BCrypt hash.
SessionNo login session.Gateway stores the authenticated security context in Redis, shared by Gateway replicas.
Backend accessBackend APIs are reached through service names or direct local ports.Society and Member validate the real SESSION cookie through Gateway before trusting the identity.
Flat lookupMember uses RestClient to ask Society whether a flat exists.The same call uses a narrowly scoped Member-to-Society credential; it is not an ADMIN identity.

This map is based on the files currently shown by Git status. UPDATED means an existing file was changed for Step 7. NEW means a file was added for Step 7. The YAML files connect runtime settings and Kubernetes resources; the SQL migration creates the admin-account table owned by Member Service.

How the files work together

1. Store settingsConfigMaps hold non-secret URLs, names and ports. The emerald-security-credentials Secret is created separately in the emerald-housing namespace; it is not a committed YAML file.
2. Deployments pass valuesenvFrom loads ConfigMap values. secretKeyRef loads selected Secret keys into container environment variables.
3. Spring reads placeholdersEach service's application.yml resolves variables such as ${REDIS_HOST:localhost} or ${SESSION_VALIDATION_TOKEN:}. Kubernetes values override local fallback values.
4. Gateway uses RedisGateway reads Redis host/password and Spring Session settings. It saves and finds the authenticated session in Redis; the client cookie carries only its session ID.
5. Member migration runsMember startup runs Flyway V2 in member_db, Hibernate validates the mapping, then the optional provisioner inserts the configured admin account if it is not already present.
6. Requests use the settingsBackends call Gateway to validate a session. Member calls Society with its separate scoped token to verify a flat. Each service still reads and writes only its own database.

To provision the initial admin, set its two Member Service variables for the first start. The provisioner does not reset its password on later starts; after the row exists, remove the provisioning values from that terminal. Do not put real values in application.yml or source control.

Start in this order: Redis, Society Service, Member Service, then Gateway. Use SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=false only for localhost HTTP. In production HTTPS, set it to true. When changing between separate PowerShell terminals, set the matching token values in each terminal before launching that process. Do not paste secrets into checked-in configuration.

Kubernetes addition order for security

  1. Ensure namespace emerald-housing and existing database credentials Secret are present.
  2. Create the emerald-security-credentials Secret with the six keys shown above (admin username/password, Redis password, and three distinct internal tokens).
  3. Apply kubernetes/redis/deployment.yaml and kubernetes/redis/service.yaml. Redis is internal and must be ready before Gateway sessions can be used.
  4. Apply the updated Society and Member Deployments so they receive their validation and scoped-service tokens. Member startup applies migration V2__create_admin_users.sql, then its provisioner creates the initial admin if absent.
  5. Apply the Gateway ConfigMap and Deployment. Gateway connects to Redis; readiness includes Redis health. Both Gateway replicas use the same Redis namespace and cookie name.
  6. Access the private Gateway using the existing port-forward method for local Postman. Obtain CSRF, log in, obtain a fresh CSRF token, then call protected APIs.

The Secret is an API object in the namespace, not a ConfigMap or committed YAML file. ConfigMaps contain non-secret URLs and ports. Kubernetes Secrets need encryption-at-rest configuration for stronger protection and do not encrypt service-to-service HTTP traffic.

Authentication and authorization — what we implemented

These ideas work together, but they answer different questions. Authentication asks “Who are you?” Authorization asks “What are you allowed to do?” First the project verifies the admin and creates a session; then it checks the admin role and permissions for each protected operation.

Authentication — prove identity

The client gets a CSRF token, then sends a username and password to the Gateway. Gateway asks Member Service to verify them. Member Service looks up the account in member_db and checks the password with BCrypt. If valid, Gateway creates a session in Redis and returns a SESSION cookie.

In this project: Member Service owns admin credentials. There is no public admin sign-up. The cookie carries a session ID; Redis stores session data.

Authorization — check permission

For a protected request, Gateway finds the session in Redis and checks the ADMIN role. The selected backend independently validates the actual session through Gateway, then applies its own service-layer permission checks.

In this project: protected member and society operations require ADMIN. Member's flat lookup call to Society uses a separate limited service authority, not an ADMIN identity.

Actual code and file responsibilities

ConcernActual implementationWhat it does
Gateway login and route authorizationinfrastructure/api-gateway/src/main/java/com/emerald/gateway/security/GatewaySecurityConfiguration.javaConfigures reactive security, CSRF, session handling, login/logout access, and ADMIN protection for /api/v1/**.
Gateway credential verificationinfrastructure/api-gateway/src/main/java/com/emerald/gateway/security/AdminAuthenticationManager.java and AuthController.javaThe manager calls Member Service's private credential endpoint. The controller exposes CSRF, login and current-admin operations.
Admin identity and passwordservices/member-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/member/auth/AdminAccountEntity and its repository represent the account; AdminUserDetailsService loads it; AdminAccountProvisioner creates the configured initial account if absent and hashes its password.
Admin database tableservices/member-service/src/main/resources/db/migration/V2__create_admin_users.sqlFlyway creates the admin table in Member Service's member_db. It contains no default plaintext password.
Shared sessioninfrastructure/api-gateway/src/main/resources/application.yml and kubernetes/redis/Gateway uses Spring Session with Redis so its replicas can read the same server-side session.
Member backend identityservices/member-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/member/security/BackendSecurityContextFilter.java and MemberSecurityConfiguration.javaValidates the presented SESSION cookie through Gateway and installs the verified identity for backend authorization.
Society backend identityservices/society-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/society/security/RequestIdentityFilter.java and SocietySecurityConfiguration.javaPerforms the corresponding session validation and security-chain setup for Society APIs.
Service-layer authorizationMemberService.java, MembershipService.java, FlatService.java, FloorService.java, and SocietyService.javaUses @PreAuthorize checks so permission rules are enforced in business services as well as at the Gateway.
1. Prove identityMember Service verifies the supplied password against its BCrypt hash.
2. Establish sessionGateway stores the authenticated ADMIN context in Redis and gives the client a SESSION cookie.
3. Check permissionGateway and the backend verify the session and required authority before protected work.
4. Allow or denyAllowed work reaches the owning service; missing identity gets 401 and denied permission gets 403.
Keep these checks distinct: CSRF is an additional protection for cookie-based requests that change data; it is not the login itself or the ADMIN permission check. A missing/invalid CSRF token is rejected with 403. A missing/expired session is 401. A valid session without permission is 403. Backends fail closed if they cannot validate the session.

Complete authentication architecture and call flow

This single diagram combines the login architecture with the successful and failed request paths. Follow the numbered arrows for the normal flow. Red paths show where processing stops. Postman users call the CSRF endpoint manually; a future frontend can call it automatically when its login page opens.

Purple arrows: login, routing and internal request flow.Green arrows: successful validation and data path.Red arrow: rejection; business work stops.Dashed backend arrows show private session validation calls back to Gateway.

Positive flow — what happens in order

  1. Postman manually, or a future frontend automatically, calls GET /api/v1/auth/csrf. Gateway returns a CSRF token and sets the XSRF-TOKEN cookie.
  2. The client calls POST /api/v1/auth/login with credentials, that cookie and matching X-XSRF-TOKEN header.
  3. Gateway checks CSRF and privately asks Member Service to verify the credentials. Member Service checks the BCrypt hash in member_db and returns identity/role only.
  4. Gateway creates the ADMIN session, changes the session ID, stores the server-side session in Redis and returns the HttpOnly SESSION cookie. The client obtains a fresh CSRF token after login.
  5. For a protected request, the client sends SESSION; state-changing requests also send the XSRF cookie and header. Gateway checks Redis and the ADMIN role, then routes by path.
  6. The backend independently validates the real SESSION cookie through Gateway's private endpoint. If valid, its service-layer authorization and business logic run.
  7. For flat assignment, Member uses RestClient and a restricted service credential to ask Society to check society_db. Only a confirmed flat allows Member to save the membership in member_db.
  8. Logout invalidates the Redis session and clears the cookie. The old cookie should then receive 401.
Success: authorized requests reach the owning service and its own database.

Negative flow — where processing stops

  • CSRF missing/invalid: 403; a state-changing operation does not run.
  • Bad username/password: generic 401; no authenticated session is created.
  • SESSION missing/expired/invalid: 401; protected business processing is denied.
  • Permission or role denied: 403. Current provisioning creates ADMIN accounts only; another role must be configured to demonstrate this case.
  • Backend cannot reach session validation: 503; backend fails closed and does not process the protected operation.
  • Flat does not exist: controlled not-found response; no membership is saved.
  • Society is unavailable: dependency error; Member cannot assume the flat is valid and does not save the membership.
  • Old cookie after logout: 401; client must log in again.
Failure: reject safely; do not save data when authorization or flat validation fails.

What each participant does

ParticipantResponsibility
Postman or frontendObtains CSRF before login, sends credentials, keeps cookies and sends the CSRF header for changes. Postman does this manually; frontend code can automate it.
API GatewayChecks CSRF, delegates credential verification, creates and reads Redis-backed sessions, checks ADMIN access, routes requests and provides private session validation.
Member ServiceOwns admin credentials in member_db, checks BCrypt hashes, validates sessions for its APIs and owns member/membership records.
Society ServiceValidates sessions for its APIs, owns Society/floor/flat data in society_db, and answers Member's scoped flat-validation call.
RedisStores Gateway session state for sharing between Gateway replicas. The SESSION cookie contains a session ID, not the session data.

Identity separation: the admin's SESSION cookie authenticates a person. Member-to-Society uses a distinct, restricted service credential. That machine identity is not an ADMIN login and does not provide cross-database access.

Authentication endpoints and responses

Method and pathUseExpected result
GET /api/v1/auth/csrfObtain a CSRF token before login or a mutation.200 JSON with token, headerName and parameterName; XSRF-TOKEN cookie is set.
POST /api/v1/auth/loginJSON body has username and password.200 with adminId, username, role and authenticated; invalid credentials return generic 401.
GET /api/v1/auth/meRead the signed-in administrator.200 if logged in; otherwise 401.
POST /api/v1/auth/logoutEnd the session; requires SESSION cookie and CSRF header.200; server session and cookies are cleared; old session no longer authenticates.
Protected business APIsSend the SESSION cookie; mutations also send the XSRF cookie and matching header.401 without a valid login; 403 if access/CSRF rules deny the request.

Passwords, provisioning and configuration

There is no public admin signup. The only credential owner is Member Service, in member_db.admin_users. Migration V2__create_admin_users.sql creates the table. At startup, AdminAccountProvisioner inserts the configured initial account only if its username is not already present; it BCrypt-hashes the password and does not reset an existing account. Leave the provisioning variables unset after initial setup if you do not need to provision another account.

For local development, provide ADMIN_PROVISIONING_USERNAME, ADMIN_PROVISIONING_PASSWORD, GATEWAY_AUTHENTICATION_TOKEN, SESSION_VALIDATION_TOKEN, MEMBER_TO_SOCIETY_TOKEN, and Redis settings as process environment variables. Use distinct randomly generated service tokens with at least 32 characters. Do not put real values in this HTML, Postman export, source files or Git.

For Kubernetes, the Deployments read these keys from the emerald-security-credentials Secret. The Gateway reads its auth token, session-validation token and Redis password; Member reads the Gateway auth token, session-validation token, Member-to-Society token and initial admin credentials; Society reads the session-validation token and Member-to-Society token. Store the Secret in namespace emerald-housing. Redis is a private ClusterIP and is allowed inbound traffic from Gateway Pods by kubernetes/networkpolicies.yaml. This Redis Deployment uses emptyDir: losing/restarting Redis loses sessions, so this is not a high-availability production Redis setup.

Example: create the Step 7 Kubernetes Secret in PowerShell

Run only after the namespace exists. Replace each placeholder with locally generated random tokens or private credentials. This is an example command; it was not run. Keep the values out of Git and clear them from shell history where appropriate. Kubernetes Secrets are base64-encoded in the API, not encrypted automatically unless cluster encryption at rest is configured.

kubectl create secret generic emerald-security-credentials -n emerald-housing `
  --from-literal=admin-username='your-admin-name' `
  --from-literal=admin-password='a-private-password-at-least-14-characters' `
  --from-literal=redis-password='a-private-random-redis-secret' `
  --from-literal=gateway-authentication-token='unique-random-token-32-characters-or-more' `
  --from-literal=session-validation-token='different-random-token-32-characters-or-more' `
  --from-literal=member-to-society-token='another-random-token-32-characters-or-more'

The member service reads the initial admin values during startup and inserts the account only if the username is absent. The random service tokens must be different from each other; they authorize distinct internal operations.

CSRF and browser cookies

Login creates a cookie-based session, so the application does not turn off CSRF protection at the Gateway. Get a token, keep the XSRF-TOKEN cookie, and put the returned token in the X-XSRF-TOKEN header for POST, PUT, PATCH and DELETE. After login, get a fresh CSRF token because login clears the old CSRF cookie. The SESSION cookie is HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax and scoped to /. Local HTTP configuration uses Secure=false; set Secure=true when serving over HTTPS. Do not send session IDs in URLs or logs.

Postman walkthrough

  1. Import the existing Phase 1–6 collection and local environment. Select the environment and set gatewayBaseUrl to your accessible Gateway address.
  2. Enter the initial username and password only in local Postman variables or the Postman UI. Do not export or commit the real password.
  3. Run Authentication → Get CSRF Token. Copy the response's token into csrfToken. Postman should retain cookies for the Gateway host.
  4. Run Authentication → Admin Login with {{adminUsername}} and {{adminPassword}}, and header X-XSRF-TOKEN: {{csrfToken}}.
  5. Run Get CSRF Token again and replace csrfToken with the returned fresh token. Run Get Current Admin to confirm session identity.
  6. Run business reads. For each mutation, send X-XSRF-TOKEN: {{csrfToken}}; the cookie jar sends the SESSION and XSRF cookies. Run Logout with the CSRF header, then retry Get Current Admin and expect 401.

Without scripts, Postman does not automatically copy the JSON token into an environment variable; copying it manually is intentional here. To demonstrate CSRF denial, sign in, omit the header on a mutation and expect 403. The current system provisions ADMIN only, so a non-ADMIN role-denial example is not available until a separately authorized role is introduced.

Security limits to understand

  • This implementation uses synchronous internal HTTP calls. The local/Kubernetes examples currently use HTTP. Secrets and NetworkPolicies restrict callers, but they do not encrypt traffic. Use TLS between services and Gateway before exposing this across an untrusted network.
  • Redis is a single ephemeral learning instance, not a highly available session store. Redis loss signs administrators out.
  • Session validation adds a Gateway call for each backend request. Gateway availability is therefore part of backend authentication availability.
  • Postman cookie behavior and CSRF token rotation need manual verification in the actual Postman client.
  • Provisioning is create-if-absent. Password rotation and account-disable administration need a controlled operational process.

Phase 7 source-level checklist

  • Gateway login delegates password verification to Member Service; password hashes never leave Member Service.
  • BCrypt password hashes and the ADMIN role live in Member Service's own database.
  • Redis-backed Gateway sessions support multiple Gateway replicas without sticky sessions.
  • Both backend services verify the actual session through Gateway and enforce ADMIN service-layer rules.
  • Gateway CSRF protection, session cookie settings, logout, and 401/403 JSON handling are configured.
  • Internal flat lookup uses a scoped service token, not an ADMIN role or caller-supplied identity header.
Verification status: this is a source and configuration description. The repository changes were not compiled, started, deployed or tested in this task, so runtime behavior still needs to be verified by you.
Implementation referenceAdd a secure outside entrance in front of Spring Gateway

Add a secure outside entrance in front of Spring Gateway

NGINX Gateway Fabric + Kubernetes Gateway API

Phase 8 adds an HTTPS edge for people outside the Kubernetes cluster. NGINX accepts the outside request, ends HTTPS, and forwards the API request to the existing Spring Cloud Gateway. Spring Gateway still chooses Member or Society Service.

HTTPS edge request path

Easy comparison: NGINX is the secure entrance gate. Spring Cloud Gateway is the receptionist inside who sends a request to the correct application. NGINX does not replace Spring Gateway and does not send public requests directly to Member Service or Society Service.

What changed in Phase 8?

AreaBeforeNow
Outside accessLocal users reach the private Gateway through a development access method.NGINX Gateway Fabric provides an HTTP/HTTPS edge.
TLSNo NGINX edge terminates browser HTTPS.The HTTPS listener uses a Kubernetes TLS Secret; NGINX decrypts the outside connection.
RoutingSpring Cloud Gateway routes API paths to backend Services.NGINX forwards public /api/ requests to the existing api-gateway Service. Spring Gateway keeps its routing job.
Gateway replicasTwo API Gateway Pods sit behind one ClusterIP Service.NGINX discovers ready endpoints of that Service; no Pod IP is stored in the route.

Request flow

Browser / Postman
  | HTTPS to emerald.local:443
  v
NGINX Gateway Fabric data plane
  | TLS ends here; forwards /api/... to api-gateway:8080
  v
Kubernetes api-gateway Service
  | selects ready Gateway Pod endpoints
  v
Spring Cloud Gateway (one of 2 Pods)
  | matches the existing application route
  +--------------------------+
  |                          |
  v                          v
member-service           society-service
  |                          |
member_db                  society_db

For flat assignment, Member Service still calls Society Service with RestClient
through Kubernetes DNS before saving the membership.

Phase 8 configuration files and how they connect

The controller watches Gateway API objects. GatewayClass selects the controller. Gateway opens listeners. HTTPRoutes attach redirect and forwarding behavior. NGINX policies tune upstream and client behavior.

FilePurposeConnection to other resources
kubernetes/nginx/helm-values.yamlLimits NGINX Gateway Fabric's watched namespace to emerald-housing.Used during controller installation; this is not an application ConfigMap.
kubernetes/nginx/gatewayclass.yamlDeclares class nginx and controller gateway.nginx.org/nginx-gateway-controller.The Gateway's gatewayClassName: nginx asks this controller to manage it. Helm installation creates the default class; this file records the expected association.
kubernetes/nginx/gateway.yamlCreates emerald-edge in namespace emerald-housing, with HTTP 80 and HTTPS 443 listeners for emerald.local.HTTPS terminates TLS with Secret emerald-local-tls. Routes can attach only from the same namespace.
kubernetes/nginx/httproutes.yamlDefines HTTP-to-HTTPS redirect (308) and HTTPS forwarding for /api/.Redirect route attaches to the HTTP listener. API route attaches to HTTPS and sends requests to api-gateway:8080.
kubernetes/nginx/policies.yamlSelects round-robin and sets proxy/client timeouts and body limits.Upstream policy targets the api-gateway Service; proxy and client policies target emerald-edge.
kubernetes/gateway/service.yamlPrivate ClusterIP Service api-gateway listens on 8080.NGINX uses this stable Service and port, never a Pod IP.
kubernetes/gateway/deployment.yamlRuns two Gateway replicas with health probes and rolling updates.The Service selects ready Pods labelled app: api-gateway; NGINX discovers their endpoints through Kubernetes.
kubernetes/networkpolicies.yamlAllows NGINX data-plane Pods for this Gateway to reach the Gateway Service while retaining internal access rules.NetworkPolicies restrict network paths but do not authenticate callers; enforcement depends on CNI support.
kubernetes/gateway/config.yamlEnables Secure session cookies and framework forwarded-header handling in Kubernetes.Helps Spring understand external HTTPS while preserving Step 7 cookies and sessions.

Resource setup order

  1. Start the Kubernetes cluster and create namespace emerald-housing.
  2. Install compatible Gateway API CRDs and NGINX Gateway Fabric. Repository instructions pin NGINX Gateway Fabric 2.4.2 and Gateway API 1.4.1; confirm the cluster runs Kubernetes 1.25 or newer.
  3. Create a local certificate for emerald.local and Secret emerald-local-tls in emerald-housing. The private key is not committed.
  4. Apply gatewayclass.yaml, gateway.yaml, httproutes.yaml, and policies.yaml. The controller reconciles these objects and creates its data plane.
  5. Ensure the existing api-gateway Deployment has two ready Pods and its ClusterIP Service selects them.
  6. For local kind access, port-forward the generated NGINX data-plane Service, add 127.0.0.1 emerald.local to the Windows hosts file, and use https://emerald.local:8443.
Operator commands for a local NGINX edge

These are instructions, not commands run during this documentation update. The repository's kind configuration has no host port mappings, so this example uses port-forwarding.

kubectl version
kubectl kustomize "https://github.com/nginx/nginx-gateway-fabric/config/crd/gateway-api/standard?ref=v2.4.2" | kubectl apply -f -
helm install ngf oci://ghcr.io/nginx/charts/nginx-gateway-fabric --version 2.4.2 --create-namespace -n nginx-gateway -f .\kubernetes\nginx\helm-values.yaml
kubectl rollout status deployment/ngf-nginx-gateway-fabric -n nginx-gateway

kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\namespace.yaml
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force .\local-certs | Out-Null
openssl req -x509 -nodes -days 90 -newkey rsa:2048 -keyout .\local-certs\emerald.local.key -out .\local-certs\emerald.local.crt -subj "/CN=emerald.local" -addext "subjectAltName=DNS:emerald.local"
kubectl create secret tls emerald-local-tls -n emerald-housing --cert .\local-certs\emerald.local.crt --key .\local-certs\emerald.local.key

kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\nginx\gatewayclass.yaml
kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\nginx\gateway.yaml
kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\nginx\httproutes.yaml
kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\nginx\policies.yaml
kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\networkpolicies.yaml

kubectl get gateway emerald-edge -n emerald-housing
kubectl get service -n emerald-housing -l gateway.networking.k8s.io/gateway-name=emerald-edge
kubectl port-forward -n emerald-housing service/<generated-nginx-service-name> 8443:443
# In a second terminal, only for the HTTP redirect demonstration:
kubectl port-forward -n emerald-housing service/<generated-nginx-service-name> 8080:80

Trust the local self-signed certificate or CA in Windows and Postman. Do not make disabled certificate verification the normal setting. Keep private keys under ignored local-certs/, outside Git.

Postman and local browser access

Add 127.0.0.1 emerald.local to the Windows hosts file and set the existing Postman environment variable externalBaseUrl to https://emerald.local:8443. Use the existing Authentication folder to get a CSRF token, log in, then get a fresh CSRF token. Postman should keep the SESSION and XSRF-TOKEN cookies for emerald.local. The exported environment contains no real credentials; direct Society, Member and Spring Gateway URLs remain for diagnostics. Trust the local certificate rather than disabling verification as the normal setting. For the redirect demonstration, use the separate HTTP port-forward and turn off Postman auto-follow redirects so you can see the 308 response.

HTTPS, redirects and certificates

  • The HTTPS listener on 443 references Secret emerald-local-tls and uses TLS termination. The browser-to-NGINX connection is HTTPS.
  • The HTTP listener on port 80 redirects to HTTPS with status 308, preserving the method and body if the client follows the redirect. Send credentials only over HTTPS because the initial HTTP request reaches the edge before redirecting.
  • TLS termination means NGINX decrypts traffic. NGINX-to-Spring-Gateway uses HTTP unless TLS is configured separately for that hop.
  • For production, use a trusted certificate authority, automate renewal, rotate the TLS Secret, and decide whether the internal NGINX-to-Gateway hop also needs TLS.

Load balancing and Gateway Pod recovery

UpstreamSettingsPolicy selects round_robin across API Gateway endpoints. NGINX Gateway Fabric discovers ready endpoints behind api-gateway; no Pod IP is saved in the configuration. When a Pod is unready or replaced, endpoint discovery updates the upstreams. The Deployment replaces a deleted Pod, and the new Pod receives traffic after it becomes ready.

NGINX Gateway Fabric also supports least-connections and randomized approaches such as random two least_conn; without this policy, its documented default is random two least_conn. Keep-alive connections and uneven request durations mean a small sample may not alternate evenly. Gateway API backend weights apply to Service backends; this project has one API Gateway backend, so weights do not select individual Pods. Some in-flight calls may fail during termination; zero downtime is not promised.

Forwarded headers, security and sessions

NGINX supplies X-Forwarded-For, X-Forwarded-Proto, X-Forwarded-Host, and X-Forwarded-Port. It preserves cookies, CSRF headers, and X-Correlation-ID. Kubernetes enables Spring framework forwarded-header handling; local direct runs default to NONE. Spring Security remains responsible for login and authorization.

Trust boundary: do not treat client-supplied forwarding headers as identity or authorize using X-Forwarded-For. If a trusted load balancer is later placed before NGINX, configure only its known addresses before trusting client-IP headers. The application does not rely on RFC Forwarded. Redis sessions remain shared by both Gateway replicas; sticky sessions are not needed for session continuity.

Timeouts, errors and private services

The NGINX policies set a 2-second upstream connection timeout, 8-second upstream read and send inactivity timeouts, a 15-second request-body read timeout, and a 1 MiB body limit. These are separate from Spring Gateway and RestClient timeouts. Read/send settings measure inactivity between I/O, not total request duration. Edge connection failures may return 502 or 504; valid backend responses such as 400, 404, and 409 pass through. No retry policy is configured, so unsafe POST requests are not replayed.

Only /api/ is exposed by this HTTPRoute. Actuator and private /internal/** session-validation paths are not exposed. Society, Member, Redis and PostgreSQL remain internal to the cluster.

Manual verification checklist

  1. Check GatewayClass nginx is accepted; check Gateway listeners are accepted and programmed.
  2. Check HTTPRoutes are accepted with references resolved, and NGINX policies are accepted.
  3. Inspect Pods, Services and EndpointSlices; confirm the API Gateway Service has two ready endpoints.
  4. Request an HTTP API URL and confirm a 308 redirect. Turn off automatic redirect-following in Postman for this check.
  5. Trust the local certificate and request https://emerald.local:8443; confirm the response passes through NGINX and Spring Gateway.
  6. Use the existing Authentication requests to get CSRF, sign in, fetch a fresh CSRF token, and call a protected API. Confirm cookies pass through.
  7. Delete one Gateway Pod, watch the Deployment replace it, and confirm calls work through a ready backend. Repeat an authenticated request to check Redis session continuity.
kubectl get gatewayclass nginx
kubectl describe gateway emerald-edge -n emerald-housing
kubectl get httproutes -n emerald-housing
kubectl get upstreamsettingspolicy,proxysettingspolicy,clientsettingspolicy -n emerald-housing
kubectl get pods,services,endpointslices -n emerald-housing
kubectl get deployment api-gateway -n emerald-housing
kubectl get pods -n emerald-housing -l app=api-gateway -o wide
kubectl delete pod <one-api-gateway-pod> -n emerald-housing
kubectl rollout status deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing

NGINX reverse proxy versus Spring Cloud Gateway

NGINX Gateway FabricSpring Cloud Gateway
Outside edge: accepts HTTP/HTTPS, handles the certificate and redirects HTTP to HTTPS, then forwards public API paths to the Gateway Service.Application router: matches Member, Society and authentication paths and forwards them to backend Services.
Configured with Kubernetes Gateway API resources and NGINX policies.Configured in infrastructure/api-gateway/src/main/resources/application.yml.
Does not replace Spring Security or backend authorization.Keeps the existing application security integration; backend services also enforce authorization.

Phase 8 summary

  • Added: NGINX Gateway Fabric and Kubernetes Gateway API configuration under kubernetes/nginx/.
  • Connected to: the existing API Gateway ClusterIP Service and its two Deployment replicas.
  • Preserved: separate databases, Member-to-Society RestClient calls, Spring Cloud Gateway routes, and Phase 7 session/CSRF security.
  • Still unverified: controller installation, certificate, HTTPS routes, policy acceptance, real traffic distribution and recovery need manual deployment. NetworkPolicy enforcement depends on the cluster CNI.
Implementation reference2. Authentication and authorization execution flow

2. Authentication and authorization execution flow

RoleReadable recordsWrites / accounts / audit
ADMIN / SOCIETY_ADMINSociety-wide business recordsManage business records and accounts; retrieve audit history.
COMMITTEESociety, floors, members and flatsRead-only; no account administration or audit access.
RESIDENTSociety/floors, own member and history, currently assigned flats; own entries in flat membershipsRead-only. Must link to an active member to authenticate.

Important distinction: the membership relationship OWNER, TENANT or RESIDENT describes a person's flat relationship. The application role RESIDENT describes account access. These are separate concepts.

Source: infrastructure/api-gateway/src/main/java/com/emerald/gateway/security/InternalSessionValidationController.java; services/member-service/.../security/MemberAccess.java; services/society-service/.../security/SocietyAccess.java

Implementation referenceAdmin login using Postman

Admin login using Postman

Use the Gateway session cookie to authenticate. In Postman, select Authorization → No Auth and keep the cookie jar enabled. A Bearer token is not required.

1. Keep the local Gateway connection running

kubectl --context docker-desktop port-forward service/api-gateway 8080:8080 -n emerald-housing

Leave this terminal open. Local port 8080 forwards to a Gateway Pod; this command does not start the backend services.

2. Configure and select your Postman environment

VariableValue
externalBaseUrlhttp://localhost:8080
adminUsernameYour provisioned administrator username; the account created in this session is emerald-admin-1020201500.
adminPasswordThe password shared privately in the conversation. Store it in your local Postman value or vault; do not publish it with documentation or exported environments.
csrfTokenInitially empty; copy the token returned in step 3.

3. Get the CSRF token before login

GET {{externalBaseUrl}}/api/v1/auth/csrf

Click Send. Copy the response's token into csrfToken. The response also gives the headerName, normally X-XSRF-TOKEN. Postman must retain the returned cookies.

4. Send the login request

POST {{externalBaseUrl}}/api/v1/auth/login
Content-Type: application/json
X-XSRF-TOKEN: {{csrfToken}}

Select Body → raw → JSON and enter:

{
  "username": "{{adminUsername}}",
  "password": "{{adminPassword}}"
}

Click Send. Expect 200 OK with your username and role ADMIN. Postman automatically retains the SESSION cookie. Use the same hostname throughout the workflow.

5. Confirm your authenticated identity

GET {{externalBaseUrl}}/api/v1/auth/me

Expect 200 OK with the same username and ADMIN role. This verifies the session cookie works, rather than only checking the login response.

6. Refresh CSRF after login, then call business APIs

Repeat step 3 and update csrfToken: login rotates the session and clears the old CSRF token. Send the new token and the session cookies on POST, PUT and DELETE requests. GET requests retain the session cookie but do not require the CSRF header.

GET /auth/csrf → POST /auth/login → GET /auth/me
             → GET /auth/csrf again → authenticated business requests
(All authentication paths above start with /api/v1.)
ProblemCheck
401 INVALID_CREDENTIALSCheck the selected environment and exact username/password. Provisioning variables do not reset an existing account's password.
401 on /auth/meKeep Postman cookies enabled and use the same base URL. HTTPS-only Secure cookies will not work over local HTTP; verify the deployment's cookie setting.
403 CSRF_TOKEN_INVALIDFetch a new CSRF token, retain its cookie, and send the returned token using its headerName.
429Wait before retrying: the login rate limit was reached.
503 authentication unavailableCheck Member Service readiness, Redis and the Gateway-to-Member connection.
Connection refusedKeep port-forward running and confirm local port 8080 is available.
Implementation referenceAPI security verification lab

API security verification lab

Repeatable Postman checks for authentication, permissions, revocation and common attack attempts. Each check describes its setup, expected outcome and the protection it demonstrates.

Deployment prerequisite: these checks target the current source implementation. During the October 2026 login setup, deployed Gateway and Member images lacked the new account route. Deploy compatible current images and migrations before testing account roles and revocation. A 404 or an unrelated 500 is an inconclusive test, not proof that a security control passed.

1. Prepare accounts, sessions and evidence

  1. Use your local development deployment and disposable member/flat records. Confirm Gateway, Member, Society and Redis are ready. Keep the Gateway port-forward running.
  2. Set externalBaseUrl=http://localhost:8080. Follow the Postman admin login workflow: fetch CSRF, log in, refresh CSRF, verify /api/v1/auth/me.
  3. Prepare administrator A, administrator B, COMMITTEE and two RESIDENT accounts linked to different active members. Give each resident a different active flat assignment.
  4. Keep a separate Postman cookie jar/profile for each identity, or log out, clear cookies and log in each time you switch roles. Changing an environment username does not change the existing session.
  5. Use variables targetAccountId, memberA, memberB, flatA, flatB and csrfToken. Record the acting identity, URL, status, error code and correlation ID for every test. Check database/business state after denied mutations.
Authentication → current account scope → object permission → business operation
       ↓                  ↓                   ↓
Invalid session       Disabled account    Wrong resident record
       └──────────────── denied; no business mutation ────────────────┘

Account creation, as an administrator: POST /api/v1/accounts with a unique username, private password of 14–72 UTF-8 bytes, role, societyCode: "EMERALD" and a resident member link. Never put real passwords or session cookies into shared test evidence.

2. Authentication and session security

TestStepsExpected result / explanation
No sessionClear cookies. GET /api/v1/members.401. Business APIs require an authenticated session.
Wrong passwordGet valid CSRF first. POST /api/v1/auth/login with an incorrect password.401 INVALID_CREDENTIALS. A CSRF failure would not test credential validation.
Successful loginLogin correctly; GET /api/v1/auth/me with the returned cookies.200 with the expected username and role. No password/hash in the response.
Cookie tamperingReplace SESSION with a random value and call a protected GET.401. An arbitrary cookie cannot establish identity.
Session rotationPrivately compare the pre-login cookie, if present, with the authenticated SESSION cookie.Login changes the session ID. A pre-login session must not gain the authenticated session's access.
Cookie attributesInspect login Set-Cookie headers in Postman.SESSION is HttpOnly, SameSite=Lax and Path=/. Secure follows deployment configuration: local HTTP needs Secure=false; HTTPS should use Secure=true. XSRF-TOKEN is intentionally readable by a browser client.

3. CSRF and request replay

  1. Login as admin and fetch a fresh CSRF token.
  2. Send an otherwise valid POST/PUT/PATCH business request with its session cookie but without the CSRF header. Expect 403.
  3. Repeat with an incorrect token. Expect 403. Confirm the business record did not change.
  4. Repeat with the correct token in the response's headerName, normally X-XSRF-TOKEN. Expect the normal business result.
  5. After a new login, fetch a new token before further writes. Reusing old session/CSRF state should not establish authenticated access.

Why: cookies authenticate the browser, while CSRF validation prevents a write using only automatically attached cookies. Do not use GET requests to test CSRF: safe reads do not require this header. Postman demonstrates token enforcement, but browser cross-origin behavior needs a browser test.

4. Role permissions and IDOR attacks

IDOR means changing an object ID to reach somebody else's record. Use valid UUIDs for two real test members; malformed UUID rejection tests input parsing rather than ownership.

IdentityRequest / attemptExpected result
RESIDENT AGET /api/v1/members/{{memberA}} and /api/v1/flats/{{flatA}}200 for their own member and currently assigned flat.
RESIDENT AReplace IDs with memberB or flatB.403 from record-level authorization; no other resident's details.
RESIDENT AGET member lists, page/search/cursor routes and flat membership lists.Only allowed records. An empty result is acceptable. Paging totals and search must not expose other members.
RESIDENT / COMMITTEESend valid member/flat create, edit or deactivate requests with correct CSRF.403 and unchanged business state. Valid CSRF does not grant write permission.
RESIDENT / COMMITTEEGET /api/v1/accounts/{{targetAccountId}} or an audit endpoint.403. Account administration and audit history require an administrator.
COMMITTEERead society, floors, members and flats.200 for valid records; read-only society-wide access.
ADMIN / SOCIETY_ADMINPerform the same valid administration requests.Normal success. This positive control proves the endpoint and payload work.

Cursor scope check: obtain a cursor as resident A, switch to resident B and reuse it; also change its filters or corrupt the token. Expect 400 INVALID_CURSOR for mismatched/malformed tokens. Authorization must remain effective even if a token can be decoded: cursors are not signed security credentials.

5. Revocation: remove access without waiting for logout

Use two independent sessions: administrator A changes target B while B keeps the same existing cookie. Do not log B in again between the before/after checks. Current account permissions are refreshed during backend session validation.

A. Disable an account

  1. As A, GET /api/v1/accounts/{{targetAccountId}}. Copy its current version, role, societyCode and memberId.
  2. As A, send the permissions update below with enabled: false and the current version.
  3. As B, call /api/v1/auth/me and a protected business endpoint with the old cookie.
  4. Expect 401 and no protected business data. Restore B with enabled: true using a freshly fetched version, then re-login B for subsequent tests.
PUT {{externalBaseUrl}}/api/v1/accounts/{{targetAccountId}}/permissions
Content-Type: application/json
X-XSRF-TOKEN: {{csrfToken}}

{
  "role": "COMMITTEE",
  "societyCode": "EMERALD",
  "memberId": null,
  "enabled": false,
  "version": 0
}

Replace every field with the target's actual values; version: 0 is illustrative. A resident must retain a valid member link. A stale version returns 409 VERSION_CONFLICT; re-fetch and review before retrying.

B. Downgrade an administrator

As administrator A, change administrator B to COMMITTEE while leaving it enabled. With B's existing session, a valid read should still work, but business writes and audit/account requests should return 403. Do not use a stale role displayed in a previously saved login response as proof of current permissions.

C. Remove a resident's flat access

Verify resident B can read its assigned test flat. As admin, end every open assignment linking that member to that flat using PATCH /api/v1/members/{memberId}/flats/{membershipId}/end with a valid endDate. With B's existing cookie, read the flat again: expect 403. Its own member record should remain readable. Another open membership to the same flat would legitimately preserve access.

D. Deactivate the linked resident member

As admin, deactivate the disposable linked member. The resident's next validated request should be rejected with 401 because the member is no longer active. Use a disposable fixture; this guide does not assume a member-reactivation endpoint.

E. Logout and self-lockout protection

Logout using valid CSRF, then replay the old session cookie on a protected GET: expect 401. Separately, attempt to update your own permissions: expect 409 SELF_PERMISSION_CHANGE. Use administrator A to change B, preserving an administrator for recovery.

Limit: revocation affects subsequent validated requests. It does not undo completed operations or guarantee cancellation of an already running request.

6. Attack-style inputs and boundary checks

SubtestSafe local procedureExpected protection / limitation
Identity header spoofingAs resident A, request member B while adding X-Authenticated-Role: ADMIN, X-Role: ADMIN and X-Authenticated-User: fake-admin.Still 403. Gateway strips supported identity/internal headers; backend trusts validated server identity, not user headers.
Internal endpoint probingWithout a real service token, GET Gateway /internal/auth/session. Try POST /internal/auth/credentials through Gateway.Session validation rejects a missing/invalid token with 403; Member's internal credentials endpoint is not a public Gateway route. No identity or credential data should be returned.
Privilege escalation in JSONAs resident, add "role":"ADMIN" to a valid member write request; also attempt the account permissions API.403; caller privileges must remain unchanged. Unknown JSON fields may be rejected or ignored, but must never alter authenticated permissions.
Wrong society scopeAs admin, create/update a test account with a societyCode other than EMERALD.400 INVALID_SCOPE. This is a single-society deployment, not proof of general multi-tenant isolation.
SQL-like search inputAs a resident, use Postman's Params editor to search members with ' OR 1=1 --, then % and _.Literal scoped search, usually zero matches; no unscoped data or SQL error. Ordinary text containing these characters may legitimately match. These examples are smoke tests, not a complete injection audit.
Invalid / expensive query parametersTry malformed UUIDs, invalid status, search over 120 characters, negative page, excessive size or unsupported sort fields.Validation rejects unsupported values with 400; no stack traces or database internals. Use configured bounds, not arbitrary expectations about maximum size.
HTML/script-like stored inputOn a disposable record, submit a harmless markup string such as <b>security-test</b> in an allowed text field.Backend returns valid JSON or validation errors. JSON storage alone does not demonstrate XSS safety; a frontend/browser check must confirm output is rendered safely.
Rate-limit abuseRun a short local burst of authenticated GET requests to one business route. For login, use a small controlled burst with valid CSRF.429 when the configured shared Redis bucket is exhausted, then recovery after refill. Login can include Retry-After. Sequential Postman requests may not exceed the threshold.

7. Replay, concurrent edits and audit evidence

  1. Stale edit: read a test member/flat and save its ETag. Update it once, then submit another edit with the old If-Match. Expect 412 and preservation of the newer state. Missing If-Match is allowed unless strict mode is configured.
  2. Idempotent create: send the same member-create body twice as the same admin with the same Idempotency-Key. Expect the same member ID, a replay indicator on the repeat and one creation audit event. Reuse the key with a different body: expect 409. Idempotency does not replace authentication or CSRF.
  3. Audit visibility: as admin, query member/society audit history for successful test changes. Verify actor, action, entity, timestamp and correlation. As resident or committee, confirm access is denied.
  4. Sensitive responses: inspect successful account responses, denied requests and audit snapshots. Passwords, password hashes, session cookies and service tokens must not be exposed. Member audit snapshots can contain personal information and therefore remain admin-only.

Failure policy: if the internal current-account lookup is unavailable, backend session validation must fail closed, normally 503, rather than reuse an unverified identity. A controlled dependency-outage test requires an isolated environment and restoration steps; a generic 503 alone does not establish this guarantee.

8. Record results and restore fixtures

TestActor / endpointExpectedActual / correlation IDVerdict
Unauthenticated accessNo session / members GET401Record after executionNot run
Resident IDORResident A / member B GET403; no record disclosureRecord after executionNot run
Disable accountTarget's existing session401 after disableRecord after executionNot run
Downgrade roleTarget's existing session / write403; unchanged recordRecord after executionNot run
Missing CSRFAdmin / otherwise valid write403; unchanged recordRecord after executionNot run

A check passes only when its intended layer rejects the request and the business state matches the expectation. Connection failures, invalid fixture IDs and unrelated exceptions are inconclusive. Restore test-account permissions with fresh versions, clear test cookies and end disposable memberships. These instructions add documentation; they do not claim the attack or revocation checks have been executed.

Source references: GatewaySecurityConfiguration, InternalHeaderSanitizingFilter, InternalSessionValidationController; MemberAccess / SocietyAccess; AccountController, AccountIdentityService, VersionPreconditions and CursorCodec.

Scenario questionsIDOR (Insecure Direct Object Reference)

IDOR (Insecure Direct Object Reference)

Project context: The current Gateway and backend services require an ADMIN role for the exposed Member and Society APIs, and service methods also enforce roles. Membership ending uses a lookup constrained by both membership ID and member ID. If the product adds resident or tenant self-service, every object access must additionally check that the authenticated user is allowed to access that specific member, flat, membership, or file. UUIDs alone do not provide authorization.
1. What is IDOR, and how does it differ from a general authorization failure?

IDOR is a vulnerability where an attacker changes an object reference, such as an ID in a URL, and the server returns or modifies an object the caller is not allowed to access. It is a specific object-level authorization failure: the caller may be logged in and allowed to use the endpoint, but not allowed to use that object.

2. How can an attacker exploit IDOR?

An attacker can substitute another record's ID in a path, query, body, or nested resource reference, then read, update, delete, or associate that record if the server checks only that it exists. The fix is a server-side authorization decision for every referenced object and operation.

3. Why are IDs in URLs and request bodies common IDOR targets?

IDs are easy to observe in browser tools, API responses, logs, and links. Sequential IDs are easy to guess, but unguessable IDs can also be copied or leaked. Treat every client-provided object reference as untrusted and authorize access after resolving it.

4. How is IDOR related to Broken Object Level Authorization (BOLA)?

BOLA describes the broader API security problem where a caller can act on an object without the required object-level permission. IDOR is commonly used for this class of issue when an exposed object reference can be changed. The remedy is not merely to hide the reference; enforce access rules on the server.

5. Why is authentication alone not enough?

Authentication proves who the caller is. Authorization decides what that identity can do and which object it may access. A valid session must not grant access to every record.

Solution: derive the caller identity from the verified security context, then apply ownership, tenant, role, and operation rules to the requested object.

6. How do you authorize each object access?

Check authorization on every read and write, including nested resources, bulk operations, exports, and background actions. Prefer a service-layer policy so controllers cannot accidentally omit it, and keep the object lookup and access predicate together where practical.

// Illustrative Spring pattern; principal comes from verified authentication
@GetMapping("/{memberId}")
public MemberResponse get(@PathVariable UUID memberId, @AuthenticationPrincipal AppUser user) {
    return memberService.getForOwner(memberId, user.userId());
}
7. Should IDOR checks live in controllers, services, or repositories?

Controllers handle HTTP input, services enforce business authorization, and repository queries can constrain the data returned. Use defense in depth: the service decides policy and the query scopes the lookup. Avoid relying only on the UI or one controller check.

8. How do you prevent one tenant from accessing another tenant's records?

Take the tenant identity from authenticated server-side context, not a request parameter, and include it in every data query and mutation.

Repository pattern: Optional<MemberEntity> findByIdAndTenantId(UUID id, UUID tenantId); If it returns empty, treat the object as inaccessible or absent according to the API's disclosure policy.

9. Are UUIDs enough to prevent IDOR?

No. UUIDs make guessing harder but do not prove permission. IDs can be copied, exposed, or obtained through another endpoint. Keep UUIDs for identifier quality if useful, but always perform an authorization check.

10. Where can IDOR appear beyond a path ID?

It can appear in query parameters, request bodies, nested paths, GraphQL arguments, bulk ID arrays, export jobs, and indirect references such as flatId or membershipId. Audit all inputs that select an object, not just route variables.

11. How do you secure object IDs supplied in a request body?

Do not trust fields such as ownerId, tenantId, or role in the body. Bind only fields the caller may set, derive identity and ownership from the authenticated context, and validate referenced objects server-side before saving.

Fix: map a request DTO to an entity explicitly instead of binding client JSON directly to a persistence entity.

12. How do you handle ownership changes and shared resources?

Model the policy explicitly: owner, tenant membership, delegated role, or time-bounded share. Check the current relationship for every operation and define how revocation takes effect. Do not infer access from possession of a URL or object ID.

13. How do you prevent IDOR in file uploads and downloads?

Store file metadata with its owner or tenant and authorize that relationship before returning bytes or changing metadata. Use opaque storage keys only as internal identifiers; a signed URL should be short-lived and scoped to the intended file and action.

14. How do you test for IDOR?

Create two users/tenants and objects owned by each. Authenticate as user A, substitute user B's IDs across read, update, delete, nested, and bulk endpoints, and assert that no data or side effect is exposed. Also verify user A can access allowed records and anonymous requests are denied.

15. What negative authorization tests should you add?

Test wrong-owner, wrong-tenant, missing-object, forged owner fields, revoked sharing, bulk lists containing mixed-owner IDs, and membership IDs paired with the wrong member ID. Assert both the response and that the database was not modified.

Project example: membership operations should verify the membership belongs to the member in the URL; the current repository has a findByIdAndMemberId lookup for ending a membership.

16. How do you avoid leaking whether an unauthorized object exists?

Use consistent response bodies and avoid returning object details before authorization. Some APIs return 404 for both nonexistent and inaccessible objects to conceal existence; others use 403 when disclosure is acceptable. Choose deliberately and keep the policy consistent.

17. When should an API return 403 versus 404?

Return 403 when the caller is authenticated but explicitly lacks permission and revealing the object's existence is acceptable. Return 404 when the object is absent or the API intentionally hides whether it exists. Neither response replaces the authorization check.

18. How can database queries help enforce object-level authorization?

Scope the query by both object ID and authorized owner/tenant, so unauthorized rows are never loaded for accidental use.

// Illustrative repository method
Optional<Membership> findByIdAndMemberId(UUID membershipId, UUID memberId);

Membership membership = repository.findByIdAndMemberId(membershipId, memberId)
        .orElseThrow(MembershipNotFoundException::new);

For updates, use a similarly scoped update and verify the affected-row count.

19. How do you audit and monitor IDOR attempts?

Record denied access attempts with authenticated actor, route, action, and a safe object reference or hash. Avoid logging secrets or sensitive payloads. Alert on repeated cross-owner failures, but do not expose monitoring details to the caller.

20. How would you review this project for IDOR?

Enumerate every endpoint that accepts a member, flat, floor, or membership identifier; trace it through controller, service, and repository; then verify object-level policy and negative tests. Currently the APIs are ADMIN-only, and service methods enforce roles. If self-service users are added, introduce owner/tenant-scoped lookups instead of relying on the ADMIN role or UUID secrecy.

Interview answer: “I would test with two identities and swap every object ID across read and write operations. The server must authorize the specific object on every request, and failed authorization must not reveal data or cause side effects.”

Scenario questionsGateway sessions, CSRF, and service credentials

Gateway sessions, CSRF, and service credentials

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How do session cookies, CSRF protection, and internal credentials work together?

Answer: Gateway authenticates the user and stores the server-side session in Redis; the browser sends an HttpOnly session cookie. CSRF tokens protect cookie-authenticated state-changing requests from cross-site submission. A backend independently validates the session, while service credentials authenticate service-to-service calls and must not be treated as user identity.

Security fixes: use Secure/HttpOnly/SameSite cookie settings appropriate to deployment, rotate sessions after login, keep secrets in Secret storage, scope service credentials narrowly, and use TLS for untrusted networks.

Follow-up: How should internal service credentials be rotated?

Answer: Store credentials in a secret manager or Kubernetes Secret, support overlapping old and new credentials during rotation, update workloads, verify calls, then revoke the old value. Limit each credential to the required service and operation, and never log it.

Roles of NGINX, API Gateway and Redis

Why do we need NGINX, API Gateway and Redis?
Client --HTTPS--> NGINX --> Spring API Gateway
                                  |--> Member service --> member_db
                                  |--> Society service --> society_db
Redis: sessions, rate-limit buckets, membership locks
Redis cache: Society reference-data caching and load coordination
ComponentResponsibility in this repositoryEvidence
NGINXExternal HTTPS entrance, TLS termination, HTTP-to-HTTPS redirect, forwarding /api/ to Gateway, round-robin upstream balancing, body limits and proxy timeouts.kubernetes/nginx/gateway.yaml, httproutes.yaml, policies.yaml

Kubernetes separates redis with noeviction from redis-cache with allkeys-lru. Cache entries can be evicted without competing in the same Redis instance with sessions and membership coordination. This separation does not by itself provide high availability or durable recovery.

Interview answer: NGINX manages the external transport entrance, Gateway manages application entry controls, Redis supplies shared supporting state, and PostgreSQL persists business records. These describe source configuration; they do not verify the currently deployed versions.

Framework references: Spring WebFlux and MVC applicability, Spring Boot graceful shutdown, Gateway RequestRateLimiter.
