End-of-practice cleanup

ResourceAction and remaining cost
ECS FargateSet each service's desired task count to zero and prevent autoscaling from restarting tasks. Other infrastructure remains separately billable.
RDSStop the instance when supported. Storage and applicable backups remain billable; RDS automatically restarts after seven consecutive stopped days.
ElastiCacheDelete the cache to end cache charges and recreate it for the next exercise. Deleting sessions requires signing in again; recreate both caches if retaining the existing separation.
ALB, NAT gateways, chargeable endpoints and WAFDelete what is not needed between sessions. Closing the browser does not end resource charges.
EKSDelete the cluster and worker resources after the exercise. An empty retained cluster still incurs a control-plane fee.
S3, ECR, logs and snapshotsRetain only necessary data; storage can continue costing money.

Credits and budget expectations

Eligible usage across both repos consumes one shared account credit balance. Free-plan access ends after six months or credit exhaustion, whichever comes first; an ordinary upgrade to Paid permits remaining credits to be used until their expiry, normally twelve months from account creation. Paid-plan charges not covered by credits are payable. Service and feature access depends on the account plan.

Earlier monthly figures in the discussion were rough planning examples, not a priced or measured deployment. Exact cost requires the region, task memory/CPU, one versus two caches, database size, networking, retained resources and actual running hours. Low traffic alone does not stop hourly charges. Create a configuration-specific estimate before provisioning.

References: AWS Free Tier rules; RDS stop and restart behavior; ElastiCache cleanup; EKS pricing; AWS Pricing Calculator.

Read the deployment as a chain of resources

  1. Image

    A Docker image packages the application. Building an image does not by itself update a running Pod.

  2. Namespace, configuration, and credentials

    The namespace groups resources. ConfigMaps hold non-secret settings. Secrets supply private values referenced by the Deployments.

  3. Deployment and Pod

    The Deployment declares the Pod template and desired replica count. Pods receive labels, containers, environment variables, probes, and resource limits.

  4. Service and DNS

    A Service selects Pods by labels. Its stable DNS name is the application’s destination even when Pod IPs change.

  5. Gateway and optional edge

    The Spring Gateway routes business paths to backend Services. The NGINX Gateway API configuration provides the separate HTTPS edge when installed.

Why both replicas use the same database

Two Society Pods both serve the same Society data. Giving them unrelated databases would divide the application state unless a separate replication or partitioning design existed. Service replicas scale execution; data ownership still belongs to the service.

Health and shutdown

Startup probes allow initialization time. Readiness controls whether a Pod should receive traffic. Liveness decides when to restart a container. Avoid turning a temporary remote dependency failure into repeated restarts of otherwise functioning applications. The source configures graceful shutdown with a 20-second shutdown-phase timeout; compare that with Pod termination grace and actual request duration.

Scaling is a capacity change

Replica count, CPU and memory limits, HTTP concurrency, Redis capacity, and database pools are connected. An HPA manifest expresses a policy; it does not prove metrics-server is available or the policy is active. Verify the installed resources and the capacity of stateful dependencies before claiming automatic scalability.

Use versioned images for reviewable rollouts. A Deployment rollback changes a retained Pod template; it does not reverse database migrations or independently changed configuration.

Project questions · explanation and verification

Horizontal vs vertical scaling: which do we use?

Horizontal scaling adds instances; vertical scaling gives an instance more resources. Three Member Pods instead of one is horizontal scaling. Giving each Member Pod more CPU or memory is vertical scaling.

ComparisonHorizontal scalingVertical scaling
ChangeIncrease or decrease the number of Pods or application instances.Increase or decrease CPU and memory available to an instance.
ExampleMember Service: 1 Pod → 3 Pods behind the same Kubernetes Service.Member container: raise CPU/memory requests and limits to suit measured demand.
BenefitDistributes concurrent requests and can tolerate an instance failure when enough healthy replicas remain.Can help CPU-heavy work, memory pressure, or an application that cannot easily distribute work.
LimitShared database, Redis, and downstream capacity can become bottlenecks. More replicas do not automatically make one request faster.A larger instance still has a finite size; resource changes may require Pod replacement. One instance remains one failure domain.
Project supportOptional HPA manifests exist for Gateway, Member, and Society. Member HPA specifies 1–3 replicas and a 70% CPU-utilization target.Deployment resource requests and limits describe per-container capacity. No VPA manifest is being claimed here.

Important: an HPA YAML file proves configuration exists, not that autoscaling is installed and working. CPU utilization is relative to CPU requests, and resource metrics must be available. More Pods also multiply connection-pool demand.

How to verify the deployed setup

kubectl --context docker-desktop get deployments,hpa -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop describe hpa member-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop top pods -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop get deployment member-service -n emerald-housing -o yaml

Check ready replicas, HPA conditions/current metrics, and container requests/limits. top requires a working metrics source. In a controlled load exercise, compare replica count, throughput, latency, errors, and database saturation before and after load. Do not change replicas on an active shared environment just to demonstrate a definition.

Source: Member HPA and Member Deployment.
