Implementation references and scenarios

Implementation referenceFind services without memorizing Pod addresses

Find services without memorizing Pod addresses

Kubernetes DNS

A Pod can be replaced and get a different IP address, so Member Service must not save or call a particular Society Pod IP. Instead, Member Service calls a Kubernetes Service name that stays the same while the Pods behind it can change.

On your PC:        http://localhost:8081
Inside Kubernetes: http://society-service:8080

There is no project file that creates a DNS record. Kubernetes cluster DNS (normally CoreDNS) automatically makes a DNS name for each Kubernetes Service. The project YAML creates the Namespace, Deployment and Service that Kubernetes DNS uses.

  1. Create the namespace: kubernetes/namespace.yaml declares emerald-housing, the project area in the cluster.
  2. Create labeled Society Pods: kubernetes/society/deployment.yaml asks for two replicas and puts app: society-service on each Pod.
  3. Create the stable Service: kubernetes/society/service.yaml names the Service society-service and selects Pods with app: society-service. It accepts traffic on port 8080.
  4. Kubernetes provides DNS: the Service file kubernetes/society/service.yaml declares metadata.name: society-service and metadata.namespace: emerald-housing. The cluster DNS service, usually CoreDNS, automatically uses that Service name and namespace to provide society-service.emerald-housing.svc.cluster.local. Its parts are: society-service is the Service name; emerald-housing is the namespace; svc marks the Kubernetes Service DNS area; cluster.local is the cluster DNS domain, commonly the default. There is no DNS YAML file to create.
  5. Configure Member to call it: kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml sets SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL to http://society-service:8080. kubernetes/member/deployment.yaml loads that ConfigMap with envFrom. The Member app's application.yml reads the variable for its RestClient; locally its fallback is http://localhost:8081.
  6. Route to ready Pods: Kubernetes matches the Service selector to Society Pod labels and sends traffic to ready endpoints. When a Pod is replaced, the Service name stays unchanged; its endpoints update.
In short: the Service's metadata.name provides the name; cluster DNS resolves it; its spec.selector finds matching Pod labels; the Deployment creates those Pods; the ConfigMap gives Member Service the URL. Eureka is not used.
Implementation referenceResource creation order and connections

Resource creation order and connections

One complete sequence

Follow this order for the repository's local kind setup. PostgreSQL, Docker, kind, and kubectl must be ready first. The databases must already exist before the backend applications start: Flyway creates tables inside them, not the databases themselves.

  1. Prepare PostgreSQL: start PostgreSQL and create society_db and member_db. Make sure the credentials you will place in the Kubernetes Secret can connect.
  2. Start the kind cluster: run scripts/kubernetes/prepare-kind.ps1. It uses kubernetes/kind-config.yaml. The cluster must exist before image loading because the image-loading script transfers images into the kind nodes.
  3. Create the namespace: apply kubernetes/namespace.yaml. It creates the emerald-housing area inside the cluster.
  4. Create the database Secret: run the documented kubectl create secret command in namespace emerald-housing. It creates emerald-db-credentials; this Secret is created as a Kubernetes object, not from a committed Secret YAML file.
  5. Build and load Society and Member images: run scripts/kubernetes/build-load-images.ps1. This makes the backend images available to the kind cluster.
  6. Apply the backend ConfigMap: apply kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml. It creates emerald-app-config with non-secret settings such as POSTGRES_HOST and SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL.
  7. Apply backend Deployments and Services: apply the YAML resources under kubernetes/society/ and kubernetes/member/. Deployments request Society 2 replicas and Member 1 replica. Services select their Pods using matching labels. Once the namespace and configuration exist, each Deployment and its Service may be applied in either order.
  8. Kubernetes starts the backend containers: each Deployment creates or updates a ReplicaSet; the ReplicaSet creates Pods; the node's kubelet and container runtime start containers from the images. The Dockerfiles run the packaged applications with java -jar /app/app.jar.
  9. Spring Boot loads application settings: Society reads services/society-service/src/main/resources/application.yml; Member reads services/member-service/src/main/resources/application.yml. Kubernetes supplies ConfigMap values through envFrom and each service's database credentials through secretKeyRef.
  10. Flyway runs during backend application startup: Society connects to society_db and reads services/society-service/src/main/resources/db/migration/V1__create_society_tables.sql, followed by V2__seed_society_and_floors.sql. Member connects to member_db and reads services/member-service/src/main/resources/db/migration/V1__create_member_tables.sql. Flyway checks each database's flyway_schema_history and applies pending migrations. It does not run for each API request.
  11. Hibernate validates each backend schema: after migrations, Hibernate checks that the database tables match the Java mappings. Society and Member own separate databases. Both Society Pods use the same society_db; extra Pods do not create extra databases.
  12. Wait for backend readiness: startup and readiness probes check the applications. When a Pod is ready, its backend Service can route normal traffic to it. A running Pod that has not passed readiness should not receive normal Service traffic.
  13. Build and load the Gateway image: run scripts/kubernetes/build-load-images.ps1 -IncludeGateway after the backend services are ready. This option builds and loads the Gateway image as well as rebuilding and reloading the two backend images with their configured tags. Loading an image does not restart Pods that are already running; a Deployment rollout needs a change to the Deployment Pod template.
  14. Apply the Gateway ConfigMap: apply kubernetes/gateway/config.yaml. It creates api-gateway-config, which the Gateway Deployment reads.
  15. Apply Gateway Deployment and Service: apply the YAML resources under kubernetes/gateway/. The Deployment requests two Gateway Pods. Gateway starts as a separate Spring Boot application and does not connect to a database or run Flyway.
  16. Wait for Gateway readiness: after its readiness probe succeeds, the api-gateway Service can send requests to ready Gateway Pods.
  17. Apply the NetworkPolicies: apply kubernetes/networkpolicies.yaml after the namespace and workloads exist, before the traffic checks. It allows Gateway to reach Member and Society, and Member to reach Society on TCP port 8080. These policies limit incoming traffic only; they do not restrict application egress to PostgreSQL.
  18. Give Postman a way to reach Gateway: the current Gateway Service is ClusterIP, which is private inside the cluster. For local Postman usage, follow the manual Kubernetes checklist and its port-forward step.
  19. Send requests through Gateway: Gateway reads the path rules in infrastructure/api-gateway/src/main/resources/application.yml and forwards Member paths to member-service, and Society, floor, and flat paths to society-service. These Kubernetes Services select ready backend Pods.
  20. For a flat assignment: Member Service uses RestClient over HTTP to call the stable society-service name. Society checks society_db; if the flat is valid, Member saves the membership in member_db.
PostgreSQL databases exist first
        |
kind cluster -> namespace -> Secret + ConfigMaps
        |
backend images -> Deployments -> ReplicaSets -> Pods start
        |
Spring Boot -> Flyway migrations -> Hibernate schema validation
        |
readiness passes -> backend Services have ready endpoints
        |
Gateway image -> Gateway ConfigMap -> Gateway Deployment/Service -> readiness
        |
Postman -> Gateway route -> backend Service -> backend Pod -> service-owned database
Remember the roles: A Deployment creates and maintains application Pods. A Service does not create Pods; it selects matching ready Pod labels and provides a stable name. Kubernetes DNS automatically resolves Service names inside the cluster. The detailed field explanations are in the Kubernetes YAML property guide; DNS details are in Find services without memorizing Pod addresses.
Implementation referenceRead the YAML property by property

Read the YAML property by property

What it says, why it exists

These are Kubernetes instructions written as YAML. The actual files in this repository use the .yaml extension. Kubernetes reads these fields when you apply the files to the cluster. A field only has an effect when it is under the correct parent such as metadata or spec.

Four different jobs: Namespace groups project resources. Deployment creates and maintains Pods. Service gives selected Pods a stable network name. NetworkPolicy limits which Pods may connect to selected Pods.

1. Namespace: kubernetes/namespace.yaml

apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: emerald-housing
  labels:
    app.kubernetes.io/part-of: emerald-housing
PropertyCurrent valuePurpose and reason
apiVersionv1Selects Kubernetes' core API group and version. Namespace is a built-in core resource.
kindNamespaceTells Kubernetes what kind of object this YAML describes. This file creates a Namespace, not an application Pod.
metadata.nameemerald-housingThe namespace name. Society, Member, Gateway, ConfigMaps, and the database Secret use this same namespace so they can be grouped and communicate by short Service names.
metadata.labelsapp.kubernetes.io/part-of: emerald-housingAdds searchable descriptive metadata saying this namespace belongs to the Emerald Housing project. It does not create Pods or route traffic.

Apply this before creating namespaced resources. A Namespace is a cluster-level object; it does not have its own metadata.namespace.

2. Deployment: Society, Member, and Gateway

Deployment files:

  • kubernetes/society/deployment.yaml
  • kubernetes/member/deployment.yaml
  • kubernetes/gateway/deployment.yaml

They share the same main structure. Values below are shown per application where they differ.

Property pathCurrent value in this repoPurpose and reason
apiVersionapps/v1Uses the stable Kubernetes Apps API for Deployments.
kindDeploymentDeclares a desired application state. Kubernetes controllers keep trying to make actual Pods match it.
metadata.namesociety-service, member-service, or api-gatewayNames the Deployment object. This is an object name; network callers use the separate Service object name.
metadata.namespaceemerald-housingPlaces each Deployment in the project namespace.
metadata.labelsapp.kubernetes.io/name: ...Labels the Deployment object for organization and selection by management tools. The Pod labels are separately declared under template.metadata.labels.
spec.replicasSociety 2; Member 1; Gateway 2How many Pods Kubernetes should maintain. It is a desired count, not a promise that every Pod is always healthy.
spec.revisionHistoryLimit3Retains a small number of old ReplicaSets so previous Deployment revisions can be inspected or rolled back.
spec.strategy.typeRollingUpdateReplaces application versions gradually instead of deleting every old Pod at once.
rollingUpdate.maxUnavailable0During an update, do not intentionally take an existing desired Pod below the available count. Scheduling limits can still prevent an update from progressing.
rollingUpdate.maxSurge1Allows Kubernetes to create up to one extra Pod above the desired count while updating, subject to available resources.
spec.selector.matchLabelsapp: society-service, app: member-service, or app: api-gatewayTells the Deployment which Pods it owns. The value must match the labels in its Pod template.
spec.template.metadata.labelsSame app value as the selector, plus app.kubernetes.io/nameThese labels are copied onto every Pod. The Kubernetes Service uses the app label to find its backend Pods.
spec.template.specPod configurationEverything below this path describes how to create each Pod, including containers, security, probes, resources, and temporary storage.
terminationGracePeriodSeconds30Gives the application time to stop gracefully when Kubernetes replaces or removes the Pod.

Pod security settings

PropertyCurrent valuePurpose and reason
Pod securityContext.runAsNonRoottrueAsks Kubernetes to prevent the Pod process from running as the root user.
Pod runAsUser / runAsGroup10001 / 10001Sets the numeric Linux user and group used by the app container.
seccompProfile.typeRuntimeDefaultUses the container runtime's default syscall filter profile as an extra security boundary.
Container allowPrivilegeEscalationfalsePrevents the process from gaining more privileges than it started with.
readOnlyRootFilesystemtrueMakes the container's root filesystem read-only, reducing places an attacker or app can alter. The configured /tmp volume remains writable.
capabilities.drop["ALL"]Removes extra Linux capabilities that these applications do not need.

Container image, ports, and configuration

PropertyCurrent valuesPurpose and reason
containers[].namesociety-service, member-service, api-gatewayNames a container within its Pod. The Pod can contain one or more containers.
containers[].imageemerald/society-service:step4-audit; emerald/member-service:restclient-fix; emerald/api-gateway:step6-auditSelects the packaged application image and tag to run. The local kind preparation script builds and loads these images.
imagePullPolicyIfNotPresentUses the image already on the node when present; otherwise Kubernetes may try to pull it from a registry.
ports[].namehttpGives the container port a name. The Services and health probes use this name as targetPort or probe port.
ports[].containerPort8080Documents the application listening port inside each container. This is not itself a public exposure rule.
envFrom.configMapRef.nameBackend: emerald-app-config; Gateway: api-gateway-configCopies ConfigMap key/value entries into the container environment. Backend ConfigMap is kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml; Gateway ConfigMap is kubernetes/gateway/config.yaml.
env[].nameNames such as INSTANCE_ID, SOCIETY_DB_USERNAME, or MEMBER_DB_PASSWORDThe environment variable name that Spring or the application reads. The exact name must match the placeholder or configuration lookup in the application.
env[].valueMember timeouts: 2s connect, 3s readSets explicit environment values. Member's RestClient uses them to limit waiting on Society Service.
env[].valueFrom.fieldRef.fieldPathSociety metadata.name for INSTANCE_IDUses the Kubernetes Downward API to provide the running Pod's name as its instance identifier, without hardcoding a Pod name.
env[].valueFrom.secretKeyRef.name and keySecret emerald-db-credentials; Society keys society-username/society-password; Member keys member-username/member-passwordMaps only that service's database credentials to its DB username/password environment variables. Gateway has no database credentials.

Startup, readiness, and liveness probes

PropertyCurrent valuePurpose and reason
startupProbe.httpGet.path/actuator/health/livenessChecks that the Spring process has started. Startup probe gives slower-starting applications time before liveness checking begins.
readinessProbe.httpGet.path/actuator/health/readinessChecks whether the Pod should receive normal traffic. The backend readiness health group includes database health, so an unavailable DB prevents the backend Pod becoming ready.
livenessProbe.httpGet.path/actuator/health/livenessChecks whether the application process should be restarted. It is separate from readiness so a database outage need not automatically restart the app.
Probe porthttpUses the named container port declared above, rather than repeating the number.
periodSecondsStartup/readiness: 5; liveness: 10How often Kubernetes repeats the probe after it becomes active.
timeoutSeconds2How long one probe request may take before it counts as a failure.
failureThresholdSociety/Member startup: 36; Gateway startup: 24; readiness/liveness: 3How many consecutive failures trigger the probe's action. A failed readiness probe removes the Pod from normal Service traffic; failed startup/liveness can cause restart behavior.

Resources and temporary storage

PropertySociety / MemberGatewayPurpose and reason
resources.requests.cpu100m100mCPU scheduling request; 100m means one tenth of a CPU core.
resources.requests.memory256Mi192MiMemory Kubernetes uses when scheduling the Pod.
resources.limits.cpu750m750mCPU usage limit configured for the container.
resources.limits.memory640Mi512MiMemory ceiling; exceeding it can cause the container to be terminated for memory use.
volumeMounts[].nametmptmpConnects the container mount to a Pod volume with the same name.
volumeMounts[].mountPath/tmp/tmpProvides a writable temporary directory despite the read-only container root filesystem.
volumes[].nametmptmpNames the Pod volume. It must match volumeMounts[].name so this storage is mounted into the container.
volumes[].emptyDir{}{}Creates temporary storage for the life of that Pod. It is not persistent database or member storage.

3. Service: stable address and Pod selection

Service files: kubernetes/society/service.yaml, kubernetes/member/service.yaml, and kubernetes/gateway/service.yaml.

PropertyCurrent valuePurpose and reason
apiVersionv1Service is a Kubernetes core API resource.
kindServiceDeclares a stable network endpoint in front of matching Pods.
metadata.namesociety-service, member-service, or api-gatewayNames the Service. Kubernetes DNS makes the name resolvable inside the cluster; for example, Member calls http://society-service:8080.
metadata.namespaceemerald-housingPlaces the Service in the same namespace as its Pods.
metadata.labelsapp.kubernetes.io/name: ...Descriptive labels for the Service resource itself; they are distinct from its Pod selector.
spec.typeClusterIPCreates a private in-cluster Service address. It does not make backend services public.
spec.selectorapp: society-service, app: member-service, or app: api-gatewayFinds Pods whose labels match. This must match spec.template.metadata.labels in the corresponding Deployment.
ports[].namehttpNames this Service port; useful for readable configuration.
ports[].port8080Port callers use on the Service.
ports[].targetPorthttpForwards to the container port named http in the selected Pod (container port 8080).
ports[].protocolTCPUses TCP for the HTTP connection.
Caller uses Service name: society-service:8080
        ↓
Service selector: app=society-service
        ↓
Matches Pod template label: app=society-service
        ↓
Service targetPort: http
        ↓
Container named port: http, containerPort 8080

If the selector and Pod label do not match, the Service can exist but have no ready endpoints.

4. NetworkPolicy: allowed incoming app traffic

File: kubernetes/networkpolicies.yaml. It contains two NetworkPolicy documents separated by ---.

PropertyCurrent valuePurpose and reason
apiVersionnetworking.k8s.io/v1Selects the stable Kubernetes networking API group for NetworkPolicy.
kindNetworkPolicyDeclares rules for allowed network traffic to or from selected Pods.
metadata.namesociety-ingress-from-applications or member-ingress-from-gatewayNames each policy so it can be inspected and managed independently.
metadata.namespaceemerald-housingPolicies apply to Pods in this namespace.
spec.podSelector.matchLabelsSociety policy selects app: society-service; Member policy selects app: member-serviceSelects the destination Pods whose incoming traffic the policy controls.
spec.policyTypes[Ingress]This policy controls incoming traffic only. It does not define egress restrictions; outbound traffic remains governed by the cluster's default policy behavior and any other policies.
spec.ingressIngress rules for selected destination PodsDefines incoming traffic rules. Each rule combines allowed sources and ports.
spec.ingress[].from[].podSelector.matchLabelsSociety allows member-service and api-gateway; Member allows api-gatewayAllows incoming traffic from Pods with those labels. Because there is no namespaceSelector, these selectors refer to Pods in this policy's namespace.
spec.ingress[].ports[].protocolTCPAllows the specified application traffic over TCP.
spec.ingress[].ports[].port8080Limits the allowed incoming application traffic to port 8080.

What these policies allow: Gateway to Member on TCP 8080; Gateway to Society on TCP 8080; and Member to Society on TCP 8080. The second direction is needed for Member's RestClient flat check.

Important limit: A NetworkPolicy YAML object only enforces traffic rules if the cluster's network plugin supports NetworkPolicy. These files select Society and Member ingress; they do not create a public Gateway entry point, and they do not add egress restrictions.

How the four files connect

namespace.yaml creates emerald-housing
        ↓
Deployment creates Pods with app labels
        ↓ labels match
Service selector finds ready Pods and gives a stable name
        ↓
NetworkPolicy decides which labeled app Pods may connect to Society/Member
        ↓
ConfigMap and Secret provide runtime settings; application.yml uses them
        ↓
Spring starts; backend Flyway migrates its own database; readiness allows traffic

For the actual apply order and request path, see Resource creation order and connections. For the interactive cluster-to-file walkthrough, see Kubernetes configuration walkthrough.

Implementation referenceKeep more than one copy running

Keep more than one copy running

Scale and self-heal

A replica means one running copy. Society's Deployment requests two copies. Its Kubernetes Service sends traffic to ready copies. The caller still uses the same service name.

If one Pod stops, Kubernetes tries to replace it to reach the requested count. This is self-healing, but it does not promise every request succeeds during a failure.

The X-Instance-ID header can help show which copy answered. Calls may not alternate evenly because HTTP connections can be reused. To request three copies: kubectl scale deployment society-service --replicas=3 -n emerald-housing.

Readiness means a Pod should receive requests. Liveness means the process still appears healthy enough to keep running.

Implementation referenceUse Spring Cloud Gateway

Use Spring Cloud Gateway

Routes by URL

Postman normally needs different addresses for different apps. A Gateway provides one address. It looks at the request path and forwards it to the correct service.

Member paths such as /api/v1/members go to Member Service. Society, floor and flat paths go to Society Service. Actual routes are in infrastructure/api-gateway/src/main/resources/application.yml, lines 11–21.

The Gateway forwards calls and checks authenticated ADMIN sessions in Phase 7. Business rules still belong in the backend services, which also validate identity and authorization.

Implementation referenceDocker Desktop Kubernetes: click-through infrastructure file guide

Docker Desktop Kubernetes: click-through infrastructure file guide

Option B · read in order

This guide follows the environment you started: Docker Desktop context → namespace → Deployments and Services → settings and dependencies → deployment and optional layers. Click a numbered diagram box or a step link to open its explanation. Each explanation links to the actual repository files.

Current Option B scope: It targets docker-desktop, applies the namespace, app configuration, both Redis folders, Society and Member folders, and Gateway files. It does not apply all of kubernetes/kustomization.yaml: NGINX routes, NetworkPolicies, and HPAs listed there are not applied by the Option B script. The active local entry is the Gateway port-forward at http://localhost:8080.
Step 1: ClusterContext and Kind-only filesStep 2: NamespaceResource grouping and KustomizeStep 3: WorkloadsEvery application Deployment and ServiceStep 4: ConfigurationConfigMaps, Secrets, Redis, PostgreSQLStep 5: DeploymentOption B and optional layers
1. Cluster context: Docker Desktop is the cluster Option B uses

Run kubectl config current-context to see the default context and kubectl --context docker-desktop get nodes to query Docker Desktop explicitly. A context is the kubectl connection choice; it is not a namespace.

scripts/kubernetes/deploy-option-b.ps1: param defines startup options; $clusterContext = 'docker-desktop' fixes the target; Invoke-Kube adds that context to each kubectl command and stops on errors. This is the script used by Option B.

kubernetes/kind-config.yaml: kind: Cluster declares a Kind cluster; nodes lists one control-plane and one worker. scripts/kubernetes/prepare-kind.ps1 checks Kind, creates that separate cluster if missing, then checks its kind-emerald-housing context. These are not used by the Docker Desktop Option B path; skip them for now.

Connection: kubectl context → cluster → namespace → namespaced resources.

2. Namespace and resource index: where objects live and what is declared

kubernetes/namespace.yaml, line by line by field: apiVersion: v1 selects the core API; kind: Namespace selects the object type; metadata.name creates emerald-housing; metadata.labels tags it as part of this project.

kubernetes/kustomization.yaml: apiVersion/kind: Kustomization identify the Kustomize file; every path under resources adds YAML files or folders to the base set. The list includes app ConfigMaps, Deployments, Services, Redis, NetworkPolicies, NGINX routes/policies, and HPAs. Kustomize combines declarations; it does not create a cluster.

Declared versus active: Option B applies resources individually. It does not execute kubectl apply -k kubernetes, so entries in the base list do not automatically mean they run in your current Option B cluster.

Connection: namespace YAML names the destination; Kustomize is the base resource index; the Option B script determines its actual apply commands.

3. Workloads and Services: read every application file and how it links

metadata.name, selector labels, and container names can all be different.

The required match is: every label in spec.selector.matchLabels must appear with the same value in spec.template.metadata.labels. The Pod template can have additional labels.

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: member-deployment       # Deployment name
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: member              # Must match the Pod label below
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: member
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: member-container # Independent container name
          image: member-service:1.0
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8080

Here, member-deployment, member, and member-container are valid different names. The selector tells the Deployment which Pods it manages.

A Deployment creates and manages Pods. A Service provides a stable IP and DNS name to reach those Pods.

They connect through Pod labels and the Service selector.

# deployment.yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: member-deployment
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: member
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: member             # Label applied to each Pod
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: member-container
          image: member-service:1.0
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8080
# service.yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: member-service          # Becomes the Service DNS name
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  type: ClusterIP
  selector:
    app: member                 # Finds Pods with this label
  ports:
    - port: 80                  # Port callers use
      targetPort: 8080          # Port the application listens on

DNS lets callers use a name instead of remembering the Service IP. Kubernetes cluster DNS resolves member-service to this Service’s ClusterIP.

From a Pod in the same namespace, call:

http://member-service:80

From a Pod in another namespace, include the Service’s namespace:

http://member-service.emerald-housing:80

With the usual cluster.local cluster domain, the full DNS name is:

member-service.emerald-housing.svc.cluster.local

The request flows like this:

1. DNS lookup:
   member-service → Service ClusterIP

2. Request:
   Caller → Service ClusterIP:80 → a matching ready Pod:8080

The roles are:

  • Deployment: creates, replaces, and scales Pods.
  • Pod labels: identify the Pods.
  • Service selector: selects Pods with matching labels in its namespace.
  • Service: provides a stable ClusterIP and routes traffic to selected Pods.
  • DNS: resolves the Service name to its ClusterIP.

The Service does not reference the Deployment name. It finds Pods through labels. When Pods are replaced and their IPs change, callers continue using the same Service DNS name.

Common Service fields: kind: Service, metadata name/namespace, spec.type: ClusterIP, selector matching Pod labels, and ports.port/targetPort map stable Service traffic to the container port. Deployment starts Pods; Service finds matching ready Pods.

FileWhat its lines configureFile connections
gateway/deployment.yamlImage, 8080 port, imported Gateway ConfigMap, token Secret refs, probes, CPU/memory bounds, non-root security and temporary /tmp volume.gateway/config.yaml; gateway/service.yaml; security Secret.
gateway/service.yamlStable ClusterIP name api-gateway, matching Pod selector, port 8080 to named http port.Option B port-forward; other apps/edge route can call Gateway.
member/deployment.yamlImage, shared ConfigMap, Society timeouts, database/internal-token Secret refs, probes, resource bounds, non-root security and /tmp.member/service.yaml; member DB; Society URL; Redis lock dependency.
member/service.yamlStable ClusterIP name member-service, selector app: member-service, port 8080.Gateway config sends Member API paths here; Society calls remain direct.
society/deployment.yamlImage, shared ConfigMap, Pod-name INSTANCE_ID, database/token/cache Secret refs, probes, resource bounds, non-root security and /tmp.society/service.yaml; society DB; redis-cache.
society/service.yamlStable ClusterIP name society-service, selector app: society-service, port 8080.Gateway routes Society paths here; Member calls it for flat validation.

These Deployment files omit a replica count, so Kubernetes defaults to one unless a HorizontalPodAutoscaler changes it. Option B does not apply the HPA files. Readiness decides when a Pod receives traffic; liveness/startup probes help detect or wait for startup. The probes use Actuator endpoints.

Connection: Deployment label selector ↔ Pod labels ↔ Service selector; callers use stable Service DNS, not Pod IPs.

4. Configuration, Secrets, Redis and PostgreSQL: how apps find dependencies

kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml is the shared non-secret ConfigMap. apiVersion/kind identify a ConfigMap; metadata names emerald-app-config; entries in data become environment settings. Key lines set POSTGRES_HOST=host.docker.internal, SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL=http://society-service:8080, REDIS_HOST=redis, and cache Redis to redis-cache. The remaining lines tune connection pools, cache TTLs, timeouts, retry and circuit-breaker values, server ports, and tracing.

kubernetes/gateway/config.yaml is a Gateway-only ConfigMap. Its data maps Member/Society URL variables to Kubernetes DNS names; it also configures credential lookup, Redis, session cookie/timeout, connection timeouts, and rate limits. The Gateway Deployment imports it with envFrom.

Secret fields in each Deployment use valueFrom.secretKeyRef: name selects a Secret object and key selects one value. emerald-db-credentials supplies DB usernames/passwords; emerald-security-credentials supplies Redis passwords and internal credentials. Option B preserves existing Secrets and generates its security Secret only when missing; secret values are not kept in these YAML files.

redis/deployment.yaml runs Redis 7.4 with password auth, 128 MB cap, noeviction, probes, bounded resources, and ephemeral emptyDir; redis/service.yaml exposes stable DNS redis:6379 for sessions, rate limits and locks. redis-cache/deployment.yaml runs a distinct password-protected Redis with allkeys-lru; redis-cache/service.yaml exposes redis-cache:6379 for Society caching. emptyDir is not durable storage.

PostgreSQL is not a Pod created by this folder. Each service has its own configured database URL/database name and credentials, and uses Flyway after connecting. The host config and DB Secret connect to existing PostgreSQL databases outside these app resources.

Connection: Deployment envFrom → ConfigMap; secretKeyRef → Secret; Service DNS → app/Redis; POSTGRES_HOST + DB Secret → external PostgreSQL.

5. Deployment and optional resources: Docker, Option B, NGINX, policies, HPA

scripts/kubernetes/deploy-option-b.ps1 is Option B’s orchestrator. Its parameters select database credential input, image building, infrastructure-only, or verify-only mode. It sets Docker Desktop context and namespace; checks/preserves Secrets; optionally builds three uniquely tagged images; applies app config and Redis; waits for Redis; applies Society, Member, Gateway config/Deployment/Service; sets local cookie security; updates image tags; waits for rollout; and prints a Gateway port-forward command. With -BuildImages it updates Deployments to the new image tag; otherwise it restarts the existing images. It does not itself start the port-forward. Your current running forward makes http://localhost:8080 available.

Each of infrastructure/api-gateway/Dockerfile, services/member-service/Dockerfile, and services/society-service/Dockerfile packages one Java app into an image. scripts/kubernetes/build-load-images.ps1 its parameters set service/Gateway tags and optional Gateway build; its loop runs docker build for each source context, then kind load docker-image into Kind. It belongs to the separate Kind workflow, not Option B.

kubernetes/networkpolicies.yaml contains NetworkPolicy objects: each podSelector chooses protected Pods; ingress from selectors choose allowed callers; ports limits the accepted traffic. It is listed in base Kustomize but not applied by Option B.

Files in kubernetes/nginx/: GatewayClass names the controller; Gateway declares HTTP/HTTPS listeners and TLS Secret; HTTPRoutes redirect HTTP and map HTTPS /api/ to api-gateway; policies tune proxy/client behavior; Helm values configure controller watches. The NGINX controller and Gateway API CRDs install separately. Option B does not apply these manifests and uses port-forward.

Files in kubernetes/autoscaling/ define HPAs: scaleTargetRef picks a Deployment, CPU utilization is the metric, and min/max replicas bound scaling (Gateway/Society 2–4; Member 1–3). An available metrics API is required. These are listed in base Kustomize but not applied by Option B.

kubernetes-observability/kustomization.yaml is an optional overlay: it includes base resources plus Prometheus, Grafana, Loki, Tempo and Alloy and enables tracing. Option B does not apply this overlay.

Exact files in the optional groups

nginx/gatewayclass.yaml binds Gateway API to the NGINX controller name. nginx/gateway.yaml declares the edge listeners. nginx/httproutes.yaml contains the HTTP redirect and HTTPS API backend match. nginx/policies.yaml tunes NGINX upstream balancing and timeout/body settings. nginx/helm-values.yaml limits the separately installed controller to watch the app namespace.

gateway-hpa.yaml targets api-gateway (2–4 replicas); member-hpa.yaml targets member-service (1–3); society-hpa.yaml targets society-service (2–4). In each, apiVersion/kind identify HPA v2, metadata names it, scaleTargetRef selects a Deployment, metrics describes the CPU target, and behavior controls scale-up/down stabilization and rates.

The observability overlay references prometheus.yaml (metrics collection), grafana.yaml (dashboards), loki.yaml (logs), tempo.yaml (traces), and alloy.yaml (collects/forwards telemetry). They are included by its Kustomization overlay, not the Option B script.

Manual click order: use this diagram 1→5; in each opened panel click the linked file; read its fields in the order described; follow the “Connection” sentence to the next panel. For live state, run kubectl --context docker-desktop get deployments,pods,services -n emerald-housing.

Implementation referenceKubernetes configuration walkthrough

Kubernetes configuration walkthrough

Follow one layer at a time

What is autoscaling?

Autoscaling means automatically increasing or decreasing resources based on application demand.

In Kubernetes, Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) adjusts the number of Pods managed by a Deployment.

For example:

apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata:
  name: member-hpa
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: member-deployment     # Deployment to scale
  minReplicas: 2                # Keep at least 2 Pods
  maxReplicas: 5                # Allow up to 5 Pods
  metrics:
    - type: Resource
      resource:
        name: cpu
        target:
          type: Utilization
          averageUtilization: 70

Here, HPA aims to keep average CPU usage across Pods around 70% of their requested CPU. For example, if each Pod requests 100m CPU, 70% means 70m per Pod on average.

Traffic increases → CPU usage rises → HPA increases Pods
Traffic decreases → CPU usage falls → HPA decreases Pods

Minimum: 2 Pods
Maximum: 5 Pods

Scaling happens periodically and includes stabilization rules, so it is not instantaneous.

The components work together like this:

HPA → adjusts Deployment replica count
Deployment → creates or removes Pods
Service → routes traffic to matching ready Pods
DNS → keeps the same Service name for callers

HPA needs a metrics source, commonly Metrics Server for CPU and memory, and CPU requests configured on the relevant containers for this CPU utilization target to work.

What is ConfigMap?

A ConfigMap stores non-secret application configuration in Kubernetes as key-value pairs.

It keeps settings separate from the application image, so you can use the same image with different configuration.

# configmap.yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: member-config
  namespace: emerald-housing
data:
  SERVER_PORT: "8080"
  SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL: "http://society-service:8080"
  LOGGING_LEVEL_ROOT: "INFO"

A Deployment can load these values into its containers as environment variables:

# Inside deployment.yaml
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: member-container
          image: member-service:1.0
          envFrom:
            - configMapRef:
                name: member-config  # Matches ConfigMap metadata.name

The application then receives:

SERVER_PORT=8080
SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL=http://society-service:8080
LOGGING_LEVEL_ROOT=INFO

The connection is:

ConfigMap: member-config
          ↓ referenced by envFrom.configMapRef.name
Deployment's Pod template
          ↓
Container environment variables
          ↓
Application reads the settings
  • ConfigMap name: must match the Deployment’s configMapRef.name.
  • Namespace: the ConfigMap and the Pod using it must be in the same namespace.
  • Values: entries under data must be strings; quote numbers and booleans in YAML.
  • Sensitive settings: use a Secret for passwords, tokens, and API keys.

ConfigMaps can also supply files mounted into containers through volumes.

Updating a ConfigMap does not automatically update environment variables in existing containers. Replace or restart the Pods to load the new values.

What is kind: Deployment?

kind: Deployment tells Kubernetes that this YAML defines a Deployment resource. A Deployment manages application Pods and keeps the desired number running.

apiVersion: apps/v1           # API group and version
kind: Deployment              # Resource type to create
metadata:
  name: member-deployment      # Deployment name
spec:
  replicas: 2                 # Desired number of Pods
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: member
  template:                   # Blueprint for creating Pods
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: member
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: member-container
          image: member-service:1.0
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8080

Here, Kubernetes reads:

kind: Deployment → Create a Deployment
replicas: 2      → Keep 2 Pods running
template         → Use this blueprint to create the Pods

A Deployment uses a ReplicaSet to maintain the Pod count:

Deployment → ReplicaSet → Pods
  • Pod replacement: if a Pod fails or is deleted, its ReplicaSet creates a replacement.
  • Scaling: changing replicas increases or decreases the Pod count.
  • Rolling updates: changing the Pod template, such as the container image, triggers a rollout that gradually replaces Pods by default.
  • Rollback: you can return to an earlier retained Deployment revision.

kind identifies the resource type. Other examples include kind: Service, kind: ConfigMap, and kind: Secret.

What is kind: Service?

kind: Service tells Kubernetes that this YAML defines a Service resource. A Service provides a stable way to reach a group of Pods, even when those Pods are replaced and their IP addresses change.

apiVersion: v1                # Core API version
kind: Service                 # Resource type to create
metadata:
  name: member-service        # Service name used in DNS
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  type: ClusterIP             # Exposes the Service inside the cluster
  selector:
    app: member               # Selects Pods with this label
  ports:
    - port: 80                # Port callers use on the Service
      targetPort: 8080        # Port the application listens on

Here, Kubernetes reads:

kind: Service → Create a Service
type: ClusterIP → Assign an internal cluster IP
selector: app: member → Find Pods labeled app: member
port: 80 → Accept Service traffic on port 80
targetPort: 8080 → Forward traffic to Pod port 8080

A Pod in the same namespace can call:

http://member-service:80

The connection works like this:

DNS resolves member-service → Service ClusterIP

Caller → Service ClusterIP:80 → a matching ready Pod:8080
  • Stable address: the Service IP and DNS name remain stable while the Service exists.
  • Pod selection: the selector matches Pod labels in the Service’s namespace.
  • Traffic distribution: traffic is directed to matching ready Pods.
  • Deployment relationship: the Service selects Pods directly; it does not reference the Deployment name.

Common Service types are:

TypePurpose
ClusterIPExposes the Service inside the cluster; the default type.
NodePortAlso exposes the Service on a port on each node.
LoadBalancerRequests an external load balancer from a supported infrastructure provider or implementation.
ExternalNameReturns a DNS alias for another hostname, without selecting or proxying traffic to Pods.

A Deployment manages Pods; a Service makes them reachable through a stable address.

What is kind: NetworkPolicy?

kind: NetworkPolicy tells Kubernetes that this YAML defines a NetworkPolicy resource. A NetworkPolicy controls which network traffic can reach selected Pods or leave them.

apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1 # API group and version
kind: NetworkPolicy            # Resource type to create
metadata:
  name: allow-gateway-to-member
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: member              # Pods this policy protects
  policyTypes:
    - Ingress                  # Controls incoming traffic
  ingress:
    - from:
        - podSelector:
            matchLabels:
              app: api-gateway # Allow callers with this Pod label
      ports:
        - protocol: TCP
          port: 8080           # Allowed destination Pod port

Here, Kubernetes reads:

kind: NetworkPolicy → Create a NetworkPolicy
podSelector: app: member → Apply it to member Pods
policyTypes: Ingress → Restrict incoming traffic
from: app: api-gateway → Allow Gateway Pods in the same namespace
port: 8080 → Allow TCP traffic to destination port 8080

For incoming connections from other application Pods, this policy allows:

Gateway Pod → member Pod:8080 → Allowed
Other Pod   → member Pod:8080 → Blocked unless another policy allows it
  • podSelector: selects the Pods the policy applies to in its namespace.
  • Ingress: controls incoming traffic to those Pods.
  • Egress: controls outgoing traffic from those Pods.
  • Allowed traffic: rules specify permitted sources, destinations, ports, and protocols.
  • Combined policies: permissions add together; any applicable policy can allow traffic. There is no rule ordering or explicit deny rule.

By default, Pods allow incoming and outgoing traffic. Once a NetworkPolicy selects a Pod for a direction, that direction becomes restricted to traffic allowed by applicable policies. Reply traffic for allowed connections is automatically permitted.

NetworkPolicy requires a cluster network implementation that supports enforcement. Creating the YAML alone does not guarantee traffic will be filtered.

The components work together like this:

Deployment → creates Pods
Service → provides a stable address for Pods
DNS → resolves the Service name
NetworkPolicy → controls whether traffic to or from Pods is allowed

A Service makes Pods reachable; a NetworkPolicy restricts which connections are permitted.

Why do kubernetes/redis and kubernetes/redis-cache each have a Deployment and a Service?

Your project has two separate Redis instances because they store data with different purposes and eviction requirements.

FolderPurposeMemory policy
kubernetes/redisGateway sessions, rate limiting, and Member distributed locksnoeviction
kubernetes/redis-cacheSociety Service cacheallkeys-lru

1. redis: shared operational state

This Redis holds data such as login sessions, rate-limit counters, and locks used to coordinate operations.

Its Deployment configures:

--maxmemory 128mb
--maxmemory-policy noeviction

noeviction means Redis will not automatically remove existing keys to make room when its memory limit is reached. Commands requiring additional memory can fail instead. Keys with expiration can still expire.

This helps prevent cache pressure from unexpectedly removing sessions or locks.

2. redis-cache: replaceable cached data

This Redis stores cached Society Service data to reduce database queries.

Its Deployment configures:

--maxmemory 128mb
--maxmemory-policy allkeys-lru

allkeys-lru lets Redis remove approximately the least recently used keys when it needs space.

Cached data can be loaded again from the database, so removing it is acceptable.

Gateway → redis:6379 → sessions and rate limits
Member  → redis:6379 → distributed locks

Society → redis-cache:6379 → cached data

Why does each folder have both a Deployment and a Service?

  • Deployment: runs and manages that Redis Pod, including its image, password, memory policy, and probes.
  • Service: provides a stable internal IP and DNS name for applications to connect to that Redis Pod.
redis Service
    → selects Pods labeled app: redis
    → Redis Pod:6379

redis-cache Service
    → selects Pods labeled app: redis-cache
    → Redis cache Pod:6379

Your application-config.yaml chooses the destinations:

REDIS_HOST: "redis"
SOCIETY_CACHE_REDIS_HOST: "redis-cache"

These are separate Redis servers, not replicas of each other. Separating them prevents cache growth and cache eviction from consuming the memory reserved for sessions, rate limits, and locks.

Both currently use emptyDir storage, so their data is not durable across Pod replacement. noeviction protects against memory-driven eviction; it does not provide persistence.

What does each kind in kubernetes/nginx mean?

Your kubernetes/nginx folder defines six resource kinds that configure NGINX as the entry point to your application.

Client → NGINX edge → Spring Cloud Gateway → backend services

1. What is kind: GatewayClass?

A GatewayClass identifies which controller manages a Gateway.

Your gatewayclass.yaml contains:

apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: GatewayClass
metadata:
  name: nginx
spec:
  controllerName: gateway.nginx.org/nginx-gateway-controller

Here, Kubernetes reads:

kind: GatewayClass → Define a class of Gateways
name: nginx → Name this class nginx
controllerName → Identify the NGINX Gateway Fabric controller

A Gateway references this class using:

gatewayClassName: nginx

GatewayClass is cluster-scoped, so it does not have a namespace. Creating it does not install the NGINX controller; that controller must be installed separately.

2. What is kind: Gateway?

A Gateway defines the network entry point, including ports, protocols, hostnames, and TLS settings.

Your gateway.yaml defines a Gateway named emerald-edge. Its main settings are:

apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: emerald-edge
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  gatewayClassName: nginx
  listeners:
    - name: http
      hostname: emerald.local
      port: 80
      protocol: HTTP
      allowedRoutes:
        namespaces:
          from: Same

    - name: https
      hostname: emerald.local
      port: 443
      protocol: HTTPS
      tls:
        mode: Terminate
        certificateRefs:
          - kind: Secret
            name: emerald-local-tls
      allowedRoutes:
        namespaces:
          from: Same

Here, Kubernetes reads:

gatewayClassName: nginx → Use the nginx GatewayClass
http listener → Accept HTTP for emerald.local on port 80
https listener → Accept HTTPS for emerald.local on port 443
mode: Terminate → Decrypt HTTPS at NGINX
certificateRefs → Read the certificate from emerald-local-tls
from: Same → Allow routes from the Gateway's namespace

The Gateway defines where traffic enters. HTTPRoute defines where it goes.

The kind: Secret inside certificateRefs references an existing Secret; it does not create one.

3. What is kind: HTTPRoute?

An HTTPRoute matches HTTP requests and defines forwarding or redirect rules.

Your httproutes.yaml contains two HTTPRoutes.

The first redirects HTTP requests to HTTPS:

apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata:
  name: emerald-http-to-https
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  parentRefs:
    - name: emerald-edge
      sectionName: http
  hostnames:
    - emerald.local
  rules:
    - matches:
        - path:
            type: PathPrefix
            value: /
      filters:
        - type: RequestRedirect
          requestRedirect:
            scheme: https
            port: 443
            statusCode: 308
parentRefs.name → Attach to Gateway emerald-edge
sectionName: http → Use its HTTP listener
PathPrefix: / → Match all request paths
RequestRedirect → Tell the client to use HTTPS
statusCode: 308 → Permanent redirect preserving method and body

The second forwards HTTPS API requests to Spring Cloud Gateway:

apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: HTTPRoute
metadata:
  name: emerald-api-to-spring-gateway
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  parentRefs:
    - name: emerald-edge
      sectionName: https
  hostnames:
    - emerald.local
  rules:
    - matches:
        - path:
            type: PathPrefix
            value: /api/
      backendRefs:
        - name: api-gateway
          port: 8080
          weight: 1
sectionName: https → Use the HTTPS listener
hostnames → Match emerald.local
PathPrefix: /api/ → Match API paths
backendRefs.name → Send requests to Service api-gateway
port: 8080 → Use the Service's port 8080

The connection is:

http://emerald.local/api/...
          ↓ redirect
https://emerald.local/api/...
          ↓ NGINX forwards
api-gateway Service:8080
          ↓
Spring Cloud Gateway Pod

NGINX Gateway and Spring Cloud Gateway are separate components. NGINX handles the external entry point; Spring Cloud Gateway handles application routing.

4. What is kind: UpstreamSettingsPolicy?

An UpstreamSettingsPolicy configures how NGINX communicates with upstream application backends.

Your policies.yaml includes:

apiVersion: gateway.nginx.org/v1alpha1
kind: UpstreamSettingsPolicy
metadata:
  name: api-gateway-round-robin
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  targetRefs:
    - group: core
      kind: Service
      name: api-gateway
  loadBalancingMethod: round_robin

Here, the intended configuration is:

targetRefs → Apply settings to Service api-gateway
round_robin → Distribute requests among available backend endpoints

For example, with two eligible Gateway Pods:

Request 1 → Gateway Pod 1
Request 2 → Gateway Pod 2
Request 3 → Gateway Pod 1

This policy is specific to NGINX Gateway Fabric and requires its policy CRD and a compatible controller. NGINX documentation

5. What is kind: ProxySettingsPolicy?

A ProxySettingsPolicy configures the connection between NGINX and upstream applications, including timeouts.

Your policies.yaml includes:

apiVersion: gateway.nginx.org/v1alpha1
kind: ProxySettingsPolicy
metadata:
  name: emerald-edge-upstream-timeouts
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  targetRefs:
    - group: gateway.networking.k8s.io
      kind: Gateway
      name: emerald-edge
  timeout:
    connect: 2s
    read: 32s
    send: 32s

Here, NGINX reads:

targetRefs → Apply settings to Gateway emerald-edge
connect: 2s → Allow up to 2 seconds to establish an upstream connection
read: 32s → Allow up to 32 seconds between upstream read operations
send: 32s → Allow up to 32 seconds between upstream write operations

read and send are inactivity timeouts, not total request-duration limits. A request can take longer if data continues flowing within those intervals. NGINX documentation

6. What is kind: ClientSettingsPolicy?

A ClientSettingsPolicy configures the connection between the client and NGINX, including request-body limits.

Your policies.yaml includes:

apiVersion: gateway.nginx.org/v1alpha1
kind: ClientSettingsPolicy
metadata:
  name: emerald-edge-client-limits
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  targetRef:
    group: gateway.networking.k8s.io
    kind: Gateway
    name: emerald-edge
  body:
    maxSize: 1m
    timeout: 15s

Here, NGINX reads:

targetRef → Apply settings to Gateway emerald-edge
maxSize: 1m → Limit the client request body to 1 MiB
timeout: 15s → Allow up to 15 seconds between request-body reads

A body exceeding the size limit receives HTTP 413. If the client stops sending the body beyond the timeout, the request receives HTTP 408. The timeout does not limit the total upload duration. NGINX documentation

7. Why does helm-values.yaml have no kind?

Your helm-values.yaml contains:

nginxGateway:
  watchNamespaces:
    - emerald-housing

This is Helm chart configuration, so it does not define a Kubernetes resource directly.

It configures the NGINX Gateway controller to watch the emerald-housing namespace.

GatewayClass → identifies the controller
Gateway → defines HTTP and HTTPS entry points
HTTPRoute → redirects or forwards requests
UpstreamSettingsPolicy → sets backend load balancing
ProxySettingsPolicy → sets upstream connection timeouts
ClientSettingsPolicy → sets client request-body limits
helm-values.yaml → configures the controller installation

What commands do I run to add, update, delete, debug, or view logs for a Deployment YAML?

Editing a YAML file changes only the file. Run kubectl apply to send those changes to Kubernetes. Debugging and logs inspect the running resources.

Assume your file is member-deployment.yaml and contains:

apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: member-deployment
  namespace: emerald-housing
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: member
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: member
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: member-container
          image: member-service:1.0
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8080

The commands below use your Docker Desktop cluster explicitly.

1. How do I add a new Deployment?

Save the YAML, then run:

kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f member-deployment.yaml
-f → Read the specified file
apply → Create the resource if missing, or update it if it exists

The namespace must already exist, and the image must be available to the cluster.

2. How do I update an existing Deployment?

Edit the YAML—for example:

image: member-service:2.0

Apply the same file again:

kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f member-deployment.yaml

Watch the update:

kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/member-deployment -n emerald-housing --timeout=120s
Edit YAML → apply → Deployment updates → rollout status checks progress

Changing the Pod template, such as its image or environment variables, triggers a rollout. Changing only replicas changes the Pod count without creating a new rollout revision. Deployment documentation

3. How do I check changes before applying them?

Validate against the cluster without saving the changes:

kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f member-deployment.yaml --dry-run=server

Compare the file with the live resource:

kubectl --context docker-desktop diff -f member-deployment.yaml

These checks help catch invalid fields and review proposed changes; they do not prove the application will start successfully.

4. How do I check whether the Deployment and Pods are running?

kubectl --context docker-desktop get deployment member-deployment -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop get pods -l app=member -n emerald-housing -o wide
get deployment → Desired and available replica counts
get pods → Pod readiness, status, restarts, IPs, and nodes
-l app=member → Filter Pods by label

5. How do I debug a Deployment?

Inspect its configuration, conditions, and events:

kubectl --context docker-desktop describe deployment member-deployment -n emerald-housing

Get the Pod names:

kubectl --context docker-desktop get pods -l app=member -n emerald-housing

Replace POD_NAME below with an actual Pod name:

kubectl --context docker-desktop describe pod POD_NAME -n emerald-housing

Check recent namespace events:

kubectl --context docker-desktop get events -n emerald-housing --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp
SymptomWhat to inspect
PendingScheduling events, resource capacity, volumes
ImagePullBackOffImage name, tag, registry access
CrashLoopBackOffContainer logs, previous logs, startup configuration
Running but not readyReadiness probe, application health, dependencies
Stalled rolloutDeployment conditions and new Pod events

6. How do I view logs?

Logs belong to containers in Pods, not to the YAML file.

Show the latest logs from one Pod:

kubectl --context docker-desktop logs POD_NAME -c member-container -n emerald-housing --tail=100

Follow logs as new lines arrive:

kubectl --context docker-desktop logs POD_NAME -c member-container -n emerald-housing --tail=100 -f

Press Ctrl+C to stop following.

Show logs from the previous container instance after a restart:

kubectl --context docker-desktop logs POD_NAME -c member-container -n emerald-housing --previous

Show recent logs across matching Pods, with source prefixes:

kubectl --context docker-desktop logs -l app=member -c member-container -n emerald-housing --tail=100 --prefix=true

describe helps explain Kubernetes failures; logs shows application output. kubectl reference

7. How do I restart the Deployment without changing its YAML?

kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout restart deployment/member-deployment -n emerald-housing

Then check completion:

kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/member-deployment -n emerald-housing --timeout=120s

This replaces its Pods through a rollout. It is useful after updating a ConfigMap consumed through environment variables.

8. How do I roll back a Deployment update?

View retained revisions:

kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout history deployment/member-deployment -n emerald-housing

Return to the previous retained revision:

kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout undo deployment/member-deployment -n emerald-housing

Rollback changes the live Pod template; it does not rewrite your local YAML. Update the file too, or a later apply can reintroduce the unwanted configuration. Deployment documentation

9. How do I delete the Deployment?

Using the file:

kubectl --context docker-desktop delete -f member-deployment.yaml

Or using its resource name:

kubectl --context docker-desktop delete deployment member-deployment -n emerald-housing

This normally removes the Deployment and its managed ReplicaSets and Pods. A separately created Service or ConfigMap remains.

Deleting the local YAML file does not delete the live Deployment. Likewise, deleting only a managed Pod usually causes its ReplicaSet to create a replacement.

ActionMain command
Add or updatekubectl apply -f FILE
Validate without savingkubectl apply -f FILE --dry-run=server
Preview changeskubectl diff -f FILE
Check statuskubectl get deployment / kubectl get pods
Debugkubectl describe pod POD_NAME
Read application logskubectl logs POD_NAME
Restart Podskubectl rollout restart deployment/NAME
Watch rolloutkubectl rollout status deployment/NAME
Roll backkubectl rollout undo deployment/NAME
Deletekubectl delete -f FILE

For your actual project, use its real YAML path, metadata.name, Pod labels, and container name in place of these example values.

What is WebFlux, and where is it used here?

Yes, your Spring Cloud Gateway uses WebFlux. You can see its configuration in application.yml:

spring:
  cloud:
    gateway:
      server:
        webflux:
          routes:
            # Member and Society routes

Spring WebFlux is Spring’s reactive web framework. It supports non-blocking request processing: while waiting for network operations, a thread can handle other work instead of remaining blocked.

For example:

Request arrives at Gateway
          ↓
Gateway calls Member Service
          ↓
While waiting, its thread can handle other requests
          ↓
Member responds
          ↓
Gateway continues processing and returns the response

This makes it useful for a Gateway that handles many concurrent requests and forwards them to other services.

Spring MVCSpring WebFlux
Typically uses a servlet thread for each active requestTypically uses a small set of event-loop threads
Commonly uses blocking callsDesigned for non-blocking calls
Commonly returns objects or ResponseEntityCommonly uses Mono and Flux

WebFlux uses these reactive types:

  • Mono<T>: produces zero or one value, such as one response.
  • Flux<T>: produces zero to many values, such as a stream of events.

In your request flow:

Browser
   ↓ HTTPS
NGINX
   ↓ HTTP
Spring Cloud Gateway — uses WebFlux
   ↓ HTTP
Member / Society services

WebFlux and HTTPS are separate concepts. WebFlux controls how requests are processed; HTTP/HTTPS controls how they are transported.

Blocking work, such as JDBC calls or Thread.sleep(), must not run directly on WebFlux event-loop threads because it prevents those threads from serving other requests.

What features of NGINX have we used, and how does a request flow through them?

Your project uses NGINX Gateway Fabric as the external entry point. It receives browser requests, handles HTTPS, matches routes, applies limits, and forwards requests to Spring Cloud Gateway.

Spring Cloud Gateway then handles application security and routes requests to Member or Society Service.

Browser
   │
   │ HTTPS
   ▼
NGINX
   │ TLS termination
   │ Hostname and path matching
   │ Request-body limits
   │ Backend load balancing
   │ Connection timeouts
   │
   │ HTTP
   ▼
Spring Cloud Gateway
   │ Authentication and authorization
   │ Redis-based rate limiting
   │ Application routing
   │
   │ HTTP
   ▼
Member / Society Service

Let’s follow this request through the configuration:

https://emerald.local/api/v1/members

1. Finding NGINX: DNS or the hosts file

Before NGINX receives anything, the browser needs the IP address for emerald.local.

For local development, you can configure the Windows hosts file:

C:\Windows\System32\drivers\etc\hosts

For example, if NGINX is exposed on your computer’s loopback address:

127.0.0.1 emerald.local

The browser interprets the URL as:

https                 → Establish a TLS-protected connection
emerald.local         → Resolve this hostname to an IP
443                   → Default port for HTTPS
/api/v1/members       → Request this path
Browser
   ↓ Look up emerald.local
Hosts file / DNS
   ↓ Return the configured IP
Browser connects to that IP on port 443

The IP must be an address where NGINX is actually reachable. A hosts entry alone does not start or expose NGINX.

2. GatewayClass: selecting the NGINX controller

File: gatewayclass.yaml

apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: GatewayClass
metadata:
  name: nginx
spec:
  controllerName: gateway.nginx.org/nginx-gateway-controller

This identifies the controller responsible for Gateways using this class.

Your Gateway selects it in gateway.yaml:

gatewayClassName: nginx

The relationship is:

Gateway: emerald-edge
          ↓ gatewayClassName
GatewayClass: nginx
          ↓ controllerName
NGINX Gateway Fabric controller
          ↓
Configures the NGINX data plane

The controller manages configuration. The NGINX data plane handles browser traffic. Browser requests do not pass through the controller.

3. HTTP and HTTPS listeners: accepting connections

File: gateway.yaml

Your Gateway defines two listeners:

listeners:
  - name: http
    hostname: emerald.local
    port: 80
    protocol: HTTP

  - name: https
    hostname: emerald.local
    port: 443
    protocol: HTTPS

A listener defines the entry point for a particular protocol, port, and hostname.

http://emerald.local/...
          ↓
HTTP listener on port 80

https://emerald.local/...
          ↓
HTTPS listener on port 443

Listener names such as http and https also let HTTPRoutes attach to a specific listener using sectionName.

4. HTTP-to-HTTPS redirect: moving the browser to encryption

File: httproutes.yaml

Your first HTTPRoute attaches to the HTTP listener:

parentRefs:
  - name: emerald-edge
    sectionName: http

Its rule redirects requests to HTTPS:

filters:
  - type: RequestRedirect
    requestRedirect:
      scheme: https
      port: 443
      statusCode: 308

If someone enters:

http://emerald.local/api/v1/members

The flow is:

Browser ── HTTP request ──→ NGINX:80
Browser ←─ 308 redirect ─── NGINX

Browser ── HTTPS request ─→ NGINX:443

NGINX tells the browser to make a new HTTPS request. It does not turn the existing HTTP connection into HTTPS.

Status 308 indicates a permanent redirect and preserves the request method and body. However, sensitive data sent in the initial HTTP request has already travelled without TLS, so clients should use HTTPS directly.

5. TLS termination: securing the browser connection

File: gateway.yaml

tls:
  mode: Terminate
  certificateRefs:
    - kind: Secret
      name: emerald-local-tls
  options:
    nginx.org/ssl-protocols: "TLSv1.2 TLSv1.3"

TLS provides encryption, integrity, and server authentication.

The Secret supplies the certificate and private key:

emerald-local-tls
   ├── tls.crt → Certificate
   └── tls.key → Private key

When the browser connects:

Browser connects to NGINX:443
          ↓
NGINX presents its certificate
          ↓
Browser verifies hostname, validity, and trust
          ↓
Browser and NGINX establish encryption keys
          ↓
Encrypted HTTP communication begins

The certificate must cover emerald.local, and the browser must trust its issuing authority.

TLS termination means the encrypted connection ends at NGINX.

Browser ═══ HTTPS ═══▶ NGINX ─── HTTP ───▶ Gateway Pod
          encrypted              unencrypted by TLS

Your configuration specifies TLS versions 1.2 and 1.3.

6. Hostname and path routing: choosing the API route

File: httproutes.yaml

Your second HTTPRoute attaches to the HTTPS listener:

parentRefs:
  - name: emerald-edge
    sectionName: https

It matches the hostname and path:

hostnames:
  - emerald.local
rules:
  - matches:
      - path:
          type: PathPrefix
          value: /api/

For your request:

https://emerald.local/api/v1/members
        └─────┬─────┘ └──────┬──────┘
          hostname          path
              ↓               ↓
       emerald.local       /api/ prefix
              └───────┬───────┘
                    Match
RequestMatches this API route?
https://emerald.local/api/v1/membersYes
https://emerald.local/api/v1/societyYes
https://emerald.local/images/logo.pngNo
https://another.local/api/v1/membersNo

An unmatched request requires another applicable route to be forwarded. Your shown API route does not configure static website hosting.

7. Request-body limits: controlling incoming payloads

File: policies.yaml, ClientSettingsPolicy

body:
  maxSize: 1m
  timeout: 15s

These settings govern the client-to-NGINX side.

maxSize: 1m limits the request body to 1 MiB:

Small member JSON request → Within limit → Can proceed

Large upload over 1 MiB → Exceeds limit → HTTP 413

timeout: 15s limits inactivity while NGINX reads the request body:

Client sends body data
          ↓
Client stops sending
          ↓
No additional body data within 15s
          ↓
Request times out with HTTP 408

This is not a 15-second total upload limit. An upload may last longer if data keeps arriving within the allowed intervals.

These controls limit payload size and stalled uploads; they do not validate your application’s JSON fields.

8. Reverse proxy: forwarding to Spring Cloud Gateway

File: httproutes.yaml

backendRefs:
  - name: api-gateway
    port: 8080
    weight: 1

This identifies:

Backend resource → Service
Service name     → api-gateway
Service port     → 8080
Namespace        → Same namespace as the HTTPRoute

NGINX accepts the browser connection and makes a separate backend connection.

Browser connection:
Browser ── HTTPS ──→ NGINX

Backend connection:
NGINX ── HTTP ──→ Gateway backend:8080

The request path stays:

/api/v1/members

Your route does not configure a rewrite that removes /api/.

There is one configured backend Service, so weight: 1 gives it the entire share among the listed backends. It does not mean one Pod or one percent.

9. Round-robin balancing: selecting a Gateway endpoint

File: policies.yaml, UpstreamSettingsPolicy

targetRefs:
  - group: core
    kind: Service
    name: api-gateway
loadBalancingMethod: round_robin

The Service identifies Gateway Pods through its selector. Kubernetes publishes their ready endpoints, which NGINX Gateway Fabric uses to configure upstream servers.

api-gateway Service
          ↓
Ready backend endpoints
          ├── Gateway Pod A:8080
          └── Gateway Pod B:8080

Round-robin selection conceptually alternates between eligible endpoints:

Request 1 → Pod A
Request 2 → Pod B
Request 3 → Pod A
Request 4 → Pod B

Actual distribution can be affected by endpoint availability and runtime behavior. With one eligible endpoint, all requests go there.

NGINX can connect directly to Pod endpoints. The Service identifies the backend group; its ClusterIP is not necessarily a hop in the traffic path.

10. Upstream timeouts: controlling backend waits

File: policies.yaml, ProxySettingsPolicy

timeout:
  connect: 2s
  read: 32s
  send: 32s

These settings govern the NGINX-to-backend connection.

SettingMeaningExample
connect: 2sTime allowed to establish the backend connectionGateway endpoint cannot be reached promptly
read: 32sMaximum inactivity between reads from the backendGateway stops sending response data
send: 32sMaximum inactivity between writes to the backendNGINX cannot continue sending the request
NGINX
   │ Establish connection: up to 2s
   ▼
Gateway Pod
   │ Return response data
   │ No read gap longer than 32s
   ▼
NGINX

The read and send settings are not total request-duration limits. A long response can continue beyond 32 seconds if data keeps flowing.

11. Namespace restrictions: controlling configuration scope

File: gateway.yaml

allowedRoutes:
  namespaces:
    from: Same

This permits routes from the Gateway’s own namespace to attach to that listener:

Gateway namespace: emerald-housing

HTTPRoute in emerald-housing → Permitted by this namespace rule
HTTPRoute in another namespace → Not permitted by this rule

File: helm-values.yaml

nginxGateway:
  watchNamespaces:
    - emerald-housing

This configures the controller’s namespace scope.

These settings control which configuration it watches and accepts. They do not restrict client IP addresses or replace NetworkPolicies.

12. Spring Cloud Gateway takes over application processing

File: application.yml

After NGINX forwards /api/v1/members, Spring Cloud Gateway applies application security, rate limiting, and route selection.

NGINX
   ↓ HTTP /api/v1/members
Spring Cloud Gateway
   ├── Security checks
   ├── Rate limiting
   └── Member route match
          ↓
Member Service

In your project, authentication and Redis-based rate limiting belong to Spring Cloud Gateway; the NGINX files shown configure the external networking features.

The response returns through both proxies:

Member Service
   ↓ HTTP response
Spring Cloud Gateway
   ↓ HTTP response
NGINX
   ↓ TLS-encrypted HTTPS response
Browser

The YAML files configure this behavior beforehand through the controller. They are not opened for each request. These features apply when you use the deployed NGINX entry point. Your Option B http://localhost:8080 port-forward reaches Spring Cloud Gateway directly and bypasses NGINX.

How are the API Gateway roles implemented in this repository, and where are the files and locations?

These roles come from Spring framework dependencies, Gateway configuration, and custom Java classes in your repository.

All Java files below are under:

infrastructure/api-gateway/src/main/java/com/emerald/gateway/

1. API routing

File: application.yml, line 22 Location: spring.cloud.gateway.server.webflux.routes

Each route defines a path predicate and destination URI:

/api/v1/members → member-api route → MEMBER_SERVICE_URL
/api/v1/society → society-api route → SOCIETY_SERVICE_URL

Kubernetes supplies the actual destinations in config.yaml, line 12:

MEMBER_SERVICE_URL: "http://member-service:8080"
SOCIETY_SERVICE_URL: "http://society-service:8080"

Spring Cloud Gateway performs the forwarding; you do not need to write a controller for every proxied endpoint.

      ↓
Gateway retrieves the authenticated session

Shared Redis storage lets multiple Gateway Pods access the session.

Rules include:

/api/v1/**                      → Authentication required
/actuator/metrics/**             → ADMIN role required
Portal files                    → Allowed without login
Other unmatched requests        → Denied

Backend services separately enforce their own authorization and business permissions.

5. CSRF protection

          ↓
Sends token with state-changing requests
          ↓
Spring Security validates it
          ↓
Missing/invalid token → 403

This protects cookie-authenticated requests against cross-site request forgery.
