These locations show the repository implementation and configuration; runtime behavior also depends on the deployed environment values and dependencies.

What is the API Gateway execution order, how does each file get called, and what does each call do?

Let’s follow GET https://emerald.local/api/v1/members through your project, including which file gets involved, who calls it, and what it does.

First, an important distinction: Java files do not call other files directly. Spring creates objects from your classes. Framework filters, routing rules, and method calls determine which object runs next.

Browser
   ↓
NGINX
   ↓
Gateway WebFilter
   ↓
Spring Security
   ↓
Gateway route selection and filters
   ↓
Member Service
   ↓
Response returns

1. NGINX receives the browser request

Configuration:

  • gateway.yaml, line 20: HTTPS listener and TLS certificate.
  • httproutes.yaml, line 30: hostname, path matching, and backend destination.
https://emerald.local/api/v1/members
          ↓
NGINX accepts HTTPS on port 443
          ↓
Decrypts the request
          ↓
Matches emerald.local and /api/
          ↓
Forwards using HTTP to an api-gateway backend on port 8080

Who calls the Gateway? NGINX makes an HTTP request to a ready Spring Cloud Gateway backend.

The YAML files configured NGINX beforehand; they are not read again for every request.

2. Spring’s WebFlux server receives the request

Dependency: pom.xml, line 42

<artifactId>spring-cloud-starter-gateway-server-webflux</artifactId>

Configuration: application.yml, line 58

server:
  port: ${SERVER_PORT:8080}

Who calls the Java code? The running WebFlux server receives the network request and passes it into Spring’s HTTP processing chain.

Incoming HTTP request
          ↓
WebFlux request processing
          ↓
Registered WebFilters

Spring discovers your @Component filters and @Configuration classes during startup.

3. CorrelationAndErrorFilter.filter() starts

File: CorrelationAndErrorFilter.java, line 50

Method:

filter(ServerWebExchange exchange, WebFilterChain chain)

Who calls it? Spring’s WebFilter chain. The class is registered as a component and has HIGHEST_PRECEDENCE.

What it does:

  • Reads X-Correlation-ID.
  • Accepts it if valid; otherwise generates a UUID.
  • Adds the ID to the request forwarded downstream.
  • Sets correlation and Gateway-instance response headers.
  • Starts measuring duration.
  • Wraps the remaining processing with error handling and completion logging.

Conceptually:

prepareCorrelationId();

return chain.filter(request)
    .onErrorResume(/* write a Gateway error */)
    .doFinally(/* log completion */);

How does execution move forward?

chain.filter(correlated)

That delegates to the next WebFilter. The filter’s completion work runs later, when downstream processing finishes.

Correlation filter starts
          ↓ chain.filter(...)
Rest of request processing
          ↓ completes
Correlation filter logs completion

4. Spring Security processes the request

File: GatewaySecurityConfiguration.java, line 44

Method:

gatewaySecurityFilterChain(...)

Who calls this method? Spring calls the @Bean method during application startup to construct the security chain.

It does not rebuild security for every request. The resulting security filters run for each matching request.

The configuration supplies:

  • Session-based security-context handling.
  • CSRF protection.
  • URL access rules.
  • Authentication and access-denied error handling.
  • Logout handling.

For /api/v1/members, this rule applies:

.pathMatchers("/api/v1/**").authenticated()

What happens for this GET?

Read the session cookie
          ↓
Resolve session/security context through Spring Session
          ↓
Check authentication required for /api/v1/**
          ↓
Authenticated → Continue
Unauthenticated → Return 401

Redis and session settings are in application.yml, line 4.

A normal GET does not need a CSRF token. State-changing requests generally do.

If security rejects the request, the routing and Member business logic do not run.

Security errors are written through GatewaySecurityErrorWriter.java.

5. Spring chooses the request handler and Gateway route

Configuration: application.yml, line 22

Relevant route:

- id: member-api
  uri: ${MEMBER_SERVICE_URL:http://localhost:8082}
  order: -10
  predicates:
    - Path=/api/v1/access/**,/api/v1/families/**,/api/v1/owners/**,/api/v1/accounts,/api/v1/accounts/**,/api/v1/members,/api/v1/members/**,/api/v1/flats/*/memberships/**

Who reads this? Spring Cloud Gateway reads the route configuration during application setup and constructs route definitions and predicates.

For each request, Gateway’s framework handler mapping evaluates those predicates.

Request path: /api/v1/members
          ↓
Matches member-api predicate
          ↓
Select member-api route
          ↓
Use MEMBER_SERVICE_URL as destination

Kubernetes supplies the destination in config.yaml, line 12:

MEMBER_SERVICE_URL: "http://member-service:8080"

There is no Gateway MemberController needed for this forwarding. The framework routes the HTTP request.

order: -10 affects route-selection priority; it does not place this route before Spring Security.

6. Gateway builds the matched route’s filter chain

After selecting the route, Spring Cloud Gateway combines:

Global filters
      +
Filters configured on member-api
      +
Framework routing filters
      ↓
One ordered Gateway filter chain

This is a separate chain from the outer WebFilter chain.

6a. InternalHeaderSanitizingFilter.filter() runs early

File: InternalHeaderSanitizingFilter.java, line 19

Who calls it? Spring Cloud Gateway’s filter chain. The class implements GlobalFilter and Ordered.

It removes incoming internal headers, including:

X-Gateway-Authentication-Token
X-Internal-Session-Validation-Token
X-Member-Service-Token
X-Authenticated-User
X-Authenticated-Role
X-User
X-Role

Why? A browser must not be able to impersonate an internal service or trusted identity by supplying these headers.

It creates a modified request, then delegates:

chain.filter(exchange.mutate().request(request).build())
Incoming request headers
          ↓
Remove reserved internal headers
          ↓
Continue with sanitized request

It does not remove the browser’s session cookie, which the backend needs for session validation.

6b. The route’s RequestRateLimiter runs

Configuration: application.yml, line 28

filters:
  - name: RequestRateLimiter
    args:
      key-resolver: "#{@authenticatedAdminKeyResolver}"
      redis-rate-limiter.replenishRate: ${GATEWAY_MEMBER_RATE_REPLENISH:10}
      redis-rate-limiter.burstCapacity: ${GATEWAY_MEMBER_RATE_BURST:20}
      redis-rate-limiter.requestedTokens: 1

Who creates this filter? Spring Cloud Gateway resolves the name RequestRateLimiter to its framework-provided filter factory.

Who supplies the rate-limit key?

File: GatewayRateLimitConfiguration.java, line 14

Method:

authenticatedAdminKeyResolver()

Spring creates the KeyResolver bean during startup. The rate-limit filter invokes it during request processing.

Authenticated principal
          ↓
Resolve admin ID
          ↓
Key: admin:<adminId>
          ↓
Redis token-bucket check for this route

Result:

Token available → Consume token and continue
No token        → Return 429; do not call Member Service

The default Member settings replenish 10 tokens per second, with a burst capacity of 20.

7. Framework routing filters call Member Service

Destination configuration: config.yaml, line 12

http://member-service:8080

HTTP-client settings: application.yml, line 19

httpclient:
  connect-timeout: ${GATEWAY_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_MS:2000}
  response-timeout: ${GATEWAY_RESPONSE_TIMEOUT:28s}

Who makes the network call? Spring Cloud Gateway’s framework routing machinery uses its reactive HTTP client.

Gateway request
          ↓
Destination: http://member-service:8080
Path:        /api/v1/members
          ↓
HTTP request to Member Service

Kubernetes Service networking connects that destination to an eligible Member Pod.

The default connection timeout is 2 seconds; the configured response timeout is 28 seconds.

AdminAuthenticationManager is not the proxy client for this normal Member request. It is used for credential verification and current-account lookups.

8. Member Service validates the session identity

Entry point: BackendSecurityContextFilter.java

Client: GatewaySessionValidationClient.java, line 35

Who calls the backend filter? Member Service’s security/filter infrastructure when the forwarded HTTP request arrives.

For the session-authenticated request, the backend validation client calls the Gateway:

Member receives the forwarded request
          ↓
GatewaySessionValidationClient.validate(request)
          ↓
GET Gateway /internal/auth/session

The internal call includes the session cookie and the configured internal validation token.

Why call back? Member Service obtains a validated current identity rather than trusting a username or role header supplied by the client.

9. InternalSessionValidationController.validate() handles the callback

File: InternalSessionValidationController.java, line 32

Endpoint:

GET /internal/auth/session

Who calls it? Member Service’s GatewaySessionValidationClient makes the HTTP request. Spring WebFlux maps the endpoint to the controller method.

This is a new HTTP request into the Gateway, so its outer WebFilters and security processing also apply. It is handled locally by the controller, rather than proxied through member-api.

The method:

  1. Validates X-Internal-Session-Validation-Token.
  2. Loads the session’s security context.
  3. Checks that it contains an authenticated AdminIdentity.
  4. Fetches current account details.
  5. Returns identity, roles, and permissions.

Although the URL is permitAll in the security configuration, the controller performs its own internal-token validation.

How does it fetch current account details?

It calls:

accounts.current(principal.adminId())

Implementation: AdminAuthenticationManager.java, line 39

That method uses WebClient to call Member Service’s internal account endpoint with the Gateway authentication token.

Member request filter
   ↓ HTTP
Gateway /internal/auth/session
   ↓ Java method call
AdminAuthenticationManager.current(adminId)
   ↓ HTTP with internal service token
Member internal account endpoint
   ↓ Account identity
Gateway validation response
   ↓
Original Member request continues

These are distinct requests. The internal account lookup uses the service-authenticated internal path.

10. Member Service runs authorization and business logic

After successful identity validation:

Backend security context established
          ↓
Backend authorization checks
          ↓
Matching Member controller
          ↓
Service/business logic
          ↓
Repository/database operations
          ↓
HTTP response

The Gateway does not execute Member database queries or replace backend authorization.

A user may be authenticated at the Gateway and still be denied a particular operation by Member Service.

11. The response completes the Gateway filter chain

The reactive HTTP client receives the backend response, and Gateway returns its status, headers, and body through response processing.

Member response
      ↓
Gateway response handling
      ↓
Filter completion callbacks

Your custom rate-limit metrics filter is:

RateLimitMetricsFilter.java, line 24

Who calls it? The Gateway filter chain.

It delegates to the rest of the chain and registers a doFinally callback. At completion, it checks whether the response is 429 and increments a metric:

emerald.gateway.rate_limited.requests

Its metric is tagged with the route name.

Because filters can short-circuit the chain, a filter’s completion callback only runs when that filter was reached.

12. The outer filter handles exceptions and logs completion

File: CorrelationAndErrorFilter.java, line 75

If an exception propagates to this filter, it calls:

writeGatewayError(...)

Examples of handled mappings:

FailureResponse
Route-not-found exception404 ROUTE_NOT_FOUND
Downstream timeout504 GATEWAY_TIMEOUT
Other handled downstream failure503 GATEWAY_UNAVAILABLE

A normal backend error response is not automatically an exception or rewritten by this handler.

Completion logging occurs at line 81:

correlationId + HTTP method + path + status + durationMs

The response returns:

Member → Gateway → NGINX → Browser
          HTTP       HTTPS to browser

Login uses a separate execution path

For POST /api/v1/auth/login, Spring selects a local controller instead of the member-api proxy route.

File: AuthController.java, line 60

Browser login request
          ↓
CorrelationAndErrorFilter
          ↓

          ↓

          ↓
AdminAuthenticationManager.authenticate()
          ↓ WebClient POST with internal service token
Member Service credential endpoint
          ↓
Authenticated identity returned
          ↓
Rotate session ID
          ↓
Save security context in Redis-backed session
          ↓
Return login response

The main difference is:

RequestHandler
/api/v1/auth/loginGateway AuthController
/internal/auth/sessionGateway InternalSessionValidationController
/api/v1/membersGateway proxy route → Member Service

Configuration classes create the processing components at startup; the framework invokes those components for each request. Calls such as chain.filter(...) continue a filter chain, Java calls such as accounts.current(...) invoke another object, and WebClient or Gateway routing performs an actual HTTP call.

Begin with the Cluster. Click it to reveal the Namespace, then reveal the objects inside. Select a Deployment, Pod, Service, ConfigMap or Gateway route to see its real configuration and how it connects to the next object.

How to use this: Every click opens another level. The explanation shows the file path and lines, a small YAML extract, what its fields mean, and what to inspect if the connection fails. Use Back or Start over any time.

Cluster: the whole Kubernetes environment

File: kubernetes/kind-config.yaml, lines 1–5. The local cluster is created by scripts/kubernetes/prepare-kind.ps1.

kind: Cluster
apiVersion: kind.x-k8s.io/v1alpha4
nodes:
  - role: control-plane
  - role: worker

kind uses Docker containers to make this local cluster. The cluster is the outside boundary; the Emerald namespace and app resources are created inside it.

Namespace: a folder inside the cluster

File: kubernetes/namespace.yaml, lines 1–6.

kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: emerald-housing

All these Kubernetes resources name this namespace so Kubernetes groups them together.

Kubernetes ConfigMap: non-secret application settings

File: kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml, lines 1–13. The ConfigMap is named emerald-app-config in namespace emerald-housing. Both backend Deployments load it with envFrom.

data:
  POSTGRES_HOST: "host.docker.internal"
  SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL: "http://society-service:8080"
  SOCIETY_SERVER_PORT: "8080"
  MEMBER_SERVER_PORT: "8080"
ConfigMap objectemerald-app-config
Namespace emerald-housing
Deployment envFromSociety and Member Deployment files load the ConfigMap.
Container environmentEach ConfigMap key becomes an environment variable with the same name.
Spring placeholdersapplication.yml reads the environment variables.

For example, the ConfigMap sets POSTGRES_HOST=host.docker.internal. The JDBC URL placeholder uses that instead of its local fallback, localhost. In a Pod, localhost means that Pod itself. The Society URL tells Member Service to use Kubernetes DNS at society-service.

Settings vs. credentials: A ConfigMap holds ordinary settings such as hostnames and ports. Database usernames and passwords come separately from the Kubernetes Secret explained in the next item.

Kubernetes Secret: database credentials inside the namespace

Created by: the PowerShell command under Manual Kubernetes checklist → Action 2. kubectl create secret generic emerald-db-credentials -n emerald-housing ... sends values to the currently selected Kubernetes cluster and creates a Secret object named emerald-db-credentials in namespace emerald-housing. It does not create a repository file.

1 · You enter credentialsPowerShell variables hold them while the command runs.
2 · Kubernetes stores the objectemerald-db-credentials is in namespace emerald-housing.
3 · Deployment selects keyssecretKeyRef maps each key to an environment variable.
4 · Spring reads the variableThe matching ${...} placeholder supplies the database login.
Deployment file and linesSecret keyContainer environment variableSpring setting
kubernetes/society/deployment.yaml, lines 47–52society-usernameSOCIETY_DB_USERNAMEspring.datasource.username
kubernetes/society/deployment.yaml, lines 52–57society-passwordSOCIETY_DB_PASSWORDspring.datasource.password
kubernetes/member/deployment.yaml, lines 47–52member-usernameMEMBER_DB_USERNAMEspring.datasource.username
kubernetes/member/deployment.yaml, lines 52–57member-passwordMEMBER_DB_PASSWORDspring.datasource.password

Each Deployment maps only its own service's two keys. The Pod and Secret must be in the same namespace. If the Secret or required key is missing, the container cannot start with its required configuration.

Keep credentials private: Do not commit real credentials to Git or print them in logs/screenshots. Kubernetes Secret data is encoded; encoding alone is not encryption. Access also depends on cluster configuration and permissions. kubectl get secret emerald-db-credentials -n emerald-housing confirms the object exists without displaying its values.

Kubernetes provides Secret values when it creates the Pod. Spring reads them during startup. If a Secret changes, restart/roll out the Pods so the new process receives the new environment values.

Deployment: desired copies and labels

Files: Society kubernetes/society/deployment.yaml, lines 8–23; Member and Gateway use the same structure.

replicas: 2
selector:
  matchLabels:
    app: society-service
template:
  metadata:
    labels:
      app: society-service

replicas tells Kubernetes how many copies to maintain. The selector tells it which Pods belong to this Deployment. The template label is copied to each Pod. The Service uses the same label to find those Pods.

Service: selector and port connection

File: kubernetes/society/service.yaml, lines 8–16.

type: ClusterIP
selector:
  app: society-service
ports:
  - name: http
    port: 8080
    targetPort: http

The selector must match the Pod label. Callers use Service port 8080. targetPort: http points to the container port named http in the Deployment (container port 8080). Kubernetes DNS makes http://society-service:8080 usable in the namespace.

Society Pod: label, port and readiness

File: kubernetes/society/deployment.yaml, lines 19–23, 32–38 and 64–70.

labels:
  app: society-service
ports:
  - name: http
    containerPort: 8080
readinessProbe:
  httpGet:
    path: /actuator/health/readiness
    port: http

The label matches the Service selector. The named port matches targetPort. Readiness tells Kubernetes if this Pod should receive normal requests.

Member Pod: call Society using its stable name

Files: kubernetes/member/deployment.yaml, lines 39–56; Member application.yml, lines 51–55.

SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL: "http://society-service:8080"
SOCIETY_SERVICE_CONNECT_TIMEOUT: 2s
SOCIETY_SERVICE_READ_TIMEOUT: 3s

society:
  service:
    base-url: ${SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL:http://localhost:8081}

Locally the fallback address is localhost:8081. Kubernetes supplies the DNS name through the ConfigMap. Timeouts limit how long Member waits.

Gateway Pod: backend service names

Files: kubernetes/gateway/config.yaml, lines 1–11; kubernetes/gateway/deployment.yaml, lines 8–23 and 32–41.

MEMBER_SERVICE_URL: "http://member-service:8080"
SOCIETY_SERVICE_URL: "http://society-service:8080"
SERVER_PORT: "8080"

Gateway uses stable Service names, not Pod IPs. Its Deployment requests two copies.

Society Service: stable name and matching label

File: kubernetes/society/service.yaml, lines 9–16.

selector:
  app: society-service
ports:
  - name: http
    port: 8080
    targetPort: http

Same-namespace callers use society-service. Across namespaces, use society-service.emerald-housing.svc.cluster.local. EndpointSlices list ready matching Pods.

Member Service: Gateway's stable destination

File: kubernetes/member/service.yaml, lines 9–16.

selector:
  app: member-service
ports:
  - name: http
    port: 8080
    targetPort: http

Gateway calls http://member-service:8080. If there are no endpoints, compare the selector to Pod labels and inspect readiness.

Gateway Service: one private address

File: kubernetes/gateway/service.yaml, lines 9–16.

selector:
  app: api-gateway
ports:
  - name: http
    port: 8080
    targetPort: http

Use port-forward from your laptop to reach the private Gateway. Cluster callers use its Service name.

Gateway route rules: URL path picks the backend

File: infrastructure/api-gateway/src/main/resources/application.yml, lines 11–21.

- id: member-api
  uri: ${MEMBER_SERVICE_URL:http://localhost:8082}
  predicates:
    - Path=/api/v1/members,/api/v1/members/**,/api/v1/flats/*/memberships/**
- id: society-api
  uri: ${SOCIETY_SERVICE_URL:http://localhost:8081}
  predicates:
    - Path=/api/v1/society,/api/v1/floors/**,/api/v1/flats/**

A path predicate is a matching rule. Gateway forwards member paths to Member Service, and society/floor/flat paths to Society Service. Gateway routes; it does not own these business rules.

NetworkPolicy: which Pods may talk

File: kubernetes/networkpolicies.yaml, lines 1–40. These rules use Pod labels to allow Member and Gateway to reach Society, and Gateway to reach Member. They only enforce traffic when the cluster network plugin supports NetworkPolicy.

Pod-to-database connections: replicas share service-owned data

This is an architecture view, not a Kubernetes resource file. It combines the repository's replica settings, environment configuration and Spring datasource settings so you can see which application connects to which database.

Society Deploymentreplicas: 2 · kubernetes/society/deployment.yaml
Society Pod 1
Society Pod 2
Both Pods connect to the same database ↓
society_db
Member Deploymentreplicas: 1 · kubernetes/member/deployment.yaml
Member Pod 1
Member's own database connection ↓
member_db
Separate service-to-service path
Member Pod → HTTP via http://society-service:8080 → Society Service → society_db

How the settings reach each application

  1. kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml creates the emerald-app-config ConfigMap. It provides ordinary settings, including POSTGRES_HOST=host.docker.internal and SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL=http://society-service:8080.
  2. Each backend Deployment loads that ConfigMap using envFrom. It separately maps its own database username and password from emerald-db-credentials using secretKeyRef.
  3. Society's application.yml builds a JDBC URL ending in /society_db. Member's application.yml builds one ending in /member_db. Both connect to the configured PostgreSQL host, but use different databases and credentials.
  4. Each Pod starts its own Spring application and reads those environment variables. The two Society replicas use the same society_db; they do not each get a separate database. Member Service does not connect to society_db.
  5. When Member needs to check a flat, its RestClient calls the stable Society Service name over HTTP. Society Service reads its own database and returns the result.
Simple rule: A Pod is an application copy, not a database copy. Scaling Society from one Pod to two adds another application process; both copies use the same Society database. Member replicas (currently one) use the Member database.

Files to inspect: kubernetes/society/deployment.yaml (2 replicas and Society credentials), kubernetes/member/deployment.yaml (1 replica and Member credentials), kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml (shared non-secret host and Society URL), services/society-service/src/main/resources/application.yml (society_db), and services/member-service/src/main/resources/application.yml (member_db and RestClient URL).

About host.docker.internal: In this repository's local kind/Docker Desktop setup, this configured hostname lets Pods reach PostgreSQL running on the Windows host. It is not a Kubernetes database Service and does not discover PostgreSQL automatically. If PostgreSQL is later moved into Kubernetes, configure the host to use that database's Kubernetes Service DNS name.

Common connection mistakes

ProblemWhat you may noticeFirst thing to compare
Service selector and Pod label differService exists but has no endpoints.Service selector vs Deployment template.metadata.labels.
Wrong target portName resolves but HTTP cannot connect.Service targetPort vs named container port.
Wrong service name or namespaceDNS or connection failure.ConfigMap URL and resource namespace.
Pod is not ReadyPod gets no normal traffic.Readiness probe, events and startup logs.
Secret is missingConfiguration or database login failure.Secret namespace, name and key spellings.
PostgreSQL host is localhostApp looks for DB inside its own Pod.Use a host reachable from the Pod network.

Desired settings versus what is running

YAML describes what Kubernetes should try to keep running; it does not prove the live objects are healthy. Check actual objects and endpoints:

kubectl get deployments,pods,services,endpointslices -n emerald-housing
kubectl describe service society-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl logs deployment/member-service -n emerald-housing --all-pods=true

The Deployment manages a ReplicaSet, which keeps the desired Pod count. A Service matches ready Pods by labels. EndpointSlices show current destinations.

Implementation referenceFinish and verify the local Kubernetes setup

Finish and verify the local Kubernetes setup

Action guide moved here

These actions change your local PostgreSQL and Kubernetes environment. They are instructions only; this page does not run them. Use your own database password. Never commit it or permit database trust authentication from Pod networks.

Check your current state first: this is a manual checklist. Confirm the database, cluster, namespace and Secret before applying commands. The page does not run them for you.

Recommended order

Set a known password for the PostgreSQL application role, create the Kubernetes Secret, start backend Pods, inspect PostgreSQL logs for the source address, then add only the needed SCRAM host rules and reload PostgreSQL. Do not guess a client subnet.

Action 1 · Prepare PostgreSQL access safely

Services default to database role emerald. From an Administrator PowerShell prompt, connect as PostgreSQL administrator:

& 'C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL\18\bin\psql.exe' -h localhost -U postgres -d postgres

At the psql prompt, confirm the role and set its password without placing it in SQL history:

\du emerald
\password emerald
\q

Open the active pg_hba.conf as Administrator. The previously inspected location was C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL\18\data\pg_hba.conf. If different, run SHOW data_directory; in psql. After a Pod attempts a connection, read the PostgreSQL log and use the client source IP that PostgreSQL reports. Add narrowly scoped rules before broader matching rules:

host  society_db  emerald  <reported-source-ip>/32  scram-sha-256
host  member_db   emerald  <reported-source-ip>/32  scram-sha-256

Repeat for each real source IP shown. Reload PostgreSQL:

& 'C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL\18\bin\psql.exe' -h localhost -U postgres -d postgres
SELECT pg_reload_conf();
\q

The reload query should return true. Do not use trust for Pod traffic. The source address must be observed from your cluster.

Action 2 · Create the Kubernetes database Secret

From the repository root in PowerShell. Enter the actual database username/password. If both databases share a role, enter the same values for both.

$societyUser = Read-Host 'Society DB username'
$societyPasswordSecure = Read-Host 'Society DB password' -AsSecureString
$memberUser = Read-Host 'Member DB username'
$memberPasswordSecure = Read-Host 'Member DB password' -AsSecureString
$societyPassword = [System.Net.NetworkCredential]::new('', $societyPasswordSecure).Password
$memberPassword = [System.Net.NetworkCredential]::new('', $memberPasswordSecure).Password
kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\namespace.yaml
kubectl create secret generic emerald-db-credentials -n emerald-housing --from-literal="society-username=$societyUser" --from-literal="society-password=$societyPassword" --from-literal="member-username=$memberUser" --from-literal="member-password=$memberPassword"
kubectl get secret emerald-db-credentials -n emerald-housing

The last command confirms the Secret exists without printing its values. If it already exists, update it using your normal local secret-management procedure rather than creating a duplicate.

Action 3 · Build images and deploy Society and Member

From the repository root build and load backend images, then apply their manifests:

.\scripts\kubernetes\build-load-images.ps1
kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\config\application-config.yaml
kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\society\
kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\member\
kubectl get deployments,pods,services,endpointslices -n emerald-housing
kubectl rollout status deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl rollout status deployment/member-service -n emerald-housing

Expected desired counts: Society 2, Member 1, Gateway 2. Pods must be ready and Service EndpointSlices must have ready endpoints. Each app runs Flyway against its own database at startup.

Action 4 · Check discovery and flat validation

For direct local checks, run each port-forward in a different terminal:

kubectl port-forward -n emerald-housing service/society-service 8081:8080
kubectl port-forward -n emerald-housing service/member-service 8082:8080

Check the Society APIs and health endpoints:

Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8081/actuator/health
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8081/api/v1/society
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8081/api/v1/floors
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8081/api/v1/flats
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8082/actuator/health/readiness

Create a flat and member, then assign. The floor must be 1–18:

$flatBody = @{ floorNumber = 1; flatNumber = 'A-101' } | ConvertTo-Json
$flat = Invoke-RestMethod -Method Post -Uri 'http://localhost:8081/api/v1/flats' -ContentType 'application/json' -Body $flatBody
$memberBody = @{ fullName = 'Rahul Sharma'; mobileNumber = '9876543210'; email = 'rahul@example.com' } | ConvertTo-Json
$member = Invoke-RestMethod -Method Post -Uri 'http://localhost:8082/api/v1/members' -ContentType 'application/json' -Body $memberBody
$assignment = @{ flatId = $flat.flatId; relationshipType = 'RESIDENT'; startDate = (Get-Date -Format 'yyyy-MM-dd') } | ConvertTo-Json
Invoke-RestMethod -Method Post -Uri "http://localhost:8082/api/v1/members/$($member.memberId)/flats" -ContentType 'application/json' -Body $assignment

Try a random UUID for the flat; it should fail and create no membership. Compare the same X-Correlation-ID in the Member and Society logs.

Action 5 · Scale and check replacement
kubectl scale deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing --replicas=3
kubectl rollout status deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl get pods -n emerald-housing -l app=society-service -o wide
kubectl get endpointslices -n emerald-housing -l kubernetes.io/service-name=society-service
kubectl scale deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing --replicas=2
kubectl rollout status deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing

Inspect X-Instance-ID using separate requests/connections. Traffic is not guaranteed to alternate because connections may be reused. To check self-healing, identify a Society Pod, delete it, and watch Kubernetes replace it:

kubectl get pods -n emerald-housing -l app=society-service
kubectl delete pod <society-pod-name> -n emerald-housing
kubectl get pods -n emerald-housing -l app=society-service -w

Then check that two ready endpoints return and a flat assignment works. A brief request failure during replacement is possible.

Optional rolling update check

Build and load a genuinely new image tag before updating the Deployment. Do not reuse a mutable tag as proof that a new image was deployed.

kubectl set image deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing society-service=emerald/society-service:<new-tag>
kubectl rollout status deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing
Action 6 · Send requests through Gateway

Start Gateway port-forward:

kubectl port-forward -n emerald-housing service/api-gateway 8080:8080

Import postman/Emerald-Housing-Option-B.postman_collection.json and postman/Emerald-Housing-Option-B.postman_environment.json. Select Emerald Housing - Option B Localhost, set gatewayBaseUrl to http://localhost:8080, then create or retrieve a flat and member before assigning them. Copy generated IDs into flatId and memberId if needed. Keep direct-service URLs available for troubleshooting.

Simple PowerShell checks:

Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/actuator/health
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/api/v1/society
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/api/v1/floors
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/api/v1/flats
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/api/v1/members

Delete one Gateway Pod and confirm Kubernetes replaces it:

kubectl get pods -n emerald-housing -l app=api-gateway
kubectl delete pod <gateway-pod-name> -n emerald-housing
kubectl rollout status deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing

Implementation referenceLet Kubernetes run and look after the applications

Let Kubernetes run and look after the applications

Kubernetes is the coordinator for containers. It compares the requested setup with what is running, creates or replaces Pods, gives applications steady Service names, and can change replica counts when CPU demand changes. This phase consolidates the repository’s existing Kubernetes resources and adds autoscaling and one Kustomize entry point.

Deployment, traffic, and autoscaling flow

UPDATED — PHASE 16: The Society, Member and API Gateway Deployments now get their replica counts from three HorizontalPodAutoscalers (HPAs). Their minimums preserve 2 Society, 1 Member and 2 Gateway Pods. kubernetes/kustomization.yaml gathers the standard resources and HPAs. Application Deployments use RollingUpdate.
Static configuration only: The manifests were inspected, not applied. HPA needs a resource metrics provider such as metrics-server; the repository does not install one. Readiness, DNS, traffic distribution, recovery, and autoscaling still need manual cluster verification.

Implemented orchestration features

FeatureCurrent configurationRuntime status
Workload managementKubernetes Deployments and Services for Society, Member, API Gateway, and Redis in the emerald-housing namespace.Manifests are present; cluster application has not been verified in this phase.
Replica scalingHPAs target CPU utilization: Society 2–4 replicas, Member 1–3, Gateway 2–4.Requires a metrics provider; HPA behavior has not been verified.
Pod health and rolloutStartup/readiness/liveness probes, resource requests/limits, and rolling update settings support safe scheduling and replacement.Probe behavior, rollout capacity, and recovery require cluster checks.
Configuration and secretsConfigMaps hold non-secret settings; database/security credentials are consumed through Kubernetes Secret references.Secret values are supplied during setup and are not committed as a populated Secret manifest.

RollingUpdate for application Deployments

The Gateway, Member and Society Deployments explicitly use RollingUpdate with maxUnavailable: 0 and maxSurge: 1. Apply the updated Deployment YAML, monitor rollout status, and use rollout undo if a previous retained Pod template is needed.

1. What Kubernetes does

Docker packages an application and its runtime into an image. Kubernetes runs those images as containers inside Pods and continually reconciles the cluster toward the desired state written in YAML. The control plane accepts the desired state; worker nodes run the Pods. If one Pod disappears, a Deployment and its ReplicaSet create another. Kubernetes is therefore an orchestrator, while Docker Compose starts a declared group of containers on one Docker host. Compose is useful locally; Kubernetes adds cluster scheduling, Services, rolling updates, self-healing and autoscaling.

Desired YAML (what we ask for)
       |
       v
Kubernetes control plane — schedules and reconciles
       |
       +---- worker node: Society Pod(s)
       +---- worker node: Member Pod(s)
       +---- worker node: Gateway Pod(s)
                    |
                    v
          Services and EndpointSlices
          point to ready Pod addresses

2. Current Emerald Housing cluster picture

kind cluster: emerald-housing
└── Namespace: emerald-housing
    ├── NGINX Gateway Fabric data plane
    │     └── Gateway API: emerald-edge → HTTPS HTTPRoute → api-gateway:8080
    ├── Deployment api-gateway → HPA 2–4 → ClusterIP Service api-gateway:8080
    │     ├── Spring Cloud Gateway → member-service:8080
    │     └── Spring Cloud Gateway → society-service:8080
    ├── Deployment member-service → HPA 1–3 → ClusterIP Service member-service:8080
    │     ├── member_db (external PostgreSQL at configured POSTGRES_HOST)
    │     └── RestClient → society-service:8080
    ├── Deployment society-service → HPA 2–4 → ClusterIP Service society-service:8080
    │     ├── society_db (same PostgreSQL host, separate database)
    │     └── Redis Service redis:6379 (cache)
    └── Deployment redis → ClusterIP Service redis:6379
          └── Redis also stores Gateway sessions and rate-limit state

Redis is one replica backed by an emptyDir in the current manifest. It is private, but its data is lost if the Redis Pod is replaced. That means Redis-backed sessions and cache entries can be lost on Redis replacement; the current setup is a learning configuration, not highly available Redis. PostgreSQL is outside this namespace in the current setup and is reached through POSTGRES_HOST.

3. Pods, Deployments and ReplicaSets

A Pod is the smallest scheduled unit and contains the application container. A Pod name and IP can change after replacement, so callers use a Service name instead. A Deployment declares which image and Pod template to run. Its ReplicaSet maintains the number requested by the Deployment’s scale. Here, the HPA writes that scale so a fixed spec.replicas in the Deployment cannot continually reset it.

ResourceRepository exampleJob
PodCreated from a Deployment templateRuns the container; replaceable and temporary.
ReplicaSetManaged by each DeploymentKeeps the desired number of matching Pods.
Deploymentsociety-service, member-service, api-gatewayDeclares image, template, rollout, probes and resources.
HPANew files under kubernetes/autoscaling/Adjusts a target Deployment’s replica count using CPU metrics.

4. Services and Kubernetes DNS

The Service files select Pods by labels: Society selects app: society-service, Member selects app: member-service, and Gateway selects app: api-gateway. Their selectors match the Deployment Pod-template labels. Each Service is ClusterIP, so it provides an in-cluster address rather than a public backend endpoint. Kubernetes maintains EndpointSlices for ready matching Pods and DNS resolves the Service name. For example, Member continues to use http://society-service:8080 when Society Pods are replaced.

Member Pod
   | GET http://society-service:8080/...
   v
Service society-service:8080  (stable DNS name / ClusterIP)
   |
   +--> EndpointSlice: ready Society Pod IP A
   `--> EndpointSlice: ready Society Pod IP B
         Pod IPs may change; the caller keeps the Service name.

5. Actual Kubernetes files and their purpose

FileResource / purposeImportant current settings
kubernetes/namespace.yamlCreates the emerald-housing namespace.Labels the namespace as part of Emerald Housing.
kubernetes/society/deployment.yamlSociety application Deployment.Image emerald/society-service:step4-audit; HTTP 8080; requests 100m CPU/256Mi, limits 750m/640Mi; non-root UID 10001; RollingUpdate, maxUnavailable 0, maxSurge 1; 30-second termination grace.
kubernetes/member/deployment.yamlMember application Deployment.Image emerald/member-service:restclient-fix; HTTP 8080; requests 100m/256Mi, limits 750m/640Mi; non-root; same rollout and termination policy. Secret references provide database, session, internal-call and admin-provisioning credentials.
kubernetes/gateway/deployment.yamlStable Spring Cloud Gateway Deployment.Image emerald/api-gateway:step6-audit; HTTP 8080; requests 100m/192Mi, limits 750m/512Mi; non-root; rolling update.
kubernetes/society/service.yaml, member/service.yaml, gateway/service.yamlPrivate ClusterIP endpoints.Service port 8080 to the named container port http; selectors match app labels.
kubernetes/config/application-config.yamlemerald-app-config ConfigMap.Non-secret URLs, ports, cache TTLs, timeout and resilience values. In particular, SOCIETY_SERVICE_BASE_URL=http://society-service:8080 and REDIS_HOST=redis.
kubernetes/gateway/config.yamlapi-gateway-config ConfigMap.Gateway’s Member/Society Service URLs, session behavior, forward-header strategy, timeouts and rate limits.
kubernetes/redis/deployment.yaml and service.yamlPrivate single Redis Pod and ClusterIP Service.Redis 7.4 Alpine, port 6379, password from Secret, 128 MB max memory with noeviction; current storage is ephemeral emptyDir.
kubernetes/networkpolicies.yamlRestricts selected Pod ingress.Allows app-to-app paths on 8080 and Gateway/Society to Redis on 6379. These are ingress policies; they do not define egress restrictions.
kubernetes/nginx/gatewayclass.yaml, gateway.yaml, httproutes.yaml, policies.yamlNGINX Gateway Fabric entry point.emerald-edge, emerald.local, HTTP-to-HTTPS 308 redirect, TLS termination via externally-created emerald-local-tls, HTTPS API forwarding to api-gateway:8080, round-robin upstream policy.
kubernetes/kind-config.yamlLocal kind cluster shape.One control-plane and one worker node.
kubernetes/kustomization.yamlNew Kustomize package root.References the existing app, Redis, NetworkPolicy and Gateway API files plus the new HPAs; does not manufacture credentials or install controllers.
kubernetes/autoscaling/*-hpa.yamlNew HPA resources.CPU target 70%; minimums Society 2, Member 1, Gateway 2; maximums 4, 3, 4.

6. Probes: startup, readiness and liveness

Society and Member probe /actuator/health/liveness during startup and liveness; their readiness probes call /actuator/health/readiness, whose application configuration includes the database. Gateway uses the same liveness and readiness paths; its readiness group includes Redis. Redis uses authenticated redis-cli ping for all three probes. A startup probe gives an application time to launch (36 checks every 5 seconds for the backends, up to about 180 seconds; Gateway allows 24 checks, about 120 seconds). Readiness keeps an unready Pod out of Service endpoints; liveness restarts a stuck container; startup delays liveness until initialization has had time. Probe values are in each workload’s Deployment YAML.

# Society and Member Deployment probe pattern (Gateway has the same paths)
startupProbe:
  httpGet: { path: /actuator/health/liveness, port: http }
  periodSeconds: 5
  timeoutSeconds: 2
  failureThreshold: 36
readinessProbe:
  httpGet: { path: /actuator/health/readiness, port: http }
  periodSeconds: 5
  timeoutSeconds: 2
  failureThreshold: 3
livenessProbe:
  httpGet: { path: /actuator/health/liveness, port: http }
  periodSeconds: 10
  timeoutSeconds: 2
  failureThreshold: 3

The application startup path runs Spring Boot and Flyway before readiness can succeed. Society and Member readiness includes PostgreSQL, so Flyway/database initialization must finish before they receive normal Service traffic. Liveness uses the process liveness state, not a remote Society check, which helps avoid restart storms during a dependency outage.

7. Resource requests and limits

Requests are the scheduling baseline and the denominator used for CPU utilization in these HPAs. Limits cap CPU and memory consumption. For example, the Society HPA’s 70% CPU target is relative to its 100m CPU request, not a promise about a specific request rate. The current values are conservative learning defaults, not measured production sizing. Java’s container-aware heap still must fit inside the Pod memory limit after non-heap memory is included. Actual sizing needs runtime measurement; this task did not run the services.

8. Horizontal Pod Autoscaling

Each autoscaling/v2 HPA reads CPU usage and adjusts the matching Deployment between its configured minimum and maximum. Scale-up can add up to 100% or two Pods per 60 seconds. Scale-down waits 300 seconds and is limited to 25% or one Pod per 60 seconds, using the more conservative permitted change. These are learning defaults to reduce oscillation. HPA needs the Kubernetes resource metrics API, commonly provided by metrics-server; no metrics provider is installed by these manifests. When an HPA manages a Deployment, omit that Deployment’s fixed spec.replicas so a later apply does not fight the HPA. The HPA min/max values own the scale range.

apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: society-service
  minReplicas: 2
  maxReplicas: 4
  metrics:
    - type: Resource
      resource:
        name: cpu
        target:
          type: Utilization
          averageUtilization: 70

Actual HPA files are kubernetes/autoscaling/society-hpa.yaml, member-hpa.yaml and gateway-hpa.yaml. Society keeps at least two replicas for the existing multi-Pod design; Gateway keeps at least two; Member starts at one but can grow to three. HPA actions remain unverified until a metrics provider and cluster are available.

9. ConfigMaps, Secrets and network boundaries

ConfigMaps hold non-sensitive values. Deployments load the shared app ConfigMap with envFrom and use a separate Gateway ConfigMap. Sensitive database, Redis, session-validation, internal-service and admin-provisioning values come from Secret references. The manifests expect Secrets named emerald-db-credentials and emerald-security-credentials; the security Secret supplies redis-password for sessions/locks and redis-cache-password for Society cache Redis. The manifests do not create secret values. The TLS Secret emerald-local-tls is also supplied separately. Base64 encoding is not encryption: protect Secret access with RBAC and appropriate cluster-level encryption. No secret values or TLS private keys are committed by this Kustomize package.

All three application Services and Redis are ClusterIP. NGINX Gateway Fabric terminates external TLS and forwards API traffic only to the Gateway Service. Spring Cloud Gateway then routes to Member or Society. NetworkPolicies limit selected ingress paths, while the current policies leave egress governed by the cluster’s default behavior. Policy enforcement depends on a CNI that supports NetworkPolicy. No direct external backend, Redis, or PostgreSQL Service is created here.

10. NGINX Gateway API, rolling updates and self-healing

GatewayClass nginx identifies the NGINX controller. Gateway emerald-edge declares HTTP/HTTPS listeners for emerald.local; TLS ends at the HTTPS listener. HTTPRoute redirects HTTP to HTTPS and forwards /api/ requests to the existing api-gateway Service. NGINX’s upstream policy is round-robin across eligible Gateway endpoints. Spring Cloud Gateway remains the application router; NGINX does not route around it to backend services.

# Existing HTTPRoute backend reference
backendRefs:
  - name: api-gateway
    port: 8080
    weight: 1

# Existing Deployment rollout settings
strategy:
  type: RollingUpdate
  rollingUpdate:
    maxUnavailable: 0
    maxSurge: 1

Society, Member and Gateway Deployments use RollingUpdate with maxUnavailable: 0 and maxSurge: 1. Kubernetes creates a replacement during rollout, waits for readiness before it receives normal Service traffic, and then removes an old Pod. A Deployment also recreates a deleted Pod to meet the HPA-selected count. A rollout can still interrupt an in-flight request; this configuration is not a zero-downtime guarantee.

11. Kustomize package and safe apply prerequisites

The repository has no prior Kustomize root or Helm chart, so this change adds one Kustomize root at kubernetes/kustomization.yaml. It references the existing resources without copying them. The package includes the namespace, ConfigMaps, Deployments, Services, Redis, NetworkPolicies, Gateway API objects and HPAs. The Gateway API/NGINX custom resource definitions and controller must already be installed for those custom resources to be accepted. Create the referenced DB/security/TLS Secrets first, and make PostgreSQL reachable at the configured host.docker.internal address in this kind setup. Images must be built and loaded separately using the existing scripts/kubernetes/build-load-images.ps1; its defaults match the tags in the application Deployments. None of these prerequisites was executed here.

# Documentation only — not run for this change
kubectl apply -f kubernetes/namespace.yaml
# Create the required Secrets using local values; do not commit them.
kubectl apply -k kubernetes/
kubectl get deployments,pods,services,endpointslices -n emerald-housing
kubectl get hpa -n emerald-housing

12. Manual inspection, rollout and recovery guide

# Documentation only — run manually when the cluster is ready
kind get clusters
kubectl get namespaces
kubectl get deployments,pods,services,endpointslices -n emerald-housing -o wide
kubectl describe deployment society-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl get hpa -n emerald-housing
kubectl describe hpa society-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl top pods -n emerald-housing             # requires a resource metrics provider
kubectl rollout status deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl rollout history deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing
kubectl get events -n emerald-housing --sort-by=.lastTimestamp
kubectl logs deployment/member-service -n emerald-housing --all-pods=true
# Delete one Pod to observe Deployment replacement (manual exercise only):
kubectl delete pod <pod-name> -n emerald-housing
# Local diagnostic access to the private Gateway Service:
kubectl port-forward service/api-gateway 8080:8080 -n emerald-housing
# Remove resources only after checking what data/volumes they own:
kubectl delete -k kubernetes/

The last command removes the Kustomize-managed resources. It is included as a learning reference, not as a cleanup recommendation; inspect persistent data and externally managed Gateway/Secret resources before cleanup.

14. Interview practice: explain the choices and their costs

In a system design interview, describe each choice in four parts: what this project chose, what benefit it gives, what it costs, and what you would change as traffic or availability needs grow.

Choice in this projectBenefitCost or tradeoffWhat to discuss as it grows
Separate Society and Member databasesEach service owns its data and can change its schema independently.A cross-service operation cannot use one ordinary database transaction.Define consistency expectations and recovery behavior for multi-service workflows.
Synchronous Member-to-Society RestClient callMember gets an immediate authoritative answer about a flat.Assignment depends on Society being reachable and adds network latency.Keep timeouts bounded; consider asynchronous workflows only if the product can accept delayed confirmation.
Redis cache for Society reference readsRepeated reads can avoid repeated PostgreSQL queries.Values can be stale until expiry or invalidation; Redis adds another dependency.Measure hit rate and database load; keep PostgreSQL authoritative and make invalidation correct.
Redis-backed Gateway sessionsAny Gateway replica can find a user session.The current Redis deployment is one Pod with ephemeral storage, so it is a failure point and replacement can lose sessions.Choose a persistence, backup, and failover design before treating sessions as highly available.
CPU-based HPAKubernetes can add or remove application replicas automatically.CPU does not always track request latency or queueing; more Pods also consume resources and database connections.Measure workload, set safe maximum replicas, and budget total database connections.
NGINX Gateway Fabric before Spring Cloud GatewayNGINX handles the external HTTPS edge; Spring Gateway handles application routes.There are two routing layers to configure and troubleshoot.Keep responsibilities clear and avoid routing public traffic around Spring Gateway.
Kustomize manifestsExisting Kubernetes resources can be gathered as a repeatable package.Kustomize does not install external prerequisites or create secret values.Manage controller/CRD, metrics provider, TLS and secret provisioning separately.

15. Interview practice: walk through failure scenarios

FailureExpected behavior in this designTradeoff or point to explain
Society is unavailable during flat assignmentMember’s bounded HTTP call and resilience rules fail safely; it must not save an unvalidated membership.Assignment is unavailable while the required validation dependency is down; independent Member operations can continue.
A Society Pod becomes unready or disappearsThe Service should stop sending new traffic to its unready endpoint; the Deployment should replace a missing Pod.In-flight calls can still fail, and recovery depends on probes, scheduling capacity, and the cluster.
Redis is unavailableThe affected Redis-backed feature is impacted. Society cache Redis outage triggers bounded PostgreSQL fallback; session/lock Redis outage can affect login, rate limits, and Member writes.Both Redis services currently use one ephemeral Pod; cache entries can be rebuilt, while session loss or unavailable membership locks have direct user impact.
PostgreSQL is unavailableOperations requiring database access cannot safely complete; readiness should prevent database-dependent Pods from receiving normal traffic.Database availability is a system dependency; this repository does not configure PostgreSQL HA or recovery.
A Gateway Pod is replacedThe Service should route to ready Gateway Pods; another replica can validate the session through shared Redis.Redis must remain available, and a request already in flight during termination can fail.
A flat changes after validation but before membership saveSociety validation and the Member database write are separate operations.There is a consistency window; the two services do not share an atomic transaction.
HPA has no resource metrics providerCPU-based autoscaling cannot make decisions until a provider such as metrics-server is available.An HPA YAML file alone does not mean autoscaling is operational.
A rolling update has insufficient spare capacityThe Deployment requests one extra Pod while keeping existing replicas available.The new Pod may remain Pending if the cluster cannot schedule the surge capacity; zero downtime is not guaranteed.

16. Interview practice: explain the scaling plan

  • Scale application replicas: HPA currently uses CPU. It is simple, but request rate or latency may be a better signal for some workloads. The project has not installed a metrics provider or measured a workload.
  • Set replica limits: HPA ranges are Society 2–4, Member 1–3, Gateway 2–4. The maximums should fit cluster capacity and the databases’ connection budgets.
  • Budget database connections: each additional application replica may create another connection pool. Estimate the maximum possible total before increasing HPA limits.
  • Plan Redis availability: one Redis Pod is easy to learn with, but production session continuity and cache availability need persistence and failover planning.
  • Balance cache freshness and load: shorter TTLs reduce how long stale data may remain but cause more database reads. Invalidation can improve freshness but must cover all affected cache entries.
  • Keep retries bounded: retrying a safe read may recover from a brief fault, but retries during an outage add load. Do not retry unsafe writes or multiply retries across Gateway and Member Service.
  • Separate readiness and liveness: readiness controls whether a Pod receives new calls; liveness determines whether Kubernetes restarts it. Making liveness depend on a remote service can cause restart storms.
A useful interview answer: “We use a stable Service name so callers survive Pod replacement. We scale replicas within limits, but CPU scaling can increase database connections, so we need capacity measurements. Flat assignment remains synchronous because it needs authoritative validation; if Society is down, we fail safely instead of saving uncertain data.”

17. Phase 16 completion status

  • Present and reused: namespace, app Deployments and ClusterIP Services, ConfigMaps, Redis services, NetworkPolicies, NGINX Gateway API/TLS routing, probes, resource requests/limits and rolling updates.
  • Added: three CPU-based HPAs, kubernetes/kustomization.yaml. App Deployment replica fields remain HPA-managed.
  • Preserved: Kubernetes DNS, independent Society/Member databases, RestClient validation, Redis sessions/rate limiting/cache, Spring Security, Resilience4j and NGINX-to-Spring-Gateway routing.
  • Observability: optional Prometheus, Grafana, Loki, Tempo and Alloy overlay at kubernetes-observability/; setup and access are documented in Operations recommendations guide.
  • Not added: Kafka, automated deployment pipeline, or automatic traffic analysis/promotion.
  • Pending cluster verification: verify rolling updates, metrics-server/HPA behavior, NetworkPolicy enforcement, and database reachability in kind or a target cluster.
Scenario questionsKubernetes health probes and graceful shutdown

Kubernetes health probes and graceful shutdown

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: What do readiness, liveness, and startup probes do?

Answer: Readiness controls whether a Pod receives new traffic; liveness restarts a stuck container; startup gives slow initialization time before the other checks apply. On shutdown, stop accepting traffic, fail readiness, drain in-flight work, then exit before the termination grace period.

Solution: set terminationGracePeriodSeconds longer than the app shutdown timeout plus drain allowance. Avoid making liveness depend on a downstream service outage, or Kubernetes may restart every Pod unnecessarily.

Follow-up: Should liveness fail whenever PostgreSQL or Redis is unavailable?

Answer: Usually no. A downstream outage does not mean the process is dead; failing liveness can restart every Pod and amplify the incident. Use readiness to reflect whether this Pod can safely serve traffic, and reserve liveness for a stuck or irrecoverable process state.

Scenario questionsHPA scaling and rolling updates

HPA scaling and rolling updates

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How can HPA and rolling updates affect database capacity and availability?

Answer: HPA adds Pods from resource metrics, but each Pod may open its own Hikari pool. Budget connections at maximum replicas. Rolling updates temporarily run old and new Pods together, so the connection and resource budget must include surge capacity. Readiness prevents unready Pods receiving requests.

Budget: (replicas + maxSurge) × poolMaxPerPod ≤ databaseConnectionBudget. Tune HPA, pool size, and rollout surge as one capacity plan.

Follow-up: How do you prevent HPA scale-out from exhausting database connections?

Answer: Calculate maximum application Pods including rollout surge, multiply by each Pod pool maximum, and compare with the database budget. Set pool limits and HPA maximums together; consider queueing or a connection proxy if measured concurrency requires more application replicas than the database can serve directly.

Scenario questionsGraceful shutdown in the current repository

Graceful shutdown in the current repository

What is graceful shutdown, and how is it configured here?

Graceful shutdown stops accepting new application work and gives requests already being processed a bounded opportunity to finish during normal termination. For example, a member-creation transaction may complete during a rolling deployment before the old process exits.

# Configured in all three application.yml files
server:
  shutdown: graceful
spring.lifecycle:
  timeout-per-shutdown-phase: 20s

# Configured in the Gateway, Member and Society Pod specifications
terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 30

The Spring value is a timeout per lifecycle phase; the Kubernetes value is the Pod's overall termination window. Multiple phases and any termination hooks must fit within the overall budget. Check readiness, routing propagation and real termination behavior when validating deployments.

Limitation: this does not guarantee completion during a crash, power loss, forced kill or work exceeding the available timeout. Transactions, idempotent retries and deployment verification remain necessary.

Evidence: the three application resource files and kubernetes/gateway/deployment.yaml, kubernetes/member/deployment.yaml and kubernetes/society/deployment.yaml.

Chapter 10

Observability and design tradeoffs

Read operational signals, distinguish evidence, and explain next steps.

Use the right signal for the question

QuestionEvidence to inspectWhat it cannot prove alone
Is this Pod ready?Readiness, restart count, events, and rollout status.Business correctness for every API flow.
Why did this request fail?Correlation ID, application logs, dependency timings, and error response.Fleet-wide error rate from one request.
Is the service under pressure?Request rates, latency, errors, saturation, pools, cache metrics, and rejected work.The cause of an individual failure without request context.
Which services were involved?Distributed trace spans when configured and exported.Complete coverage when sampling or export is disabled.
Did this business write commit?Read-back state and the transaction’s audit record.A guarantee inferred from a successful readiness probe.
Is the documented version deployed?Image tags/digests, rollout state, and runtime verification.Current deployment from a saved report.

Separate three kinds of statements

Implemented in source
The current code or manifest contains the behavior. Deployment and runtime checks are still separate.
Recorded verification
A dated report captures a particular environment and application version. Re-run the relevant checks after changes.
Design extension
A proposed capability such as sharding, multi-society onboarding, or a stronger cross-service consistency mechanism.

Use low-cardinality measurements

Route names, operation names, and bounded outcomes make useful metric labels. Member IDs, session IDs, idempotency keys, and arbitrary request values create unbounded series and can expose sensitive context. Put an appropriate request identifier in logs or trace context instead.

Discuss a design change with its cost

A read replica can offload selected reads but introduces lag and routing rules. More service replicas add execution capacity but increase downstream connections. A cache reduces repeated reads but adds expiration, invalidation, and outage behavior. Stronger consistency may require serialized work, compensation, or a workflow redesign. Explain the requirement that justifies the extra mechanism.

Project questions · explanation and verification

Throughput, latency, readiness, liveness, and related terms

Throughput is how much work completes in a period. Latency is how long one operation takes. A service can complete 100 requests per second while each request takes 200 milliseconds because requests overlap.

TermPlain meaning and exampleHow to interpret or verify it
ThroughputCompleted work per unit of time, for example 100 requests/second.State the measurement window, workload, and whether failures count. Successful throughput can fall even when the incoming request rate rises.
Latency / response timeTime for one measured operation, for example 200 ms from sending a request until receiving its response.Name the boundary: client, Gateway, service, or database. Compare p50, p95, and p99 rather than only the average.
p50 / p95 / p99The 50th, 95th, or 99th percentile of measured durations. A p95 of 400 ms means approximately 95% of observations are at or below that duration.Tail latency shows slow requests hidden by an average; report enough samples and the measurement period.
ConcurrencyOperations in progress at the same time.With a stable flow, average in-flight work is approximately throughput × average time in the same measured system. At 100 requests/s and 0.2 s, that is about 20 requests.
ReadinessWhether an instance should receive normal Service traffic now.A failing readiness probe marks the Pod unready; it does not by itself restart the container.
LivenessWhether a container is considered alive enough to keep running.Repeated liveness failures cause Kubernetes to restart the container according to its restart policy. Do not tie it indiscriminately to remote dependencies.
Startup probeAllows an application to finish startup before normal readiness/liveness probing takes over.While startup probing is active, the other probes wait. Persistent startup failure can also cause a restart.
AvailabilityHow reliably the required service is usable over the measured period.Define success and the measurement method. A single HTTP 200 or an UP health result does not establish long-term availability.
Error rateThe proportion of requests that fail according to the chosen success definition.Separate client validation failures, authorization failures, rate limits, and server/dependency failures.
SaturationHow close a limited resource is to its usable capacity.Check CPU, memory, connection pools, queues, and rejected work—not CPU alone.
TimeoutA bound on a particular wait.A connect timeout, HTTP read timeout, and outer proxy timeout measure different waits; none automatically acts as one shared deadline.
Backpressure / bounded admissionLimiting incoming or concurrent work when a resource cannot keep up.Member’s dependency bulkhead and Society’s bounded cache fallback reject excess work instead of permitting unlimited waiting.
SLO / SLAAn SLO is an operational target; an SLA is a service agreement with its stated obligations.The presence of metrics in this repository does not establish a contractual SLA.

Which dependencies affect readiness here?

ApplicationConfigured readiness groupMeaning
GatewayreadinessState, redisMain Redis health participates in Gateway readiness.
MemberreadinessState, db, redisIts database and main Redis health participate. Society availability is not included in this readiness group.
SocietyreadinessState, dbIts database participates. Cache Redis is deliberately absent from this readiness group.

The Java workload manifests use /actuator/health/readiness for readiness and /actuator/health/liveness for liveness/startup. The liveness group is based on application liveness state rather than the readiness dependency list.

How to verify without simulating an outage

kubectl --context docker-desktop get pods -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop describe deployment member-service -n emerald-housing
# With the Gateway port-forward running:
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/actuator/health/readiness
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/actuator/health/liveness

Those localhost checks inspect Gateway. Inspect each backend’s probe configuration and its own health endpoint through a separate port-forward when needed. Actual thresholds, restart counts, and events distinguish an unready Pod from a restarting container.

Sources: the three applications’ application.yml files and the Member, Society, and Gateway manifests.
