Implementation referenceSee how the implemented design fits together

See how the implemented design fits together

This phase gathers the system design ideas already present in Emerald Housing and connects each one to its implementation. It describes the current project architecture and its operational boundaries.

Repository architecture: images, runtime and request paths

How to read the application code: inside each Spring service, the usual request path is controller → service → repository → its owned database. Member additionally calls Society over authenticated HTTP when it needs to verify a flat. These are code layers inside the service Pods, not separate Kubernetes services.

Checked against base Kustomize resources, NGINX HTTPRoutes, runtime service and database settings, Gateway API routes, and observability overlay.

Complete system architecture

Complete request flow and key outcomes

Implemented system design features

Design areaImplemented featureTradeoff or boundary
Service decompositionSociety Service owns building, floor, and flat APIs; Member Service owns members, memberships, and admin credentials; Spring Cloud Gateway routes client traffic.Services can be deployed separately, while cross-service workflows need explicit communication and failure handling.
Data ownershipSociety and Member each use a separate PostgreSQL database with JPA and Flyway migrations.There is no single transaction spanning both databases; each service remains responsible for its own records.
Synchronous service validationMember Service uses an authenticated RestClient request to check a flat with Society before saving a membership.Assignment depends on Society availability and has a consistency window between validation and Member's local write.
Layered API routingNGINX Gateway Fabric is configured as the external HTTPS edge; Spring Cloud Gateway routes API requests to internal services.Two routing layers add configuration and troubleshooting work, with separate edge and application-routing responsibilities.
Shared state and cacheOne Redis service backs Gateway sessions, rate limits, and Member locks; a separate Redis service backs Society's cache-aside reference reads.Both Redis services are single-replica learning deployments, not highly available production clusters.
Resilience and safe retriesMember-to-Society reads have timeouts, bounded retries, a circuit breaker, and a bulkhead. Member creation supports durable idempotency keys.Retries apply to selected transient reads; mutations are not automatically retried. Circuit-breaker state is per Member Pod.
Orchestration and scalingKubernetes Deployments, Services, health probes, resource requests/limits, CPU-based HPA manifests, NetworkPolicies, and Kustomize resources are present.HPA needs a metrics provider and cluster runtime behavior still requires verification.
Security controlsAdmin sessions, CSRF checks, backend session validation, service credentials, Secret references, and network boundaries are configured.Session continuity depends on Redis; external HTTPS and NetworkPolicy behavior depend on the installed cluster components.
Operations and observabilityCorrelation IDs, Actuator health and Prometheus metrics, sampled OpenTelemetry traces, readiness/liveness probes, graceful shutdown, and controlled error responses are implemented. An optional Prometheus, Grafana, Loki, Tempo, and Alloy overlay centralizes metrics, dashboards, logs, and traces.The overlay uses ephemeral storage for learning and requires cluster deployment; persistent production storage and runtime verification remain operational work. Setup: kubernetes-observability/ and Operations recommendations guide.
Current request path: client → NGINX HTTPS edge → Spring Cloud Gateway → owning backend service → its PostgreSQL database. For flat assignment, Member Service also calls Society Service and only writes after Society confirms the flat.

This overview summarizes features documented in the related phases. It does not claim unimplemented database replicas, a Saga workflow, Kafka messaging, gRPC, or production-grade Redis/PostgreSQL high availability.

Implementation referenceDesign recommendations: implementation and operations

Design recommendations: implementation and operations

Converted from the operations recommendations guide.

Design recommendations: implementation and operations

This runbook turns the selected microservice recommendations into repeatable repo workflows. Existing application features such as service-owned databases, synchronous assignment validation, Redis caching and membership locks remain in place. Messaging, sharding, a service mesh, and other conditional patterns stay out until a measured requirement calls for them.

Optional observability overlay

The base kubernetes/ package remains usable without a telemetry stack. kubernetes-observability/ layers on Prometheus, Grafana, Loki, Grafana Alloy, and Tempo:

  • Prometheus scrapes each annotated Pod, so replicas are reported separately. It retains seven days of local metrics.
  • Grafana provisions Prometheus, Loki, and Tempo data sources and an Emerald service dashboard.
  • Alloy collects namespace Pod logs through the Kubernetes API and receives OTLP traces from the three Spring applications.
  • Tempo and Loki use local ephemeral storage for this learning setup. Their data is lost when their Pods are replaced; production deployments should use persistent or object storage, authentication, retention planning, and resource sizing.
  • Spring traces use 10% sampling by default. The observability overlay enables OTLP export. Trace IDs and span IDs are included in application log lines.
  • /actuator/prometheus is exposed by the apps and permitted through the existing ingress policies only to the Prometheus Pod. NetworkPolicy enforcement depends on the cluster CNI.

Create the Grafana password Secret, then apply the overlay:

.\scripts\create-observability-secret.ps1
kubectl apply -k .\kubernetes-observability
kubectl wait -n emerald-housing --for=condition=Available deployment/prometheus deployment/grafana deployment/loki deployment/tempo deployment/grafana-alloy --timeout=240s
kubectl port-forward -n emerald-housing service/grafana 3000:3000

Open http://localhost:3000, sign in as admin with the password used by the script, and open Emerald Housing Service Overview. Prometheus and Grafana are reachable only through ClusterIP Services; use kubectl port-forward for local access.

Useful checks:

kubectl port-forward -n emerald-housing service/prometheus 9090:9090
Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:9090/api/v1/targets
kubectl logs -n emerald-housing deployment/grafana-alloy

Generate a trace with an API call, then search Tempo in Grafana by service name or trace ID. Generate a log by making a request that emits a service log, then search Loki with {namespace="emerald-housing"}. Sampling means a trace is not expected for every request.

Rolling update validation and rollback

Apply the application Deployment YAML, then check rollout progress and readiness. Inspect Pod events and logs if an update fails.

kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing --timeout=180s
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout history deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout undo deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing

Rollback restores a retained Pod template; update the repository YAML to match before applying it again.

Database capacity and replica operation

Read replicas remain disabled until PostgreSQL has an externally provisioned replica and observed read load justifies it. The dashboard shows per-Pod HTTP volume and Hikari active connections. Compare that with PostgreSQL connection usage and read/write load before changing MEMBER_DB_REPLICA_ENABLED or SOCIETY_DB_REPLICA_ENABLED.

Before enabling a replica:

  1. Provision PostgreSQL streaming replication and a dedicated least-privilege reader account outside this repo.
  2. Measure primary connections and read load during representative traffic; compare the result with pool maxima multiplied by the maximum Pod counts.
  3. Monitor replication lag on the database platform. On PostgreSQL, pg_stat_replication reports primary-side sender state; on a standby, pg_last_xact_replay_timestamp() helps estimate replay delay when writes have occurred.
  4. Enable the service-specific replica flag and JDBC URL only after the replica is healthy. Keep write and read-after-write decisions on the primary.
  5. If lag or replica health breaches the chosen service objective, disable routing and investigate before enabling it again. Application routing currently does not retry a failed replica query against the primary.

Secrets and deployment access

Application Pods do not need Kubernetes API credentials, so application Deployments disable automatic ServiceAccount token mounting. Prometheus and Alloy keep scoped, namespace-only ServiceAccounts for Pod discovery and log collection.

Kubernetes Secret values used as environment variables are read when a Pod starts. After rotating database or service credentials, update the Secret and roll the affected Deployment so it reads the new values. Use distinct credentials per service and least-privilege database accounts. A production environment should use an external secret manager and a controlled rotation process; Kubernetes Secret objects alone do not encrypt values at rest unless the cluster is configured to do so.

Recommendation decisions

AreaRepo action
DatabaseKeep database-per-service, explicit Hikari pools, constraints, and optional replica routing. Measure load and replica lag before enabling replicas.
CommunicationKeep the synchronous Member-to-Society assignment check. Add asynchronous messaging only for work that can complete later.
Service discoveryKeep Kubernetes Service DNS and endpoint balancing; no separate Eureka server.
API GatewayKeep routing, authentication, and rate limiting. No BFF until client APIs need distinct composition.
ResilienceKeep bounded timeouts, retries, circuit breaker, bulkhead, cache fallback, and rate limits; monitor behavior through Prometheus.
ConsistencyKeep database transactions/constraints, primary reads for freshness-sensitive decisions, Redis membership locking, and row locking.
DeploymentUse RollingUpdate; verify readiness and rollout status, and use rollout undo when a retained revision is needed.
ObservabilityOptional centralized metrics, logs, and traces are provided by the observability overlay.
SecurityKeep Gateway and backend authorization, internal credentials, NetworkPolicies, and disable unused Pod API tokens. Rotate secrets with Pod restarts.
CachingKeep Redis cache-aside; measure before adding new cache tiers or changing database read routing.
DecompositionKeep Society and Member ownership boundaries; strengthen domain translation only if their contracts begin evolving independently.
ConfigurationKeep environment-driven settings, ConfigMaps, and Secret references; adopt a vault/central config service when rotation or coordinated runtime changes require it.
Implementation reference9. Verification and rollout flow

9. Verification and rollout flow

  1. Verify contracts: run the shared Member consumer and Society provider checks.
  2. Run service and gateway tests: include access scope, HTTP preconditions, cursor behavior and account/session tests. PostgreSQL integration tests require Docker; skipped tests do not demonstrate database correctness.
  3. Build all three images: the new principal representation and internal scope lookup require compatible Gateway and Member versions.
  4. Deploy with existing databases: let Flyway apply the new migrations. Do not delete PostgreSQL data as a deployment step.
  5. Sign in again: the Redis session representation changed. Verify each role, account disablement and resident scope after rollout.
  6. Enable strict conditional writes later: first update clients to preserve and send ETags.
powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File .\scripts\test-contracts.ps1
.\services\member-service\mvnw.cmd -B -f .\services\member-service\pom.xml test
.\services\society-service\mvnw.cmd -B -f .\services\society-service\pom.xml test
.\services\society-service\mvnw.cmd -B -f .\infrastructure\api-gateway\pom.xml test
Implementation versus operational proof: these sections describe source behavior. They do not assert that your live cluster has the new images, that tests passed in this documentation update, or that production load, recovery and security checks have been completed.

Future business phase: maintenance bills, payment tracking and receipts remain proposed work. Multi-society tenancy, automatic audit retention and online payments are not supplied by these code changes.

Detailed reference: backend access and query feature notes.

Scenario questionsCorrelation IDs, metrics, and distributed tracing

Correlation IDs, metrics, and distributed tracing

Questions, explanation, and solution

Question: How do correlation IDs, metrics, and traces complement each other?

Answer: A correlation/trace ID links one request's logs; metrics summarize rates, errors, and latency over time; distributed traces show spans and delays across service boundaries. Propagate W3C traceparent or a validated request ID, and keep metric labels bounded.

Example: logger.info("membership created traceId={} memberId={}", traceId, memberId); do not use trace IDs, member IDs, or session IDs as metric labels.

Follow-up: What is the difference between a correlation ID and a trace ID?

Answer: A correlation ID is an application-level identifier used to connect related logs and may cover a business workflow. A trace ID identifies a distributed trace with timed spans. Propagate standard trace context across services and use IDs in logs, not as metric dimensions.

Chapter 11

Run, call, and verify

Choose a runtime, sign in, exercise APIs, and investigate failures.

Choose one runtime and verify what already exists

Option A runs the Java applications locally; Option B runs them on Kubernetes with local access through Gateway port-forwarding. Read the detailed setup for the chosen option. Reuse a healthy environment and its credentials rather than treating reset commands as normal startup.

Inspect the Kubernetes environment

kubectl --context docker-desktop get deployments,pods,services -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop get events -n emerald-housing --sort-by=.lastTimestamp

These commands read the existing Docker Desktop cluster. They do not establish that database credentials, images, or optional edge resources are correct; use rollout status and the relevant logs to investigate failures.

Connect to Gateway

kubectl --context docker-desktop port-forward service/api-gateway 8080:8080 -n emerald-housing

Keep that terminal open. In another terminal, check readiness:

Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/actuator/health/readiness

Exercise a complete client workflow

  1. Import the repository’s collection and environment. Supply private account values locally.
  2. Obtain CSRF material, log in, and refresh CSRF material where the collection requires it.
  3. Read one permitted record and inspect its representation and ETag.
  4. Perform one authorized change, then read it back and inspect its audit history where permitted.
  5. Repeat with a restricted identity and an out-of-scope identifier to verify the access boundary.
  6. For a cache exercise, compare cold and repeated reads through available metrics; do not infer a cache hit from speed alone.

Keep verification focused

Run the checks affected by a change. Source parsing validates an artifact; tests validate their assertions; a rollout validates deployment progress; live requests validate the exercised runtime behavior. Record the date, version, environment, request, and expected result so another person can reproduce the evidence.

Commands are reference material

This page does not execute its examples. Destructive cleanup and rollback procedures in the detailed setup are exceptional recovery actions, not prerequisites for reading or verifying the system.

Implementation referenceRun from IntelliJ IDEA: local and Kubernetes setup

Run from IntelliJ IDEA: local and Kubernetes setup

Choose Option A (local Java) or Option B (Kubernetes). Check the current applications before changing them. Keep a healthy existing environment and its credentials. Option B has an optional destructive reset; it is not a normal startup or verification step. External PostgreSQL databases are preserved.

0. Start here every time

  1. Stop previously running Java applications and port-forwards with Ctrl+C in their terminals. Close old terminal tabs.
  2. Start Docker Desktop with Linux containers and start PostgreSQL.
  3. In IntelliJ IDEA, set Settings > Tools > Terminal > Shell path to powershell.exe -NoLogo -NoProfile. Open new Terminal tabs. Use the Terminal tool window for all commands below.

If your terminal currently shows a CMD prompt, run this once to enter PowerShell:

powershell.exe -NoLogo -NoProfile

In a new PowerShell tab, check the shell, folder and tools:

Set-Location 'D:\Devendra Ghag Interview Preparation\02_Spring\emerald-housing'

docker info

Expected: PowerShell 5.1 or newer, Java 21, and Docker server information. If a command is missing, install/configure that tool and restart IntelliJ. The green Run button does not inherit variables from these terminals.

Now run the following inside psql, not PowerShell. Run CREATE statements only when that role/database does not already exist; inspect with \du and \l.

\password emerald

CREATE DATABASE member_db OWNER emerald;

function New-Secret {
    $bytes = New-Object byte[] 32
    $rng = [System.Security.Cryptography.RandomNumberGenerator]::Create()
    $rng.GetBytes($bytes)
    $rng.Dispose()
    [Convert]::ToBase64String($bytes)
}
Write-Host "GATEWAY_AUTHENTICATION_TOKEN=$(New-Secret)"
Write-Host "SESSION_VALIDATION_TOKEN=$(New-Secret)"
Write-Host "MEMBER_TO_SOCIETY_TOKEN=$(New-Secret)"

Option B: Kubernetes, simple localhost setup

Verified 24 October 2026: all five workloads Ready; 111 automated Postman requests and 123 assertions passed with zero failures, including after fresh-tag redeployment. See verification notes, Postman results and environment status. These are verification snapshots; rerun the documented checks after changes.

Use Docker Desktop Kubernetes with the localhost HTTP Gateway port-forward. Verify an existing deployment first; do not delete its namespace or rotate working credentials. For a first deployment, use B1 through B9. B0 is an optional reset only.

Repeatable setup without deleting data: The helper below preserves existing Secrets and external databases, builds all three current applications with a unique image tag, and waits for all five workloads. If the database Secret is missing, provide a private JSON file containing society-username, society-password, member-username and member-password. Keep that file outside this repository. It must contain the existing database credentials; the helper does not reset passwords or create replacement databases.
powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File .\scripts\kubernetes\deploy-option-b.ps1 -BuildImages -DatabaseCredentialsFile "$env:TEMP/emerald-db-private.json"

If the database Secret already exists and is correct, omit -DatabaseCredentialsFile. If you only want to verify an already healthy environment, start with the B9 readiness check and the automated Postman run below.

B0. Optional destructive reset: skip for normal startup

Deletes existing project resources: This removes the entire emerald-housing namespace, including its Pods, Deployments, Services, Secrets, ConfigMaps, policies and any other resources in that namespace. All project requests stop. Redis sessions, cache and locks are discarded. Other namespaces, Docker images and external PostgreSQL databases are preserved. Only run this reset when you explicitly want to replace the namespace. This is a fresh Kubernetes deployment, not a database reset.

First stop old port-forwards and local Java applications with Ctrl+C in their terminal tabs. Start Docker Desktop and PostgreSQL. Enable Kubernetes in Docker Desktop and wait for it to be ready. Open a new IntelliJ PowerShell terminal and run:

Set-Location 'D:\Devendra Ghag Interview Preparation\02_Spring\emerald-housing'
kubectl config use-context docker-desktop
kubectl config current-context
kubectl --context docker-desktop get nodes

Check before deleting: current-context must print docker-desktop and the node must be Ready. The following command targets only that cluster's emerald-housing namespace:

kubectl --context docker-desktop delete namespace emerald-housing --ignore-not-found=true --wait=true --timeout=300s

Wait for deletion to finish. If it times out or the namespace remains Terminating, stop and inspect it; do not remove finalizers blindly:

kubectl --context docker-desktop get namespace emerald-housing

Expected after cleanup: NotFound. This is the one expected error here: it confirms the old namespace is gone. If it never existed, the delete command safely skips it. Continue with B1 to recreate it. Do not run the cleanup command again after B1.

YOUR INPUT later: B2 asks for the existing PostgreSQL usernames/passwords. B3 generates new application admin credentials; save the printed values for Postman. Existing database rows and old admin accounts remain; this walkthrough creates a new admin account. No database deletion is included.

B1. Terminal 1: select Docker Desktop Kubernetes

First enable Kubernetes in Docker Desktop Settings and wait until it reports running. Both Docker Desktop kind and kubeadm provisioning can be used with its containerd image store. No separate kind installation is required. This host uses Docker Desktop kind; local-image startup was verified on it. Build using the desktop-linux Docker context. See Docker Desktop Kubernetes documentation.

Set-Location 'D:\Devendra Ghag Interview Preparation\02_Spring\emerald-housing'
docker info
kubectl version --client
kubectl config use-context docker-desktop
kubectl config current-context
kubectl get nodes

Required: current-context must print docker-desktop and the node must be Ready. If the context is missing, finish enabling Kubernetes in Docker Desktop before continuing. Stop on any error.

kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\namespace.yaml

Before changes or when checking progress, inspect the current applications:

kubectl --context docker-desktop get pods -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop get deployments -n emerald-housing

1/1 Running means ready. 0/1 Running needs time or readiness investigation. CrashLoopBackOff needs startup logs. ImagePullBackOff needs image/event checks. Pending needs Pod/event inspection. No restart is needed merely because an application is already healthy.

B2. Terminal 1: enter database credentials

YOUR INPUT: This local setup uses the PostgreSQL username emerald for both society_db and member_db, with the same existing password. Enter that password at the single hidden prompt below. This updates the Kubernetes connection Secret only; it does not change PostgreSQL passwords. Once the Secret is correct, preserve it on subsequent starts.
$societyUser = 'emerald'
$memberUser = 'emerald'
$databasePassword = [System.Net.NetworkCredential]::new('', (Read-Host 'Existing PostgreSQL password for emerald (both databases)' -AsSecureString)).Password
$societyPassword = $databasePassword
$memberPassword = $databasePassword
kubectl --context docker-desktop create secret generic emerald-db-credentials -n emerald-housing --from-literal="society-username=$societyUser" --from-literal="society-password=$societyPassword" --from-literal="member-username=$memberUser" --from-literal="member-password=$memberPassword" --dry-run=client -o yaml | kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f -
Database password rejected or psql not recognized?

Use this when logs show password authentication failed. Verify the database password first; it is different from the application admin password.

psql -h localhost -U emerald -d society_db -W

YOUR INPUT: enter the database password. After successful login, exit psql:

\q
psql -h localhost -U emerald -d member_db -W

Exit this session with \q too. If psql is unrecognized, find your version under C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL. YOUR INPUT: replace 18 below with your installed folder version.

& 'C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL\18\bin\psql.exe' -h localhost -U emerald -d society_db -W

If PostgreSQL rejects the password, correct or reset it through your PostgreSQL administrator before continuing. If local verification succeeds but Pods reject it, confirm POSTGRES_HOST targets the same server. Rerun B2 with the verified credentials, then restart both consumers:

kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout restart deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout restart deployment/member-service -n emerald-housing

No Docker image rebuild is needed for a Secret change. Wait for both rollouts using B6.

B3. First deployment only: generate security values

If emerald-security-credentials already exists and is working, skip B3 and preserve it. Only the first deployment needs this generation block. It creates matching internal tokens, separate Redis passwords and an application administrator.

function New-Secret {
    $bytes = New-Object byte[] 32
    $rng = [System.Security.Cryptography.RandomNumberGenerator]::Create()
    $rng.GetBytes($bytes)
    $rng.Dispose()
    [Convert]::ToBase64String($bytes)
}
$adminUser = 'admin-' + [Guid]::NewGuid().ToString('N')
$adminPassword = [Guid]::NewGuid().ToString('N')
$redisPassword = New-Secret
$cachePassword = New-Secret
$gatewayToken = New-Secret
$sessionToken = New-Secret
$memberToken = New-Secret
kubectl --context docker-desktop create secret generic emerald-security-credentials -n emerald-housing --from-literal="admin-username=$adminUser" --from-literal="admin-password=$adminPassword" --from-literal="redis-password=$redisPassword" --from-literal="redis-cache-password=$cachePassword" --from-literal="gateway-authentication-token=$gatewayToken" --from-literal="session-validation-token=$sessionToken" --from-literal="member-to-society-token=$memberToken" --dry-run=client -o yaml | kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f -
Write-Host "Postman adminUsername: $adminUser"
Write-Host "Postman adminPassword: $adminPassword"
YOUR INPUT: Save the printed adminUsername and adminPassword privately for Postman. On this fresh-start path all generated credentials rotate; complete the remaining restart steps before using the apps. Existing database records are retained.

B4. Terminal 1: build current images with a unique tag

$imageTag = 'option-b-' + (Get-Date -Format 'yyyyMMddHHmmss')
docker --context desktop-linux build -t "emerald/society-service:$imageTag" .\services\society-service
docker --context desktop-linux build -t "emerald/member-service:$imageTag" .\services\member-service
docker --context desktop-linux build -t "emerald/api-gateway:$imageTag" .\infrastructure\api-gateway

Wait until all three images build successfully. Do not continue after a build error. Build them in the Docker Desktop desktop-linux engine and keep the $imageTag value in this terminal for B6 and B7. If Pods report ImagePullBackOff, check that these exact image tags exist with docker images and that Docker Desktop uses the kubeadm Kubernetes provisioning method.

Use a new image tag after source changes. A restart with an unchanged tag and IfNotPresent can reuse an older node image. The current Member source already contains the REST client starter. Account/session changes require rebuilding Society, Member and Gateway together.

B5. Terminal 1: configure and start Redis

YOUR INPUT: Usually none: the ConfigMap uses host.docker.internal for PostgreSQL. If your database is elsewhere, update POSTGRES_HOST in kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml before this step. PostgreSQL must accept connections from Docker: check listen_addresses, pg_hba.conf and the firewall for your actual network.
kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\config\application-config.yaml
kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\redis\
kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\redis-cache\

kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/redis -n emerald-housing --timeout=180s
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/redis-cache -n emerald-housing --timeout=180s

Both must finish successfully. Restarting these development Redis instances clears sessions/cache/locks; use this sequence without active traffic.

B6. Terminal 1: start Society and Member

B4 already built all three applications. Apply the workloads, then select that unique image tag. Do not reset database passwords or delete the namespace to fix an image or dependency problem.

kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\society\
kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\member\
kubectl --context docker-desktop set image deployment/society-service "society-service=emerald/society-service:$imageTag" -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop set image deployment/member-service "member-service=emerald/member-service:$imageTag" -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing --timeout=300s
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/member-service -n emerald-housing --timeout=300s

Both rollouts must succeed before B7. Run kubectl --context docker-desktop get pods -n emerald-housing and confirm Society and Member are 1/1 Running. The latest Member logs should show Started MemberServiceApplication; readiness should return HTTP 200. Check the current Pod logs, since older failed Pods or previous-container logs can still show the already-fixed error. No password changes or namespace deletion are needed for the REST client fix. If the same error persists, inspect the failing Pod logs and verify its image tag before continuing.

Rollout waits for old replicas, or Society/Member repeatedly restart?

Old replicas pending termination means the rollout has not completed. Check the replacement Pods before deleting anything:

kubectl --context docker-desktop get pods -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop logs deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing --tail=100
kubectl --context docker-desktop logs deployment/member-service -n emerald-housing --tail=100

If multiple Pods exist, inspect the specific failing one. YOUR INPUT: replace PASTE_POD_NAME with the name from get pods:

kubectl --context docker-desktop logs PASTE_POD_NAME -n emerald-housing --tail=100
kubectl --context docker-desktop logs PASTE_POD_NAME -n emerald-housing --previous --tail=100
kubectl --context docker-desktop describe pod PASTE_POD_NAME -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop get events -n emerald-housing --sort-by=.lastTimestamp

Use --previous only when a previous container instance exists. Connection refused from a startup probe means the app was not listening yet; the application logs identify why. Do not force-delete old Pods as the first fix.

B7. Terminal 1: start Gateway for localhost HTTP

kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\gateway\config.yaml
kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\gateway\deployment.yaml
kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\gateway\service.yaml
kubectl --context docker-desktop set image deployment/api-gateway "api-gateway=emerald/api-gateway:$imageTag" -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop set env deployment/api-gateway SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=false -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout restart deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing --timeout=300s
kubectl --context docker-desktop get pods -n emerald-housing

Wait for Running/Ready Pods. This localhost HTTP path does not require the optional NGINX edge or HPA setup. If your existing cluster already has NetworkPolicies, those continue to apply.

Gateway rollout is stuck: inspect logs and fix the Redis port error

First inspect Gateway startup:

kubectl --context docker-desktop get pods -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop logs deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing --tail=100

To follow new messages (Ctrl+C stops viewing, not Gateway):

kubectl --context docker-desktop logs -f deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing

If it restarted, use the specific Pod's --previous logs as shown under B6. If logs say Redis port is tcp://...:6379, apply the corrected Gateway ConfigMap: it now explicitly sets REDIS_HOST=redis and REDIS_PORT=6379.

kubectl --context docker-desktop apply -f .\kubernetes\gateway\config.yaml
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout restart deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing --timeout=300s

No image rebuild or password input is needed for this ConfigMap fix. After any Gateway rollout, restart its port-forward if disconnected.

B8. Terminal 2: keep Gateway port-forward running

kubectl --context docker-desktop port-forward service/api-gateway 8080:8080 -n emerald-housing

Leave this terminal open. If port 8080 is occupied, stop the old local Gateway or old port-forward, then retry.

B9. Terminal 1: check readiness

Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/actuator/health/readiness

Expect UP. Also confirm Society, Member, Gateway, Redis and Redis Cache are all ready. Gateway readiness alone does not verify backend authentication or business endpoints. Open the society portal, then run the automated Postman checks below.

Unable to connect to localhost:8080?

The Gateway port-forward must remain running in Terminal 2. If you restarted Gateway, the previous forward may have disconnected. For this HTTP walkthrough keep the cookie setting false:

kubectl --context docker-desktop set env deployment/api-gateway SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=false -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing --timeout=300s

After rollout succeeds, stop any old forward with Ctrl+C and rerun B8 in Terminal 2. Then rerun B9 in Terminal 1. If port 8080 is occupied, stop the previous local Gateway or port-forward. The cookie setting affects login; a connection failure first needs a running Pod and active port-forward.

Optional HTTPS switch only: skip this during the localhost HTTP walkthrough

Complete the Phase 8 HTTPS edge setup first. Only then remove the HTTP cookie override:

kubectl --context docker-desktop set env deployment/api-gateway SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE- -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop rollout status deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing --timeout=300s

This restores the ConfigMap's Secure cookie setting and triggers a rollout. Use the configured HTTPS address for authenticated requests. Do not run these commands while following localhost HTTP steps.

B10. When to run which command

SituationAction
Java source or Maven dependency changedBuild a new image, deploy its tag, then check rollout.
Database Secret changedRestart Society and Member; check both rollouts. No image rebuild.
Gateway ConfigMap changedApply the file, restart Gateway, then check rollout.
kubectl set env or set image changed the Pod templateA rollout starts automatically. Wait for readiness; an extra restart is normally unnecessary.
Rollout stuck or container restartingCheck Pods, current/previous logs, describe and events.
localhost cannot connectCheck Gateway is ready and restart the port-forward.
All Pods are 1/1 RunningContinue to readiness and Postman login checks.

Postman: automated Option B verification

Two separate logins: PostgreSQL emerald is used by the backend Pods. Postman signs in as the application administrator provisioned by Member Service. The automatic runner reads that application login from emerald-security-credentials; do not put the PostgreSQL password in Postman adminPassword.

The collection now has an Option B - Automated verification folder. It captures CSRF tokens, IDs, versions and ETags, and verifies independent administrator, owner and family sessions. It creates uniquely named test records and disables its test accounts. Existing business records are not modified. Original exploratory, outage, load and HTTPS requests are preserved under Manual reference (opt-in) and skipped by default.

npm.cmd install --prefix "$env:TEMP/emerald-postman-runner" --no-audit --no-fund newman
node .\scripts\run-postman-option-b.cjs

Keep B8 running. The runner reads the provisioned admin credentials from the Kubernetes Secret in memory; no credential is printed or written into the committed environment JSON. It writes only a sanitized docs/verification/option-b/option-b-verification-summary.json. Success means every request and assertion passes and the process exits 0. Review failures and rerun; readiness alone is not sufficient.

For a ready-to-import private environment, run the export command below. It writes the provisioned application login to your temporary directory, outside this repository. Import the printed file and select Emerald Housing - Option B Localhost (private).

node .\scripts\run-postman-option-b.cjs --export-environment

For the Postman UI, import the collection and Emerald-Housing-Option-B.postman_environment.json, choose Emerald Housing - Option B Localhost, privately fill the admin credentials from B3, and run only the automated folder in order. Keep cookies enabled. Leave runManualRequests=false. Optional HTTPS and deliberate outages require their separate setup and are not part of simple localhost verification.

Use Gateway for protected APIs. Direct backend health requests on 8081/8082 apply only to Option A; Option B exposes only Gateway in this walkthrough. Run manual 401/403 negative tests separately. Mutations need the current CSRF token and session cookies.

If a step fails

Stop at the first failing step. For local Java, read that service terminal's error. For Kubernetes, use:

kubectl --context docker-desktop get pods -n emerald-housing
kubectl --context docker-desktop get events -n emerald-housing --sort-by=.lastTimestamp
kubectl --context docker-desktop logs deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing --tail=100
kubectl --context docker-desktop logs deployment/member-service -n emerald-housing --tail=100
kubectl --context docker-desktop logs deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing --tail=100

Database errors need the host, role, password and Flyway history checked. Missing Secret keys cause container configuration errors. Keep applied migration files unchanged; add a new migration for schema changes.

Stop after testing

Option A: Ctrl+C in Society, Member and Gateway tabs, then:

docker stop emerald-local-redis emerald-local-redis-cache

Option B: Ctrl+C stops the port-forward only; cluster workloads continue running. Start from step 0 for the next walkthrough. No database or cluster deletion is required.

Implementation referenceDocker basic commands

Docker basic commands

Docker images are packaged applications. Containers are running or stopped instances of those images. Start Docker Desktop before using these commands.

Check Docker version

Shows the Docker command-line version.

docker --version

Check Docker is running

Shows client and server details. A server connection error means Docker Desktop is not ready.

docker info

Check the Docker connection

Shows the selected Docker engine context. Use the Docker Desktop Linux engine for this project.

docker context show

List running containers

Shows containers currently running, their names and exposed ports.

docker ps

List all containers

Includes stopped containers. Check this before creating a container with an existing name.

docker ps -a

List images

Shows images already available on the selected Docker engine.

docker images

Download an image

Downloads Redis without starting a container.

docker pull redis:7.4-alpine

Create and start local Redis

First time only: creates the project session/lock Redis container. The published port is available only on localhost. Use the Redis setup in Option A for the full project configuration.

docker run -d --name emerald-local-redis -p 127.0.0.1:6379:6379 redis:7.4-alpine redis-server --maxmemory 128mb --maxmemory-policy noeviction

Stop a container

Stops local Redis and interrupts apps using it. Run after testing.

docker stop emerald-local-redis

Start an existing container

Starts a container previously created with docker run. It does not create another container.

docker start emerald-local-redis

Restart a container

Stops and starts the existing Redis container. This development setup can lose sessions and locks; do this without active traffic.

docker restart emerald-local-redis

Read container logs

Shows the messages printed by Redis.

docker logs emerald-local-redis

Follow new log messages

Keeps showing new messages. Ctrl+C stops viewing logs, not the container.

docker logs -f emerald-local-redis

Check Redis responds

Runs redis-cli inside the Option A Redis container. Expected answer: PONG. This command assumes the local Redis has no password.

docker exec emerald-local-redis redis-cli ping

Build the Society image

Run the folder command first. Docker reads the service Dockerfile and builds the exact tag used by Kubernetes. Option B lists the other two builds.

Set-Location 'D:\Devendra Ghag Interview Preparation\02_Spring\emerald-housing'
docker build -t emerald/society-service:step4-audit .\services\society-service

Inspect container details

Shows configuration, network and state details. Output may include environment values, so avoid sharing secrets.

docker inspect emerald-local-redis

See resource usage

Shows live CPU and memory usage. Ctrl+C exits this display.

docker stats

Remove a stopped container (optional cleanup)

Deletes this container and its writable-layer data. Run only if you intend to recreate it; stop it first. This is not required for a normal fresh start.

docker stop emerald-local-redis
docker rm emerald-local-redis

Remove an unused project image (optional cleanup)

Deletes the local Society image when no container needs it. Rebuild it before the next deployment.

docker rmi emerald/society-service:step4-audit
Implementation referenceKubernetes basic commands

Kubernetes basic commands

These examples use Docker Desktop Kubernetes and namespace emerald-housing. A context selects the cluster. A namespace groups project resources. A Deployment manages application Pods; a Service gives Pods a stable network address. Enable Kubernetes in Docker Desktop and wait for it to be ready.

Before changing resources: run the context commands below. Continue only when current-context prints docker-desktop. Commands use the currently selected context. Replace PASTE_POD_NAME with an actual name from kubectl get pods -n emerald-housing.

Check kubectl version

Shows the client version without requiring a running cluster.

kubectl version --client

List cluster contexts

Shows the clusters configured on this computer. An asterisk marks the selected context.

kubectl config get-contexts

Select Docker Desktop

Changes the cluster used by subsequent kubectl commands. Stop if this context does not exist.

kubectl config use-context docker-desktop

Confirm the selected cluster

Must print docker-desktop for this guide.

kubectl config current-context

Check cluster connection

Shows cluster endpoint information.

kubectl cluster-info

Check nodes

Shows cluster machines. Wait until the Docker Desktop node is Ready.

kubectl get nodes

List namespaces

Shows project areas in the current cluster.

kubectl get namespaces

Create the project namespace

Run from the repository root. Applying the existing file is safe to repeat.

Set-Location 'D:\Devendra Ghag Interview Preparation\02_Spring\emerald-housing'
kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\namespace.yaml

List Deployments

Shows desired and ready application replicas.

kubectl get deployments -n emerald-housing

List Pods

Shows application instances and their status. Copy a Pod name from this output when needed.

kubectl get pods -n emerald-housing

Watch Pods start

Continuously displays changes. Ctrl+C stops watching without stopping Pods.

kubectl get pods -n emerald-housing --watch

List Services

Shows stable network addresses and ports. ClusterIP addresses are normally internal to Kubernetes.

kubectl get services -n emerald-housing

List ConfigMaps

Shows configuration resource names; it does not show their complete contents.

kubectl get configmaps -n emerald-housing

List Secrets

Shows Secret names and metadata, not the stored passwords. Do not print or share Secret contents.

kubectl get secrets -n emerald-housing

Apply application configuration

Creates or updates the ConfigMap from its file. Existing Pods using environment variables need a restart to receive changes.

kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\config\application-config.yaml

Apply a service workload

Creates or updates Society Deployment and Service. Complete Option B secrets, configuration and images first.

kubectl apply -f .\kubernetes\society\

Wait for Society rollout

Waits until the Deployment is ready, or reports a timeout. A timeout needs investigation.

kubectl rollout status deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing --timeout=300s

Read Society logs

Shows logs from a Society Pod selected by the Deployment. Use a Pod name instead if investigating a specific replica.

kubectl logs deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing

Read Member logs

Helps diagnose database, provisioning and dependency startup errors.

kubectl logs deployment/member-service -n emerald-housing

Read Gateway logs

Helps diagnose routing, Redis and authentication errors.

kubectl logs deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing

Follow Gateway logs

Streams new log messages. Ctrl+C stops viewing.

kubectl logs -f deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing

Describe one Pod

YOUR INPUT: replace PASTE_POD_NAME. Shows state, probes, image errors and recent Pod events.

kubectl describe pod PASTE_POD_NAME -n emerald-housing

Read logs from the previous container

YOUR INPUT: replace PASTE_POD_NAME. Useful after a container restarts; it requires previous-container logs to exist.

kubectl logs PASTE_POD_NAME -n emerald-housing --previous

List recent namespace events

Shows scheduling, image pull and startup messages.

kubectl get events -n emerald-housing --sort-by=.lastTimestamp

Restart Society

Starts a rolling replacement of Society Pods, for example after updating environment-backed configuration.

kubectl rollout restart deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing

Restart Member

Starts a rolling replacement of Member Pods.

kubectl rollout restart deployment/member-service -n emerald-housing

Restart Gateway

Starts a rolling replacement of Gateway Pods. A port-forward may need restarting afterward.

kubectl rollout restart deployment/api-gateway -n emerald-housing

Access Gateway from localhost

Keep this terminal open. In the simple Option B HTTP setup, Postman uses http://localhost:8080. Ctrl+C stops forwarding only.

kubectl port-forward service/api-gateway 8080:8080 -n emerald-housing

Check Gateway readiness

Run in another PowerShell terminal while port-forward is running. Expected status: UP.

Invoke-RestMethod http://localhost:8080/actuator/health/readiness

View rollout history

Shows retained Society Deployment revisions.

kubectl rollout history deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing

Undo the last Society rollout (only when needed)

Restores the previous retained Pod template. It does not undo database migrations or external ConfigMap/Secret changes. Check rollout status afterward.

kubectl rollout undo deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing
kubectl rollout status deployment/society-service -n emerald-housing --timeout=300s

Set Society to two replicas

Requests two Society Pods. If an HPA is installed, it may change this count automatically.

kubectl scale deployment/society-service --replicas=2 -n emerald-housing

Check autoscalers

Lists HPAs if installed. No resources found is expected when optional autoscaling has not been configured.

kubectl get hpa -n emerald-housing
Source documentdocs/postman-option-b.md

docs/postman-option-b.md

Kubernetes localhost verification (Option B)

Use indexLatest.html#option-b. The Gateway is http://localhost:8080 through a Kubernetes port-forward. Both backend services and both Redis workloads must also be Ready before full API verification.

The postman directory contains exactly these two import files:

  • Emerald-Housing-Option-B.postman_collection.json
  • Emerald-Housing-Option-B.postman_environment.json

Documentation lives in docs/postman-option-b.md. Generated reports live in docs/verification/option-b/; running verification does not add files to postman.

The collection contains:

  • Option B - Automated verification: 111 ordered requests, including CSRF/login, validation failures, flat/member CRUD, conditional requests, idempotency, membership history, custom roles, multiple-role accounts, owner/family privacy, live permission changes, ownership transfer, audit, and account disablement.
  • Manual reference (opt-in): original exploratory requests, deliberate outages/load and optional HTTPS examples. These are skipped unless runManualRequests=true; their prerequisites are not part of Option B.

Install Newman outside the repository, then run from the repository root:

npm.cmd install --prefix "$env:TEMP/emerald-postman-runner" --no-audit --no-fund newman
node scripts/check-postman-option-b.cjs
node scripts/run-postman-option-b.cjs
node scripts/run-postman-option-b.cjs --all

The full runner reads the existing Kubernetes provisioning credentials into memory. Alternatively provide EMERALD_ADMIN_USERNAME and EMERALD_ADMIN_PASSWORD in the runner process environment. EMERALD_GATEWAY_URL overrides the localhost base URL. No real credential belongs in the committed environment JSON.

The full run writes a sanitized docs/verification/option-b/option-b-verification-summary.json and exits nonzero on any failure. It never exports raw Newman request/response data, session cookies, or passwords. Test records use unique run identifiers; existing records are not modified. Test accounts are disabled at the end, and audit/ownership history is retained. Failed runs can leave their uniquely named test records for diagnosis.

--all verifies collection-wide execution as well: the same automated requests run and the manual reference requests are skipped. The runner always forces runManualRequests=false and checks that every expected automated request was sent.

node scripts/check-postman-option-b.cjs --gateway-smoke runs only readiness, anonymous CSRF/session reset, and anonymous access denial. Its option-b-gateway-smoke-summary.json is explicitly a partial check; it does not establish backend or business-endpoint health.

In the Postman UI, import both JSON files, select Emerald Housing - Option B Localhost, privately fill the admin credentials, enable cookies, and run the automated folder in order. Tokens, record IDs, ETags, versions, and separate owner/family sessions are captured automatically.

To generate an immediately usable private Postman environment with the provisioned application login:

node scripts/run-postman-option-b.cjs --export-environment

Import the printed file from the temporary directory and select Emerald Housing - Option B Localhost (private). This export contains the application admin credentials; it stays outside the repository. It does not contain the PostgreSQL password. Public environment JSON deliberately keeps credentials blank.

For deployment without namespace deletion:

powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File scripts/kubernetes/deploy-option-b.ps1 -BuildImages -DatabaseCredentialsFile "$env:TEMP/emerald-db-private.json"

The private database file must contain the four existing credential fields society-username, society-password, member-username, and member-password. Keep it outside the repository. If emerald-db-credentials already exists and is correct, omit this parameter. The helper preserves existing Secrets by default and never resets database passwords or replaces existing databases.

Chapter 12

Interview preparation

Build a project pitch, answer follow-ups, and practise concrete scenarios.

Build an answer from a request, not a technology list

Begin with the user and business operation. Name the owning service and data. Trace authentication, authorization, any dependency calls, the transaction, and the response. Then describe one failure, the protection for it, and the remaining limitation.

Your 90-second project story

“Emerald Housing is scoped to one society. Society owns flats and building data; Member owns people, assignments, accounts, and access. Gateway provides the entry path. For a membership assignment, Member checks authority, validates the flat through Society, coordinates the write, and commits business and audit state. Redis supplies shared sessions and limits, membership locks, and a separate disposable response cache. The main tradeoffs are dependency availability, transaction duration, cache consistency, and database capacity.”

Practise in three rounds

RoundExerciseEvidence of a strong answer
5 minutesDraw the services and trace a membership assignment.Clear ownership, current permissions, and the actual transaction boundary.
10 minutesExplain concurrent writes, cache misses, and a dependency outage.Mechanism, reason, failure behavior, and a specific remaining limit.
15 minutesPropose multi-society support or higher capacity.Requirements first, followed by data changes, rollout compatibility, and measurements.

Follow-ups you should be able to answer

  • Why are there two Redis instances, and what happens if each is lost?
  • Why does a Redis lock still need database constraints?
  • How do you prevent one authenticated resident from reading another resident’s data?
  • What is the difference between an ETag, an idempotency key, and a correlation ID?
  • Why can increasing Pod replicas make the database slower?
  • Which statements describe code, which describe a verified run, and which are proposals?

The detailed questions now sit beside their relevant topic: Redis scenarios in the Redis chapter, sharding and read replicas in Data, IDOR in Security, and rollout questions in Kubernetes. Use those sections for deeper practice.
