Practise the interview in three rounds

RoundPractise aloudWhat a good answer includes
Project discussion · 5 minutesIntroduce the project, draw services, trace flat assignment.Business purpose, data ownership, one actual request and an honest limitation.
Deep dive · 15 minutesConcurrency, idempotency, ETags, caching, dependency failure and revocation.Cause → mechanism → result → trade-off, with the relevant code location.
System design · 20 minutesDesign this from requirements, then increase traffic and introduce failures.Explicit assumptions, measured capacity plan and a clear separation of implemented and proposed work.
Two users create the same membership at once. What happens?

Say it simply: The member-scoped Redis lock coordinates requests; the database row lock and uniqueness constraint protect the persisted invariant.

Explain the reason: Application checks can race. The database must enforce the rule too.

Be ready for: What if the Redis lease expires? What exact combination is unique? Where does flat validation occur?

The create succeeded, but the response was lost. What do you do?

Say it simply: Retry the same member create with the same actor-scoped idempotency key and the same body.

Explain the reason: The saved response is replayed rather than creating another member. A changed body with the same key conflicts.

Be ready for: How is idempotency different from a lock, and does it guarantee exactly-once behavior across unrelated services?

Society is slow. Do all Member operations stop?

Say it simply: Only the workflows needing that dependency directly require the Society lookup, but shared resource exhaustion can affect other operations.

Explain the reason: Bounded calls, bulkheads and short database transactions reduce the blast radius.

Be ready for: Do not hold the database transaction open during the remote flat lookup. Consider remaining shared dependency risks.

What is your most important design limitation?

Say it simply: The scope is one society, and flat validation plus assignment is not one atomic distributed transaction.

Explain the reason: Naming the limit shows you understand the boundary rather than hiding it behind patterns.

Be ready for: Describe a justified improvement, its failure modes and how you would validate it.

Five architecture questions to practise aloud

What is WebFlux, and where do we use it?

Say it simply: WebFlux supports reactive, nonblocking HTTP processing. Our API Gateway uses it to route requests and call the credential service without dedicating a thread to waiting for each network response. Member and Society use MVC and JPA.

Be ready for: Explain Mono, Flux and why a reactive Gateway does not change backend database calls. Read the full answer.

What is graceful shutdown in this project?

Say it simply: On normal shutdown, each application gives in-flight requests time to finish. All three configure graceful shutdown with 20 seconds per lifecycle phase; their Kubernetes Pods have a 30-second termination window.

Be ready for: Explain timeout limits, traffic draining and why a crash or forced kill cannot guarantee completion. Read the full answer.

Does Spring Boot guarantee business correctness, security or nonblocking services?

Say it simply: Boot supplies infrastructure. We still write active-member checks, authorization and transaction rules, and enforce uniqueness in PostgreSQL. Gateway is reactive, but Member and Society use blocking JPA/JDBC.

Be ready for: Keep MVC/JPA where appropriate, measure bottlenecks and protect the Gateway event loop. A full reactive backend would require reactive database access as well. Read the full answer.

Where do we enforce rate limits?

Say it simply: Spring API Gateway applies Redis-backed token buckets to Member and Society routes, keyed by authenticated account. Login has a separate shared unauthenticated bucket. The inspected NGINX policies configure size and timeout limits rather than request-rate limits.

Be ready for: Explain refill versus burst capacity and the fairness limitation of a shared login bucket. Read the full answer.

Why do we have NGINX, API Gateway and Redis?

Say it simply: NGINX handles the external HTTPS entrance and proxy controls. Gateway handles application routing, sessions, authentication and rate limits. Redis supplies shared sessions, rate-limit state, locks and caches; PostgreSQL persists business records.

Be ready for: Draw the request path and distinguish the coordination Redis deployment from the Society cache deployment. Read the full answer.

A debugging story you can defend

During the login setup, the Gateway and Redis were running while Member was absent. After Member started, the deployment still lacked newer account endpoints even though they existed in source. Explain the evidence: inspect Pods and routes, compare image versions, check readiness and logs, then verify login and /auth/me. This demonstrates why repository code, image contents and deployed state are different things. Describe this as an observed troubleshooting case; do not claim every newer feature was deployed.

Common statements to correct before an interview

  • “Redis is the database” → PostgreSQL owns business records; Redis has supporting roles.
  • “Gateway handles all security” → backends also enforce method/object access and current identity.
  • “Retries always make things reliable” → retries can amplify overload or duplicate unsafe writes.
  • “Multiple Pods mean everything is highly available” → databases, Redis, routing and recovery also matter.
  • “UUIDs prevent IDOR” → ownership checks prevent unauthorized access.
  • “We use Kafka/saga/sharding” → only say this for implemented features; otherwise explain a proposed design.

Readiness checklist

For each major topic, answer without reading, draw the flow, explain one failure, identify one trade-off and point to supporting code or a test. Mark a topic ready only when you can do all five. The preserved question bank follows for deeper practice.

Implementation referenceREADME

README

Converted from the project README.

Emerald Housing Society

The project documentation and learning guide, including Phases 1-8, is maintained in index.html. This README adds the Step 9 implementation notes below.

Step 9 - Resilience and Fault Tolerance

Step 9 adds bounded failure handling around Member Service's read-only Society flat lookup, and durable idempotency for member creation. This is source/configuration work only; the services, builds and Kubernetes configuration have not been run as part of this change.

Member-to-Society lookup

services/member-service/src/main/java/com/emerald/member/client/SocietyClient.java wraps each flat GET in a Resilience4j circuit breaker and semaphore bulkhead, with a bounded retry around the physical calls. It preserves the typed flat response, internal service credential and correlation ID. Membership assignment still checks the member locally, calls Society to validate the flat, then writes through MembershipWriter. The remote wait occurs outside the database transaction.

Settings are in services/member-service/src/main/resources/application.yml and are passed to Kubernetes from kubernetes/config/application-config.yaml:

SettingDefaultMeaning
SOCIETY_SERVICE_CONNECT_TIMEOUT2sMaximum time to establish a Society HTTP connection.
SOCIETY_SERVICE_READ_TIMEOUT3sMaximum wait for one Society response.
SOCIETY_RETRY_MAX_ATTEMPTS3Three total attempts: the first request plus at most two retries.
SOCIETY_RETRY_INITIAL_INTERVAL200msInitial exponential retry delay.
SOCIETY_RETRY_MULTIPLIER2.0Multiplies the delay for later attempts.
SOCIETY_RETRY_JITTER0.2Adds random variation to avoid synchronized retries.
SOCIETY_RETRY_MAX_INTERVAL1sCaps an individual retry delay.
SOCIETY_CB_WINDOW_SIZE10Number of recent physical HTTP attempts considered by the breaker.
SOCIETY_CB_MINIMUM_CALLS5Minimum calls before failure rates can open the breaker.
SOCIETY_CB_FAILURE_RATE50Failure percentage that opens the breaker.
SOCIETY_CB_SLOW_CALL_RATE60Slow-call percentage that can open it.
SOCIETY_CB_SLOW_CALL_DURATION2sDuration treated as a slow call.
SOCIETY_CB_OPEN_WAIT15sTime before the breaker permits recovery trials.
SOCIETY_CB_HALF_OPEN_CALLS2Maximum trial calls in half-open state.
SOCIETY_BULKHEAD_MAX_CONCURRENT12Concurrent flat lookups allowed per Member Service process.
SOCIETY_BULKHEAD_MAX_WAIT0msExcess lookups fail promptly instead of queueing.

Only transient transport failures and remote 502/503/504 responses are retried. Flat-not-found (404), bad requests, conflicts, malformed responses and other permanent failures are not retried. Technical dependency failures are counted by the circuit breaker; a normal 404 does not open it. When OPEN, or when the bulkhead is full, assignment returns a controlled 503 and does not save a membership. A Society response timeout maps to 504. A failed flat lookup is never treated as a valid flat.

Call order: local member check -> circuit-breaker and bulkhead permit -> bounded Society GET -> at most two transient retries with capped exponential randomized delay -> active-flat check -> local membership transaction. Retry is outside the circuit breaker, so the breaker records each physical HTTP attempt. Each Member Pod has its own circuit-breaker state; it is not shared through Redis. Member reads and other operations that use only member_db remain independent of Society availability.

Gateway rate limits, health and shutdown

The WebFlux Gateway uses Spring Cloud Gateway's Redis rate limiter for protected Member and Society routes. The authenticated key comes from the security principal; client-supplied identity headers are not used. Login attempts use a shared Redis bucket and fail closed with 503 if the limiter cannot be reached; an exhausted bucket returns 429 with Retry-After. Default login settings are 2 new tokens/second with burst 5; Kubernetes overrides these to 1/second with burst 3. Member route defaults are 10/second with burst 20; Society route defaults are 20/second with burst 40. These are learning defaults and should be tuned to measured traffic. The unauthenticated login bucket is shared, so all unauthenticated login attempts use the same bucket.

Gateway response timeout defaults to 28 seconds; NGINX upstream read/send timeouts are 32 seconds to leave outer-layer overhead around the Member retry budget and the backend's session-validation call. These are client/proxy wait limits, not a single end-to-end deadline. Member Service has bounded Tomcat threads, connections and accept queue, a zero-wait dependency bulkhead, graceful shutdown with a 20-second phase timeout, and existing Kubernetes readiness/liveness probes. Member readiness checks its local database; Society availability does not control Member liveness. Actuator metrics are exposed by the applications, while Gateway metrics require ADMIN authorization. Resilience4j circuit-breaker, retry and bulkhead meters are bound to Micrometer. The Society client records physical lookup duration and timeout counts; Gateway records 429 responses by fixed route name and login limiter errors. Do not use member IDs, session IDs or idempotency keys as metric labels.

Durable member-creation idempotency

POST /api/v1/members accepts an optional Idempotency-Key (1 to 128 letters, digits, period, underscore, colon or hyphen). Without it, the existing create behavior remains. With it, Member Service scopes the key to the authenticated admin and CREATE_MEMBER, fingerprints the normalized request with SHA-256, and stores the response with the create in one member_db transaction. Repeating the same key and payload returns the stored logical response and Idempotency-Replayed: true; using that key with different input returns 409. A unique database constraint coordinates concurrent submissions, so a repeated request cannot create two members.

The new migration is services/member-service/src/main/resources/db/migration/V3__create_idempotency_records.sql; earlier migrations are unchanged. Records expire after 24 hours by default (IDEMPOTENCY_RECORD_TTL). Expired rows are removed opportunistically during a later idempotent create; no background cleanup job is configured. The stored response can contain member contact fields, so keep the retention short, restrict database access, and account for this data in database backup/retention policy.

Member creation and flat assignment are not automatically retried. Idempotency makes a repeated create safe only when the client deliberately resubmits the same key and payload; it does not justify a Gateway retry. Assignment is a mutation with mandatory live flat validation, so any dependency failure fails the request without persisting membership.

Manual verification guide (not run here)

  1. Send a valid assignment while Society is available; confirm the membership is stored in member_db.
  2. Stop or make Society unavailable, then send an assignment. Expect a controlled 503/504 and verify that no membership row was added. Do not retry the POST automatically.
  3. While Society remains unavailable, retrieve a member through the Gateway. That local database operation should still work.
  4. Repeat safe flat GET attempts during an outage. After the configured threshold, that Member Pod's breaker should OPEN and reject lookups without contacting Society. After 15 seconds, limited HALF_OPEN trials can check recovery; successful trials close the breaker.
  5. To observe bulkhead rejection, create controlled concurrent assignment traffic above 12 calls for one Member Pod. Excess validation work should fail promptly with 503; do not use production traffic for this exercise.
  6. Send a short burst of Society/member API requests and login attempts through Gateway; inspect 429 and Retry-After. Shared rate limiting requires Redis to be reachable.
  7. Create a member with an Idempotency-Key, repeat the exact request, then reuse that key with a changed email. Expect the same member response on replay and 409 for the changed payload.
  8. Inspect protected Actuator metrics for Resilience4j circuit-breaker, retry, bulkhead, rate-limit and idempotency measurements. Circuit state can differ between Member Pods.

The combined collection postman/Emerald-Housing-Option-B.postman_collection.json now includes manual idempotency examples and outage/bulkhead descriptions. The local environment has example idempotency variables; replace the mobile number with an unused value before creating a member. No Postman scripts were added.

Verification status

Static source/configuration review only. No test files were created, edited or executed. No Maven/Gradle commands, Docker builds, Kubernetes commands, application starts or Postman collection runs were performed. Runtime behavior, dependency compatibility, rate limiting, Flyway V3 execution, failure timing, and metrics still require manual verification in the configured environment.

Source documentREADME.md

README.md

Emerald Housing

Members, homes and society operations—connected through a service-based Java application.

Java 21

Spring Boot

PostgreSQL

Redis

Kubernetes

Emerald Housing is a housing society management project with dedicated Member and Society services behind an API Gateway. It brings together member records, flats, floors and memberships, with backend support for scoped permissions, auditing, search and concurrent updates.

Features · Architecture · Build and tests · API guide · Repository map · Deployment resources

Features

AreaBackend capabilities
Society operationsSociety, floor and flat information
Member managementMember records, memberships and account mappings
Scoped accessAdministrator, society administrator, committee and resident roles
Audit historyTransactional audit events for business and permission changes
Search and navigationServer-side filters, page-number queries and cursor pagination
Concurrent updatesJPA versioning and ETag-based conditional writes
Integration checksShared consumer/provider contract fixtures

Current scope: the documented deployment serves one society, EMERALD. It is not a multi-tenant schema. Account management, audit history, server search and conditional updates are documented backend features; existing frontend screens do not yet expose all of them.

Architecture

flowchart TD
    UI[HTML interface] --> Gateway[API Gateway]
    Gateway --> Member[Member Service]
    Gateway --> Society[Society Service]
    Member -->|Flat validation| Society
    Member --> Data[(PostgreSQL persistence)]
    Society --> Data

This is a logical overview; PostgreSQL database settings and deployment topology are defined by each service and its manifests.

ComponentResponsibility
API GatewayAPI entry point, routing and session/account permission validation
Member ServiceMembers, memberships, application accounts and related auditing
Society ServiceSociety, floor and flat information, with flat auditing
PostgreSQL and FlywayRelational persistence and versioned database migrations
RedisSession/cache and coordination mechanisms described in the backend guide
HTML interfacesChecked-in browser interface; an additional interface file is also included

Backend services independently validate sessions and enforce record-level permissions. See the access and query guide for the precise behavior and limitations.

Build and tests

Prerequisites
  • JDK 21 and Git.
  • Network access for Maven dependencies and the wrapper distribution.
  • Docker running for Testcontainers PostgreSQL integration tests.
  • Kubernetes and kubectl when exploring the deployment manifests.
git clone https://github.com/devendraghag/emerald-housing.git
cd emerald-housing
java -version
Build a service

The repository contains separate Maven applications. For example, build Society Service from its own directory.

Windows PowerShell

cd services/society-service
.\mvnw.cmd clean package

macOS / Linux

cd services/society-service
sh mvnw clean package

A successful build packages the service; it does not provision PostgreSQL, Redis or a running deployment. Review the service configuration and Kubernetes resources before starting the application.

Verify service contracts

From the repository root on Windows:

powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File .\scripts\test-contracts.ps1

The script runs Member consumer and Society provider checks against the shared flat-service contract. No separately hosted contract broker is required.

PostgreSQL integration tests cover migrations, scoped search, cursor continuation, audit rollback and concurrent entity updates. These tests require Docker; the backend guide notes that Testcontainers tests are skipped when Docker is unavailable. A skipped integration test is not evidence of a passing database integration.

API guide

The existing backend access and query documentation explains authentication context, request examples, response behavior and operational caveats.

WorkflowExample route
Create an application accountPOST /api/v1/accounts
Update account permissionsPUT /api/v1/accounts/{accountId}/permissions
Search membersGET /api/v1/members/page?search=asha&status=ACTIVE&page=0&size=20
Browse flats with a cursorGET /api/v1/flats/cursor?limit=20&floorNumber=1
Read member audit historyGET /api/v1/members/audit?entityType=MEMBER&limit=50
Read an individual flatGET /api/v1/flats/{flatId}

These routes require the appropriate authenticated session and permissions. Mutation requests use the existing CSRF protection. For conditional member/flat updates, retrieve the current ETag and send it with If-Match; see the guide for compatibility settings and conflict responses.

Repository map

PathWhat to explore
services/Member and Society backend applications
infrastructure/api-gateway/Gateway application
contracts/Shared service contract fixtures
docs/Backend feature and Java language documentation
postman/API exploration resources
kubernetes/Deployment configuration
kubernetes-observability/Observability resources
scripts/Contract and operational helpers

Deployment resources

The repository includes Dockerfiles, Kubernetes manifests, rolling update configuration and observability resources. Review their environment-specific values and required secrets before deployment.

For changes affecting session representations or internal authorization, the backend guide calls for compatible Gateway and backend versions and a fresh sign-in after deployment. Keep internal service endpoints within the trusted service path.

Explore the engineering

  • Authorization: follow how account permissions become scoped backend access.
  • Consistency: examine transactional auditing and optimistic concurrency control.
  • Service boundaries: compare the shared contract with consumer/provider verification.
  • Operations: inspect the separation of core deployment and observability resources.

Start with the backend feature guide, then review the Java language feature notes and service source.

Original Markdown source
# Emerald Housing

**Members, homes and society operations—connected through a service-based Java application.**

![Java 21](https://img.shields.io/badge/Java-21-ED8B00?style=flat-square)
![Spring Boot](https://img.shields.io/badge/Spring_Boot-6DB33F?style=flat-square&logo=springboot&logoColor=white)
![PostgreSQL](https://img.shields.io/badge/PostgreSQL-4169E1?style=flat-square&logo=postgresql&logoColor=white)
![Redis](https://img.shields.io/badge/Redis-DC382D?style=flat-square&logo=redis&logoColor=white)
![Kubernetes](https://img.shields.io/badge/Kubernetes-326CE5?style=flat-square&logo=kubernetes&logoColor=white)

Emerald Housing is a housing society management project with dedicated Member and Society services behind an API Gateway. It brings together member records, flats, floors and memberships, with backend support for scoped permissions, auditing, search and concurrent updates.

[Features](#features) · [Architecture](#architecture) · [Build and tests](#build-and-tests) · [API guide](#api-guide) · [Repository map](#repository-map) · [Deployment resources](#deployment-resources)

## Features

| Area | Backend capabilities |
| --- | --- |
| Society operations | Society, floor and flat information |
| Member management | Member records, memberships and account mappings |
| Scoped access | Administrator, society administrator, committee and resident roles |
| Audit history | Transactional audit events for business and permission changes |
| Search and navigation | Server-side filters, page-number queries and cursor pagination |
| Concurrent updates | JPA versioning and ETag-based conditional writes |
| Integration checks | Shared consumer/provider contract fixtures |

> **Current scope:** the documented deployment serves one society, **EMERALD**. It is not a multi-tenant schema. Account management, audit history, server search and conditional updates are documented backend features; existing frontend screens do not yet expose all of them.

## Architecture

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    UI[HTML interface] --> Gateway[API Gateway]
    Gateway --> Member[Member Service]
    Gateway --> Society[Society Service]
    Member -->|Flat validation| Society
    Member --> Data[(PostgreSQL persistence)]
    Society --> Data
```

This is a logical overview; PostgreSQL database settings and deployment topology are defined by each service and its manifests.

| Component | Responsibility |
| --- | --- |
| [API Gateway](infrastructure/api-gateway) | API entry point, routing and session/account permission validation |
| [Member Service](services/member-service) | Members, memberships, application accounts and related auditing |
| [Society Service](services/society-service) | Society, floor and flat information, with flat auditing |
| PostgreSQL and Flyway | Relational persistence and versioned database migrations |
| Redis | Session/cache and coordination mechanisms described in the backend guide |
| [HTML interfaces](indexLatest.html) | Checked-in browser interface; an [additional interface file](index.html) is also included |

Backend services independently validate sessions and enforce record-level permissions. See the [access and query guide](docs/backend-access-and-query-features.md) for the precise behavior and limitations.

## Build and tests

### Prerequisites

- JDK 21 and Git.
- Network access for Maven dependencies and the wrapper distribution.
- Docker running for Testcontainers PostgreSQL integration tests.
- Kubernetes and kubectl when exploring the deployment manifests.

```bash
git clone https://github.com/devendraghag/emerald-housing.git
cd emerald-housing
java -version
```

### Build a service

The repository contains separate Maven applications. For example, build Society Service from its own directory.

**Windows PowerShell**

```powershell
cd services/society-service
.\mvnw.cmd clean package
```

**macOS / Linux**

```bash
cd services/society-service
sh mvnw clean package
```

A successful build packages the service; it does not provision PostgreSQL, Redis or a running deployment. Review the service configuration and Kubernetes resources before starting the application.

### Verify service contracts

From the repository root on Windows:

```powershell
powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File .\scripts\test-contracts.ps1
```

The script runs Member consumer and Society provider checks against the shared [flat-service contract](contracts/member-to-society-flat-v1.json). No separately hosted contract broker is required.

PostgreSQL integration tests cover migrations, scoped search, cursor continuation, audit rollback and concurrent entity updates. These tests require Docker; the backend guide notes that Testcontainers tests are skipped when Docker is unavailable. A skipped integration test is not evidence of a passing database integration.

## API guide

| --- | --- |
| Create an application account | `POST /api/v1/accounts` |
| Update account permissions | `PUT /api/v1/accounts/{accountId}/permissions` |
| Search members | `GET /api/v1/members/page?search=asha&status=ACTIVE&page=0&size=20` |
| Browse flats with a cursor | `GET /api/v1/flats/cursor?limit=20&floorNumber=1` |
| Read member audit history | `GET /api/v1/members/audit?entityType=MEMBER&limit=50` |
| Read an individual flat | `GET /api/v1/flats/{flatId}` |

These routes require the appropriate authenticated session and permissions. Mutation requests use the existing CSRF protection. For conditional member/flat updates, retrieve the current ETag and send it with `If-Match`; see the guide for compatibility settings and conflict responses.
